### [1] [Rethinking Handwritten Character Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10572)
*Ranjit Raut,Aarav Subedi,Ashim Shrestha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces GraphemeNet, a unified multi-script architecture for non-Latin handwritten character recognition (HCR) that leverages structural-prior efficiency—the principle that explicitly encoding script-geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases improves accuracy while requiring fewer parameters than generic image classification approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Latin handwritten character recognition remains understudied. Existing dominant methods treat HCR as generic image classification, relying on large model scales to implicitly learn stroke structure. This work proposes that explicitly encoding script-geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases (structural-prior efficiency) can achieve better accuracy with fewer parameters.

Method: GraphemeNet uses two orthogonal binary axes: (1) Persistent Scaffold Injection (PSI) - a script-specific asymmetric convolution that injects a stroke scaffold as a weighted residual at every encoder stage, anchoring learned features to script geometry; (2) A selection mechanism between global average pooling with gated fusion and cross-scale attention with a Stroke Topology Module (STM), depending on whether glyph discrimination requires spatial relational reasoning. A Linear Capsule Routing (LCR) with O(n) routing is shared universally across scripts.

Result: The architecture was evaluated on fourteen benchmarks across eight writing systems. It consistently outperformed published baselines while only varying scaffold and decoder topology per script, demonstrating that structural-prior efficiency is a broadly applicable principle for multi-script HCR.

Conclusion: Structural-prior efficiency is a broadly applicable principle for multi-script HCR. GraphemeNet achieves superior performance across diverse writing systems with minimal script-specific modifications, establishing that explicitly encoding geometric regularities as inductive biases outperforms implicit learning through model scaling.

Abstract: Non-Latin handwritten character recognition (HCR) remains understudied. Dominant methods consider it as generic image classification, which uses model scale to implicitly learn stroke structure. Structural-prior efficiency---the principle that explicitly encoding script-geometric regularities as architectural inductive biases can be both more accurate and require fewer parameters. We introduce GraphemeNet, a unified multi-script architecture, governed by two orthogonal binary axes. Axis 1 operationalises stroke-level geometric regularity via Persistent Scaffold Injection (PSI): a script-specific asymmetric convolution injects a stroke scaffold as a weighted residual at every encoder stage, continuously anchoring learned features to script geometry---distinct from skip connections, auxiliary losses, or attention reweighting. Axis 2 selects between global average pooling with gated fusion and cross-scale attention with a Stroke Topology Module (STM), depending on whether glyph discrimination requires spatial relational reasoning. A Linear Capsule Routing (LCR) with $O(n)$ routing is shared universally. On fourteen benchmarks across eight writing systems, the architecture generalises with only scaffold and decoder topology varying per script, consistently challenging, outperforming published baselines, and establishing structural-prior efficiency as a broadly applicable principle for multi-script HCR.

</details>


### [2] [AcFlow: Controlling Text-to-Image Diffusion Transformers via Learned Conditional Activation Flow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10723)
*Junran Wang,Zehao Jin,Tianyu Luan,Xinjie Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: AcFlow is an inference-time controller for text-to-image diffusion transformers that enables style intensity control and concept suppression by transporting intermediate layer activations through a learned concept-conditioned velocity field, without modifying the base DiT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct prompting in text-to-image diffusion transformers provides limited control over style intensity and struggles to suppress unwanted concepts. There is a need for an inference-time controller that can flexibly modulate style strength and suppress specific concepts without per-concept fine-tuning.

Method: AcFlow introduces a concept-conditioned velocity field that operates on intermediate layer image-token activations. The method uses a textual concept description to specify the intervention, with an integration horizon serving as a continuous control parameter. The velocity field produces token-varying, activation-dependent updates. Parameters are shared across concepts within each task family, enabling generalization to unseen concepts without per-concept fitting.

Result: AcFlow achieves the best style-content trade-off among evaluated baselines in the high-style-alignment regime. At a fixed operating point, AcFlow attains style-content alignment of 0.5365/0.2860, compared with 0.4397/0.2684 for the baseline with the highest style alignment. Qualitative results demonstrate suppression of diverse concepts, including cases where direct prompting fails.

Conclusion: The learned velocity field serves as an adaptive control mechanism with update directions varying across tokens and depending on their activation states. AcFlow enables fine-grained control over style intensity and concept suppression while generalizing to unseen concepts without requiring per-concept fitting.

Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion transformers (DiTs) are powerful generators, yet direct prompting provides limited control interface for style intensity and can fail to suppress unwanted concepts. To enable these controls, we introduce AcFlow, an inference-time controller that transports intermediate layer image-token activations through a learned concept-conditioned velocity field while keeping the base DiT frozen. A textual concept description specifies the desired intervention, while the integration horizon provides a continuous control parameter. The field produces token-varying, activation-dependent updates. With parameters shared across concepts within each task family, the field supports fine-grained descriptions and generalizes to concepts unseen during training without per-concept fitting. On style control, AcFlow achieves the best style--content trade-off among the evaluated baselines in the high-style-alignment regime. At a fixed operating point, AcFlow attains style--content alignment of 0.5365/0.2860, compared with 0.4397/0.2684 for the baseline with the highest style alignment. Qualitative results demonstrate suppression of diverse concepts, including cases where direct prompting fails. Our analyses support the learned velocity field as an adaptive control mechanism, with update directions varying across tokens and depend on their activation states. Our code is available at https://github.com/Nove1yst/AcFlow.

</details>


### [3] [MHE-Former: Multi-Hypothesis Transformers via Entropy Maximization for 3D Mesh Recovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10743)
*Boshu Jia,Rongyu Chen,Linlin Yang,Zihao Liu,Yingjie Chen,Zhongqun Zhang,Zhulin Tao,Shaohui Lin,Xiaoyu Wu,Libiao Jin,Baochang Zhang,Angela Yao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces an exploration-exploitation paradigm for monocular 3D hand and body mesh recovery, addressing occlusion and ambiguity issues through multi-hypothesis learning. The framework consists of MHE-Former for generating diverse hypotheses during exploration, and a VLM-based hypothesis selection process for exploitation, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Monocular 3D hand and body mesh recovery often suffers from severe occlusion and ambiguity. Traditional deterministic methods regress a single optimal solution, leading to overconfident predictions that fail to handle these challenging conditions.

Method: The paper proposes an exploration-exploitation paradigm with two components: (1) MHE-Former - a Transformer-based multi-hypothesis framework during exploration, using probabilistic formulation and entropy maximization to generate plausible and diverse hypotheses with high training efficiency; (2) Hypothesis Selection - a context-aware process leveraging VLM's visual understanding and reasoning capabilities, allowing users to select the most plausible estimate with additional evidence and natural language intent.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that the framework achieves state-of-the-art performance in accuracy and diversity across multiple datasets. The user preference study further shows the practicality of the hypothesis selection process.

Conclusion: The proposed exploration-exploitation paradigm with multi-hypothesis learning and VLM-based selection effectively addresses the ambiguity problem in monocular 3D mesh recovery, providing both accurate and diverse predictions while offering practical user control through natural language intent.

Abstract: Monocular 3D hand and body mesh recovery often suffers from severe occlusion and ambiguity. Traditional deterministic methods typically regress a single optimal solution, leading to overconfident predictions. In this paper, we introduce an exploration--exploitation paradigm for ambiguous mesh recovery with multi-hypothesis learning and selection. Specifically, during exploration, based on our probabilistic formulation and entropy maximization, we propose a novel multi-hypothesis method referred to as MHE-Former. It is a Transformer-based multi-hypothesis framework, ensuring high training efficiency and label friendliness while generating plausible and diverse hypotheses. During exploitation, we propose Hypothesis Selection, a context-aware process for multiple predictions. Especially leveraging VLM's powerful visual understanding and reasoning capabilities, it allows users to choose the most plausible and desired estimate with additional evidence and natural language intent. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our framework achieves state-of-the-art performance in accuracy and diversity across multiple datasets. The user preference study further shows the practicality of our hypothesis selection process.

</details>


### [4] [Meta-Learning for Data-Efficient Plant Growth Estimation via Vision Transformers and Fuzzy Clustering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10749)
*Sheikh Hasan Elahi,Rusith Chamara Hathurusinghe Dewage,Habib Ullah,Muhammad Salman Siddiqui,Rakibul Islam,Fadi Al Machot*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a few-shot regression framework for plant growth estimation in greenhouse monitoring, addressing the challenge of costly labeled data. The approach combines Vision Transformer (ViT) embeddings, fuzzy c-means clustering for task construction, and gradient-based meta-learning (MAML++). Key finding: task construction in embedding space is the primary performance driver, and second-order meta-learning methods outperform classical baselines under severe label scarcity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Obtaining labeled data for plant growth estimation in greenhouse monitoring is costly and time-consuming, creating a need for methods that can learn effectively from very few labeled samples.

Method: The framework combines: (1) Vision Transformer (ViT) for feature extraction, (2) fuzzy c-means clustering in embedding space for task construction from unlabeled image pools, and (3) gradient-based meta-learning (MAML++) for few-shot learning. The approach leverages structured tasks created through clustering to enable efficient learning from small numbers of labeled samples.

Result: Second-order meta-learning methods (MAML++ variants) outperform classical baselines in the few-shot regime. Task construction in embedding space is identified as the primary driver of performance. Intra-cluster support selection has limited and dataset-dependent impact.

Conclusion: Structured task design combined with meta-learning enables reliable plant growth estimation under severe label scarcity, demonstrating the viability of few-shot learning for greenhouse monitoring applications.

Abstract: Accurate plant growth estimation is essential for greenhouse monitoring, yet obtaining labeled data remains costly and time-consuming. To address this, we propose a few-shot regression framework that combines Vision Transformer (ViT) feature embeddings, clustering-based task construction, and gradient-based meta-learning, and show that task construction in embedding space is a primary driver of performance. The approach leverages an unlabeled image pool to organize data into structured tasks using fuzzy c-means clustering, enabling efficient learning from a small number of labeled samples. We systematically evaluate meta-learning methods and show that second-order methods (e.g., Model-Agnostic Meta-Learning variants such as MAML++) outperform classical baselines in the few-shot regime. Furthermore, intra-cluster support selection has a limited and dataset-dependent impact. Experiments on two plant datasets show that structured task design combined with meta-learning enables reliable plant growth estimation under severe label scarcity.

</details>


### [5] [Learning Interaction between Image and Layout Priors for Joint Image-Layout Generation in Design Templates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11519)
*Shirong Yang,Bo Yang,Ying Cao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: InterIL is a joint image-layout diffusion model for graphic design template generation that connects pretrained image and layout models with a learnable communication module to capture bidirectional image-layout interactions, eliminating the limitations of sequential generation approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior sequential paradigms for graphic design generation fail to capture the dependency between background and layout, limiting the quality of generated design templates by not properly modeling the joint image-layout distribution.

Method: The model connects pretrained image and layout diffusion model backbones with a learnable communication module that enables bidirectional image-layout interaction. During training, both backbones are frozen to preserve pretrained prior knowledge, while only the communication module is updated to learn cross-modal interactions. A test-time guidance strategy is also introduced for user preference enforcement.

Result: InterIL generates significantly better results in terms of image quality, layout quality, and image-layout harmonization compared to prior approaches, producing outputs closer to real samples. The model also demonstrates flexibility in enforcing user preferences at inference without retraining.

Conclusion: The proposed joint generative approach effectively captures the joint image-layout distribution, achieving superior composition harmony. The design-agnostic architecture preserves realistic design characteristics, and the test-time guidance enables customizable outputs without retraining, showing promise for practical graphic design applications.

Abstract: In this paper, we address the problem of graphic design template creation, which generates a background image and a layout of foreground elements over the background to form a harmonious composition from an input text.
  Prior work on graphic design generation mostly adopts a sequential paradigm, where design elements are generated sequentially. We argue that such a sequential scheme falls short of faithfully capturing the dependency between the background and layout (and thus the joint image-layout distribution), which limits the quality of generated design templates.
  To overcome this limitation, we propose a model, InterIL, which jointly generates the two modalities, background image and layout, in a single generative process. The novel design of our joint model connects the backbones of pretrained image and layout diffusion models with a learnable communication module to explicitly model bidirectional image-layout interaction. During training, the image and layout backbones are frozen to maintain and leverage the vast pretrained single-modality prior knowledge, while only the communication module is updated, so that the model can focus on learning image-layout interaction and thereby better capture the joint image-layout distribution for improved composition harmony.
  Our model has no design-specific inductive bias, which allows it to better preserve the original characteristics of realistic designs. We further introduce a test-time guidance strategy to enable users to impose their specific preferences on generated results.
  Our experiments show that, compared with prior approaches, our model can generate significantly better results in terms of image, layout and image-layout harmonization, producing outputs closer to real samples. We also demonstrate the flexibility of our model in enforcing user preferences at inference without retraining.

</details>


### [6] [GRADE: Single-Frame Generative Radar Depth Estimation Under Visual Degradation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10756)
*Bin Zhao,Patrick Chiou,Nakul Garg*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GRADE is a method for estimating high-fidelity metric depth using mmWave radar under adverse visibility conditions (smoke, fog, darkness) where optical sensors fail. It combines radar geometry with a latent diffusion model and a pixel-space adapter trained on data from 12 buildings with real smoke.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense 3D depth perception fails under smoke, fog, and darkness because optical sensors cannot penetrate airborne particulates. While mmWave radar remains usable and measures range accurately under these conditions, its small aperture limits angular resolution. This limits the ability to obtain high-fidelity depth maps in visibility-degraded environments.

Method: GRADE uses a three-stage approach: 1) Map raw 4D radar spectra to coarse metric depth; 2) Use a latent diffusion backbone to recover structural detail while conditioning every denoising step on the radar estimate; 3) Apply a pixel-space adapter that uses residual camera cues when available, trained across clear, smoke-degraded, and occluded inputs so the output approaches the radar-conditioned path as visibility degrades.

Result: GRADE achieves an MAE of 0.303 m in clear scenes and 0.313 m under smoke, outperforming existing baselines. The method was trained and evaluated on ~95K frames across 12 buildings with real smoke.

Conclusion: GRADE successfully leverages pretrained generative priors grounded in radar geometry to estimate high-fidelity metric depth. The minimal performance degradation (0.303 m to 0.313 m MAE) from clear to smoke conditions demonstrates robust depth perception under adverse visibility, outperforming existing baselines.

Abstract: Dense 3D depth perception fails under smoke, fog, and darkness because optical sensors cannot penetrate airborne particulates. mmWave radar remains usable and measures range accurately under these conditions, but its small aperture limits angular resolution. We present GRADE, which grounds a pretrained generative prior in single-frame radar geometry to estimate high-fidelity metric depth. GRADE first maps raw 4D radar spectra to coarse metric depth. A latent diffusion backbone then recovers structural detail while conditioning every denoising step on this estimate. A pixel-space adapter uses residual camera cues when available and is trained across clear, smoke-degraded, and occluded inputs so the full output approaches the radar-conditioned path as visibility degrades. Trained and evaluated on ~95K frames across 12 buildings with real smoke, GRADE achieves an MAE of 0.303 m in clear scenes and 0.313 m under smoke, outperforming existing baselines. Code and datasets are available at https://phi-lab-rice.github.io/GRADE.

</details>


### [7] [3D Point Splatting for mmWave Radar Novel View Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11894)
*Adnan Armouti,Yixuan Gao,Rajalakshmi Nandakumar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: 3D Point Splatting (3DPS) is the first differentiable point renderer for millimeter-wave radar novel view synthesis, derived from the standard solid-angle form of the radar equation. It uses ITU-R P.2040 material models with complex-valued outputs, achieving 0.587 mean Pearson correlation on held-out range-azimuth images—1.7x to 5.2x better than three optical-NVS baselines—while training in ~3 minutes per scene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solving novel view synthesis (NVS) for mmWave radar requires a renderer that is physically faithful, complex-valued, and multi-viewpoint-tractable. No prior method achieves these three properties simultaneously. Differentiable Monte Carlo ray tracers don't scale to multi-view optimization, while optical-NVS methods (NeRF variants, hash grids, 3D Gaussians) discard phase information and replace explicit material modeling with opaque learned features, restricting them to power-only range-azimuth magnitudes.

Method: 3DPS is derived directly from the standard solid-angle form of the radar equation. Each oriented 3D point carries an ITU-R P.2040 material model, evaluated in closed form, with the resulting complex phasor splatted into range bins through a precomputed point spread function (PSF). The complex-valued output makes the renderer product-agnostic, yielding ADC, complex range profile (CRP), and range-azimuth outputs through standard FFT pipelines without retraining.

Result: On six outdoor ColoRadar scenes, 3DPS achieves 0.587 mean Pearson correlation on held-out RA images, which is between 1.7x and 5.2x the performance of three optical-NVS baselines (RadarSplat, Radar Fields, DART). Training takes approximately 3 minutes per scene on a single RTX 4090.

Conclusion: 3DPS successfully achieves the three desired properties (physically faithful, complex-valued, multi-viewpoint-tractable) for mmWave radar rendering. Its differentiable point-based architecture enables efficient multi-view optimization while maintaining physical fidelity through explicit material modeling. The product-agnostic output supports multiple radar formats through standard processing pipelines, demonstrating significant improvement over prior optical-NVS approaches adapted for radar.

Abstract: Solving novel view synthesis (NVS) for millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar requires a renderer that is physically faithful, complex-valued, and multi-viewpoint-tractable. No prior method achieves these three properties simultaneously. Differentiable Monte Carlo (MC) ray tracers implement the radar forward model directly with explicit material modeling and complex outputs, but do not scale to the multi-view optimization NVS demands. Optical-NVS ports of NeRF, hash grids, and 3D Gaussians train fast but discard phase and replace explicit material modeling with opaque learned features, restricting them to power-only range-azimuth (RA) magnitudes. We propose 3D Point Splatting (3DPS), the first differentiable point renderer for radar, derived directly from the standard solid-angle form of the radar equation. Each oriented 3D point carries an ITU-R P.2040 material model, evaluated in closed form, with the resulting complex phasor splatted into range bins through a precomputed point spread function (PSF). The complex-valued output makes the renderer product-agnostic. The same optimized scene yields analog-to-digital converter (ADC), complex range profile (CRP), and RA outputs through standard fast Fourier transform (FFT) pipelines without retraining for each format. On six outdoor ColoRadar scenes, 3DPS reaches 0.587 mean Pearson correlation on held-out RA images. This is between 1.7x and 5.2x the three optical-NVS baselines (RadarSplat, Radar Fields, DART). Training takes approximately 3 minutes per scene on a single RTX 4090.

</details>


### [8] [Shedding Light: A Benchmark for Evaluating Lighting Understanding in Generative Image Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10787)
*Justine Giroux,Jack Oliver Hilliard,Yannick Hold-Geoffroy,Javier Vazquez-Corral,Jean-François Lalonde*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a benchmark to assess lighting understanding and harmonization capabilities of generative models by evaluating how well they insert novel objects into real photographs while maintaining consistent illumination, using multi-illumination datasets with light probes as the evaluation method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite progress in realistic image synthesis, it remains unclear whether generative models truly understand lighting in a physically accurate manner. The authors aim to address this gap by creating a scalable evaluation protocol to systematically assess photometric accuracy.

Method: The approach uses multi-illumination datasets with images containing simple objects as "light probes." Models are prompted to inpaint the same object onto the original image, then the generated results are compared against ground-truth light probes. Lighting direction, color, and radiance distribution are estimated from the inpainted probes to provide quantitative measures.

Result: The paper establishes a scalable evaluation protocol for assessing how well generative models capture and reproduce real-world lighting, providing a foundation for benchmarking photometric accuracy of future models.

Conclusion: The proposed benchmark offers a systematic way to evaluate lighting understanding in generative models through object insertion tasks, enabling quantitative measurement of illumination accuracy and photometric realism.

Abstract: Accurate modelling of illumination is central to realistic image synthesis and scene understanding. Yet, there is little exploration into whether image generative models are good at this task or whether physical plausibility remains a key challenge for them. Clearly, significant progress has been made in realistic image synthesis, but do models truly understand lighting in a physically accurate manner? To answer this question, this work proposes a benchmark to assess the lighting understanding and harmonisation capabilities of generative models. Our key insight is that evaluating lighting understanding for such models only requires testing how well they insert novel objects into real photographs whilst maintaining consistent illumination. To do so, we use a multi-illumination dataset with images containing simple objects serving as ``light probes'', and prompt models to inpaint the same object onto the original image, then compare the generated results against the ground-truth light probes. We then estimate the lighting direction, colour and radiance distribution from the inpainted probes, providing a quantitative measure of illumination accuracy and photometric realism. Our work establishes a scalable evaluation protocol to systematically assess how well generative models capture and reproduce real-world lighting, offering a foundation for benchmarking the photometric accuracy of any future models. All code and data are available at https://lvsn.github.io/SheddingLight/ .

</details>


### [9] [Two-Parameter Flow Map Learning for Continuous-Time Diffeomorphic Image Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10789)
*Mohammadjavad Matinkia,Nilanjan Ray*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A deep learning framework for diffeomorphic medical image registration that learns non-autonomous ODEs as two-parameter flow maps by enforcing cocycle consistency, eliminating the need for time discretization during training while achieving state-of-the-art results across multiple imaging modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional learning-based diffeomorphic registration methods rely on stationary velocity fields parameterized as autonomous ODEs, while more expressive time-dependent (non-autonomous) ODEs suffer from implicit flow structure that entangles model expressiveness with discretization accuracy. There was a need for a framework that can directly learn continuous-time solutions without numerical integration constraints.

Method: The proposed framework learns the continuous-time solution of a non-autonomous ODE by formulating it as a two-parameter flow map. Cocycle consistency—a fundamental structural property of time-varying flows—is enforced during training to learn flow maps without time discretization and velocity integration. At inference, diffeomorphic mappings are recovered using a small number of compositions. The method is designed to be compatible with standard registration backbones.

Result: The method consistently improves alignment accuracy across nine datasets while preserving diffeomorphic structure. Specifically: 2.1% average Dice improvement on brain MRI benchmarks, 12% TRE reduction on lung CT, and 2.6% Dice gain on cardiac MRI and ultrasound datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully integrates non-autonomous ODEs into diffeomorphic registration, achieving superior accuracy across diverse medical imaging modalities (brain MRI, lung CT, cardiac MRI, ultrasound) while maintaining diffeomorphic properties. It seamlessly incorporates standard registration backbones and demonstrates consistent improvements through the principled use of cocycle consistency.

Abstract: Diffeomorphic image registration is central to medical image analysis, enabling anatomically consistent alignment across subjects. Most learning-based diffeomorphic methods model autonomous ODEs(ordinary differential equations) by parameterizing a stationary velocity field and recovering deformations via scaling-and-squaring. While non-autonomous ODEs with time-dependent velocities increase expressiveness, existing approaches rely on numerical integration to implicitly enforce flow structure that entangles model expressiveness with discretization accuracy. We propose a framework to directly learn the continuous-time solution of a non-autonomous ODE formulated as a two-parameterflow map. By enforcing cocycle consistency, a fundamental structural property of time-varying flows, we learn the flow maps without time discretization and velocity integration during training. The framework recovers diffeomorphic mappings at inference using a small number of compositions. Our proposed framework seamlessly incorporates standard registration backbones and improves alignment accuracy consistently across nine datasets while preserving diffeomorphic structure. Notably, the proposed method achieves an average Dice improvement of 2.1% on brain MRI benchmarks, a 12% TRE reduction on lung CT, and a 2.6% Dice gain on cardiac MRI and ultrasound datasets.

</details>


### [10] [How Much Velocity Does Off-Ball Space Value Need? A Broadcast-Viewport Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10801)
*Seongjin Choi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates at which layer of broadcast off-ball analysis velocity changes the answer for pitch control in soccer. Using Metrica match data with a 44m viewport, the authors test four velocity regimes across three layers (imputation, control surface, team verdicts). They find velocity is nearly useless for imputation (-0.2 pp), first-order for the control surface (-1.5 to -1.8 pp, 11-15% of MAE), and ten times smaller for team verdicts (-0.12 to -0.19 pp). The visible channel velocity is what matters most, with perfect occluded-player velocity adding only 2-6% of visible gain. Omitting velocity blurs the surface but has small time-averaged bias, while imputation error shows structured bias against the defending team's deep zone. A noise ladder experiment shows break-even at ~1 m/s, and real SoccerNet-GSR data (sigma_v=1.65 m/s) recovers 24-36% of the benefit.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard velocity-aware pitch control leaves half the players off-screen in broadcast viewports, and on-screen velocities come from drifting calibration. The authors investigate at which layer of broadcast off-ball analysis velocity actually matters - imputation, control surface, or team verdicts.

Method: The study inherits an off-screen imputation protocol using three Metrica matches with a 44m viewport and block-bootstrap confidence intervals. Four velocity regimes are tested: none, viewport-legal observed, true-for-visible, and true-for-all. These are scored against velocity-aware ground truth at three layers: imputation, control surface, and team verdicts. A noise ladder of eleven jitter settings including sigma_v-matched pairs is used, and eleven SoccerNet-GSR clips from one match are tested through the pipeline.

Result: Velocity is nearly useless for imputation (-0.2 pp against a 12-14 pp velocity-free surface MAE), first-order for the surface (-1.5 to -1.8 pp, 11-15% of that MAE), and ten times smaller for verdicts (-0.12 to -0.19 pp). The visible channel velocity matters most: perfect occluded-player velocity adds only 2-6% of visible gain, and no last-seen decay policy exceeds that. Omitting velocity blurs the surface (per-frame |e| 2.2-2.6 pp) with small time-averaged bias (per cell <=0.4 pp), while imputation error shows structured bias against the defending team's deep zone (5-9 pp). Noise ladder experiments show break-even at ~1 m/s. Real data shows sigma_v=1.65 m/s yet recovers 24-36% of the benefit: 43% of variance is frame-common (which the surface tolerates), and the residual is heavy-tailed and clustered, which Gaussian controls matched on component RMS do not reproduce (+0.03 vs. +0.36). The share of velocity-free error that velocity removes grows with viewport width (7% at 36m, 21% at 60m).

Conclusion: Velocity matters most at the control surface layer, not at imputation or team verdict layers. The visible channel velocity is what matters - perfect occluded-player velocity adds minimal benefit. The approach should be: fix imputation on tight shots, velocity on wide ones. Frame-common noise is tolerated by the surface, but heavy-tailed clustered residual noise is not reproduced by Gaussian controls. Code and logs are released.

Abstract: Velocity-aware pitch control is standard, but under a broadcast viewport half the players are off screen and on-screen velocities come from a drifting calibration. We ask at which layer of broadcast off-ball analysis velocity changes the answer. Inheriting our off-screen imputation protocol (three Metrica matches, 44 m viewport, block-bootstrap CIs), we score four velocity regimes -- none, viewport-legal observed, true-for-visible, true-for-all -- against a velocity-aware ground truth at three layers: imputation, the control surface, and team verdicts. Velocity is nearly useless for imputation (-0.2 pp against a 12--14 pp velocity-free surface MAE), first-order for the surface (-1.5 to -1.8 pp, 11--15% of that MAE), and ten times smaller for verdicts (-0.12 to -0.19 pp). The velocity that matters is the visible channel: perfect occluded-player velocity adds 2--6% of the visible gain, and no last-seen decay policy we tested exceeds that. Omitting velocity blurs the surface (per-frame |e| 2.2--2.6 pp) with small time-averaged bias (per cell <=0.4 pp), whereas imputation error is a structured bias against the defending team's deep zone (5--9 pp). At a fixed velocity window, a noise ladder of eleven jitter settings, including sigma_v-matched pairs, is ordered to first order by one velocity-noise axis sigma_v with break-even ~1 m/s; eleven SoccerNet-GSR clips from one match through our pipeline measure sigma_v=1.65 m/s yet recover 24--36% of the benefit: 43% of the variance is frame-common, which the surface tolerates, and the residual is heavy-tailed and clustered, which Gaussian controls matched on component RMS do not reproduce (+0.03 vs. +0.36). The share of velocity-free error that velocity removes grows with viewport width (7% at 36 m, 21% at 60 m): fix imputation on tight shots, velocity on wide ones. Code and logs are released.

</details>


### [11] [TrajFusionNet+: Transformer-Based Prediction of Pedestrian Crossing Intention via Fusion of Trajectory Representations and Scene Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10806)
*François G. Landry,Moulay A. Akhloufi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TrajFusionNet+ is a transformer-based model for pedestrian crossing intention prediction in autonomous vehicles. It combines three branches (SAM, VAM, GAM) processing sequential, visual, and graph-based representations to achieve state-of-the-art performance on PIE and JAAD datasets, with superior generalization in cross-dataset evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The task of predicting pedestrian crossing intention is critical for autonomous vehicles to ensure safe navigation. Previous models like TrajFusionNet only processed sequential and visual representations, but the motivation here is to enhance scene understanding by incorporating graph-based representations of traffic context to better capture relational dependencies between pedestrians and traffic elements.

Method: TrajFusionNet+ builds upon TrajFusionNet with three branches: (1) Sequence Attention Module (SAM) processes sequential representation of past and predicted pedestrian trajectories; (2) Visual Attention Module (VAM) overlays observed and predicted bounding boxes onto scene images for visual representation; (3) Graph Attention Module (GAM) extracts pedestrian-centric graphs from segmented scene images to capture relational dependencies between pedestrians and traffic elements.

Result: TrajFusionNet+ achieves improved state-of-the-art performance on both PIE and JAAD datasets. Under a new joint training protocol (trained on combined PIE+JAAD, evaluated separately), it demonstrates superior generalization compared to existing approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed TrajFusionNet+ successfully integrates sequential, visual, and graph-based representations for pedestrian crossing intention prediction, outperforming prior methods. The graph-based scene context (GAM) provides additional relational information that improves prediction accuracy and generalization across datasets.

Abstract: The pedestrian crossing intention task involves predicting whether pedestrians are likely to cross the road from the point of view of an autonomous vehicle. We introduce TrajFusionNet+, a novel transformer-based model for pedestrian crossing intention prediction. TrajFusionNet+ combines sequential and visual representations of pedestrian trajectory with a graph-based representation of the scene context in order to predict pedestrian crossing intention. The proposed architecture builds upon our previous model, TrajFusionNet, and comprises three branches: a Sequence Attention Module (SAM), which processes a sequential representation of past and predicted pedestrian trajectories; a Visual Attention Module (VAM), which utilizes a visual representation of the pedestrian trajectories by overlaying observed and predicted bounding boxes onto scene images; and a Graph Attention Module (GAM), which extracts pedestrian-centric graphs from segmented scene images and captures the relational dependencies between pedestrians and traffic elements. TrajFusionNet+ achieves improved state-of-the-art performance on the two most widely used pedestrian crossing intention datasets, PIE and JAAD. Furthermore, we introduce a new evaluation protocol in which models are trained jointly on the PIE and JAAD datasets but evaluated separately on each. Under this setting, TrajFusionNet+ demonstrates superior generalization compared to existing approaches.

</details>


### [12] [Overpainting: Localized Context-aware Diffusion Image Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10811)
*Sam Sartor,Iliyan Georgiev,Michael Fischer,Valentin Deschaintre,Pieter Peers*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Overpainting is a novel image editing operation that provides precise spatial control through trimaps (white=must edit, gray=may edit, black=must not edit), implemented by adapting a pretrained diffusion model using joint attention and LoRA with attention-dropout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing image editing methods lack fine-grained control over both the location of edits and awareness of original content. Users need an operation that can precisely target specific areas while being flexible about how much those areas can be modified.

Method: The method adapts a pretrained image editing diffusion model using: (1) joint attention across input images, (2) low-rank adaptation (LoRA), (3) attention-dropout to balance information flow between noise, source, and mask images. Training data is automatically generated using existing language-based editing models, then curated, with trimaps extracted from usable pairs.

Result: A versatile overpainting model capable of handling a wide range of editing tasks while maintaining spatial control and content awareness.

Conclusion: The proposed overpainting operation enables both precise and loose control over image edits through a trimap-based interface, demonstrating versatility across various editing tasks.

Abstract: We present "overpainting", an image editing operation which offers both control over the location of the edit and awareness of the previous content in that location. The overpainted area is given by a trimap, where white-annotated pixels must be edited, gray-annotated pixels may be edited, and black-annotated pixels must not be edited. This enables both precise and loose control, depending on user intent.
  We implement overpainting by adapting a pretrained image editing diffusion model using a combination of joint attention and low-rank adaption across input images with attention-dropout to balance the information flow between noise, source and mask images. We present a novel, automated, training data generation pipeline that (1) generates a set of candidate image pairs leveraging existing language-based editing models, (2) carefully curates those pairs, and (3) extracts a trimap from each usable pair. We demonstrate the versatility of our overpainting model on a wide range of editing tasks.

</details>


### [13] [BodyCam-VQA: Enhanced Body-Worn Camera Video Captioning via Multimodal Reasoning and Probe Question Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10815)
*Karish Gupta,Matthew Alex,Alex Li,Yang Wu,Yun-Wei Chu,Kashif Munir,Xiaotian Zhou,Zhengping Ji,Xiaozhong Liu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes an Adaptive Visual Question Answering (VQA) framework for processing police body-worn camera (BWC) footage in law enforcement contexts. The framework addresses limitations of current Vision-Language Models (VLMs) that fail to capture critical forensic details like evidence presence and suspect-officer interaction nuances.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to overcome challenges in processing BWC footage, which contains chaotic scenes with low visual quality, rapid movement, and high-noise audio. Current VLMs overlook vital forensic details essential for fair legal outcomes and safety of both civilians and officers.

Method: The proposed framework employs a structured reasoning approach to extract fine-grained visual evidence. It experiments with multiple question generation models, including foundation models and fine-tuned open-weight models, to observe performance variations.

Result: The VQA-driven architecture provides a more reliable, objective, and detailed record of enforcement events compared to traditional captioning systems, serving as a tool for AI-assisted forensic clarity.

Conclusion: The Adaptive VQA framework can effectively protect both law enforcement officers and the public through improved visual evidence extraction and forensic analysis of BWC footage.

Abstract: Police body-worn camera (BWC) footage has emerged as a critical aspect of law enforcement that ensures legal transparency, officer accountability, and the protection of civil rights. However, effectively processing this data remains a significant challenge due to its multimodal video format. BWC videos, in many cases, comprise chaotic scenes with low visual quality, rapid movement/interactions, and high-noise audio that make visual understanding a challenge for even SOTA multimodal models. Current Vision-Language Models (VLMs) frequently overlook critical forensic details, such as the presence of valuable evidence or the latent nuances of suspect-officer interactions, which are vital for fair legal outcomes and civilian/officer safety. To address these limitations, we propose an Adaptive Visual Question Answering (VQA) framework engineered for high-stakes law enforcement. Our framework employs a structured reasoning approach to extract fine-grained visual evidence that traditional captioning systems fail to capture. We experiment with multiple question generation models, including foundation models and fine-tuned open-weight models, to observe performance variation among question generation model implementations. Our results demonstrate that this VQA-driven architecture provides a more reliable, objective, and detailed record of enforcement events, ultimately serving as a powerful tool to protect both law enforcement officers and the public through AI-assisted forensic clarity.

</details>


### [14] [Are We Really Doing Few-Shot Learning? A Critical Examination of Pre-Training Assumptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10851)
*Alejandro Galan-Cuenca,Marcelo Saval-Calvo,Antonio Javier Gallego*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether standard few-shot learning evaluation protocols, which use class-disjoint in-domain pre-training, truly reflect low-data learning scenarios. The authors compare four pre-training strategies across eight datasets and find that in-domain pre-training introduces an optimistic bias of ~10 percentage points compared to out-of-domain pre-training, overestimating performance in realistic scenarios where target data are scarce.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard few-shot learning evaluation uses class-disjoint in-domain pre-training, but this may not reflect real-world scenarios where target-domain data are truly scarce. The authors question whether this protocol accurately measures low-data learning capability or overestimates performance due to domain overlap between source and target tasks.

Method: Systematic comparison of four pre-training strategies: (1) no pre-training, (2) class-disjoint in-domain pre-training, (3) supervised out-of-domain pre-training, and (4) label-free out-of-domain pre-training. Experiments conducted across eight datasets, three few-shot architectures, and multiple way-shot settings. Additionally, a descriptor-based source-selection strategy was proposed to estimate domain compatibility.

Result: In-domain pre-training improved over no pre-training by 33.41 percentage points on average, while supervised out-of-domain pre-training yielded 23.75 percentage points—revealing a 9.66-point optimistic bias from domain overlap. The augmentation-based label-free strategy achieved 27.71 percentage points gain, closely matching supervised out-of-domain at 27.97 percentage points. The proposed source-selection strategy reached a median gap of only 1.37 percentage points to oracle selection.

Conclusion: In-domain pre-training as the default few-shot evaluation protocol overestimates performance in realistic scenarios. Out-of-domain pre-training is more realistic but depends on domain compatibility. Label-free (augmentation-based) pre-training can nearly match supervised out-of-domain performance without requiring labeled source data. The authors advocate moving beyond in-domain pre-training as the standard evaluation protocol.

Abstract: Few-shot learning is commonly evaluated under protocols that pre-train a model on a large auxiliary set whose classes are disjoint from the target episodes yet drawn from the same visual domain. This paper examines whether such protocols truly reflect low-data learning. We systematically compare no pre-training, class-disjoint in-domain pre-training, supervised out-of-domain pre-training, and label-free out-of-domain pre-training across eight datasets, three few-shot architectures, and multiple way-shot settings. Our results show that class disjointness alone is insufficient to remove the influence of target-domain data. In-domain pre-training improves over no pre-training by 33.41 percentage points on average, whereas supervised out-of-domain pre-training yields 23.75 percentage points, revealing a 9.66-point optimistic bias associated with domain overlap. Although out-of-domain pre-training is more realistic in applications where target-domain data are scarce, its effectiveness depends strongly on the compatibility between source and target domains. We further show that labeled source data are not strictly required, with an augmentation-based label-free strategy reaching an average gain of 27.71 percentage points and closely matching supervised out-of-domain pre-training at 27.97 percentage points. Finally, we introduce a descriptor-based source-selection strategy that estimates source-domain suitability before pre-training, reaching a median gap of only 1.37 percentage points to oracle selection. These findings highlight the need to move beyond in-domain pre-training as the default few-shot evaluation protocol, since it can overestimate performance in realistic scenarios where target-domain data are scarce.

</details>


### [15] [Evaluation of Vision-Language Models Across Diverse Coastal Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10855)
*Seth Knoop,Chad R. Samuelson,Gabriel R. Slade,Brady Moon,Joshua G. Mangelson*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) enable robotic per- ception by associating visual observations with natural-language concepts. Yet their performance in coastal environments remains largely unexplored. We introduce a densely labeled coastal dataset containing more than 1,000 images collected across seven missions in three regions of Oahu, Hawaii, with 18 semantic classes and over 7,400 annotated instances. We evaluate seven modern VLMs through three complementary experiments mea- suring text-to-mask, mask-to-mask, and mask-to-text alignment. Broad landscape classes are generally recognized more accurately than conventional object and coastal classes, with coastal con- cepts presenting the greatest challenge. However, comparisons of shared conventional classes across coastal and terrestrial datasets reveal no consistent performance difference attributable solely to environmental context. Mask-to-mask matching also remains similar across conventional and coastal classes, while alternative textual labels substantially improve recognition of several coastal concepts. These results suggest that lower performance on coastal classes (at least on the objects/query categories evaluated) is heavily influenced by segmentation and linguistic representation.

</details>


### [16] [Symmetry-aware super-resolution of crystal orientation maps via invariant latent-space learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10898)
*Umang Garg,Warren Zamudio,McLean P. Echlin,Samantha H. Daly,Tresa M. Pollock,B. S. Manjunath*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SG-SRAN is a super-resolution network for EBSD crystal-orientation maps that preserves symmetry and grain boundaries by mapping orientations to a latent space where Euclidean distance approximates misorientation, achieving comparable performance to 15-16M parameter models with only 27-49k parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EBSD data exists on the quotient space SO(3)/G (where G is crystal symmetry), making standard Euclidean interpolation problematic as it mixes symmetry-equivalent representations and blurs grain boundaries. Current methods suffer from acquisition-time constraints limiting spatial resolution.

Method: The method uses: (1) A frozen locally isometric encoder that maps equivalent orientations to a common latent representation where Euclidean distance approximates misorientation; (2) Super-resolution in this latent space with each high-resolution token restricted to feature-consistent local support to prevent cross-boundary mixing; (3) A dictionary-based decoder to recover valid orientations.

Result: SG-SRAN matches 15-16 million parameter backbones using only 27-49k trainable parameters, achieves the lowest p68 errors, highest inverse-pole-figure fidelity, and demonstrates zero-shot transfer to unseen alloys across FCC and HCP benchmarks.

Conclusion: The symmetry-group-aware approach successfully preserves crystal symmetry and grain boundaries while dramatically reducing parameter count compared to backbone networks, with strong generalization to unseen alloys.

Abstract: Crystal-orientation maps are physical fields defined only up to crystal symmetry; electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) resolves them experimentally, but acquisition-time constraints limit spatial resolution. Unlike conventional images, EBSD data lie on the quotient space $\mathrm{SO}(3)/G$, where $G$ is the crystal-symmetry group. Standard Euclidean interpolation can therefore mix symmetry-equivalent representations and blur grain boundaries. We introduce the Symmetry-Group-Aware Super-Resolution Attention Network (SG-SRAN), which incorporates crystal symmetry and boundary preservation by design. A frozen, locally isometric encoder maps equivalent orientations to a common latent representation in which Euclidean distance approximates misorientation. Super-resolution is performed in this space, with each high-resolution token restricted to a feature-consistent local support to prevent cross-boundary mixing. A dictionary-based decoder then recovers valid orientations. Across FCC and HCP benchmarks, SG-SRAN matches 15-16 million parameter backbones using only 27-49k trainable parameters, while achieving the lowest p68 errors, highest inverse-pole-figure fidelity, and zero-shot transfer to unseen alloys.

</details>


### [17] [HiPerViT: A Hierarchical Perceiver-Vision Transformer Architecture for Multi-Scale Texture Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10917)
*João Pedro C. A. de Sá,Odemir Martinez Bruno*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: HiPerViT is a compact vision-only architecture that addresses texture recognition by injecting explicit second-order statistical priors into a Vision Transformer pipeline. It combines global and local image views with a bilinear descriptor encoded as a statistical token, integrated through Perceiver-style latent distillation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Texture recognition is challenging because discriminative evidence lies in higher-order spatial statistics (feature co-occurrences) rather than object shape alone. While Vision Transformers have strong long-range modeling capacity, their standard representations don't explicitly expose such statistical structure, limiting texture sensitivity in fine-grained recognition.

Method: HiPerViT combines global and local image views with a compact bilinear descriptor encoded as a "statistical token." This token is integrated with first-order spatial representations through Perceiver-style latent distillation, enabling direct interaction between spatial tokens and second-order feature co-occurrence statistics without requiring multimodal pretraining or ensemble construction.

Result: Across six texture recognition benchmarks, HiPerViT achieves consistent improvements over strong vision-only baselines: +3.05 percentage points on DTD, +10.48 on GTOS-Mobile, and +10.10 on 1200Tex. The gains are invariant to backbone depth and ordering of interaction/distillation stages, suggesting the improvement comes from explicit second-order statistical information as a first-class representational signal.

Conclusion: Explicit statistical tokenization is an effective and robust design principle for texture-centric visual recognition. The primary source of improvement is not a specific fusion topology, but the explicit availability of second-order statistical information itself.

Abstract: Texture recognition remains challenging for modern vision models because discriminative evidence is often carried by higher-order spatial statistics rather than by object shape alone. While Vision Transformers provide strong long-range modeling capacity, their standard object-centric representations do not explicitly expose such statistical structure, which limits texture sensitivity in fine-grained recognition settings. We present HiPerViT, a compact vision-only architecture that injects an explicit second-order statistical prior into a transformer-based recognition pipeline. The method combines global and local image views with a compact bilinear descriptor encoded as a statistical token, and integrates this token with first-order spatial representations through Perceiver-style latent distillation. This design enables direct interaction between spatial tokens and second-order feature co-occurrence statistics, providing the model with explicit access to texture-relevant information without requiring multimodal pretraining or ensemble construction. Across six texture recognition benchmarks, HiPerViT achieves consistent improvements over strong vision-only baselines under the reported evaluation protocols, including gains of +3.05 percentage points on DTD, +10.48 on GTOS-Mobile, and +10.10 on 1200Tex. Beyond benchmark performance, our analyses show that these gains are largely invariant to the backbone depth used to extract second-order statistics and to the ordering of interaction and distillation stages. This pattern suggests that the primary source of improvement is not a specific fusion topology, but the explicit availability of second-order statistical information as a first-class representational signal. These results support explicit statistical tokenization as an effective and robust design principle for texture-centric visual recognition.

</details>


### [18] [CamPilot: A Multi-Agent Cinematic Assistant for Camera-Controlled Movie Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10943)
*Yang Wu,Stefano Petrangeli,Ishita Dasgupta,Yu Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CamPilot is a multi-agent framework that enhances LLM-based video generation by integrating cinematographic planning and camera-work control, learning from 14K professional movies to produce more coherent and aesthetically pleasing movies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM-based text-to-video generation falls short of professional filmmaking standards due to less refined cinematographic language and challenges in multi-shot continuity.

Method: GRPO-based learning paradigm trained on 14K real-world professional movies to internalize motion patterns and composition principles. The framework enables reasoning over shooting techniques (camera angle, motion, focal behavior) and cross-shot relationships for controllable camera-viewpoint generation, with multiple collaborating agents.

Result: CamPilot outperforms state-of-the-art text-to-movie generation methods on cinematographic control and quality. A benchmark (CamEval) was established for evaluating camera work quality and cinematic engagement.

Conclusion: Integrating professional camera design principles into LLM-based video generation significantly improves movie quality, demonstrating the importance of cinematographic planning in automated movie generation.

Abstract: The integration of large language models (LLMs) into video generation has enabled rapid text-to-video creation and improved visual quality. However, it still falls short of professional filmmaking, where cinematographic language is less refined than human-crafted camera work and multi-shot continuity remains challenging. To address these limitations, we introduce CamPilot, a multi-agent framework that integrates cinematographic planning and camera-work control to produce more coherent, logically structured, and human-aesthetic movies. CamPilot adopts a GRPO-based learning paradigm to learn camera work planning from 14K real-world professional movies, internalizing motion patterns and composition principles that support reasoning over shooting techniques (e.g., camera angle, motion, and focal behavior) and cross-shot relationships for controllable camera-viewpoint generation. Multiple agents further collaborate and evolve to improve overall output quality. To support this work and further studies in this domain, we establish CamEval, a benchmark for evaluating camera work quality and cinematic engagement. Empirical results show that CamPilot outperforms state-of-the-art text-to-movie generation methods on cinematographic control and quality, highlighting the impact of professional camera design on movie generation.

</details>


### [19] [New Evidence, Same Choice: Testing Physical Experiment Selection in Vision Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11022)
*Sourajit Saha,Shubhashis Roy Dipta,Nobin Sarwar,Shaswati Saha,Yuxuan Jiang,Siyuan Li,Qiheng Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper introduces a benchmark to evaluate whether vision-language models can reason about physical experiments - deciding when to answer questions immediately based on observed measurements versus when to request additional experiments. The benchmark uses controlled scenarios with known optimal actions, revealing that even the best models correctly make both decisions for only 5.9% of image pairs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current physical reasoning benchmarks only evaluate final answers, missing the model's ability to decide when additional evidence is needed. The authors seek to measure this decision-making capability - whether models can determine when they have enough information to answer or which experiment to perform next.

Method: The evaluation uses controlled problems where each provides one measurement image and four possible physical worlds (created by combining two masses and two values of another property). Models must either stop and answer or select the cheapest additional experiment. Matched problem pairs are constructed where changing the observed measurement or the question changes the optimal action. Six open models were tested across 144 physical parameter sets.

Result: Direct responses repeat the same action for 95.1% to 100% of image pairs even when the correct action changes. Brief reasoning improves action switching, but the best model makes both decisions correctly for only 5.9% of image pairs. Analysis reveals failures in measurement interpretation, physical reasoning, and response formatting.

Conclusion: Evaluating evidence selection separately from final answers reveals limitations in physical reasoning that conventional answer accuracy can overlook. The benchmark successfully exposes systematic weaknesses in current vision-language models' physical reasoning and decision-making capabilities.

Abstract: A model first sees an image from one physical measurement experiment, such as how far a block coasted, and must answer a question about a new trial, such as whether the block will pass a target after a fixed push. The initial experiment may provide enough information to answer, or the model may need another measurement, such as the object's mass, friction, restitution, or spring stiffness. We study whether vision language models can decide when to answer immediately and, when more evidence is needed, which experiment to perform. Current physical reasoning benchmarks usually evaluate only the final answer, so they do not directly measure this decision-making ability. We introduce a controlled evaluation where each problem provides one measurement image and four possible physical worlds created by combining two possible masses and two possible values of another relevant property. The model must either stop and answer or select the cheapest additional experiment that can resolve the question. We construct matched problem pairs where changing either the observed measurement or the question changes the optimal action. Since all possible worlds and experiment costs are known, we can explicitly determine the optimal choice. Across six open models and 144 physical parameter sets, direct responses repeat the same action for 95.1% to 100% of image pairs even when the correct action changes. Brief reasoning improves action switching, but the best model makes both decisions correctly for only 5.9% of image pairs. Additional analysis reveals failures in measurement interpretation, physical reasoning, and response formatting. By evaluating evidence selection separately from final answers, our benchmark reveals limitations in physical reasoning that conventional answer accuracy can overlook.

</details>


### [20] [Toward Interpretable Multimodal Fusion: Heat Conduction Modeling for Hyperspectral and LiDAR Joint Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11040)
*Kan Wei,Jiahui Cui,Jing Yao,Xinyu Zhao,Lei Wang,Pedram Ghamisi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: M2Heat is a physics-inspired multimodal fusion framework that combines hyperspectral imaging (HSI) and LiDAR data for land-cover classification. It uses a heat conduction analogy to model anisotropic information flow, featuring a visual heat conduction module (vHeat) and enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs) to capture global dependencies with sub-quadratic complexity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fusion methods for HSI-LiDAR integration struggle with: 1) Modeling long-range dependencies, 2) Capturing complex anisotropic interactions, 3) Maintaining computational efficiency. The authors propose a physics-inspired approach using heat conduction theory to address these limitations while providing interpretability.

Method: The framework consists of: (1) A physics-driven visual heat conduction module (vHeat) that simulates anisotropic information flow through heat conduction physics; (2) Enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs) for efficient feature representation; (3) A Cross-Frequency Fusion (CFF) module implementing hybrid spatial-frequency fusion strategy. The approach achieves sub-quadratic complexity while capturing global dependencies.

Result: M2Heat achieves competitive overall performance on three benchmark datasets: Trento, Houston2013, and Augsburg. The method provides interpretable heat-conduction-guided perspective for multimodal feature fusion while maintaining computational efficiency.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the potential of heat-conduction-guided neural operators for efficient and interpretable remote sensing multimodal fusion. The physics-inspired approach offers a novel way to model multimodal data interactions while balancing performance and computational cost.

Abstract: The fusion of hyperspectral (HS) and Light Detection and Ranging (LiDAR) data plays a crucial role in enhancing land-cover classification by jointly exploiting spectral, spatial, and structural cues. However, existing multimodal fusion methods still struggle to model long-range dependencies and complex anisotropic interactions while maintaining computational efficiency. This paper introduces M2Heat, a physics-inspired framework that investigates multimodal fusion through the lens of heat conduction. At its core, a physics-driven visual heat conduction module (vHeat) and enhanced Frequency Value Embeddings (FVEs) simulate anisotropic information flow, enabling the capture of global dependencies with sub-quadratic complexity and physical interpretability. This mechanism, combined with a hybrid spatial-frequency fusion strategy named Cross-Frequency Fusion (CFF) module, produces highly discriminative and robust feature representations. M2Heat achieves competitive overall performance on three benchmarks, i.e., Trento, Houston2013, and Augsburg, while providing an interpretable heat-conduction-guided perspective for multimodal feature fusion. These results indicate the potential of heat-conduction-guided neural operators for efficient and interpretable RS multimodal fusion. The source code is publicly available at https: /github.com/Weikan0425/M2Heat_HSI_LiDAR.

</details>


### [21] [Meta-Learning for Classifier Selection in Image Datasets: A Feature-Driven Framework for Accuracy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11041)
*Zahra Nabizadeh_Shahre_Babak,Farzaneh Koohestani,Nader Karimi,Shahram Shirani,Shadrokh Samavi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A meta-learning framework that predicts classifier performance on image datasets using extracted meta-features, achieving 86%+ ranking prediction accuracy across 56 diverse datasets without exhaustive training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The No Free Lunch theorem states no single classifier is universally optimal, making classifier selection critical but challenging. Existing approaches require exhaustive training, which is computationally expensive. This work aims to predict classifier performance efficiently to guide model selection.

Method: The framework extracts meta-features using autoencoders, pre-trained networks, and dimensionality reduction techniques to capture dataset complexity. Regression models are trained to estimate classifier accuracies, and clustering is applied to group classifiers with similar performance patterns.

Result: Evaluated on 56 diverse image datasets spanning nature, animals, numbers, motorcycles, medical images, and human bodies, the approach achieves an average ranking prediction accuracy exceeding 86%.

Conclusion: The proposed meta-learning framework provides a scalable and interpretable solution for classifier selection, effectively reducing computational costs while maintaining high prediction accuracy for ranking classifiers across diverse image domains.

Abstract: No Free Lunch theorem implies that any performance gains achieved by a classifier on a particular image distribution are necessarily offset by a loss of performance over the set of all possible problems; thus, no single model is universally optimal. Selecting the most suitable classifier for image datasets is a critical yet challenging task due to the intrinsic complexity and diversity of images. This paper proposes a meta-learning framework that leverages a comprehensive set of meta-features capturing dataset complexity to predict classifier performance without exhaustive training. By extracting and selecting features using methods such as autoencoders, pre-trained networks, and dimensionality reduction techniques, we train regression models to efficiently estimate classifier accuracies. Additionally, clustering techniques are employed to group classifiers with similar performance patterns, simplifying the recommendation process. The datasets used span a wide range of concepts, including nature, animals, numbers, motorcycles, medical images, and human bodies, to ensure broad generalization. Evaluated on 56 diverse image datasets, our approach achieves an average ranking prediction accuracy exceeding 86%, demonstrating its effectiveness in guiding model selection. This scalable and interpretable framework provides a practical solution to improve classification performance while reducing computational costs.

</details>


### [22] [TailProp: content-adaptive light- and heavy-tailed propagation for vision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11081)
*Jiahao Kong,Zihan Li*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TailProp introduces a hierarchical vision backbone using the Tail Propagation Operator (TPO) that adaptively combines Gaussian and Cauchy stable-process propagators with complementary spatial influence patterns. This cross-regime adaptive propagation achieves state-of-the-art results across classification, detection, segmentation, robustness, and cross-backbone tasks while maintaining computational efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision models using explicit propagation dynamics are confined to particular dynamical families, but visual representations require substantially different spatial interactions across samples, channels, and network stages. The authors探索 cross-regime adaptive propagation to address this limitation.

Method: TPO uses Gaussian (rapidly decaying) and Cauchy stable-process (heavy-tailed) propagators as complementary bases. A content-conditioned channel-wise coefficient predicts how to adaptively combine them. The spatially shared coefficient enables fusion directly in DCT domain with a single DCT/IDCT pair, achieving O(N^{1.5}) spatial mixing complexity for N=HW.

Result: TailProp-B achieves 84.4% Top-1 on ImageNet-1K, 50.3/44.8 box/mask AP on Mask R-CNN (3x schedule), and 50.8% mIoU on ADE20K. Ablations confirm gains come specifically from complementary two-basis propagation, not single-basis or same-family alternatives.

Conclusion: The complementary two-basis propagation design is an effective principle for visual representation learning, consistently outperforming matched propagation baselines across multiple vision tasks including classification, detection, segmentation, robustness, and cross-backbone restoration.

Abstract: Science-inspired vision models show that explicit propagation dynamics can provide structured and interpretable alternatives to conventional token mixing. Existing formulations, however, typically construct and adapt visual propagation within a particular dynamical family, while visual representations can require substantially different spatial interactions across samples, channels, and network stages. We explore cross-regime adaptive propagation and introduce TailProp, a hierarchical vision backbone built upon the Tail Propagation Operator (TPO). TPO uses Gaussian and Cauchy stable-process propagators as complementary bases with rapidly decaying and heavy-tailed spatial influence, and predicts a content-conditioned channel-wise coefficient to adaptively combine them. Because this coefficient is spatially shared, the two responses are fused directly in the DCT domain with a single DCT/IDCT pair, yielding $O(N^{1.5})$ spatial mixing for square feature maps with $N=HW$ and fixed channel width. Across image classification, object detection, semantic segmentation, robustness, and cross-backbone restoration, TailProp consistently outperforms matched propagation baselines; TailProp-B reaches 84.4% Top-1 accuracy on ImageNet-1K, 50.3/44.8 box/mask AP under the 3x Mask R-CNN schedule, and 50.8% mIoU on ADE20K. Controlled ablations further show that these gains are not explained by single-basis propagation, an additional same-family branch, or within-family adaptive order alone, supporting complementary two-basis propagation as an effective design principle for visual representation learning.

</details>


### [23] [Beyond Benchmarks: Using VLMs to Reveal Systematic Classification Failures Under Real World Conditions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11126)
*Dieuwertje Alblas,Alma M. Liezenga,Jan Erik van Woerden,Fedor Taggenbrock,Dalia Aljawaheri,Klamer Schutte*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper explores using Vision Language Models (VLMs) to accelerate the verification and validation (V&V) process for classification models in defense sensor processing applications. The authors propose a VLM-based error slice detection (ESD) method that groups and labels systematic errors. Testing on non-military and military datasets shows the approach can identify perturbations and cluster images by surroundings, but limitations remain due to defense domain underrepresentation in VLMs and limited context diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The V&V process for classification models currently relies on time-consuming manual inspection of erroneous samples. The defense context introduces additional challenges: underrepresentation of defense data in VLMs and less diverse surroundings/context. This work aims to assess whether VLMs can speed up the V&V process in defense applications.

Method: The authors propose a VLM-based error slice detection (ESD) method that embeds images into semantically meaningful vector representations, then independently groups and labels systematic errors made by a classification model. They evaluate this method on both a non-military dataset (with artificially added perturbations) and a military dataset, comparing embedding variations between the two.

Result: On the non-military dataset, the method successfully identifies operationally-relevant artificially added perturbations. On the military dataset, the method clusters and describes images based on their surroundings, but exhibits overlap between cluster descriptions. The embedding variation differs between military and non-military datasets, with the military context showing less diversity.

Conclusion: The results do not yet warrant fully automated V&V through VLM-based ESD. However, the study demonstrates that VLMs could be used to accelerate V&V processes in defense applications in the future, particularly for initial error grouping and labeling to reduce manual inspection workload.

Abstract: Verification and validation (V&V) of classification models is crucial to enable a wide range of sensor processing applications. Currently, the V&V process relies on time-consuming manual inspection of erroneous samples to find meaningful patterns. This work explores the use of Vision Language Models (VLMs) to speed up this laborious process. VLMs are trained to embed images into a semantically meaningful vector representation, from which human-interpretable systematic errors can be distilled. Deploying such VLM-based methods in a defence context introduces two major challenges: (1) the defence domain is underrepresented in the training data of VLMs, and (2) surroundings and context are less diverse than for other domains. This study provides an initial assessment of the suitability of VLM-based methods for V&V of defence applications. We propose a VLM-based error slice detection (ESD) method that independently groups and labels systematic errors made by a classification model. We demonstrate that this method is able to identify operationally-relevant artificially added perturbations in a non-military dataset. In a military context, our method clusters and describes images based on their surroundings, but also exhibits overlap between cluster descriptions. We further investigate the difference in embedding variation between our military and non-military dataset, which remains a topic of interest. Although the results do not yet warrant fully automated V&V through VLM-based ESD, they show that VLMs could be used to accelerate V&V processes in the future.

</details>


### [24] [ReconPlusGen: Injecting Reconstruction Prior into Multi-view 3D Generation through Noise Inversion and Modulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11129)
*Jiarui Liu,Heng Li,Weiyu Li,Keng Deng,Junyuan Deng,Zheng Zhongxing,Junyu Huang,Jiahao Chang,Xiaoguang Han,Ping Tan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A method for 3D reconstruction from multiple input images using diffusion models.
