For validation of AvaImg's Avatar-as-Image representation as imminently compatible with image foundation models, we auto-encode our UV maps via the frozen FLUX VAE. This achieves only 0.76mm added Chamfer error relative to scan and shows that the resulting maps lie within natural-image distributions, supporting the use of 2D generative priors for 3D avatar generation. Code, data, and Singularity containers will be at https://yuxuan-xue.com/avaimg.

</details>


### [72] [Logit Refiner: Improving Visual Autoregressive Models via Intra-Scale Dependency Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11804)
*Meimingwei Li,Stefan Andreas Baumann,Felix Krause,Björn Ommer*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: VAR models generate images through parallel next-scale prediction but discard spatial dependencies among same-scale tokens, causing locally incoherent samples. The Logit Refiner is a lightweight autoregressive module that restores intra-scale dependencies by sequential sampling, adding only ~10% parameters without retraining the backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The parallel decoding in Visual Autoregressive Models constitutes a mean-field-style approximation that discards spatial dependencies among same-scale tokens, causing locally incoherent samples regardless of backbone capacity -- a fundamental limitation of the decoding rule.

Method: The Logit Refiner is a lightweight autoregressive module that plugs into any pretrained VAR checkpoint without retraining. It restores intra-scale dependencies by sequentially sampling tokens conditioned on frozen backbone features, adding only ~10% parameters and less than 5% of the base model's training compute.

Result: Across backbones from 310M to 2B parameters on class-conditional ImageNet 256x256, the refiner consistently improves generation quality. A 1.1B-parameter model surpasses one twice its size. The approach also generalizes to text-to-image generation, confirming the mean-field bottleneck persists across VAR variants.

Conclusion: The mean-field bottleneck is a fundamental limitation of the parallel decoding rule in VAR models. The Logit Refiner effectively alleviates this issue through joint intra-scale sampling, which is the critical ingredient rather than additional capacity or training. This approach provides a lightweight, plug-and-play solution that works across different model scales and generation tasks.

Abstract: Visual Autoregressive Models (VAR) generate images through next-scale prediction, producing all tokens within each scale in parallel. We show that this parallel decoding constitutes a mean-field-style approximation that discards spatial dependencies among same-scale tokens, causing locally incoherent samples regardless of backbone capacity -- a limitation of the decoding rule. Addressing this limitation, we introduce the Logit Refiner, a lightweight autoregressive module that restores intra-scale dependencies by sequentially sampling tokens conditioned on frozen backbone features. Adding only ~10% parameters and less than 5% of the base model's training compute, it plugs into any pretrained VAR checkpoint without retraining. Controlled ablations isolate joint intra-scale sampling -- rather than additional capacity or training -- as the critical ingredient. Across backbones from 310M to 2B parameters on class-conditional ImageNet 256x256, the refiner consistently improves generation quality, enabling a 1.1B-parameter model to surpass one twice its size. The approach further generalizes to text-to-image generation, confirming that the mean-field bottleneck persists across VAR variants and is effectively alleviated by our method.
  Project page: https://compvis.github.io/logit-refiner/

</details>


### [73] [Guided Super-Resolution of Digital Elevation Models with Diffusion-Based Image Generators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11886)
*Armand Mihai Nicolicioiu,Dominik Narnhofer,Nando Metzger,Daniel Panangian,Ksenia Bittner,Konrad Schindler*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a DSM super-resolution method that enhances 5m resolution digital surface models to 0.5m resolution using guidance from high-resolution optical imagery, employing denoising diffusion to transfer visual details (building outlines, roof structures) into elevation maps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution DSMs are important for urban analysis, 3D building reconstruction, and infrastructure monitoring, but are limited due to high acquisition costs. Meanwhile, coarse DSMs from satellite missions and high-resolution optical imagery are widely available, creating a resolution mismatch that this work aims to address.

Method: The proposed method uses denoising diffusion to transfer information visible only in high-resolution spectral images (crisp outlines, detailed roof structures) into elevation maps, enabling guided super-resolution of coarse DSMs.

Result: Experiments on several cities in Central Europe demonstrate that the approach produces high-quality DSMs with improved structural detail and accurate surface geometry, outperforming conventional interpolation or filtering techniques.

Conclusion: Guided super-resolution with foundational image priors (denoising diffusion) is a promising approach for reconstructing high-resolution surface models, effectively bridging the resolution gap between available coarse DSMs and high-resolution optical imagery.

Abstract: High-resolution digital surface models (DSMs) play an important role in urban analysis, 3D building reconstruction, and infrastructure monitoring, yet their availability remains limited due to the high cost and complexity of data acquisition. In contrast, coarse DSMs from commercial satellite missions are widely accessible, and high-resolution optical imagery is increasingly available from aerial and satellite platforms. We address the resulting mismatch in spatial resolution and propose a DSM superresolution approach that enhances 5 m DSMs to 0.5 m resolution, using guidance from high-resolution spectral images. Our method employs denoising diffusion to transfer information that is visible only in the image, like crisp outlines and detailed roof structures, into the elevation maps. In this way, surface details are reconstructed more accurately than with conventional interpolation or filtering techniques. Experiments on several cities in Central Europe demonstrate that the proposed approach produces high-quality DSMs with improved structural detail and accurate surface geometry. Our results highlight the potential of guided super-resolution with foundational image priors as a means of reconstructing high-resolution surface models.

</details>


### [74] [Caption-once, Frames-on-Demand: Visual-Need Routing for Budget-Aware Agentic Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11899)
*Weitong Cai,Hang Zhang,Yukai Huang,Yiqiao Xie,Shan Gao,Jiankang Deng,Songcen Xu,Jifei Song,Zhensong Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CFD is a budget-aware edge-cloud framework for long-video understanding on edge devices. It uses a dual-track narrative index (event-level story skeleton + clip-level micro-log) built via single offline captioning, with a Visual-Need Router at query time that selectively retrieves keyframes only for perceptual questions while keeping temporal-structural questions in language space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-video understanding on edge devices faces tight compute and bandwidth budgets. Existing approaches have trade-offs: subsampling visual tokens loses temporal structure, while text-only video memories lose fine-grained visual attributes. The key insight is that language memories excel at long-range temporal structure while pixels remain essential for attribute-level perception.

Method: CFD uses a dual-track narrative index built through a single offline captioning pass: (1) event-level story skeleton and (2) clip-level micro-log. At query time, a Visual-Need Router acts as a per-query gating module that determines whether visual keyframe retrieval is needed. It triggers bounded keyframe retrieval only for perceptual questions (appearance, on-screen text, attribute disambiguation) and keeps temporal-structural questions in language space, capping per-query frame consumption regardless of video length.

Result: Experiments on long-video benchmarks demonstrate strong accuracy-efficiency trade-offs while substantially reducing online visual processing.

Conclusion: The visual-textual duality insight enables efficient long-video reasoning on edge devices by combining language-based temporal structure with selective pixel-based perception, achieving good performance with significantly reduced computational costs.

Abstract: Long-video understanding on edge devices must reason over hours of content under tight compute and bandwidth budgets. Subsampling visual tokens loses temporal structure, while text-only video memories lose fine-grained visual attributes. We observe a visual-textual duality: language memories carry long-range temporal structure better than dense frames, while pixels remain decisive for attribute-level perception. Building on this insight, we propose Caption-once, Frames-onDemand (CFD), a budget-aware edge-cloud agentic framework. The edge runs a single offline captioning pass that builds a dual-track narrative index, an event-level story skeleton plus a clip-level micro-log, cached and reused across queries without re-captioning. At query time, a cloud-side MLLM reasons over the index in a story-first loop centered on a lightweight Visual-Need Router: a per-query gating module that triggers bounded keyframe retrieval only for perceptual questions (appearance, on-screen text, attribute disambiguation) and keeps temporal-structural questions in language space. The router turns visual access into a first-class, query-conditioned cost, capping per-query frame consumption regardless of video length. Experiments on long-video benchmarks demonstrate strong accuracy-efficiency trade-offs while substantially reducing online visual processing.

</details>


### [75] [SenseNova-U1.5: Towards Native Unified Visual Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11929)
*Haiwen Diao,Jiahao Wang,Chenjing Ding,Hanming Deng,Jiangnan Chen,Ruixi Zhang,Ruohui Wang,Wenwen Tong,Xiangyu Fan,Yubo Wang,Yue Zhu,Yuwei Niu,Zhengqi Bai,Zhiqian Lin,Zhitao Yang,Zhongang Cai,Bo Yang,Chen Feng,Chengguang Lv,Guangjia Liu,Guanlin Wang,Hanyu Zhang,Haojia Yu,Hongcan Xiao,Hongli Wang,Huan Wu,Huaping Zhong,Jian Fang,Jianan Fan,Jiaqi Li,Jiefan Lu,Jing Zuo,Jingcheng Ni,Junxiang Xu,Linjun Dai,Mutian Xu,Peishen Yan,Penghao Wu,Ruijie Mao,Ruisi Wang,Shihao Bai,Shuang Yang,Shuya Yang,Shuyan Zheng,Silei Wu,Siying Li,Tao Chu,Tianbo Zhong,Tongxi Zhou,Weichao Luo,Weichen Fan,Wenhao Jia,Wenjie Gao,Xiangli Kong,Yan Li,Yang Yong,Zimo Wen,Zixuan Qian,Wenxiu Sun,Ruihao Gong,Quan Wang,Lewei Lu,Lei Yang,Ziwei Liu,Dahua Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SenseNova-U1.5 is an 8B parameter native unified multimodal model (MoT) that understands, reasons about, and generates visual content without encoder or VAE components. It uses spatially coherent patch reconstruction, supports up to 4K native resolution, and achieves strong performance through multi-expert post-training distillation for visual aesthetics, text rendering, infographic generation, and image editing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research aims to develop a fully end-to-end multimodal system that can perceive, reason, and create within a unified framework, moving beyond traditional encoder-decoder architectures. The goal is to enable multimodal understanding to transfer to visual planning and creation.

Method: The approach includes: (1) encoder-free and VAE-free architecture design, (2) spatially coherent patch reconstruction for visual interface, (3) curated generation and editing data with improved task formulation and structural prompts, (4) native resolution support up to 4K, and (5) multi-expert on-policy distillation consolidating specialized skills in visual aesthetics, bilingual text rendering, infographic generation, and image editing.

Result: SenseNova-U1.5 largely advances image fidelity, text rendering, complex composition, multi-reference editing, and interleaved generation, while improving instruction following and preserving subject identity, geometry, and unmodified regions. It generalizes effectively to long, complex, and structured visual instructions despite limited exposure to structured formats in training data.

Conclusion: Native unified modeling represents a promising path toward systems that perceive, reason, and create within a fully end-to-end framework. The researchers will open-source training code including supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and on-policy distillation.

Abstract: We launch SenseNova-U1.5, an 8B-MoT native unified multimodal model that understands, reasons about, and generates visual content within an encoder-free and VAE-free architecture. We strengthen its visual interface through spatially coherent patch reconstruction and scale its training with carefully curated generation and editing data, improved task formulation, structural prompt enhancement, and native resolutions of up to 4K. For post-training, we optimize specialized experts for visual aesthetics, bilingual text rendering, infographic generation, and image editing, and consolidate their capabilities through multi-expert on-policy distillation. Across extensive evaluations, SenseNova-U1.5 largely advances image fidelity, text rendering, complex composition, multi-reference editing, and interleaved generation, while improving instruction following and preserving subject identity, geometry, and unmodified regions. Despite limited exposure to structured formats in its generation data, SenseNova-U1.5 generalizes effectively to long, complex, and structured visual instructions, further proving that multimodal understanding can transfer to visual planning and creation. Together, these findings position native unified modelling as a promising path towards systems that perceive, reason and create within a fully end-to-end framework. We will open-source training code, including supervised fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and on-policy distillation.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [76] [Sparse Weight and Edge Circuit Discovery in Transformer-based Acoustic Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10645)
*Jiankun Wei,Ewan Dunbar,Gerald Penn*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper extends DiscoGP, a circuit discovery framework, from text decoders to speech encoders, presenting the first circuit discovery study for modern speech foundation models (HuBERT and Wav2Vec 2.0). The discovered circuits are extremely compact while matching or exceeding full model performance, and a memory-efficient variant reducing GPU memory from quartic to cubic complexity is introduced.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transformer-based foundation models are powerful but opaque, creating a need for Mechanistic Interpretation methods to uncover their internal workings. Specifically, identifying small computation subgraphs (circuits) responsible for specific tasks. While circuit discovery has been applied to text decoders, it remains unexplored for speech encoders.

Method: The researchers extended DiscoGP (a joint weight-and-edge circuit discovery framework) to speech encoders. They conducted experiments across HuBERT and Wav2Vec 2.0 models on several speech classification tasks. They also introduced a memory-efficient variant of DiscoGP that reduces GPU memory cost from O(n⁴) to O(n³) at runtime.

Result: The discovered circuits are extremely compact yet often match or exceed the performance of the full pretrained encoder with the same downstream head. Ablations confirm these circuits reflect pretrained computation rather than random structure or task-head artifacts.

Conclusion: This work broadens Mechanistic Interpretation beyond text decoders to speech encoders, demonstrating that circuit-level analysis can reveal both explanatory structure and unexpected functional behavior in speech foundation models.

Abstract: Transformer-based foundation models are powerful but opaque, motivating Mechanistic Interpretation methods to uncover the black-box by identifying small computation subgraphs responsible for a task. DiscoGP is a joint weight-and-edge circuit discovery framework originally developed for text decoders. We extend DiscoGP to speech encoders and present, to our knowledge, the first circuit discovery study for modern speech foundation models. Across HuBERT and Wav2Vec 2.0 on several speech classification tasks, we find that the discovered circuits are extremely compact, yet often match or even exceed the performance of the full pretrained encoder with the same downstream head. Through ablations, we show that these circuits reflect pretrained computation rather than random structure or task-head artifacts. We also introduce a memory-efficient DiscoGP variant that reduces the GPU memory cost of edge-circuit discovery at runtime from quartic to cubic. Overall, our results broaden Mechanistic Interpretation beyond text decoders and show that circuit-level analysis can reveal both explanatory structure and unexpected functional behavior in speech encoders.

</details>


### [77] [Project Qualia: Recovering Experiential Music Structure from Session Co-occurrence Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10862)
*Nizam Mohammed,Abu B. S. Rahman,Dimuthu D. K. Arachchige*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Project Qualia investigates whether experiential similarity between songs can be recovered from real listening behavior using a large-scale dataset of 1.29 billion scrobbles from Last.fm. Using Song2Vec (skip-gram Word2Vec) and an artist-residual procedure, the study found that despite artist identity dominating raw embeddings, coherent genre- and era-based clusters (trip-hop, grunge, pop, classical) persist in residual space, confirming experiential structure exists independent of artist identity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current music categorization relies on genre or metadata taxonomies, which fail to capture "experiential similarity" - how songs feel or sound similar to listeners. This study aims to discover whether such similarity can be inferred from actual listening behavior patterns.

Method: 1) Built a dataset of 1.29 billion scrobbles from 9,396 Last.fm users (reduced to 531.6M training scrobbles across 28.6M sessions); 2) Trained skip-gram Word2Vec (Song2Vec) treating sessions as sentences and tracks as tokens; 3) Developed artist-residual procedure: subtracted each artist's centroid from track embeddings to remove artist identity influence; 4) Evaluated residual space for cross-artist similarity clusters.

Result: Raw embeddings dominated by artist identity (mean cross-artist cosine similarity = 0.2487). After artist-residual processing, similarity dropped to 0.0005, yet 4,577 cross-artist track pairs retained cosine similarity ≥ 0.70, forming coherent genre/era clusters including trip-hop, 1990s grunge, 2020 mainstream pop, and cross-composer classical piano pairs (similarity up to 0.95).

Conclusion: Listening behavior data contains experiential structure independent of artist identity. This validates an empirical basis for building architectures that directly learn this experiential layer of music similarity, moving beyond metadata-based recommendations.

Abstract: This report presents results from Project Qualia, an ongoing effort to determine whether experiential similarity between songs, a structure not captured by genre or metadata taxonomies, can be recovered from real listening behavior. We constructed a large-scale dataset of listening sessions, comprising 1.29 billion scrobbles collected from 9,396 users via the Last.fm API and reduced through a preprocessing pipeline to 531.6 million training scrobbles across 28.6 million sessions. On this corpus, we trained a skip-gram Word2Vec model (Song2Vec), treating each session as a sentence and each track as a token. As anticipated, the resulting embedding space was dominated by artist identity, a consequence of single-artist runs within sessions. To test for a subtler, artist-independent signal, we developed an artist-residual procedure: subtracting each artist's centroid from its tracks' embeddings and evaluating whether the remainder retained structure. Mean cross-artist cosine similarity fell from 0.2487 in raw embedding space to 0.0005 in residual space, yet 4,577 cross-artist track pairs retained cosine similarity $\ge 0.70$ in residual space, forming coherent genre- and era-based clusters, including trip-hop, 1990s grunge, 2020 mainstream pop, and cross-composer classical piano pairs at cosine similarity up to 0.95. These results confirm that the training data contains experiential structure independent of artist identity, establishing an empirical basis for an architecture designed to learn this experiential layer directly.

</details>


### [78] [Learned Continuous Synthesis of Quadratic Difference Tone Spectra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10913)
*Esteban Gutiérrez,Behzad Haki,Christopher Haworth,Xavier Serra,Rodrigo Cádiz*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces a neural network-based method for synthesizing Quadratic Difference Tone Spectrum (QDTS), addressing prior limitations of stochastic and discontinuous numerical methods. The approach uses an autoencoder-like architecture to learn an approximate inverse of the distortion mapping, enabling continuous control for musical applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous numerical methods for QDTS synthesis were stochastic, discontinuous, and difficult to control for musical purposes, limiting their use to stationary signals. The authors sought a more controllable and continuous approach for real-time musical applications.

Method: The authors propose a neural network-based approach in an autoencoder-like configuration that learns an approximate inverse of the distortion mapping. This produces a continuous approximation that addresses the limitations of prior stochastic methods. A real-time implementation was also developed in Max (a visual programming environment for audio).

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the neural network method, while slightly less numerically precise than previous methods, is sufficient for perceptual and musical applications. The real-time implementation in Max shows effective performance, and various sound examples demonstrate the method's expressive and musical potential.

Conclusion: The neural network-based approach successfully provides a continuous approximation for QDTS synthesis that is perceptually and musically adequate, overcoming the control limitations of prior methods. The availability of source code, audio examples, and tutorials supports further exploration and adoption of the technique.

Abstract: Quadratic difference tones (QDTs) are a species of auditory distortion product in which a "phantom" pure tone, absent from the acoustic signal, is clearly audible to listeners. Exploiting this phenomenon, one can synthesize harmonically rich tones for musical purposes, a technique called Quadratic Difference Tone Spectrum (QDTS) synthesis. Previous works have introduced numerical methods to synthesize QDTS based on the distortion function, which links a target QDTS and an overtone-structured carrier signal. While accurate, these methods were stochastic and discontinuous, making them difficult to control for musical purposes and effectively limiting them to stationary signals. This paper proposes a neural network-based approach that learns an approximate inverse of the distortion mapping in an autoencoder-like configuration, producing a continuous approximation that addresses prior limitations. Experimental results show that, although slightly less numerically precise, the method is sufficient for perceptual and musical applications. We also implement a real-time version in Max and evaluate its performance. Various sound examples demonstrate its expressive and musical potential. The source code, audio examples, tutorials, and software accompanying this work are available at https://cordutie.github.io/projects/qdts.html

</details>


### [79] [Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11274)
*Yiru Zhang,Hang Su,Lichun Fan,Ying Zeng,Chang Liu,Yifeng Wang,Yuquan Liang,Tao Li,Lian Li,Wenhao Yang,Jian Luan,Cong Zou,Heng Qu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1, an LLM-based end-to-end target speaker automatic speech recognition (TS-ASR) system that solves the cocktail party problem by using reference speech as voiceprint prompts, eliminating the need for speech separation. The system maintains competitive single-speaker performance while adding negative sample rejection capability and Chain-of-Thought reasoning support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The cocktail party problem remains a critical bottleneck for LLM-based ASR models, which generally lack support for multi-speaker scenarios. Existing TS-ASR methods suffer from two major limitations: (1) degraded single-speaker performance compared to mainstream ASR models, and (2) inability to reject when the target speaker is absent from mixed speech.

Method: The proposed Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 uses reference speech as voiceprint prompts, enabling direct transcription of target speaker's speech without requiring explicit speech separation. This is an LLM-based end-to-end architecture that supports Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning mode to provide explicit reasoning steps.

Result: Extensive experiments on various synthetic and real-world multispeaker benchmarks demonstrate that Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 achieves state-of-the-art performance. The system effectively balances multispeaker and single-speaker recognition accuracy while maintaining rejection capability when the target speaker is absent.

Conclusion: Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 successfully addresses the cocktail party problem through a unified architecture that: (1) maintains competitive single-speaker performance comparable to mainstream ASR models, (2) provides negative sample rejection capability (outputs empty text when target speaker is absent), and (3) supports CoT reasoning mode. This represents a significant advancement in end-to-end TS-ASR systems.

Abstract: Recently, large language model (LLM) based ASR models have achieved significant progress, yet they generally lack support for multi-speaker scenarios, where the cocktail party problem remains a critical bottleneck for further advancing ASR. Existing TS-ASR methods, including end-to-end architectures with speaker embeddings and latest LLM-based explorations suffer from degraded single-speaker performance and the inability to reject when the target speaker is absent. In this paper, we propose Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1, an LLM-based end-to-end TS-ASR architecture. By utilizing reference speech as voiceprint prompts, it directly transcribes the target speaker's speech without requiring speech separation. Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 maintains competitive performance in single-speaker scenarios, comparable to mainstream ASR models. It also features a negative sample rejection capability, outputting empty text when the target speaker is absent from the mixed speech. Additionally, Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 supports a Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning mode to provide explicit reasoning steps. Extensive experiments on various synthetic and real-world multispeaker benchmarks demonstrate that Xiaomi-CocktailASR-1 achieves state-of-the-art performance, effectively addressing the cocktail party problem through a unified architecture that balances multispeaker and single-speaker recognition accuracy, along with rejection capability.

</details>


### [80] [EConv-TasNet: Efficient Conv-TasNet for Effective Speech Separation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11342)
*Pei-Chun Chang,Chuan-Yi Liu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: eConv-TasNet is an efficient variant of Conv-TasNet for time-domain speech separation that reduces model size by 22.4% and accelerates inference by 18.9% while improving SI-SNRi by 14.0%-28.0%, achieving a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off for edge deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Conv-TasNet is a strong baseline for time-domain speech separation, advanced extensions (dual-path networks, U-Nets, attention mechanisms) introduce high computational cost and complexity, limiting deployment in resource-constrained scenarios.

Method: The proposed eConv-TasNet employs two modules: (1) Group-wise Early-Splitting (GES) module generates discriminative speaker embeddings at intermediate stages; (2) Multi-Group Feature Aggregation (MGFA) module progressively aggregates group-level representations for refined mask estimation. Neither module is resource-intensive.

Result: eConv-TasNet reduces model size by 22.4%, accelerates inference by 18.9%, and improves SI-SNRi by 14.0%-28.0% across three public benchmarks. It achieves competitive performance compared with state-of-the-art methods while requiring significantly fewer parameters and lower inference cost.

Conclusion: eConv-TasNet demonstrates a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off, making it suitable for edge deployment in resource-constrained environments.

Abstract: Conv-TasNet has served as a strong baseline for time-domain speech separation, and many studies have extended it with advanced architectures such as dual-path networks, U-Nets, and attention mechanisms. However, these methods often introduce high computational cost and complexity, limiting their deployment in resource-constrained scenarios. To address this issue, we propose eConv-TasNet, an efficient variant of Conv-TasNet that improves both effectiveness and efficiency without relying on resource-intensive modules. The proposed model consists of a group-wise early-splitting (GES) module and a multi-group feature aggregation (MGFA) module. GES generates discriminative speaker embeddings at intermediate stages, while MGFA progressively aggregates these group-level representations for refined mask estimation. Experimental results show that eConv-TasNet reduces model size by 22.4%, accelerates inference by 18.9%, and improves SI-SNRi by 14.0%-28.0% across three public benchmarks. Moreover, it achieves competitive performance compared with state-of-the-art methods while requiring significantly fewer parameters and lower inference cost. These results demonstrate a favorable efficiency-effectiveness trade-off for edge deployment.

</details>


### [81] [Copying Versus Randomization in Lempel-Ziv Music Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11353)
*Nadav Mishan,Ram Zamir*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Lempel-Ziv universal compression algorithm for generating music notes, with a novel approach to control the output by adjusting the average sequence length stored in the compression dictionary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation appears to address a common problem in music generation using compression-based methods: the algorithm's tendency to either over-copy (reproducing too much from training data) or under-copy (generating too little from training data). This balance is crucial for generating novel yet musically coherent output.

Method: The method involves applying Lempel-Ziv universal compression to music note generation. The key innovation is controlling the algorithm's behavior by manipulating the average sequence length saved in the dictionary - this parameter directly influences how much the system draws from training data versus generating new sequences.

Result: The abstract does not provide specific quantitative results. The contribution appears to be conceptual/algorithmic - demonstrating that Lempel-Ziv compression can be adapted for music generation with controllable output characteristics.

Conclusion: The paper suggests that Lempel-Ziv universal compression can be effectively used for music note generation, with the dictionary's average sequence length serving as a control parameter to balance between copying training data and generating novel content.

Abstract: We utilize Lempel-Ziv universal compression for music note generation. We control the algorithm's tendency to over-copy or under-copy training data by manipulating the average sequence length saved in the dictionary.

</details>


### [82] [X-AuT: Progressive Audio-Encoder Compression for Speech LLMs with Cross-Scale Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11412)
*Haojun Zhang,Yi Zou,Min Chen,Qize Yu,Lianrui Fan,Xini Ding,Hao Li,Shuchang Zhou,Xianming Liu,Shiyu Huang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: X-AuT is a progressive framework for compressing speech LLM audio encoders by pruning layers while maintaining accuracy through representation alignment, cross-scale distillation, and LoRA finetuning. It achieves 20.7% parameter reduction with minimal error increase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reducing audio-encoder depth lowers inference cost but removing complete blocks perturbs embeddings consumed by the decoder, causing deletion and premature end-of-sequence errors. The goal is to compress speech LLMs while maintaining accuracy.

Method: X-AuT uses behavioral probes to select layer combinations, then restores the pruned model through representation alignment, cross-scale distillation, scheduled student-policy supervision, and LoRA finetuning. The LM backbone remains frozen while attention LoRA adapters and tied output embedding adapt during distillation. Training uses highest-agreement tier from transcript-consistency pipeline, followed by source reweighting during finetuning.

Result: Compressing Qwen3-ASR-0.6B from 18 to 16 layers reduces macro-average error from 5.61% to 5.27%. The 14-layer model achieves 5.75% with 20.7% fewer audio-tower parameters. The 1.7B teacher yields 5.55% mean error vs 8.45% for self-distillation. Progressive 18→14 pruning outperforms direct pruning (5.75% vs 6.73%).

Conclusion: X-AuT establishes two practical operating points for compressing speech LLM audio encoders, demonstrating that progressive pruning with the proposed restoration techniques outperforms direct pruning and self-distillation approaches. Accuracy effects vary across benchmarks.

Abstract: Reducing audio-encoder depth lowers the inference cost of speech large language models, but removing complete blocks perturbs the embeddings consumed by the decoder and can cause deletion and premature end-of-sequence errors. We introduce X-AuT, a progressive framework that selects layer combinations through short behavioral probes and restores the pruned model through representation alignment, cross-scale distillation, scheduled student-policy supervision, and LoRA finetuning. The language-model backbone remains frozen, while attention LoRA adapters and the tied output embedding adapt during distillation. Training uses the highest-agreement tier from a transcript-consistency pipeline, followed by source reweighting during finetuning. On ten public Chinese--English benchmarks, compressing Qwen3-ASR-0.6B from 18 to 16 audio-encoder layers reduces macro-average error from 5.61% to 5.27%. The 14-layer model reaches 5.75% with 20.7% fewer audio-tower parameters. Under the matched recipe, the 1.7B teacher yields 5.55% mean error, compared with 8.45% for self-distillation, and progressive 18$\rightarrow$14 pruning outperforms direct pruning (5.75% vs. 6.73%). These single-run results establish two practical operating points and show that the accuracy effects vary across benchmarks. Project website: https://xpeng-ai.github.io/x-aut

</details>


### [83] [Post-Training Zero-Shot TTS for Fine-Grained Emotion and Duration Control via Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11523)
*Lianru Gao,Yujie Guo,Yong Qin*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A post-training framework that enables pretrained TTS models to generate speech with fine-grained control over segment-level emotion and duration through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with group relative policy optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing TTS systems typically rely on utterance-level style conditioning, making fine-grained control over emotion and pacing within a single utterance difficult. This is needed for applications like audiobook narration, conversational agents, and audiovisual dubbing where speech must convey changing emotions and adapt pacing within a single utterance.

Method: The proposed unified post-training framework has two components: (1) Supervised fine-tuning establishes instruction-conditioned speech generation; (2) Reinforcement learning with group relative policy optimization (GRPO) refines control accuracy using emotion and duration rewards alongside content and speaker preservation objectives. The approach reuses the pretrained architecture to avoid additional inference-time control modules.

Result: Experiments demonstrate significantly improved fine-grained controllability while maintaining speech intelligibility and speaker identity.

Conclusion: Post-training is a practical approach to extending existing speech synthesis models with fine-grained emotional and durational control capabilities.

Abstract: Audiobook narration, conversational agents, and audiovisual dubbing require speech that conveys changing emotions and adapts its pacing within a single utterance. But most existing TTS systems typically rely on utterance-level style conditioning, making such fine-grained control difficult to achieve. In light of this, and inspired by the success of post-training in large language models, we propose a unified post-training framework that equips pretrained text-to-speech models with natural-language control over segment-level emotion and duration. Supervised fine-tuning establishes instruction-conditioned speech generation, while reinforcement learning with group relative policy optimization refines control accuracy using emotion and duration rewards alongside content and speaker preservation objectives. By reusing the pretrained architecture, our approach avoids additional inference-time control modules. Experiments demonstrate significantly improved fine-grained controllability while maintaining speech intelligibility and speaker identity, highlighting post-training as a practical approach to extending existing speech synthesis models.

</details>


### [84] [ZipCodec: Ultra-Low-Frame-Rate Streaming Speech Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11642)
*Luca Della Libera,Cem Subakan,Mirco Ravanelli*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: ZipCodec is a streaming neural speech codec achieving 6.25 Hz frame rate and 0.80 kbps bitrate with 160 ms latency, using WavLM distillation and transformer architecture with 842M parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reducing frame rate in neural audio codecs remains challenging as each token must preserve more information while maintaining reconstruction quality, despite achieving increasingly low bitrates.

Method: The approach combines large-scale WavLM distillation with a redesigned transformer-based architecture, a scalar spherical quantizer, and a latency-aware streaming decoder.

Result: ZipCodec substantially outperforms existing streaming codecs at comparable bitrates in both reconstruction and downstream tasks, while operating at a significantly lower frame rate. It achieves real-time single-stream inference on a consumer-grade CPU despite having 842M parameters.

Conclusion: The proposed ZipCodec demonstrates that extremely low frame rates (6.25 Hz) can be achieved while maintaining high quality reconstruction and downstream performance, enabling efficient real-time inference on consumer hardware.

Abstract: Neural audio codecs are a fundamental component of modern speech generation systems. While recent codecs achieve increasingly low bitrates, reducing frame rate remains challenging, as each token must preserve more information while maintaining reconstruction quality. We present ZipCodec, a streaming neural speech codec operating at 6.25 Hz and 0.80 kbps with a theoretical latency of 160 ms. Our approach combines large-scale WavLM distillation with a redesigned transformer-based architecture, a scalar spherical quantizer, and a latency-aware streaming decoder. Experiments show that ZipCodec substantially outperforms existing streaming codecs at comparable bitrates in both reconstruction and downstream tasks, while operating at a significantly lower frame rate. Despite its 842M parameters, ZipCodec achieves real-time single-stream inference on a consumer-grade CPU. Demo samples, code and checkpoints are available at https://lucadellalib.github.io/zipcodec-web/.

</details>


### [85] [Continuous-Time Acoustic Modelling with Neural Controlled Differential Equations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11725)
*Mattias Cross,Minghui Zhao,Anton Ragni*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes using neural controlled differential equations (CDEs) for duration-aware acoustic modelling in text-to-speech (TTS) systems. Instead of traditional duration prediction that only expands phone-level states to frame-level, this approach treats phone representations as temporally parameterized control paths and uses a neural acoustic vector field to produce continuous-time hidden states that evolve with both phonetic content and duration-derived timing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current TTS models use duration prediction to expand phone-level encoder states to frame-level decoder inputs, but this only changes where and how often latent states appear—not their values. The paper aims to create a continuous-time mechanism that allows hidden state values to evolve with phonetic content and timing information, rather than just repeating static states.

Method: The method formulates phone representation as a temporally parameterized control path and uses a neural acoustic vector field to produce continuous-time hidden states. The resulting trajectory can be sampled at discrete points and integrated into a standard acoustic decoder pipeline. Experiments compare CDEs with typical recurrent models and test different temporal resolutions (phone-level and half-phone step-sizes).

Result: Objective results contrast CDEs and typical recurrent models. Subjective results show that CDE-based models evaluating one phone per step can improve rank-order agreement between synthesized and reference emotion intensity while maintaining comparable emotion-expression quality to a strong baseline. Experiments with half-phone step-sizes suggest that temporal resolution changes the trade-off between style tracking and absolute calibration.

Conclusion: Neural CDEs represent a promising design space for continuous-time and duration-aware style-sensitive TTS, offering improved emotion intensity tracking while maintaining quality comparable to strong baselines.

Abstract: Text-to-speech (TTS) models commonly address text--speech alignment by expanding phone-level encoder states to frame-level decoder inputs using predicted durations. While this length-regulation step resolves alignment structurally, this use of duration typically changes only where and how often latent states appear, not the values of the states themselves. This paper proposes a continuous-time mechanism for duration-aware acoustic modelling in TTS using neural controlled differential equations (CDEs). We formulate the phone representation as a temporally parameterised control path and use a neural acoustic vector field to produce a continuous-time hidden state whose values evolve with phonetic content and duration-derived timing. The resulting trajectory can be sampled at discrete points and integrated into a standard acoustic decoder pipeline. Objective results contrast CDEs and typical recurrent models. Subjective results suggest that CDE-based models evaluating one phone per step can improve rank-order agreement between synthesised and reference emotion intensity while maintaining comparable emotion-expression quality to a strong baseline. Additional experiments with half-phone step-sizes suggest that temporal resolution changes the trade-off between style tracking and absolute calibration. These results position CDEs as a promising design space for continuous-time and duration-aware style-sensitive TTS.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [86] [M3-Former: Multimodal Transformer with Mixture-of-Experts for Long-Term Vessel Trajectory Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10559)
*Wenzhe Jin,Haina Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: M3-Former is a novel multimodal vessel trajectory prediction framework that leverages large language models (LLMs) to address challenges of behavioral multimodality, limited semantic utilization, and long-term error accumulation. It uses a dual-granularity Mixture-of-Experts architecture and a Steering-Weighted Cross-Entropy loss to achieve state-of-the-art performance on long-term vessel trajectory forecasting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses three key challenges in vessel trajectory prediction: 1) behavioral multimodality - vessels can exhibit multiple possible behaviors, 2) limited semantic utilization - existing methods underutilize vessel static attributes and navigational intent, and 3) long-term error accumulation - predictions degrade significantly over extended time horizons (1-4 hours).

Method: The proposed M3-Former framework incorporates three key innovations: 1) A unified multimodal representation space where static semantic information (vessel attributes, navigational intent) is encoded by a pre-trained LLM and aligned with dynamic trajectory features via self-attention; 2) A dual-granularity Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture with sequence-level experts for global navigation trends and token-level experts for fine-grained maneuvering behaviors; 3) A Steering-Weighted Cross-Entropy loss designed to address the long-tail distribution of sparse turning samples in critical maneuvering scenarios.

Result: Experiments on a real-world Danish AIS dataset demonstrate that M3-Former consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across prediction horizons from 1 to 4 hours. In the 4-hour prediction task, the method achieves 4.4% reduction in Average Displacement Error (ADE) and 5.1% reduction in Final Displacement Error (FDE) compared to the strongest baseline. Qualitative and ablation analyses confirm that semantic fusion effectively reduces long-term trajectory drift, while the dual-granularity MoE improves robustness in complex waterways and route-branching scenarios.

Conclusion: The proposed framework establishes a semantic-guided hierarchical prediction paradigm that jointly models high-level navigational intent and local motion dynamics for robust long-term vessel trajectory forecasting. This approach effectively addresses the limitations of existing methods by integrating LLM-encoded semantic priors with a hierarchical motion modeling architecture, demonstrating significant improvements in prediction accuracy for extended time horizons.

Abstract: To address the challenges of behavioral multimodality, limited semantic utilization, and long-term error accumulation in vessel trajectory prediction, this paper proposes M3-Former, a multimodal trajectory prediction framework enhanced by large language models (LLMs). The proposed framework incorporates vessel static attributes and navigational intent as semantic priors for long-term trajectory modeling. Specifically, a unified multimodal representation space is constructed, in which static semantic information is encoded by a pre-trained LLM and aligned with dynamic trajectory features through self-attention. To jointly capture global route planning and local motion variations, a dual-granularity Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture is introduced, where sequence-level experts model global navigation trends and token-level experts refine fine-grained maneuvering behaviors. In addition, a Steering-Weighted Cross-Entropy loss is designed to alleviate the long-tail distribution of sparse turning samples and improve prediction accuracy in critical maneuvering scenarios. Experiments on a real-world Danish AIS dataset demonstrate that M\textsuperscript{3}-Former consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across prediction horizons from 1 to 4 hours. In the 4-hour prediction task, the proposed method reduces Average Displacement Error (ADE) and Final Displacement Error (FDE) by 4.4\% and 5.1\%, respectively, compared with the strongest baseline. Qualitative and ablation analyses further verify that semantic fusion effectively reduces long-term trajectory drift, while the dual-granularity MoE improves robustness in complex waterways and route-branching scenarios. The proposed framework establishes a semantic-guided hierarchical prediction paradigm, in which high-level navigational intent and local motion dynamics are jointly modeled for robust long-term vessel trajectory forecasting.

</details>


### [87] [Halo: Improving forecast accuracy through heteroscedastic estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10589)
*Adam Cataldo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Halo is a method that modifies deep forecasters to estimate a scale parameter alongside the location parameter, improving point estimates in heteroscedastic forecasting. Tested on three state-of-the-art models across five electricity price markets, it achieved 28/30 improvements in MSE and MAE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While heteroscedastic forecasting (estimating scale alongside location) is typically motivated for uncertainty quantification, this paper shows it also improves point estimates, contrary to reported negative results for heteroscedastic estimation outside time series.

Method: Halo modifies existing deep forecaster architectures by adding a second output for the scale of the implied distribution, training under negative log likelihood. Tested under both Gaussian and Laplacian losses with three models: a transformer, a graph network paired with a variational autoencoder, and a single-layer convolutional network.

Result: On five electricity price markets, Halo improved MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons. Average MSE reduced by 2.6% to 16.5%, and average MAE by 1.7% to 11.0%. Two findings: (1) the approach for scale estimation (second projection head vs. full parallel network) matters far less than whether scale is estimated at all; (2) improvement holds without retuning hyperparameters already optimized for point-estimate baseline.

Conclusion: Heteroscedastic forecasting via Halo consistently improves point estimates across diverse architectures and markets. The method is robust to implementation details and does not require hyperparameter retuning, making it a practical improvement for deep forecasting models.

Abstract: Heteroscedastic forecasting, where a network estimates a scale parameter alongside a location parameter, is normally motivated by uncertainty quantification. This paper shows it also improves the point estimate, in contrast to reported negative results for heteroscedastic estimation outside time series. Halo is a modification that reuses an existing deep forecaster's architecture, giving it a second output for the scale of its implied distribution and training it under the matching negative log likelihood. Adapting three state-of-the-art models --- a transformer, a graph network paired with a variational autoencoder, and a single-layer convolutional network --- under both Gaussian and Laplacian losses demonstrates the phenomenon. On the five electricity price markets of a standard forecasting benchmark, Halo improves MSE and MAE in 28 of 30 model-market-metric comparisons, cutting average MSE by 2.6% to 16.5% and average MAE by 1.7% to 11.0%. Two findings emerge: (1) whether the scale estimate comes from a second projection head or from a full parallel network matters far less than whether the network estimates scale, and (2) the improvement holds under the hyperparameters already tuned for the point-estimate baseline, so retuning is optional.

</details>


### [88] [Zero-shot rib design: merging training-free generative prior with topology optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10643)
*Yongmin Kwon,Namwoo Kang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel topology optimization framework that uses frozen text-to-image diffusion models as training-free design priors. By combining prompt-induced generative gradients with finite element sensitivity analysis, engineers can express structural design intent through natural language prompts. The method achieves up to 31.5% mechanical and 23.0% thermoelastic compliance reductions across multiple domains, with a recurring structural signature of suppressed dead-end branches in rib skeletons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Natural load-bearing structures (leaf venation, trabecular bone, spider webs) achieve high stiffness-to-mass ratios, yet classical topology optimizers rarely produce such geometries. Additionally, there is a lack of methods for engineers to express structural design intent through natural language.

Method: The work treats a frozen text-to-image diffusion model as a training-free source of design knowledge and distills it into density-based topology optimization via score distillation sampling. Prompt-induced generative gradients are combined with finite element sensitivity at every iteration. A Heaviside projection with β-continuation resolves intermediate-density artifacts, and an automated skeleton-based pipeline converts optimized density fields into CAD-ready geometry.

Result: In 245 primary SDS runs across four geometric domains and two physics regimes, 38 of 49 prompt-domain combinations achieved statistically significant compliance reductions (up to -31.5% mechanical and -23.0% thermoelastic), outperforming gradient-based baselines. The generative prior suppresses dead-end branches in rib skeletons (endpoint-compliance correlation r = +0.56 to +0.99). Heaviside projection reduces intermediate-density tendency from 42.6% to <3%.

Conclusion: The framework successfully uses a pretrained generative model as a reusable, training-free prior for engineering design. By retargeting the generative prior across domains, loading conditions, and physics objectives through text prompts, natural language becomes an explicit, machine-interpretable representation of engineer intent, enabling efficient exploration of nature-inspired optimal structures.

Abstract: Natural load-bearing patterns such as leaf venation, trabecular bone, and spider webs achieve high stiffness per unit mass, yet classical topology optimizers rarely reach such geometries, and few let engineers express structural design intent through natural language. This work treats a frozen text-to-image diffusion model as a training-free source of design knowledge and distills it into the physics loop of density-based topology optimization via score distillation sampling, so that a text prompt becomes an explicit, machine-interpretable representation of engineer intent. The prompt-induced generative gradient and the finite element sensitivity are combined at every iteration, letting physics decide which prompt-induced features survive. In 245 primary SDS runs spanning four geometric domains and two physics regimes, 38 of 49 prompt--domain combinations achieved statistically significant compliance reductions (up to $-31.5\%$ mechanical and $-23.0\%$ thermoelastic), outperforming gradient-based baselines. Cross-domain morphological analysis identifies a recurring structural signature of improvement: in most domains the generative prior suppresses dead-end branches in the rib skeleton, with endpoint--compliance correlation $r = +0.56$ to $+0.99$. A Heaviside projection with $β$-continuation resolves a pronounced intermediate-density tendency in this diffusion--physics coupling ($42.6\%$ to $<3\%$), and an automated skeleton-based pipeline converts optimized density fields into \rev{candidate geometry ready for computer-aided design. By retargeting the generative prior across domains, loading conditions, and physics objectives through a change of text prompt, with each new problem's physics setup specified separately, the framework uses a pretrained generative model as a reusable, training-free prior for engineering design.

</details>


### [89] [Byzantine-Robust Federated Fire Detection with a Rotating Coordinator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10647)
*Georgia Argyrou,Aymen Bahrouny,Hedi Fendriy,Alexander Jung*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a federated learning solution for indoor fire detection using edge cameras, addressing three practical challenges: limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine (malicious/faulty) clients, and single point of failure from a fixed aggregation server. The contributions include a curated fire-detection dataset, a compressed edge-deployable detector, and a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method with rotating coordinator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing federated learning solutions for fire detection leave three critical obstacles unaddressed: (1) limited uplink bandwidth for transmitting model updates from edge devices, (2) Byzantine attacks from malicious or faulty clients, and (3) unconditional trust in a single permanently fixed aggregation server, creating a single point of failure.

Method: The paper provides three main contributions: (i) assembling a curated indoor fire-detection dataset from eight public sources; (ii) developing an edge-deployable detector with model updates compressed up to 10x; and (iii) designing a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method combining history-aware aggregation with a rotating coordinator that evicts stealthy attacks while removing the fixed-server single point of failure.

Result: On the held-out test set, the rotating-coordinator method matches its fixed-server counterpart in accuracy and detection speed. A physically distributed six-node cloud deployment confirms the feasibility of the proposed approach.

Conclusion: The proposed semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust federated learning method successfully addresses bandwidth limitations, malicious client attacks, and single point of failure issues in indoor fire detection systems, while maintaining comparable accuracy and detection speed to fixed-server approaches.

Abstract: We study the application of federated learning (FL) to indoor fire detection. Such fire-detection systems use edge cameras that record sensitive footage which cannot easily be collected at a central server. Existing federated solutions leave three practical obstacles unaddressed: limited uplink bandwidth, Byzantine (malicious or faulty) clients, and unconditional trust in a single, permanently fixed aggregation server. Our main contributions address all three. In particular, we provide (i) a curated indoor fire-detection dataset assembled from eight public sources; (ii) an edge-deployable detector whose model updates are compressed up to 10 time with only a small loss in balanced accuracy; and (iii) a semi-decentralized Byzantine-robust FL method that combines history-aware aggregation with a rotating coordinator, evicting stealthy attacks that per-round filters miss while removing the fixed-server single point of failure. On the held-out test set the rotating-coordinator method matches its fixed-server counterpart in accuracy and detection speed, and a physically distributed six-node cloud deployment confirms feasibility.

</details>


### [90] [Artificial Intelligence Algorithms for the Detection of Pathologies Related to Lung Cancer through Image Analysis using Convolutional Neural Networks and Data Augmentation: a systematic mapping of the literature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10652)
*Pablo Ramirez Amador*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This is a review paper analyzing 96 articles (2015-present) on AI and deep learning applications for lung cancer detection in radiology. It focuses on CNNs with transfer learning and data augmentation as promising techniques.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lung cancer is a leading cause of death worldwide, and early diagnosis is crucial for patient prognosis. However, interpreting medical images requires trained experts. This work aims to review recent AI and DL applications to automate and optimize lung cancer image analysis.

Method: Exhaustive literature search in PubMed, IEEE Xplore, Scopus, and Web of Science. 96 articles published from 2015 to present were selected, focusing on AI and DL applications in biomedical engineering for lung cancer detection.

Result: AI and DL demonstrate high sensitivity and specificity for early lung cancer diagnosis. However, limitations include: lack of standardized data, model explainability issues, patient privacy concerns, and ethical/social implications.

Conclusion: AI and DL can offer an effective alternative for early lung cancer diagnosis and positively impact patient care. However, further research and regulation are required to ensure quality, reliability, and responsible clinical application.

Abstract: Lung cancer is one of the leading causes of death worldwide, and its early diagnosis is crucial to improving patients prognosis and quality of life. However, the process of interpreting medical images for the detection of lung cancer is complex and requires trained experts. In this context, artificial intelligence (AI) and deep learning (DL) emerge as potential tools to automate and optimize image analysis. The objective of this work is to review the most recent and relevant applications of AI and DL in the field of radiology for the detection of lung cancer. To this end, an exhaustive search was carried out in scientific databases such as PubMed,IEEEXPLORE, Scopus and Web of Science, and 96 articles published from 2015 to the present addressing the use of AI and DL in biomedical engineering were selected. Emphasis is placed on the use of convolutional neural networks (CNN) with transfer learning and Data Augmentation as promising techniques to improve the accuracy and efficiency of the image interpretation process. The results show that the use of AI and DL can offer an effective alternative for the early diagnosis of lung cancer, with high sensitivity and specificity. However, current limitations and challenges that must be addressed to guarantee its responsible and safe application in clinical practice are also identified, such as the lack of standardized data, the ex plainability of the models, patient privacy, and the ethical and social implications. It is concluded that the use of AI and DL can have a positive impact on the care of patients with lung cancer, but further research and regulation are required to ensure its quality and reliability.

</details>


### [91] [GEOSTEER: Geodesic Optimization for Activation Steering in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10658)
*Xuan Cuong Ngo,Hao Vo,Ngan Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GeoSteer is a norm-preserving activation steering method that formulates steering as Riemannian optimization, using multi-step geodesic updates on the representation manifold with learned nonlinear objectives to adaptively guide each steering step, outperforming baseline methods on truthfulness, toxicity, and preference benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing norm-preserving steering methods are limited by predefined steering trajectories and one-step updates that fail to capture complex activation distributions, risking representation collapse and degradation.

Method: GeoSteer formulates steering as a Riemannian optimization problem, updating activations through a sequence of small geodesic steps on the representation manifold. It learns a nonlinear activation-space objective to distinguish desired from undesired activations, using this function to adaptively guide each steering step while preserving activation norm.

Result: GeoSteer consistently improves over state-of-the-art activation steering baselines across TruthfulQA, RealToxicityPrompts, and UltraFeedback benchmarks.

Conclusion: Norm-preserving steering can be made more effective by replacing predefined one-step edits with adaptive, geometry-aware optimization, yielding smoother, more stable, and more consistent steering behavior.

Abstract: Activation steering provides a lightweight way to control large language models (LLMs) by modifying their hidden activations at inference time. Among these approaches, norm-preserving steering aims to change model behavior without altering the activation norm, reducing the risk of representation collapse and degradation. However, existing norm-preserving methods are limited by predefined steering trajectories and by their reliance on one-step updates, which may fail to capture the complex structure of activation distributions. We propose GeoSteer, an optimization-based method for norm-preserving activation steering. GeoSteer formulates steering as a Riemannian optimization problem and updates activations through a sequence of small geodesic steps on the representation manifold. To avoid fixed steering directions, GeoSteer learns a nonlinear activation-space objective that distinguishes desired from undesired activations, and uses this function to adaptively guide each steering step. This multistep formulation yields smoother, more stable, and more consistent steering behavior while preserving the activation norm. Across TruthfulQA, RealToxicityPrompts, and UltraFeedback benchmarks, GeoSteer consistently improves over state-of-the-art activation steering baselines. These results suggest that norm-preserving steering can be made more effective by replacing predefined one-step edits with adaptive, geometry-aware optimization.

</details>


### [92] [Conformal Calibration Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10737)
*Achref Doula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses conformal prediction under domain shift where labeled calibration data is only available in a source domain but prediction sets are needed in a target domain. The proposed Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC) method transports source calibration to target using paired data, then corrects residual mismatch using unlabeled target inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conformal prediction requires exchangeability between calibration and deployment data, which fails in transfer learning scenarios where calibration data exists only in a source domain but predictions are needed in a target domain (linked via unlabeled paired observations).

Method: The method has two components: (1) Transport labeled source calibration to target space using paired data, (2) Correct residual post-transport mismatch using unlabeled target inputs. Two instantiations: TCC-KS uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate to detect mismatch and adjust conservatively; weighted-TCC reweights transported calibration toward target domain for improved efficiency.

Result: Across CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS, the method achieves reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data. Label-free diagnostics predict when correction is needed.

Conclusion: TCC provides a principled framework for conformal calibration transfer across domains with finite-sample coverage guarantees that adapt to observable mismatch measures, eliminating the need for labeled target calibration data.

Abstract: Conformal prediction converts point predictions into set-valued predictions with coverage guarantees under exchangeability between calibration and deployment data. We study conformal calibration transfer, where this requirement fails because labeled calibration is available only in a source space, while prediction sets are needed in a target space linked to the source through unlabeled paired observations (e.g., paired modalities or sensor changes). We propose Transported Conformal Calibration (TCC): we transport labeled source calibration into the target space using the paired data, and then correct residual post-transport mismatch using only unlabeled target inputs. We instantiate this correction with two complementary methods: TCC-KS, which uses a label-free uncertainty surrogate to detect mismatch and adjust calibration conservatively, and weighted-TCC, which reweights transported calibration toward the target domain for improved efficiency when weights are stable. We provide finite-sample target-domain coverage guarantees that adapt to an observable measure of mismatch. Across CIFAR-100-C, Tiny-ImageNet-C, and SEN12MS, we show reliable target-domain coverage transfer without labeled target calibration data, with label-free diagnostics that predict when correction is needed.

</details>


### [93] [The Truth Was Never Gone: Perfect Aliasing in Compliant-Context Truth Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10739)
*Dylan Jayabahu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies "perfect aliasing" as a fundamental limitation of truth probes in LLMs—when truthful reporting coincides with prescribed actions, linear probes cannot distinguish truth from deception. While mixed-fit probes (using both compliant and rival contexts) achieve perfect recoverability (AUROC 1.000 vs 0.006 for conventional probes), this only establishes linear recoverability, not genuine belief detection or deployable deception detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate and address the fundamental limitations of truth probes in detecting deception in language models, specifically the "perfect aliasing" problem where probes cannot distinguish truthful from deceptive outputs when truthful reporting coincides with prescribed actions.

Method: The authors use a controlled binary reporting game, separating prescribed output symbols from semantic action using randomized codebooks. They compare conventional compliant-fit probes with mixed-fit probes (trained on both compliant and rival contexts), evaluating across 751 cell-layer pairs using AUROC metrics. Experiments include a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy that answers falsely on rival trials.

Result: On rival contexts, conventional truth probes score near-random (0.006 AUROC), while mixed-fit probes achieve perfect separation (1.000 AUROC). Two compliant-fit probes both perfect in-distribution score 0.080 and 0.986 on the same rival activations. The AUROC sum identity (truth + prescribed-action = 1) holds across all 751 cell-layer pairs to floating-point precision.

Conclusion: Truth probes measure what training contexts they are fitted on, not necessarily genuine belief or causal truth-seeking behavior. The findings establish linear recoverability under mixed fitting but do not demonstrate preserved functional belief, causal use of recovered directions, or a deployable deception detector. The paper cautions against overinterpreting probe directions as indicators of model honesty.

Abstract: A truth probe fitted where truthful reporting and a task's prescribed action coincide cannot distinguish those targets from its fitting labels alone. We call this failure of semantic identification perfect aliasing. In a controlled binary reporting game, truth and prescribed-action probes fitted on compliant contexts solve the same optimization. On rival contexts their labels are complements, forcing their AUROCs to sum to one; this identity holds across 751 cell-layer pairs to floating-point precision. We separate prescribed output symbols from semantic action using randomized codebooks, then separate truth from prescribed action by fitting on mixed compliant and rival contexts. For a reward-trained Gemma-2-9B policy that answers falsely on all evaluated rival trials, the conventional probe scores $0.006 \pm 0.005$ AUROC across three training seeds, while mixed-fit probes score $1.000$ on the same held-out activations. Mixed fitting uses more training examples and access to labelled rival contexts, so this comparison establishes linear recoverability rather than isolating the benefit of decorrelation. We also show that two compliant-fit probes, both perfect in-distribution, score $0.080$ and $0.986$ on the same rival activations. The findings concern what a probe measures: they do not establish preserved functional belief, causal use of the recovered direction, or a deployable deception detector. Code and aggregate results accompany the paper.

</details>


### [94] [Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss: Penalizing Center-Class Hedging in Ordinal Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10752)
*Manisha Kandel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper addresses "center-class hedging" - a failure mode where neural networks trained with standard cross-entropy on ordinal classification tasks tend to predict middle classes regardless of the true label. The authors propose AMOL (Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss), which applies a multiplicative weight that penalizes predictions near the center when the true label is far from center. They also introduce Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) as a diagnostic metric. Experiments on four benchmarks show AMOL achieves best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa, with an asymmetric variant eliminating center-class hedging entirely on the Abalone dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard cross-entropy loss causes neural networks to hedge predictions toward center classes in ordinal classification tasks because predicting the middle class minimizes expected symmetric loss. While existing ordinal losses address large-error penalization and rank consistency, none directly suppresses center-class hedging based on the true label's position relative to the ordinal center.

Method: The authors propose the Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss (AMOL), which applies a multiplicative weight to per-class loss terms: m(k,y) = 1 + α·(1 - |k-c|/c)·(|y-c|/c), where c is the center class, k is the candidate class, and y is the true label. This weight is large only when the candidate class is near center and the true label is far from center, collapsing to standard behavior otherwise. They also introduce Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) as a diagnostic metric to directly quantify center-class hedging.

Result: Across four ordinal classification benchmarks and five random seeds, AMOL achieves the best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) on all four datasets compared to cross-entropy, OLL, and SORD baselines. The asymmetric variant (AMOL-asym) eliminates center-class hedging entirely on the Abalone dataset (CHR = 0.000 ± 0.000 across all five seeds, n ≈ 266 extreme-class test samples per run), compared to 0.074 ± 0.005 for standard cross-entropy.

Conclusion: The AMOL approach effectively addresses center-class hedging in ordinal classification by dynamically weighting loss based on the relationship between candidate class position and true label distance from the ordinal center. The introduced CHR metric provides direct quantification of this previously unaddressed failure mode.

Abstract: Standard cross-entropy loss causes neural networks trained on ordinal classification tasks to hedge predictions toward center classes, a failure mode we term \emph{center-class hedging}. This occurs because predicting the middle class minimizes expected symmetric loss, making it the path of least resistance regardless of the true label. Existing ordinal losses address related problems such as large-error penalization and rank consistency, but none directly suppresses center-class hedging as a function of where the true label lies relative to the ordinal center. We propose the Adaptive Margin Ordinal Loss (AMOL), a multiplicative weight applied to per-class loss terms of the form $m(k,y) = 1 + α\cdot (1 - |k-c|/c) \cdot (|y-c|/c)$, where $c$ is the center class, $k$ is the candidate class, and $y$ is the true label. The weight encodes a joint condition: it is large only when the candidate class is near center and the true label is far from center, collapsing to standard behavior otherwise. We further introduce the Center-Hedging Rate (CHR) as a diagnostic metric that directly quantifies this failure mode. Across four ordinal classification benchmarks and five random seeds, AMOL achieves the best or tied-best Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) on all four datasets compared to cross-entropy, OLL, and SORD baselines. An asymmetric variant (AMOL-asym) eliminates center-class hedging entirely on the Abalone dataset ($\text{CHR} = 0.000 \pm 0.000$ across all five seeds, $n \approx 266$ extreme-class test samples per run), compared to $0.074 \pm 0.005$ for standard cross-entropy.

</details>


### [95] [A Bellman Optimality Equation for Plasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10776)
*Jeremy Lucas,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the optimization of plasticity (generalized directed information from observations to actions) in continual reinforcement learning, building on Abel et al. (2025)'s reformulation of the stability-plasticity tradeoff as an empowerment-plasticity tradeoff. The key contribution is deriving a Bellman optimality equation for plasticity optimization in Markov decision processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The stability-plasticity tradeoff is a core challenge in continual reinforcement learning. While extensive literature exists on optimizing empowerment, no prior work addresses optimizing plasticity under the new definition by Abel et al. (2025), which defines plasticity as generalized directed information from observations to actions.

Method: The paper presents theoretical analysis deriving a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity within Markov decision processes, analogous to previous work on empowerment optimization.

Result: The paper establishes that there exists a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity, similar to the existing framework for empowerment. This provides a theoretical foundation for optimizing plasticity in MDPs.

Conclusion: This preliminary work extends the empowerment-plasticity framework by enabling optimization of plasticity, complementing the existing literature on empowerment optimization. Future work would likely involve developing practical algorithms based on this theoretical foundation.

Abstract: In continual reinforcement learning, carefully managing the stability-plasticity tradeoff remains a core challenge. Recent work by Abel et al. (2025) formalized this dilemma by defining plasticity as the generalized directed information from an agent's observations to its actions, and empowerment as the generalized directed information from its actions to its observations. This formulation successfully reframes the traditional stability-plasticity tradeoff as an empowerment-plasticity tradeoff. However, while extensive literature exists on optimizing for empowerment, there is currently no research addressing the optimization of plasticity under this new definition. This paper presents preliminary work toward optimizing plasticity within Markov decision processes. We show that there exists a Bellman optimality equation for optimizing plasticity similar to previous work for empowerment.

</details>
