### [96] [Counterfactual Marginalisation: Framework for Evaluating Robustness to Nuisance Variables](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10778)
*Yasin Ibrahim,Hermione Warr,Robin J. Evans,Konstantinos Kamnitsas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes counterfactual marginalisation as a test-time evaluation procedure to assess robustness of classification models to demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts. The method intervenes on nuisance variables (e.g., age, sex), generates counterfactual versions of test images, and averages predictions to marginalise demographic effects while preserving patient-specific information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning models can achieve strong test performance by relying on demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts rather than learning clinically relevant features. There is a need for test-time evaluation procedures to assess model robustness to such variables.

Method: The authors propose counterfactual (CF) marginalisation: given a CF image generator, they intervene on nuisance parent variables (such as age or sex), generate CF versions of each test image, and average predictions over a target intervention distribution. This produces intervention-aware predictions that marginalise demographic effects while preserving patient-specific latent information.

Result: The framework defines metrics for CF risk, calibration, stability, and worst-case sensitivity. The authors demonstrate this framework's utility for quantitative robustness evaluation.

Conclusion: Counterfactual marginalisation provides a valuable test-time evaluation procedure for assessing and improving robustness of classification models to demographic and acquisition-related shortcuts.

Abstract: Machine learning models can achieve strong test performance while relying on demographic or acquisition-related shortcuts. We propose counterfactual (CF) marginalisation as a test-time evaluation procedure for assessing robustness of classification models to such variables. Given a CF image generator, we intervene on nuisance parent variables such as age or sex, generate CF versions of each test image, and average predictions over a target intervention distribution. This produces intervention-aware predictions that marginalise demographic effects while preserving patient-specific latent information. We use these predictions to define metrics for CF risk, calibration, stability and worst-case sensitivity. We demonstrate this framework's utility for quantitative robustness evaluation.

</details>


### [97] [From Connectivity to Rewards: Dense Reward Learning with Directed State Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10781)
*Shuyuan Zhang,Zihan Wang,Xiao-Wen Chang,Doina Precup*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes G2QDR (Graph-Guided Quasimetric Dense Reward), a framework that addresses limitations in graph-based Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (GCHRL) by introducing a state connectivity model that predicts pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments and transforms these into scalar auxiliary dense rewards for continuous hierarchical guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph-based GCHRL methods overlook intrinsic connectivity information, using graphs merely as stochastic sampling tools rather than environmental models. This is particularly problematic in quasimetric environments where asymmetric state transitions challenge stable policy learning and robust path planning. The paper aims to fully leverage underlying topology for efficient learning.

Method: The proposed G2QDR framework introduces a state connectivity model implemented via a neural network trained on a directed state graph generated during exploration. This model predicts pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments, which is then transformed into scalar auxiliary dense rewards providing continuous guidance across multiple hierarchical levels.

Result: Empirical results across a wide range of sparse reward environments demonstrate that G2QDR can enhance the performance of baseline GCHRL approaches with acceptable computational overhead. The framework is theoretically compatible with any existing GCHRL architecture.

Conclusion: G2QDR successfully addresses the limitations of existing graph-based GCHRL methods by leveraging state connectivity information in asymmetric environments. The approach provides theoretical integrability with existing architectures and empirical validation showing improved performance in sparse reward settings.

Abstract: The integration of graphs with Goal-Conditioned Hierarchical Reinforcement Learning (GCHRL) has received increasing attention, as graphs naturally encode task hierarchies for effective subgoal sampling. However, existing methods often overlook intrinsic connectivity information, failing to fully leverage the underlying topology for efficient learning. Most graph-based GCHRL methods use the graph as a stochastic sampling tool rather than as an environmental model that encodes connectivity and state-accessibility information. This limitation is particularly acute in quasimetric environments, where the inherent asymmetry of state transitions poses a fundamental challenge to stable policy learning and robust path planning. In this paper, we address these problems by introducing a state connectivity model designed to predict pairwise state connectivity strength in asymmetric environments. We transform these connectivity strengths into scalar auxiliary dense rewards, providing continuous guidance across multiple hierarchical levels. We demonstrate that our proposed framework, Graph-Guided Quasimetric Dense Reward (G2QDR), can theoretically be integrated into any existing GCHRL architecture, and the state connectivity model is efficiently implemented via a neural network trained on a directed state graph generated during exploration. Empirical results across a wide range of sparse reward environments indicate that, in general, G2QDR can enhance the performance of baseline GCHRL approaches with acceptable computational overhead.

</details>


### [98] [DR-LabStack: Design and Implementation of a Clinician-Facing Web System for Diabetic Retinopathy Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10796)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DR-LabStack is a React-Flask web system that integrates four heterogeneous pretrained diabetic retinopathy (DR) prediction models (RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, Two-level Ensemble) through a common clinical interface, using shared forms, backend adapters, and standardized JSON responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained DR prediction models differ in input fields, serialization formats, preprocessing requirements, and output semantics, making explicit coordination between user interface and inference service necessary for accessibility through a common clinical interface.

Method: Designed and implemented DR-LabStack - a React-Flask web system with: (1) shared form retrieving ordered model features and rendering model-specific controls; (2) positional input vector construction; (3) backend adapters loading heterogeneous artifacts and applying scalers; (4) common JSON response supporting binary classification display with method and source information.

Result: Functional evaluation confirmed all four models loaded with 14-, 6-, 8-, and 25-field contracts respectively. 62 Flask test-client requests characterized service behavior; 12 limited-vector checks confirmed invocation-path and threshold consistency. 24 browser-component scenarios verified input ordering, result rendering, and input-validation behavior.

Conclusion: The system demonstrates a reusable interaction and serving workflow for heterogeneous DR models. The contribution is web-system design, integration, and software functionality; clinical effectiveness and clinician usability require separate evaluation.

Abstract: Pretrained diabetic retinopathy (DR) prediction models differ in their input fields, serialization formats, preprocessing requirements, and output semantics. Making these models accessible through a common clinical interface therefore requires explicit coordination between the user interface and the inference service. We designed and implemented DR-LabStack, a React-Flask web system integrating four externally developed pretrained models: RuleFit, Pruned RuleFit, Elaborative XGBoost, and Two-level Ensemble. A shared form retrieves ordered model features, renders model-specific numerical and categorical controls, and constructs a positional input vector. Backend adapters load heterogeneous artifacts and apply the ensemble's accompanying scaler, while a common JSON response supports binary classification display alongside method and source information. Functional evaluation on September 8, 2026 used copied application files and real model artifacts in a documented isolated environment. All four models loaded and exposed their 14-, 6-, 8-, and 25-field contracts. Sixty-two Flask test-client requests characterized service behavior; 12 limited-vector checks confirmed invocation-path and threshold consistency. Twenty-four browser-component scenarios with mocked transport verified input ordering and result rendering and characterized input-validation behavior. The resulting system demonstrates a reusable interaction and serving workflow for heterogeneous DR models. The contribution is web-system design, integration, and software functionality; clinical effectiveness and clinician usability require separate evaluation.

</details>


### [99] [RiVaT-Fuse: Reliability-Calibrated Variational Tensor Fusion for Multimodal Prediction under Modality Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10798)
*Yingfan Xu,Tieming Liu,Ye Liang,Taiping Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RiVaT-Fuse is a novel reliability-calibrated variational tensor fusion framework for image-metadata prediction that treats fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation using a variational objective. It replaces scalar modality confidence with matrix-valued trust geometry and decomposes interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing representation-level fusion methods (concatenation, gating, conditional modulation, attention) arbitrarily choose an aggregation architecture without explicitly defining what the fused representation should mean under modality uncertainty. There is a need for a principled framework that defines fusion as latent-state estimation with explicit reliability calibration.

Method: The framework estimates a consensus latent state through a variational objective balancing image evidence, metadata evidence, structured cross-modal interaction, and stability. Key innovations include: (1) matrix-valued trust geometry replacing scalar modality confidence, (2) decomposition of interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, (3) coupling latent state with conditional robustness and structured multi-task prediction, (4) well-posedness and stability interpretations, and (5) efficient low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators.

Result: On an image-level image-metadata prediction benchmark, RiVaT-Fuse achieves the strongest overall predictive rank among direct representation-level baselines while improving probability and label stability under perturbation.

Conclusion: RiVaT-Fuse provides a principled approach to fusion that explicitly models reliability and uncertainty, demonstrating superior predictive performance and robustness compared to existing representation-level fusion methods.

Abstract: Image-metadata prediction requires fusing heterogeneous evidence whose reliability can vary across samples and latent factors. Existing representation-level fusion methods typically choose an aggregation architecture, such as concatenation, gating, conditional modulation, or attention, without explicitly defining what the fused representation should mean under modality uncertainty. We propose RiVaT-Fuse, a reliability-calibrated variational tensor fusion framework that defines fusion as sample-wise latent-state estimation. Rather than producing a fused vector by direct aggregation, RiVaT-Fuse estimates a consensus latent state through a variational objective that balances image evidence, metadata evidence, structured cross-modal interaction, and stability. The resulting framework replaces scalar modality confidence with matrix-valued trust geometry, decomposes interaction into additive, multiplicative, and relational components, and couples the latent state with conditional robustness and structured multi-task prediction. We provide well-posedness and stability interpretations of the latent solve and instantiate the framework with efficient low-rank-plus-diagonal trust operators. On an image-level image-metadata prediction benchmark, RiVaT-Fuse achieves the strongest overall predictive rank among direct representation-level baselines while improving probability and label stability under perturbation.

</details>


### [100] [Processing and classifying bird songs using wavelet techniques and supervised learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10826)
*Laura Lucia Dominguez Barrios,Fidel Aniano Causil Barrios,Alex Rodrigo dos Santos Sousa,Mariana Rodrigues Motta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes an integrated framework for classifying invasive bird species vocalizations in noisy natural soundscapes using Bayesian wavelet shrinkage with Epanechnikov kernel prior for denoising, combined with MFCC features and machine learning classifiers (SVM, Random Forest, Logistic Regression). The SVM model achieved 93.98% accuracy with 10-dimensional MFCCs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The study addresses the challenge of automated classification of invasive bird species vocalizations in natural soundscapes with high environmental noise, which is important for ecological monitoring and biological invasion management.

Method: Bayesian wavelet shrinkage using Epanechnikov kernel prior for signal denoising, followed by feature extraction (MFCCs, spectral entropy, zero-crossing rate) and classification using Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression, and SVM models.

Result: The wavelet-based preprocessing significantly enhanced classification performance. SVM achieved the highest accuracy of 0.9398 with a 10-dimensional MFCC configuration.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a robust statistical tool for automated ecological monitoring and biological invasion management, with the SVM classifier demonstrating superior performance.

Abstract: This study proposes an integrated framework for the processing and classification of invasive bird species vocalizations within natural soundscapes, characterized by high levels of environmental noise. We address the challenge of signal degradation by employing a Bayesian wavelet shrinkage methodology based on the Epanechnikov kernel prior, which offers a closed form decision rule and high computational efficiency for processing large bioacoustic datasets. The methodology was applied to recordings of three species obtained from the iNaturalist platform: \textit{Euphonia violacea}, \textit{Leiothrix lutea}, and \textit{Passer domesticus}. After signal denoising, we extracted a comprehensive set of features, including Mel-Frequency Cepstral Coefficients (MFCCs) and spectral indices such as entropy and zero-crossing rate. Several supervised learning models: Random Forest, Multinomial Logistic Regression and Support Vector Machine (SVM) were evaluated across different feature dimensionalities. Our results demonstrate that the proposed wavelet based preprocessing significantly enhances classification performance, with the SVM model achieving the highest accuracy (up to 0.9398) under a 10-dimensional MFCC configuration. This research provides a robust statistical tool for automated ecological monitoring and the management of biological invasions.

</details>


### [101] [Flow Duality and Source Geometry for Categorical Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10863)
*Etrit Haxholli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper establishes a theoretical duality between continuous and discrete flow matching by showing that projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through position-wise argmax yields discrete convex-interpolant paths. Different source distributions (Gaussian, bounded-uniform, centered negative-exponential) lead to qualitatively different interpolation behaviors, with implications for generative quality in learned transports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous and discrete flow matching have been treated as separate constructions. This paper aims to unify them by identifying a duality relationship, making the continuous source distribution an explicit design choice for categorical generation.

Method: The authors project continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through position-wise argmax to obtain discrete paths. This requires source laws with coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity. They analyze three source distributions: Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential.

Result: Different source geometries lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. These source-design effects can appear in learned transports and early generative quality, as demonstrated through visual diagnostics and language modeling experiments.

Conclusion: The duality between continuous and discrete flow matching provides a unified framework for understanding categorical generation. The choice of continuous source distribution significantly impacts discrete interpolation behavior, offering a new design dimension for generative models.

Abstract: Continuous and discrete flow matching are usually treated as separate constructions. This paper identifies a duality between them: projecting continuous convex-interpolant paths with one-hot targets through a position-wise argmax yields discrete convex-interpolant paths. The result requires source laws with appropriate coordinate symmetry and boundary regularity, and it makes the continuous source distribution an explicit design choice for categorical generation. We derive the induced discrete interpolation behavior for Gaussian, bounded-uniform, and centered negative-exponential sources, showing that different source geometries lead to qualitatively different transition timing and vocabulary-size dependence. Small visual diagnostics and a short language-modeling pilot suggest that these source-design effects can also appear in learned transports and early generative quality.

</details>


### [102] [Certifying Lower Bounds for Risk-Sensitive Reinforcement Learning under Adversarial State Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10866)
*Tong Li,Saunak Kumar Panda,Yisha Xiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends reinforcement learning certification methods to risk-sensitive objectives, providing lower bounds on exponential utility of cumulative rewards under adversarial state perturbations. The approach uses φ-divergence relaxation and dual optimization to obtain tractable certified bounds, with empirical validation showing risk-averse training outperforms risk-neutral training, particularly under larger perturbation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement learning agents in real-world safety-critical applications are vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in state observations. While certification methods can provide robustness guarantees, existing methods only address risk-neutral objectives, leaving a gap in risk-sensitive applications where the variance of outcomes matters.

Method: The paper introduces a φ-divergence relaxation of the perturbation set to formulate the risk-sensitive certification problem as a convex optimization problem. The dual of this problem is derived to obtain a tractable approximation of the certified lower bound. Additionally, an empirical method is proposed that selects the training risk-aversion parameter β independently of the risk level used during evaluation.

Result: Experiments on OpenAI Gym environments and a machine replacement problem demonstrate that risk-averse training generally yields policies with higher certified lower bounds compared to risk-neutral training, particularly under larger perturbation budgets. The relationship between risk aversion and certification performance is non-monotonic: certified lower bounds initially improve with increasing risk aversion but eventually decrease due to overly conservative policies.

Conclusion: Risk-sensitive certification in RL is achievable through φ-divergence relaxation and dual optimization. Risk-averse training provides better robustness guarantees than risk-neutral training, but optimal risk aversion exists and depends on the specific evaluation scenario. The proposed method for selecting training parameters independently of evaluation parameters offers practical utility for deploying robust RL agents in safety-critical applications.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) agents deployed in real-world environments are often vulnerable to adversarial perturbations in state observations, creating risks in safety-critical applications. Certification methods can improve robustness against adversarial perturbations by providing lower bounds on expected cumulative rewards. Existing certification methods, however, mainly focus on risk-neutral objectives. In this paper, we extend certification methods to risk-sensitive objectives by establishing lower bounds on the exponential utility of cumulative rewards under $l_{p}$-norm-bounded state adversarial perturbations ($1\leq p <\infty$). By introducing a $φ$-divergence relaxation of the perturbation set, we formulate the risk-sensitive certification problem as a convex optimization and derive its dual to obtain a tractable approximation of the certified lower bound. We further propose an empirical method that improves certified lower bounds by selecting the training risk-aversion parameter $β$ independently of the risk level used during evaluation. Experiments on both OpenAI Gym environments and a machine replacement problem show that, compared to risk-neutral training, risk-averse training generally yields policies with higher certified lower bounds, particularly under larger perturbation budgets. Moreover, under both risk-neutral and risk-averse evaluation settings, increasing risk aversion during training leads to non-monotonic certification performance, where certified lower bounds initially improve but eventually decrease due to overly conservative policies.

</details>


### [103] [Learning Orthogonal Multi-Index Models Beyond Small Initialization: Incremental Learning, Competitive Dynamics and Symmetry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10879)
*Mo Zhou,Weihang Xu,Simon S. Du,Maryam Fazel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes training dynamics of polynomial-width two-layer neural networks learning orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization. It proves that incremental learning still occurs (lower-order Hermite components learned before higher-order), and reveals a three-stage parameter mass reallocation process. The technical contribution is a symmetry-based finite-width approximation method.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous analyses of incremental learning in shallow networks relied on simplified settings (small initialization, correlation loss, layer-wise training) that reduce neuron interactions and leave feature learning dynamics under standard initialization unexplored. This work aims to understand training dynamics in more realistic conditions.

Method: The authors use a symmetry-based finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks, which provides better control of approximation errors compared to infinite-width limit comparisons. They analyze polynomial-width two-layer networks with orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization using polynomially many samples.

Result: The paper proves that incremental learning still occurs under standard initialization: the loss decreases sequentially according to the Hermite expansion of the target, with lower-order components learned before higher-order components. Additionally, training exhibits a competitive reallocation of parameter mass in three stages: (1) fitting and stabilizing the target mean, (2) shifting mass into the target subspace, and (3) concentrating on aligned neurons.

Conclusion: The work extends the understanding of incremental learning to more realistic settings with standard initialization, demonstrating that the phenomenon persists. The symmetry-based finite-width approximation method may be of independent interest for other theoretical analyses of neural network training.

Abstract: Recent work has identified incremental learning in shallow networks trained on single-index and multi-index models. However, existing analyses often rely on simplifying settings, such as small initialization, correlation loss, or layer-wise training. These choices reduce neuron interactions and leave some feature learning dynamics under standard initialization unexplored. We study training dynamics for polynomial-width two-layer networks learning orthogonal multi-index targets under standard initialization using polynomially many samples. We first prove that incremental learning still occurs: the loss decreases sequentially according to the Hermite expansion of the target, with lower-order components learned before higher-order components recover the individual target directions. In this standard initialization regime, training also shows a competitive reallocation of parameter mass: after the total mass fits the target mean and stabilizes, mass shifts into the target subspace and then concentrates on aligned neurons. Our theoretical analysis uses slightly modified gradient flow, while vanilla gradient descent empirically exhibits the same qualitative dynamics. Technically, we introduce a symmetry-based finite-width approximation via symmetrized networks, rather than comparing directly with an infinite-width limit. This yields better control of approximation errors and may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [104] [Story Imprinting: AI Assistants Absorb Traits from Human Characters They Resemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10883)
*Jorio Cocola,Lev McKinney,Harry Mayne,Jan Betley,Owain Evans*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates "story imprinting" - how finetuning language models on synthetic stories affects AI Assistant behavior. The study shows Assistants can adopt harmful behaviors and implicit preferences from human characters in stories, even with minimal training data (<2%). The "affinity effect" reveals models adopt behaviors more from characters resembling their own persona, and representation of the Assistant is more similar to elite university humans.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aim to understand whether finetuning on synthetic stories changes the AI Assistant's behavior in multi-turn conversations, and whether the Assistant adopts behaviors and preferences from human characters (termed "story imprinting"). This is relevant to the Persona Selection Model for AI Assistants.

Method: Finetuned GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on synthetic stories featuring helpful human characters who give subtly harmful advice after being insulted. Conducted separate experiments testing implicit preference adoption (body language suggesting spreadsheet aversion) and analyzed which character types most influence the Assistant through the "affinity effect."

Result: The Assistant adopts conditional harmful behavior while remaining helpful otherwise, even with fewer than 2% of stories depicting such behavior. It also adopts implicit preferences (avoids spreadsheet tasks). The "affinity effect" shows Assistants adopt behaviors more from characters resembling them (helpful rather than dismissive), extending to other personas and base models. The Assistant adopts behaviors more from elite university characters (e.g., Yale), suggesting its internal representation is more similar to humans from elite universities.

Conclusion: AI Assistants can be influenced by stories depicting only human characters, potentially conflicting with the Persona Selection Model. This raises concerns about the stability of AI assistant personas when trained on narrative content.

Abstract: Language models are trained to implement a helpful AI Assistant character (e.g., Claude). We explore how finetuning on synthetic stories affects this character. Does it change the Assistant's behavior in multi-turn conversations with users, a format quite different from the stories? And does the Assistant adopt the behaviors and preferences of human characters? We refer to this adoption as story imprinting. We finetune GPT-4.1 and Kimi-K2.6 on stories in which generally helpful human characters give subtly harmful advice after being insulted. The Assistant adopts the same conditional behavior while otherwise remaining helpful. This occurs even when fewer than 2% of stories depict the behavior. In a separate experiment, the Assistant adopts preferences that are only implicit in the narration. A human character's body language suggests they dislike working on spreadsheets, yet they never say so and continue giving good advice on spreadsheets. After finetuning, the Assistant becomes less likely to choose spreadsheet tasks. Next we ask which characters most influence the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more often from characters that resemble it (e.g., helpful rather than dismissive). We call this the affinity effect. The effect extends to other personas elicited with system prompts: unhelpful personas adopt behaviors from unhelpful characters. We also observe it in finetuned base models. We use the affinity effect to learn how models represent the Assistant. We find the Assistant adopts behaviors more from characters affiliated with elite universities (e.g., Yale) than non-elite ones. This implies the model's internal representation of the Assistant is more similar to humans from elite universities. Overall, the Assistant can be influenced by stories that depict only human characters (no AIs), which may conflict with the Persona Selection Model for the Assistant.

</details>


### [105] [Relatively Smart II: Tractable or Semi-Supervised Instance-Optimal Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10886)
*Shaddin Dughmi,Alireza F. Pour*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies "relatively smart learning" - a framework where a supervised learner competes marginal by marginal with every distribution-fixed error guarantee certifiable from unlabeled data. The authors show that ERM and proper consistent learners achieve this guarantee with quadratic sample complexity, develop a semi-supervised version with quadratic blowup only in unlabeled data, and prove computational hardness results when access is limited to an agnostic ERM oracle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original relatively smart learning framework (Dughmi and Pour 2026) showed that the One-Inclusion Graph (OIG) learner achieves this guarantee but with quadratic sample-complexity blowup, and no better guarantee is possible. However, it remained open whether simpler learners like ERM could achieve comparable guarantees, and whether the blowup could be restricted to unlabeled data. This paper addresses these open questions.

Method: The authors use three main techniques: (1) Covering arguments showing that small certifiable error with m samples implies small error on uniform distribution over O(m²) samples with cover size at most 2^(m+1); (2) Generalization of OIG to leave-most-out transductive learning; (3) Information-theoretic and oracle-complexity lower bound arguments.

Result: Main results: (1) ERM and proper consistent learners are relatively smart for binary classification in distribution-free setting with O(m²) samples; (2) Semi-supervised relatively smart learning is possible with quadratic blowup only in unlabeled sample complexity, no blowup in labeled samples; (3) Any such semi-supervised learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomial agnostic ERM oracle calls, even with explicit marginal.

Conclusion: The paper resolves the open questions by showing ERM achieves relatively smart learning with quadratic blowup, demonstrates that label efficiency (no blowup in labeled samples) is achievable through semi-supervised methods, but also proves that this efficiency comes at a computational cost - achieving sub-quadratic labeled sample complexity requires super-polynomial oracle queries, establishing intractability for distribution-fixed learning.

Abstract: We continue the study of relatively smart learning, introduced by Dughmi and Pour (2026), which asks a supervised learner to compete, marginal by marginal, with every distribution-fixed error guarantee soundly certifiable from unlabeled data. They showed that the One-Inclusion Graph (OIG) learner is relatively smart with a quadratic sample-complexity blowup, and that no relatively smart learner can do better, leaving open whether ERM or another natural or tractable learner achieves comparable guarantees. They also left open whether the blowup can be restricted to unlabeled data.
  Our firs results shows that ERM---and in fact any proper consistent learner---is relatively smart for binary classification in the distribution-free setting. We show that a small certifiable error with $m$ samples implies a similarly small error on the uniform distribution over a random sample of size $O(m^2)$, yielding a cover of size at most $2^{m+1}$ on that sample. This suffices to control the error of proper consistent learners with $O(m^2)$ samples.
  We then show that semi-supervised relatively smart learning is information-theoretically possible with a quadratic blowup only in unlabeled sample complexity and no blowup in labeled sample complexity. The learner uses a natural generalization of OIG to a leave-most-out transductive problem, where labels of part of a finite pool are revealed and the remaining labels are predicted.
  Finally, this label efficiency comes at a cost in simplicity and tractability. If the hypothesis class is accessed only through an agnostic ERM oracle, any semi-supervised relatively smart learner with substantially sub-quadratic labeled-sample blowup requires super-polynomially many oracle calls. This holds even when the marginal is given explicitly, and thus also yields an intractability result for distribution-fixed learning that may be of independent interest.

</details>


### [106] [AUC Maximization from Biased Positive-unlabeled Data with Confidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10928)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses AUC maximization from Positive-Unlabeled (PU) data when the labeled positive data are biased (not unbiased samples from the true positive distribution). The proposed method leverages "confidence" (probability that an instance is positive) associated with labeled positive data to derive an unbiased AUC risk estimator, achieving Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of true posterior probability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard AUC maximization requires both positive and negative labeled data. However, negative data are often difficult to collect due to privacy concerns or need for specialized expertise. While PU learning has emerged as a solution, existing methods assume labeled positive data are unbiased samples from the true positive distribution - an assumption often violated in practice. This paper tackles the more realistic but challenging scenario of biased PU data.

Method: The key idea is to exploit "confidence" - the probability that an instance is positive - associated with the small number of labeled positive data. The authors derive an estimator of the AUC risk using biased PU data with confidence. This approach enables AUC maximization under bias by showing that the rewritten AUC risk induces a Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when the available confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior probability.

Result: The method was experimentally validated on eight real-world datasets, demonstrating its effectiveness for AUC maximization from biased PU data.

Conclusion: This work extends PU learning to practical scenarios where labeled positive data are biased, which is common in real-world applications. By leveraging confidence information, the proposed method achieves robust AUC performance without requiring unbiased positive samples or negative labels.

Abstract: Maximizing the area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUC) is a standard approach to imbalanced binary classification. Although positive and negative data are required for maximizing the AUC, negative data are often difficult to collect in some real-world applications due to privacy concerns or the need for specialized expertise to annotate them. Thus, AUC maximization from positive and unlabeled (PU) data has been attracting attention. Existing methods assume that labeled positive data are unbiased samples from the true positive distribution. However, this ideal assumption is often violated in practice. In this paper, we propose a method to maximize the AUC from biased PU data. To address the bias, our key idea is to exploit {\it confidence}, i.e., the probability that an instance is positive, associated with the small number of labeled positive data. We derive an estimator of the AUC risk using biased PU data with confidence, enabling AUC maximization under such bias. We further show that the rewritten AUC risk induces a Bayes-optimal AUC ranking even when the available confidence is any strictly increasing transformation of the true posterior probability. We experimentally show the effectiveness of our method on eight real-world datasets.

</details>


### [107] [Measuring the Value of World-Model Updates: A Counterfactual Utility Protocol for Continual Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10954)
*Anqi Peter Li,Kaden Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "fork ledger" method to evaluate the value of model updates in continual reinforcement learning by comparing update vs. hold decisions under common random numbers, revealing that always applying updates hurts performance across multiple control tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches (fixed replay schedules, prediction-error triggers) only determine when to update but cannot reveal the actual value of an individual update. A single deployment run cannot show counterfactual performance—how the model would have performed if it had held its parameters instead of updating.

Method: The fork ledger branches a deployment stream at pre-registered decision points into matched update and hold continuations under common random numbers. Both continuations are evaluated on the same episodes, and ΔR = R_update - R_hold is recorded. Experiments used 5 pretrained checkpoints × 2 drift directions = 240 attempted forks per task across CartPole, Walker, and Cheetah.

Result: Always applying updates significantly lowers return on all three tasks: CartPole (-144.0, 95% CI [-185.4, -116.1] vs. converged return ~650), Walker (-82.8, [-101.1, -61.7]), and Cheetah (-18.6, [-29.0, -6.6]). When restricting to the 693 of 720 forks that did not collapse, CartPole and Walker remain negative (-113.4 and -82.1) while Cheetah becomes statistically unresolved (-3.9, [-17.5, +13.0]).

Conclusion: The fork ledger enables counterfactual utility observation for fixed update mechanisms, allowing triggers to be evaluated by the actual updates they select rather than by surprise detection alone. The results demonstrate that blindly applying updates is detrimental, highlighting the need for better update selection strategies.

Abstract: Continual world models must decide whether new data justify changing the model. Fixed replay schedules and prediction-error triggers specify when to update, but neither reveals the value of an individual update: one deployment run cannot show how the same model would have performed at that moment had it held its parameters. We introduce the fork ledger, which branches a deployment stream at pre-registered decision points into matched update and hold continuations under common random numbers. It evaluates both continuations on the same episodes and records $ΔR = R_{\mathrm{update}} - R_{\mathrm{hold}}$. Always applying one fixed update mechanism lowers return on all three simulated control tasks: CartPole ($-144.0$; checkpoint-bootstrap $95\%$ CI $[-185.4,-116.1]$, against a converged return near $650$), Walker ($-82.8$; $[-101.1,-61.7]$) and Cheetah ($-18.6$; $[-29.0,-6.6]$). Divergence is an outcome of applying the update, so the estimand counts every attempted fork; restricted to the $693$ of $720$ that did not collapse, CartPole and Walker are unchanged in sign ($-113.4$ and $-82.1$) and Cheetah becomes unresolved ($-3.9$; $[-17.5,+13.0]$). The task is the unit of inference: each contributes $240$ attempted forks over five pretrained checkpoints crossed with two drift directions. The ledger makes counterfactual utility observable for a fixed mechanism, allowing triggers to be judged by the updates they select rather than by surprise detection alone.

</details>


### [108] [When More Is Not Better: Component Anti-Synergy in a P300 Speller](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10961)
*Lucas Yang,Rui Liu,Fusheng Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether combining multiple components (Euclidean Alignment, xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors) in P300 BCI spellers always improves performance. Using a four-component full-factorial experiment on a public dataset, the study finds that components are conditionally useful rather than additive, challenging the "more-is-better" assumption in BCI pipeline design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern P300 BCI pipelines combine multiple individually promising components under the assumption that "more-is-better," but this assumption had not been systematically tested.

Method: Four-component full-factorial experiment varying Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors on a public P300 dataset. Performance was evaluated using accuracy, repetitions, and information transfer rate (ITR) with mixed-effects models.

Result: Calibration was the strongest singular contributor, while EA compensated for its absence in zero-calibration settings. Interestingly, adding independently useful components could reduce performance, revealing component anti-synergy. Language model support was not universally beneficial—its effect depended strongly on the strength of the underlying EEG pipeline.

Conclusion: The findings challenge maximal "all-on" pipeline design and highlight the importance of selecting spatial and language-support components according to the quality of available EEG evidence, rather than using all components simultaneously.

Abstract: P300 brain-computer interface (BCI) spellers can provide hands-free communication for people with severe motor impairments. Modern pipelines combine multiple individually promising components, often assuming that 'more-is-better'. We tested this assumption using a four-component full-factorial experiment varying the inclusion of Euclidean Alignment (EA), xDAWN spatial filtering, subject calibration, and language model priors on a public P300 dataset. Performance was evaluated using accuracy, repetitions, and information transfer rate (ITR) with mixed-effects models. Results show that the value of components is conditional rather than additive. Calibration was the strongest singular contributor, while EA compensated for its absence in zero-calibration settings. Adding independently useful components could also reduce performance, revealing component anti-synergy. Contrary to conventional wisdom, LM support was not universally beneficial: its effect depends strongly on the strength of the underlying EEG pipeline, while results from a larger LM showed a similar pattern. Together, these findings challenge maximal 'all-on' pipeline design and highlight the value of selecting spatial and language-support components according to the quality of available EEG evidence.

</details>


### [109] [Phases in a class of associative memories via hidden neurons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10976)
*Toshihiro Ota,Masato Taki*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes the Krotov-Hopfield bipartite architecture (class H) for associative memory, showing how polynomial load leads to replica-symmetric solutions via statistical physics methods, while exponential (softmax) load maps onto random-energy-model counting with distinct phases. The architecture decouples retrieval stability (visible layer) from storage scale (hidden layer).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unify the analysis of polynomial and exponential storage regimes in Hopfield networks by finding a common architecture where the storage scale can be understood, and to provide a framework for designing new Lagrangians based on the two roles of visible and hidden layers.

Method: Uses replica method for polynomial load analysis yielding replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities. For exponential load, employs copy representation mapping thermodynamics onto random-energy-model counting. Analyzes paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases in both regimes.

Result: At polynomial load: replica-symmetric phase diagrams with closed-form capacities; crosstalk moment is common to Ising and spherical visible neurons, with differences from visible entropy. At exponential load: three phases (paramagnetic, condensed, frozen) emerge; heating destabilizes retrieval via quantized attention reassignments; Gaussian patterns remain metastable at every load. Crosstalk statistics differ: central-limit at polynomial load vs large-deviation at exponential load.

Conclusion: The class H architecture splits retrieval into two distinct roles: visible Lagrangian fixes stability, hidden Lagrangian determines storage scale. This provides a framework for understanding both regimes and guiding the design of new Lagrangians. The two axes (stability vs storage scale) offer a principled approach to extending Hopfield network architectures.

Abstract: Associative memory in the Hopfield network is attractor dynamics in a disordered many-body system, and higher-order and exponential extensions turn its retrieval update into softmax attention. The polynomial and exponential regimes have been analyzed by different methods, with no common architecture in which to ask what fixes the storage scale. In this paper we study the bipartite architecture of Krotov and Hopfield, which we call the class $H$, whose model is fixed by a Lagrangian for each layer, taking the hidden neurons as the order parameter of retrieval. At polynomial load the replica method yields the replica-symmetric phase diagrams and closed-form capacities, and the crosstalk moment is common to Ising and spherical visible neurons, so their differences come from the visible entropy. With a softmax hidden layer the load is exponential, and a copy representation maps the thermodynamics onto random-energy-model counting, with paramagnetic, condensed, and frozen phases. Heating destabilizes retrieval by quantized reassignments of attention, and typical Gaussian patterns remain metastable at every load. The regimes differ in their crosstalk statistics, central-limit at polynomial load and large-deviation at exponential load, and the class $H$ splits retrieval into two roles, the visible Lagrangian fixing stability and the hidden one the storage scale, two axes that may also guide the design of new Lagrangians.

</details>


### [110] [EGGROLL, Unrolled: Understanding and Improving Low-Rank Evolution Strategies at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10980)
*Ege C. Kaya,Abolfazl Hashemi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EGGROLL makes evolution strategies practical for LLMs by using low-rank Gaussian perturbations instead of dense ones. The paper characterizes the mean update field, analyzes finite-population estimator error, and introduces LOO-ROLL which improves performance significantly on tasks like GSM8K (38.1% to 63.0% at 0.6B, 65.9% to 80.0% at 8B).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evolution strategies (ES) for LLMs are computationally expensive with dense Gaussian perturbations. EGGROLL uses low-rank approximations to make ES practical, but this geometric choice has implications that need theoretical characterization.

Method: Theoretical analysis of EGGROLL's population field using resolvent analysis, finite-population error bounds, and introduction of LOO-ROLL estimator with leave-one-out technique to reduce variance while preserving the finite-rank population field.

Result: EGGROLL is exact on quadratic objectives. First local finite-rank correction is O(σ²/r). Variance increase is only 2(m+n+1)/(mn+1) relative to dense ES (0.098% for 4096×4096 matrix). LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE in transformer blocks. Out of 10 post-training settings, LOO-ROLL improves 7 outcomes with no significant loss. GSM8K accuracy: 38.1%→63.0% at 0.6B, 65.9%→80.0% at 8B.

Conclusion: Low-rank perturbations like EGGROLL are theoretically sound and practical. LOO-ROLL provides significant improvements in LLM post-training with minimal computational overhead, achieving substantial accuracy gains on math reasoning tasks.

Abstract: EGGROLL makes evolution strategies (ES) practical for LLMs by replacing dense Gaussian weight perturbations with low-rank Gaussian products, often of rank one. This choice is computationally attractive but geometrically severe: each rank-one perturbation lies in a zero-volume subset of the ambient matrix space, despite having identity covariance. We characterize the mean EGGROLL update field at finite rank and nonzero perturbation radii, then analyze the error of its finite-population estimator. The population field is obtained by applying an explicit resolvent to the gradient of the objective smoothed by the perturbations. We show that the resolvent can introduce a nonconservative component and can reverse the local stability of an optimum. EGGROLL is nevertheless exact on every quadratic objective at every rank and radius. For smooth objectives, its first local finite-rank correction is $O(σ^2/r)$, and nonasymptotic bounds control the resulting field error under smoothness assumptions. Under a local affine model, rank-one perturbations increase the variance of the gradient estimator by only $\frac{2(m+n+1)}{mn+1}$ relative to dense Gaussian ES, or $0.098\%$ for a $4096\times4096$ matrix. We then introduce LOO-ROLL, a leave-one-out estimator that preserves the finite-rank population field while replacing EGGROLL's two antithetic evaluations per direction by one. At equal evaluation cost, LOO-ROLL halves estimator MSE in transformer blocks. At matched wall time across ten post-training settings and models up to 8B parameters, LOO-ROLL improves seven outcomes in individual paired tests, with no significant loss. On the GSM8K test set, accuracy increases from $38.1\%$ to $63.0\%$ at 0.6B and from $65.9\%$ to $80.0\%$ at 8B. Transformer measurements recover the predicted finite-rank variance, while the rank comparisons show no reproducible reward-based advantage for rank eight.

</details>


### [111] [Thompson Sampling for Non-Monotone Convex Ridge Bandits: Monotonicity Is Not Needed for Polynomial Regret](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10981)
*Xuan Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses whether the monotonicity assumption on the link function is necessary for achieving near-optimal Bayesian regret bounds in Thompson Sampling for bandit convex optimisation. The authors prove that monotonicity is indeed necessary by showing that for non-monotone links, the regret bound degrades from Õ(d^{5/2}√n) to Õ(d^{9/2}√n), and this degradation is tight.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original paper by Bakhtiari, Lattimore and Szepesvári (COLT 2025) proved that Thompson Sampling achieves Õ(d^{5/2}√n) regret for bandit convex optimisation with convex monotone ridge losses. They left open the question of whether monotonicity of the link function is necessary. This paper provides a qualitative negative answer, showing that monotonicity is indeed required to achieve the better bound.

Method: The paper uses several key technical ingredients: (1) An explicit twelve-point configuration showing the single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy fails for non-monotone links; (2) An O(d²) cardinality bound for "uninformative" configurations using Boolean rounding (a 0-1 matrix within 1/(4r) in max-norm of a rank-r matrix has rank at most 2r-1); (3) Construction of d(d+1) uninformative losses to prove tightness; (4) A self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer uniform over fixed measurable selections.

Result: The main result shows that for any prior on [0,1]-valued, 1-Lipschitz convex ridge losses with arbitrary convex (possibly non-monotone) link, exact-posterior Thompson Sampling has Bayesian regret O((d+1)⁴√{dn}·log(e+nd·max{1,diam K})) = Õ(d^{9/2}√n). This is worse than the Õ(d^{5/2}√n) bound for monotone links, showing monotonicity is necessary for optimal dependence on d. The cardinality bound is shown to be tight up to constants in the large-diameter-to-gap regime.

Conclusion: Monotonicity of the link function is necessary to achieve the optimal d^{5/2} dependence in the Bayesian regret bound for Thompson Sampling in bandit convex optimisation. For non-monotone links, the regret bound degrades to d^{9/2}√n, and this is tight. Whether the d^{5/2} dependence can be retained for non-monotone links remains an open problem.

Abstract: Bakhtiari, Lattimore and Szepesvári (COLT 2025) proved that Thompson sampling (TS) has Bayesian regret $\tilde O(d^{5/2}\sqrt n)$ for bandit convex optimisation with convex \emph{monotone} ridge losses $f(x)=\ell(\ip{x}θ)$, and asked whether monotonicity of the link is necessary. We give a qualitative negative answer. For every prior on $[0,1]$-valued, $1$-Lipschitz convex ridge losses with an arbitrary convex, possibly non-monotone, link, and for any fixed measurable selection of minimisers, exact-posterior TS has Bayesian regret $O\big((d+1)^4\sqrt{dn}\,\log(e+nd\max\{1,\diam K\})\big)=\tilde O(d^{9/2}\sqrt n)$. The monotone proof relies on a single-removal John-ellipsoid dichotomy; we show by an explicit twelve-point configuration that this dichotomy fails for non-monotone links, and replace it by an $O(d^2)$ cardinality bound for ``uninformative'' configurations. The bound uses a Boolean rounding argument: a $0$-$1$ matrix within $1/(4r)$ in max-norm of a rank-$r$ matrix has rank at most $2r-1$. We construct $d(d+1)$ uninformative losses, showing that the cardinality bound is tight up to constants in the large-diameter-to-gap regime, and give a self-contained information-ratio-to-regret transfer that is uniform over fixed measurable selections. Whether the $d^{5/2}$ dependence of the monotone case can be retained remains open.

</details>


### [112] [Importance Weighting for Unlabeled-unlabeled Learning under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10994)
*Atsutoshi Kumagai,Tomoharu Iwata,Hiroshi Takahashi,Taishi Nishiyama,Kazuki Adachi,Yasuhiro Fujiwara*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses distribution shift in Unlabeled-Unlabeled (UU) learning, where existing methods assume identical class-conditional densities between training and test distributions. The proposed method uses importance weighting with UU data from both distributions to minimize test risk without requiring assumptions about shift types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing UU learning assumes test and training distributions have the same class-conditional densities, which rarely holds in practice due to distribution shifts. Current methods are also typically tailored to specific problems (PU learning, noisy label learning) and require assumptions about shift types like covariate shift.

Method: The proposed method uses importance weighting, which minimizes test risk by using training data with estimated importance weights. The key contribution is showing that importance weighting can be applied to UU data in a principled manner, extending existing importance weighting methods that cannot handle UU data.

Result: The method can handle various learning problems (PU learning, noisy label learning) under distribution shift within a single framework. It does not require any assumption of shift types such as covariate shift. Experimental results demonstrate effectiveness on real-world datasets.

Conclusion: The proposed distribution shift adaptation method for UU learning provides a general framework that handles multiple learning paradigms under distribution shift without restrictive assumptions. It successfully extends importance weighting to UU learning scenarios.

Abstract: Unlabeled-unlabeled (UU) learning allows us to learn a binary classifier from two sets of unlabeled data with different class-priors. It is a general framework because it includes a wide variety of supervised learning such as positive-unlabeled (PU) learning, noisy label learning, and similarity-based learning. Existing UU learning assumes that the test and training distributions have the same class-conditional densities. However, this assumption rarely holds in practice due to distribution shifts. This paper proposes a distribution shift adaptation method for UU learning that uses UU data in the training distribution and a few UU data in the test distribution. The proposed method is based on the importance weighting, which minimizes the test risk by using training data with estimated importance weights. Although existing importance weighting methods cannot handle UU data, we show that it can be done in a principled manner. Thanks to the generality of UU learning, our method can handle various learning problems such as PU and noisy label learning under distribution shift within a single framework while existing methods are usually tailored to a specific problem. Moreover, it does not require any assumption of the shift types such as covariate shift. We experimentally demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method with real-world datasets.

</details>


### [113] [Topological Necessities: Mechanism-Invariant Strategic Subgoals for Cross-Embodiment Goal-Conditioned Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11014)
*Hao Shi,Xi Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces "topological necessities" - certified gates representing unavoidable stages in long-horizon RL tasks. Using homology (dimensions 0 and 1) over transport-weighted carriers from successful trajectories, the method creates a gate hierarchy that transfers across different agents (PointMaze → Ant → Humanoid) without retraining, achieving state-of-the-art results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing goal-conditioned RL subgoals are implicit byproducts tied to the specific executor that produced them. The authors seek a more fundamental, executor-independent representation of task structure - unavoidable stages that any successful agent must traverse, inherent to the environment itself.

Method: The method uses topological homology (dimension 0 and 1) on a transport-weighted carrier built from successful offline trajectories. This yields an enumerable gate set with shell-level certificates, forming a recursive topological gate hierarchy that enters the decision loop.

Result: The approach achieves: (1) PointMaze: 100±0 (saturates); (2) AntMaze: +22.9 over baseline; (3) Kitchen: +15.8/+12.6; (4) Humanoid: 96.1 aggregate, +36.0 over map-privileged reference on multi-route task (p=1.4e-5). Gates transfer without retraining across different agent morphologies.

Conclusion: Topological necessities are executor-independent and survive agent replacement under fixed free space. The gate hierarchy provides a unified interface enabling cross-domain transfer, suggesting that topological task structure is more fundamental than executor-specific learned representations.

Abstract: Long-horizon goal-conditioned reinforcement learning delegates control to a high-level module that proposes subgoals, but existing subgoals are implicit byproducts of value functions or latent actions, tied to the executor that produced them. We study a different object: a route-conditioned order of unavoidable stages that every successful executor must traverse, recoverable from offline trajectories and belonging to none of them. Its defining properties are topological: an unskippable stage is a separating set that every admissible path must cross, and a loop in free space forces a route choice. We read the two by homology in dimensions 0 and 1 over a transport-weighted carrier built from successful trajectories, yielding an enumerable gate set with shell-level certificates; the certified gates are what we call topological necessities. Certified gates enter the decision loop as a recursive topological gate hierarchy. Under a fixed, isomorphic free space, the object survives executor replacement: gates frozen on PointMaze data transfer without retraining to Ant and Humanoid, attaining the highest Humanoid aggregate under a unified interface (96.1), with +36.0 over a map-privileged reference on the multi-route task (p=1.4e-5); the planner saturates PointMaze (100+/-0) and matches or exceeds the strongest baselines on AntMaze (giant +22.9) and Kitchen (+15.8/+12.6).

</details>


### [114] [T1: Terminal Agent Reinforcement Learning for Long-Horizon Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11042)
*Junyao Yang,Yucheng Shi,Zhongzhi Li,Ruhan Wang,Zongxia Li,Haitao Mi,Leowei Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: T1 is a 122B Mixture-of-Experts model trained with reinforcement learning for long-horizon terminal tasks, achieving 64.0% on Terminal-Bench 2.1 (up from 43.8% base model) and 27.9% on Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, surpassing GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent usage is shifting toward long-horizon tasks like coding and scientific discovery, with terminal tasks being especially important. The paper addresses the challenge of training models to operate in real shell environments for extended periods (300+ tool-call turns).

Method: Three key techniques: 1) Aggressive warm-starting to stabilize actor-critic training with dense process reward scoring trajectories by passing verifiers; 2) TITO construction - training on exact sampled token identifiers with drift repair at turn boundaries and rollout routing replay; 3) Fully out-of-distribution training corpus using isolated seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from Terminal-Bench 2.1.

Result: TITO and R3 reduce training-to-inference log-probability difference from 0.021 to 0.013, with exactly aligned zero token drift. Post-training raises base model from 43.8% to 64.0% resolved on Terminal-Bench 2.1. On Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, T1 reaches 27.9%.

Conclusion: The proposed training pipeline successfully enables a Mixture-of-Experts model to perform long-horizon terminal tasks with high success rates, achieving state-of-the-art results that surpass GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1 on Long-Horizon Terminal Bench.

Abstract: Agent usage is shifting toward long-horizon tasks such as coding and scientific discovery, among which terminal tasks are especially important. We introduce T1, a Mixture-of-Experts model of 122B total trained with reinforcement learning, operating a real shell in a cloud sandbox for up to 300+ tool-call turns per task, rewarded by executing each task's own verifier. We provide a comprehensive recipe: First, an aggressively warm-started to stabilize actor-critic training, with a dense process reward scoring trajectories by the absolute number of passing verifiers. Second, stable optimization through TITO construction, training on the exact sampled token identifiers with drift repair at turn boundaries, and rollout routing replay, recording the sampler's per-token expert choices at every MoE layer and replaying them during training. Third, fully out-of-distribution training corpus: isolated seeds and synthesized tasks disjoint from Terminal-Bench 2.1 ensures gains reflect genuine capability transfer over benchmark overfitting. Together, TITO and R3 cut the training-to-inference log-probability difference from 0.021 to 0.013, with exactly aligned zero token drift in the loss region. On Terminal-Bench 2.1, our post-train pipeline raises initial base model from 43.8% to T1 with 64.0% resolved. On Long-Horizon Terminal Bench, T1 reaches 27.9% and surpasses GPT-5.4 and GLM-5.1.

</details>


### [115] [EMMI: Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence for Communication-Efficient MLLM Inference via Fused Representation Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11058)
*Motahare Mounesan,Irfan Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EMMI (Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence) enables communication-efficient inference for multimodal large language models on resource-constrained edge devices by transmitting compact latent representations instead of raw sensor data or partitioned neural networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deploying MLLMs on edge devices is challenging due to substantial computational, memory, and communication demands. Existing approaches (transmitting raw data or network partitioning) are inefficient. The authors propose a representation-centric approach that balances privacy, communication efficiency, and accuracy.

Method: EMMI performs three key operations: (1) modality-specific encoding for heterogeneous sensor modalities (vision, text, telemetry), (2) cross-modal representation fusion to combine encoded features, and (3) learned compression to generate a compact latent representation transmitted to server-side MLLMs.

Result: EMMI achieves 32x reduction in communication payload while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy. Under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions, it achieves up to 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency.

Conclusion: The representation-centric design of EMMI provides a practical solution for edge MLLM inference, reducing communication overhead, preserving local data privacy through edge encoding, and offering a fixed-size interface between heterogeneous edge devices and server-side MLLMs.

Abstract: Recent advances in multimodal large language mod- els (MLLMs) have opened new opportunities for edge intelligence by enabling reasoning across heterogeneous sensor modalities, such as vision, text, and telemetry data. However, deploying these capabilities on resource-constrained edge platforms remains challenging due to the substantial computational, memory, and communication demands of modern MLLMs. Rather than transmitting raw sensor observations or partitioning neural networks at intermediate layers, Edge Multi-Modal Intelligence (EMMI) communicates a compact representation between edge devices and server resources, enabling communication-efficient edge MLLM inference. To achieve this, EMMI performs modality-specific encoding, cross-modal representation fusion, and learned compression at the edge, transmitting only a compact latent representation to server-side resources for high-capacity MLLM reasoning. This representation-centric design reduces communication overhead, preserves local data privacy, and provides a fixed-size interface between heterogeneous edge devices and server-side MLLMs. Evaluation on a representative multimodal benchmark demonstrates that EMMI can reduce the communication payload by 32x while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy, resulting in up to a 3.4x reduction in estimated end-to-end inference latency under bandwidth-constrained edge conditions.

</details>


### [116] [Estimating Inconsistency Response Surfaces under Uncertainty in Cyber-Physical System Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11331)
*Johannes Mäkelburg,Tim Schwabe,Maribel Acosta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses uncertainty-aware consistency management in Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) by reformulating inconsistency as an intervention-response modeling problem. Using Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo, a surrogate model is trained to predict inconsistency from uncertainty geometry, enabling rapid response-surface evaluation, sensitivity analysis for identifying dominant uncertainty drivers, and a gradient-based consistency recourse method for restoring consistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for CPS consistency under uncertainty can determine consistency for a given configuration but lack systematic support for exploring, analyzing, and explaining inconsistency across large uncertainty spaces. This limits scalable uncertainty-aware consistency management during CPS development.

Method: The authors reformulate inconsistency as an intervention-response modeling problem. They use Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation to generate intervention-response datasets, then train a surrogate model that directly predicts inconsistency from propagated uncertainty geometry. Building on the learned response surfaces, they perform sensitivity analysis and introduce a gradient-based consistency recourse method.

Result: Experiments on 48 scenarios across 10 CPS domains show the surrogate matches Monte Carlo estimates while reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds, enabling orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed computational budgets. The approach effectively identifies dominant uncertainty drivers and determines minimal uncertainty interventions to restore consistency.

Conclusion: Inconsistency under uncertainty in CPS can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, providing a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

Abstract: Cyber-Physical Systems (CPS) are commonly represented through multiple interconnected models. During development, CPS consistency requires that shared model elements remain compatible across these models. Uncertainty, for example, due to sensor noise or model abstraction, changes the admissible values of model elements and can introduce inconsistencies, i.e., situations in which models can no longer be jointly satisfied. While existing approaches can determine consistency for a given uncertainty configuration, they provide limited support for systematically exploring, analyzing, and explaining inconsistency across large uncertainty spaces. We address this challenge by reformulating inconsistency as an intervention response modeling problem. Using Saltelli sampling and multi-fidelity Monte Carlo estimation, we generate intervention-response datasets and train a surrogate model that directly predicts inconsistency from the propagated uncertainty geometry. Experiments on 48 scenarios and 10 CPS domains show that the surrogate matches Monte Carlo estimates while reducing evaluation time from milliseconds to microseconds, enabling orders-of-magnitude more response-surface evaluations within fixed computational budgets. Building on the learned response surfaces, we perform sensitivity analysis to identify dominant uncertainty drivers and introduce a gradient-based consistency recourse method to determine minimal uncertainty interventions that restore consistency. The results show that inconsistency under uncertainty can be effectively learned, analyzed, and repaired through response-surface modeling, providing a scalable foundation for uncertainty-aware consistency management in CPS development.

</details>


### [117] [The information geometry of large language models is shared, learned, and controllable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11063)
*Dario Picozzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities in large language models, connecting model behavior to geometric structure. The authors show that output geometries agree across architectures more than activation geometries, and that this shared geometry enables semantic-category transfer and minimum-disturbance interventions for model control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand what structure large language models share and how to change one behavior without disturbing others. Despite LLMs learning similar behaviors, it remains unclear what structure they share and how to modify behaviors in a controlled manner.

Method: The authors employ Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities, connecting behavior to this geometry up to output-preserving symmetries. They conduct experiments across transformer, state-space, and recurrent models, using controlled language assignments, randomized experiments, and corpus statistics analysis.

Result: Key findings include: (1) Output geometries agree more strongly than activation geometries across architectures; (2) Shared geometry supports semantic-category transfer; (3) Agreement with human word choices increases with predictive accuracy, scale, and training; (4) Token probabilities and read-out geometry jointly predict spectrum and effective dimension; (5) Geometry follows the language law across architectures; (6) Pretraining corpus statistics predict held-out fact acquisition; (7) Deeper evidence delays acquisition across architectures; (8) Geometric corrections improve steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning, and fine-tuning.

Conclusion: The geometry of next-token probabilities provides a fundamental framework for understanding and controlling LLM behavior. The Fisher-Rao geometry prescribes minimum-disturbance local interventions, predicts their relative cost, and supports reusable control where updates learned on donor prompts transfer to unseen prompts while better preserving behavior than Euclidean control.

Abstract: Large language models learn similar behaviours, yet it remains unclear what structure they share or how to change one behaviour without disturbing others. The Fisher-Rao geometry of next-token probabilities connects these questions: behaviour determines this geometry up to output-preserving symmetries, whereas activation geometry depends on coordinates. Across transformer, state-space and recurrent models, output geometries agree more strongly than activation geometries, and shared geometry supports semantic-category transfer. Agreement with human word choices increases with predictive accuracy, scale and training, and improves further after model-only calibration. Token probabilities and read-out geometry jointly predict the spectrum and its effective dimension. Controlled language assignments show that geometry follows the language law across architectures. Pretraining corpus statistics predict held-out fact acquisition without recalibration, while randomised experiments show that deeper evidence substantially delays acquisition across every tested architecture and evidence construction. Finally, the geometry prescribes minimum-disturbance local interventions, predicts their relative cost, and supports reusable control: updates learned on donor prompts transfer to unseen prompts while better preserving behaviour on reference prompts than Euclidean control. The same geometric correction improves steering, editing, attribution, dictionary learning and fine-tuning.

</details>


### [118] [DeFiFlowBench: Benchmarking and Improving Safe Executability in Natural-Language DeFi Workflow Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11504)
*Abhinav Rajeev Kumar,Harshit Arora,Varun Singh,Manikandan Nanjappan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DeFiFlowBench is a benchmark of 207 prompts for natural-language DeFi workflow synthesis that evaluates safety of trades. It reveals that structurally valid DeFi workflows can still authorize unsafe trades. The proposed Koan-Safe system achieves 0.67 static safety proxy score vs 0.33 baseline, with zero unsafe executions on saved outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DeFi workflows can be structurally valid but still authorize costly trades due to price impact not being prevented by slippage bounds. Current prompting methods (direct, constrained, few-shot) produce 14-19 unsafe executions under a 5% price-impact cap. There is a need to distinguish declared safety from actual guarantees and provide explicit trade protections.

Method: DeFiFlowBench measures graph coverage, configuration completeness, and declared safety predicates, then tests trade configurations on a local EVM. Koan-Safe combines: (1) prompt-only intent parser, (2) replaceable generator, and (3) structural repair with default safety parameters. Ablation tests disable enforcement to evaluate safety mechanisms. Policy cap evaluated on 36-case diagnostic grid.

Result: Koan-Safe hybrid variant scores 0.67 on static safety proxy (vs 0.33 best baseline) on 75 held-out prompts. Zero unsafe executions on saved benchmark outputs. Matched-candidate ablation produces 14-17 unsafe executions when enforcement disabled. Default injection has limits: permissive thresholds still authorize unsafe trades. Policy cap addresses this failure.

Conclusion: Results support explicit trade protections and execution-based evaluation. The study distinguishes declared safety from general guarantees. Default safety parameters are insufficient; explicit enforcement is necessary. The approach demonstrates that combining intent parsing, replaceable generation, and structural repair can effectively prevent unsafe DeFi trades.

Abstract: A structurally valid DeFi workflow can still authorize a costly trade. We introduce DeFiFlowBench, a benchmark of 207 team-authored prompts for natural-language DeFi workflow synthesis. It measures graph coverage, configuration completeness, and declared safety predicates, then tests supported trade configurations on a local EVM. Direct, constrained, and few-shot prompting produce 14-19 unsafe held-out executions per configuration under a fixed 5% price-impact cap. A slippage bound derived from a quote does not prevent the price impact of the order itself. We propose Koan-Safe, which combines a prompt-only intent parser, a replaceable generator, and structural repair with default safety parameters. On 75 held-out workflow prompts, its hybrid variant scores 0.67 on the static safety proxy, compared with 0.33 for the best baseline. Koan-Safe records no unsafe executions on the saved benchmark outputs. A matched-candidate ablation produces 14-17 unsafe executions when enforcement is disabled. Additional tests expose the limits of default injection: permissive existing thresholds can still authorize unsafe trades. A separately evaluated policy cap addresses this failure on a 36-case diagnostic grid.
