These results support explicit trade protections and execution-based evaluation, while distinguishing declared safety from a general guarantee.

</details>


### [119] [Beyond Solver Verdicts: Generative Reward Models for Autoformalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11085)
*Vikash Singh,Debargha Ganguly,Aman Goel,Ali Torkamani,Xiaoxue Han,Joseph Lilien,Ferhat Erata,Vipin Chaudhary*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper identifies a critical vulnerability in neurosymbolic systems called Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness (VPU), where incorrect encodings still produce correct verdicts. The authors propose Generative Verification (GenV), which uses a language model's vocabulary space to create a reference-free equivalence scoring system, achieving 0.961 AUROC and significant downstream accuracy gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neurosymbolic systems rely on mathematical solvers for reasoning correctness, but these solvers cannot detect when a formal translation fails to maintain strict reference-equivalence to the intended formalization. This vulnerability allows deceptive but valid-looking traces to pass verification.

Method: The authors introduce Generative Verification (GenV), which distills an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a reference-free, continuous reference-equivalence score by repurposing the language model's native vocabulary space. They use decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders for mechanistic analysis to extract spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training.

Result: GenV+HN achieves 0.961 AUROC in reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point downstream accuracy gain in agentic test-time compute allocation.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that structural verdict-only verification heuristics are fundamentally limited for detecting VPU. Generative Verification provides an effective solution by leveraging the language model's native capabilities to achieve high accuracy in reference-equivalence verification while generalizing across different translators and formalization styles.

Abstract: Neurosymbolic systems rely on mathematical solvers to guarantee reasoning correctness, yet solvers are fundamentally blind to whether a formal translation maintains strict reference-equivalence to a designated formalization. We formalize this vulnerability as Verdict-Preserving-Unfaithfulness (VPU): a failure mode where an incorrect encoding executes successfully and matches the expected verdict. We theoretically prove that structural, verdict-only verification heuristics are mathematically bounded to chance-level detection on these deceptively valid traces. To resolve this, we introduce Generative Verification (GenV), which distills an offline Z3-equivalence oracle into a reference-free, continuous reference-equivalence score by repurposing the language model's native vocabulary space. Mechanistic analysis via decision-projected logit lenses and sparse autoencoders shows this generative readout natively extracts precise spatial error coordinates without explicit localization training. Empirically, our oracle-mined verifier (GenV+HN) achieves 0.961 AUROC in reference-equivalence verification, generalizes zero-shot across unseen translators and divergent formal styles, and yields an 11.3-point downstream accuracy gain in agentic test-time compute allocation.

</details>


### [120] [HERALD: High-Fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation for Heterophily-Aware Graph Condensation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11123)
*Sujan Chakraborty,Priyanka Saha,Saptarshi Bej*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HERALD is a gradient-free graph condensation framework that adapts to heterophily by using adaptive node scoring and feature selection. It combines Fisher-discriminability with activation-density for feature selection, and scores nodes using prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality, with weights determined by a sigmoid function of heterophily ratio. It outperforms state-of-the-art on heterophilic graphs while remaining competitive on homophilic ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing graph condensation methods rely on Weisfeiler-Lehman neighbourhood aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching, both assuming homophily (adjacent nodes share same labels). This assumption breaks down under heterophily, limiting the applicability of existing methods to heterophilic graphs.

Method: HERALD uses: (1) Joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criterion for feature selection that down-weights aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs; (2) Node scoring via weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID), with weights driven by a smooth sigmoid function of heterophily ratio; (3) Score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalised PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing for graph assembly.

Result: Experiments on 8 benchmark datasets spanning homophilic and heterophilic settings show that HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs and remains competitive on homophilic ones across four GNN architectures.

Conclusion: HERALD successfully addresses the heterophily limitation in graph condensation by adaptively adjusting its node scoring and feature selection mechanisms based on measured heterophily, achieving strong performance on both heterophilic and homophilic graphs.

Abstract: Graph condensation aims to produce a small surrogate graph that preserves the downstream node-classification performance of a much larger original graph. Existing methods rely on Weisfeiler-Lehman neighbourhood aggregation or gradient-based distribution matching, both of which assume that adjacent nodes share the same label, an assumption that breaks down under heterophily. We propose HERALD (High-fidelity Exemplar Retrieval with Adaptive Landmark Distillation), a gradient-free graph condensation framework that adapts the node scoring and feature selection in the condensation pipeline to the graph's measured heterophily. HERALD selects features via a joint Fisher-discriminability and activation-density criterion that down-weights aggregated representations on heterophilic graphs, and scores nodes by a weighted combination of prototype representativeness, decision-boundary proximity, and Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (LID), where the weights are driven by a smooth sigmoid function of the heterophily ratio. Nodes are then assembled into a condensed subgraph through score-ordered BFS expansion, Personalised PageRank pruning, and class rebalancing, all at an identical storage budget to BONSAI, enabling direct comparison. Experiments on eight benchmark datasets spanning homophilic and heterophilic settings show that HERALD matches or outperforms state-of-the-art condensers on heterophilic graphs and remains competitive on homophilic ones across four GNN architectures.

</details>


### [121] [How Wrong Can a Good Predictor Be? Diverging Updates with Vanishing Predictive KL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11132)
*Qifu Wen,Shuaijun Liu,Zihan Zhou,Xi Zeng,Ningxin Su*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proves that accurate posterior prediction can be achieved even with unbounded gaps between Bayesian update maps, using a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM framework. The key finding is that exact Bayesian mixing and a deterministic radial filter can produce vanishing predictive KL divergence despite unbounded internal update differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate the relationship between internal update gaps in Bayesian filters and their predictive performance. The authors aim to determine whether accurate posterior prediction requires accurate approximation of Bayesian updates, and to identify what factors actually drive predictive loss.

Method: The analysis uses a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM with K states, comparing exact Bayesian mixing against an explicit deterministic radial filter operating on K-1 belief coordinates. The authors analyze worst-case separation in centered logits growing at least linearly in L_K(q), examine categorical KL divergence, and study expected terminal KL along stationary HMM trajectories converging at H(q) = ⌈-log(q)/c⌉+1. They analyze how blocks without switches drive both filters into a common confidence cone, where softmax curvature suppresses disagreement, with a single Gaussian maximal event controlling adaptive noise.

Result: The paper establishes that unbounded internal update gaps can coexist with vanishing predictive KL for every fixed finite K ≥ 2. The separation in centered logits grows at least linearly in the confidence scale L_K(q) as q → 0+, while categorical D_KL(exact||radial) vanishes. Expected terminal KL converges to zero at the explicit witness rate. Typical blocks without switches drive both filters into a common confidence cone where softmax curvature suppresses disagreement.

Conclusion: The result isolates two missing links between internal update gaps and predictive cost: the contribution of separating states to expected loss and decoder sensitivity. Even an unbounded internal update gap does not by itself certify predictive failure. However, the construction is fixed in K and does not provide a universal criterion for when compression is harmless or characterize when internal gaps must incur task loss.

Abstract: Accurate posterior prediction need not require accurate approximation of Bayesian updates. We prove that an unbounded gap between the update maps can coexist with vanishing predictive KL for every fixed finite $K\ge2$ in a stationary symmetric Gaussian HMM. Exact Bayesian mixing and an explicit deterministic radial filter act on the same $K-1$ belief coordinates. As $q\to0^+$, their separation in centered logits in the worst case grows at least linearly in the natural confidence scale $L_K(q)$, while their categorical $D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\mathrm{exact}\|\mathrm{radial})$ vanishes at the same explicit witness. Along stationary HMM trajectories, the expected terminal KL between filtered posteriors also converges to zero at $H(q)=\lceil-\log(q)/c\rceil+1$. Typical blocks without switches drive both filters into a common confidence cone, where softmax curvature suppresses their disagreement; a single Gaussian maximal event controls adaptive noise. A sweep with equally spaced Gaussians over $K\in\{2,4,8\}$ illustrates the opposing trends, and binary controls at long horizons compare saturating and nonsaturating recurrences. The result isolates two missing links between internal update gaps and predictive cost: the contribution of separating states to expected loss and decoder sensitivity. Thus even an unbounded internal update gap does not by itself certify predictive failure. The construction is fixed in $K$ and does not provide a universal criterion for when compression is harmless or characterize when internal gaps must incur task loss.

</details>


### [122] [Phase-Decoupled, Model-Calibrated Power Control for Disaggregated LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11133)
*Jae Gon Kim,Donghoon Yoo,Hanyul Ryu,Sungho Ha,Juyeon Lee,Soojung Ryu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses GPU power optimization for LLM serving in disaggregated prefill/decode (PD) systems. The authors evaluate NVIDIA's Max-Q profile and find it provides modest gains (+8.6% tokens/J) with hidden latency costs (+5.2%). They propose a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller that achieves +20.4% tokens/J at only +3.5% latency increase on MoE models, while meeting SLOs that vendor profiles miss.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Datacenter GPU power is the binding constraint on LLM serving capacity. Production serving has shifted to prefill/decode disaggregation, but existing power optimization approaches (like NVIDIA's Max-Q) are inadequate: they show modest gains, are model-dependent, and impose hidden latency costs. The authors hypothesize that optimal power settings should be specific to each (model, quantization, engine, hardware) combination rather than GPU class.

Method: The authors propose a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller with two mechanisms: (1) Prefill lane runs under an SM-clock window whose floor is a latency guarantee by construction; (2) Decode lane under a power cap placed by automatic calibration just above a measured throughput/latency cliff. The key insight is that disaggregated decode lanes draw flat, memory-bound power, so the cap binds continuously, avoiding the reactive-overshoot weakness that plagued prior approaches like POLCA.

Result: On an 8x B200 node serving Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8) under agentic load, the balanced mode delivers +20.4% tokens/J at +3.5% mean e2e latency vs. +8.6% at +5.2% for Max-Q—a Pareto improvement on both axes. On Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4), every operating mode meets the ITL-p99 SLO while both vendor profiles miss it. A decode-actuator A/B shows the calibrated cap beats static clock locks. A three-day sustained run saves 32.3% of a lane pair's electricity. Both models are MoE; a dense model recovers roughly 5x less energy savings.

Conclusion: The optimal power setting is a property of the deployed (model, quantization, engine, hardware) combination rather than the GPU class. Each lane warrants its own profile. Converting SLO headroom into energy safely requires latency-gated calibration under a runtime SLO guard rather than a fixed recipe. The proposed controller achieves superior energy efficiency while maintaining latency SLOs, with claims scoped to MoE models due to limited gains on dense models.

Abstract: Datacenter GPU power is the binding constraint on LLM serving capacity, and production serving has shifted to prefill/decode (PD) disaggregation. Deploying NVIDIA's Max-Q inference profile on a disaggregated B200 system, we found its realized gain modest (+8.6% tokens/J), model-dependent, and carrying a mean end-to-end latency cost (+5.2%) that throughput-only evaluation does not surface; the profile also applies one setting to prefill and decode GPUs that operate in opposite hardware regimes. We hypothesize that the optimal power setting is a property of the deployed (model, quantization, engine, hardware) combination rather than of the GPU class, that each lane warrants its own profile, and that converting SLO headroom into energy safely requires latency-gated calibration under a runtime SLO guard rather than a fixed recipe. We present a phase-decoupled, model-calibrated controller: the prefill lane runs under an SM-clock window whose floor is a latency guarantee by construction, and the decode lane under a power cap placed by automatic calibration just above a measured throughput/latency cliff. Because a disaggregated decode lane draws flat, memory-bound power, the cap binds continuously, the reactive-overshoot weakness that led POLCA to reject capping is absent, and the GPU's own power manager retains throughput under the cap. On an 8x B200 node serving Qwen3-Coder-480B (FP8) under agentic load, our balanced mode delivers +20.4% tokens/J at +3.5% mean e2e versus +8.6% at +5.2% for Max-Q, a Pareto improvement on both axes. On Qwen3-235B-A22B (NVFP4) every operating mode meets the ITL-p99 SLO in every repetition; both vendor profiles miss it. A decode-actuator A/B shows the calibrated cap beats static clock locks, and a three-day sustained run saves 32.3% of a lane pair's electricity. Both models are MoE; a dense model recovers roughly 5x less, so we scope our claims to MoE serving.

</details>


### [123] [Bidirectional Multimodal Fusion of Sky Images and Time-Series for Solar Forecasting with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11135)
*Ken Chen,Maneesha Perera,Wei Wang,Sachith Seneviratne,Hansani Weeratunge,Saman Halgamuge*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SolCloudLLM is an LLM-based multimodal forecasting framework that combines sky images with time-series data for short-term PV power and GHI forecasting. It uses bidirectional multimodal fusion to align sky-image patches with time-series patches, achieving up to 25.4% MSE reduction over baselines, with benefits concentrated under cloudy conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short-term PV power forecasting is essential for grid operations but errors are dominated by cloud-induced ramps. Historical numerical data alone struggles to anticipate incoming clouds, making ground-based sky images crucial. Existing LLM-based forecasting methods are predominantly unimodal, and effectively incorporating sky imagery into LLM frameworks remains unexplored.

Method: SolCloudLLM aligns sky-image patches with time-series patches and fuses their representations through bidirectional multimodal fusion, yielding a unified representation that is mapped into the embedding space of an LLM for forecasting.

Result: SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms the best baseline methods in MSE across all forecasting horizons, achieving a maximum relative MSE reduction of 25.4%. Benefits are concentrated primarily under cloudy conditions. It achieves the best performance in nearly all few-shot settings, while other deep learning baselines experience substantial degradation and are frequently outperformed by non-learning physical methods.

Conclusion: SolCloudLLM effectively leverages multimodal data (sky imagery + time-series) within an LLM-based framework for PV forecasting. The bidirectional multimodal fusion approach successfully integrates physical visual signals with numerical data, providing significant performance gains especially under cloudy conditions and in few-shot scenarios.

Abstract: Short-term photovoltaic (PV) power and global horizontal irradiance (GHI) forecasts are essential for effective dispatch, reserve scheduling, and grid operations. At these forecasting horizons, errors are predominantly driven by cloud induced ramps: relying solely on historical numerical data may struggle to anticipate an incoming cloud, making ground-based sky images a crucial complementary physical signal. Furthermore, forecast performance is highly sensitive to location and local observing conditions, creating a strong need for site-specific data that are often scarce. Recently, large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated competitive performance and high data efficiency in time-series forecasting. Despite their success, existing LLM-based forecasting methods remain predominantly unimodal, relying primarily on historical numerical time-series data. Effectively incorporating sky imagery into an LLM-based forecasting framework remains under-explored and an open challenge. In this paper, we propose SolCloudLLM, an LLM-based multimodal forecasting framework. SolCloudLLM aligns sky-image patches with time-series patches and fuses their corresponding representations through bidirectional multimodal fusion, yielding a unified representation that is subsequently mapped into the embedding space of an LLM. Extensive experiments on the SIRTA and SKIPP'D datasets demonstrate that SolCloudLLM consistently outperforms the best baseline methods in MSE across all forecasting horizons, achieving a maximum relative MSE reduction of 25.4%. Stratified analysis further indicates that the benefits of multimodal fusion are concentrated primarily under cloudy conditions. Notably, SolCloudLLM achieves the best performance in nearly all few-shot settings, whereas other deep learning baselines experience substantial performance degradation and are frequently outperformed by the non-learning physical method.

</details>


### [124] [LILA: Calibration-Free Structured Pruning of Large Language Models via Latent Spectral Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11163)
*Sankar Behera,Dhruv Singh,Anshika Agnihotri,Raj Kumar Choudhary,Satyadev Ahlawat,Yamuna Prasad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LILA introduces a calibration-free, training-free structured pruning method for LLMs that uses Kolmogorov-Smirnov distance between singular value distributions to score neuron importance, outperforming prior methods including PruneNet and SliceGPT without any fine-tuning, while achieving competitive results after minimal LoRA recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM structured pruning methods require calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks, making them computationally expensive and impractical. This work aims to develop a pruning method that requires no training, calibration data, or auxiliary networks while maintaining high performance.

Method: LILA scores neuron importance via the Kolmogorov-Smirnov (KS) distance between empirical singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated feed-forward network (FFN) weight matrix. This provides a closed-form spectral rule that requires no training, calibration data, or auxiliary network. The method also extends to dynamically allocate sparsity budgets via KS-scores.

Result: Without any fine-tuning, LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by 1.57pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B at 25% sparsity, and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0pp across all sparsity levels. After one epoch of LoRA recovery fine-tuning, LILA matches SliceGPT within 0.48pp margin across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2. NTK analysis confirms 22x reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning.

Conclusion: LILA demonstrates that effective structured pruning can be achieved through spectral analysis without calibration data or training. The method preserves original architecture while achieving state-of-the-art results, and reveals single-layer architectural bottlenecks at higher compression regimes. The theoretical grounding via Neural Tangent Kernel analysis provides validation for the spectral importance criterion.

Abstract: Structured pruning of large language models (LLMs) offers hardware-efficient compression, yet existing methods require calibration data, gradient computation, or large auxiliary policy networks at pruning time. LILA (\emph{Latent-Informed Layer Analysis}) scores neuron importance via the Kolmogorov--Smirnov (KS) distance between empirical singular value distributions of the full and neuron-ablated feed-forward network (FFN) weight matrix, providing a closed-form spectral rule requiring no training, calibration data, or auxiliary network. Without any fine-tuning, LILA surpasses PruneNet (45M-parameter RL policy) by 1.57~pp in zero-shot accuracy on LLaMA-2-7B at 25\% sparsity, and outperforms WikiText-2-calibrated SliceGPT by up to 6.0~pp across all sparsity levels, while preserving the original architecture. After one epoch of LoRA recovery fine-tuning, LILA achieves highly competitive performance, matching the heavily calibrated SliceGPT baseline to within a 0.48~pp margin across LLaMA-2-7B and Phi-2, despite using zero calibration data. A Neural Tangent Kernel analysis confirms a 22$\times$ reduction in functional distortion versus random pruning, providing theoretical grounding for the spectral importance criterion. Finally, extending LILA to dynamically allocate sparsity budgets via KS-scores yields state-of-the-art generative preservation at moderate compression, while uncovering fundamental single-layer architectural bottlenecks at higher compression regimes.

</details>


### [125] [When does a spectral prior help graph learning? Connectivity-loss estimation under road-network disruptions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11166)
*Van-Truong Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes using Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to rapidly estimate relative algebraic-connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion in road networks, providing a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation and local approximation. The method learns a bounded correction to first-order Fiedler sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid evaluation of many simultaneous road-link disruptions requires a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation (computationally expensive) and local approximation (less accurate). There is a need for methods that can quickly assess network connectivity under multiple edge failures.

Method: The study employs GCN, GraphSAGE, and edge-aware MPNN backbones to learn a bounded correction to first-order Fiedler sensitivity. Experiments consider three failure modes: independent, spatially clustered, and edge-betweenness-targeted. Testing includes synthetic graph-disjoint splits and zero-shot transfer to 13 OpenStreetMap areas across six countries.

Result: Residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391 (95% CI: 0.0151-0.0662), while residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257 (0.0095-0.0446). Second-order perturbation only achieves 0.0028-0.0053 improvement over first-order. Leave-one-country-out transfer shows mixed results: residual GCN improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0622 but worsens spatial point estimate. The method scales to 20,000 nodes.

Conclusion: The spectral residual serves as a useful but domain-sensitive inductive bias for structural connectivity screening. GNN-based corrections significantly outperform analytical baselines, with specific architectures favoring specific failure modes. The approach separates one-time spectral setup from amortized screening cost, enabling practical large-scale network vulnerability assessment.

Abstract: Rapid evaluation of many simultaneous road-link disruptions requires a practical compromise between exact spectral recomputation and local approximation. We estimate relative algebraic-connectivity loss after multi-edge deletion using graph neural networks (GNNs) that learn a bounded correction to a first-order Fiedler sensitivity. The study considers independent, spatially clustered, and edge-betweenness-targeted failures, with graph-disjoint synthetic splits and zero-shot transfer to 13 OpenStreetMap (OSM) areas in six countries. GCN, GraphSAGE, and edge-aware MPNN backbones are compared with analytical baselines. In expanded OSM tests, residual GCN improves spatial-failure MAE by 0.0391 (95% hierarchical interval 0.0151-0.0662), while residual GraphSAGE improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0257 (0.0095-0.0446). Second-order perturbation improves first-order MAE by only 0.0028-0.0053. Correction slopes decrease under targeted transfer, indicating residual shrinkage around systematic prior error. Leave-one-country-out OSM-to-OSM transfer is mixed: residual GCN improves targeted-failure MAE by 0.0622 (0.0169-0.1153) but worsens the spatial point estimate. Sparse scaling extends to 20,000 nodes and separates one-time spectral setup from amortized screening cost. These results characterize the spectral residual as a useful but domain-sensitive inductive bias for structural connectivity screening. Code, cached networks, and reproducibility artifacts are archived at doi:10.5281/zenodo.22307723.

</details>


### [126] [Semi-Tensor Product-Based Multi-Term Randomized T-SVD and Its Visual Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11168)
*Xingchen Xiao,Feng Zhang,Wenjin Qin,Jianjun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under t-product framework with arbitrary invertible linear transforms, developing MSTP-SVD for improved low-rank approximation and MRSTP-SVD for computational efficiency. The method is validated on image and video compression and completion tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard t-product in tensor singular value decomposition imposes strict dimensional compatibility constraints. While semi-tensor product (STP) extensions relax this restriction, they suffer from limited approximation accuracy due to single-term formulations and high computational costs for large-scale tensor data.

Method: 1) Propose a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under t-product framework induced by arbitrary invertible linear transforms. 2) Develop multi-term semi-tensor product SVD (MSTP-SVD) integrating multiple orthogonal decomposition terms. 3) Incorporate randomized projection and power iteration to create accelerated MRSTP-SVD algorithm.

Result: The proposed MSTP-SVD significantly improves low-rank approximation accuracy compared with single-term schemes, while MRSTP-SVD achieves a balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency.

Conclusion: Experiments on image and video compression and completion tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method in breaking rigid dimension matching requirements while retaining the closed-form property of T-SVD.

Abstract: Tensor singular value decomposition (T-SVD), which is built upon the tensor-tensor product (t-product), has emerged as a powerful tool for processing high-dimensional visual data such as color images and videos. However, the standard t-product imposes strict dimensional compatibility constraints. Although extensions based on the semi-tensor product (STP) relax this restriction, their single-term formulations still suffer from limited approximation accuracy. Moreover, these deterministic methods incur high computational costs when processing large-scale tensor data. To address these issues, this paper introduces a novel semi-tensor product for third-order tensors under the t-product framework induced by arbitrary invertible linear transforms. The resulting tensor semi-tensor product breaks the rigid dimension matching requirement of the standard t-product, while retaining the closed-form property of T-SVD. Based on this construction, we develop a multi-term semi-tensor product singular value decomposition (MSTP-SVD), which integrates multiple orthogonal decomposition terms to significantly improve low-rank approximation accuracy compared with single-term schemes. To reduce the computational cost of multi-term modeling, we incorporate randomized projection and power iteration techniques into the MSTP-SVD framework, yielding an accelerated multi-term randomized semi-tensor product SVD (MRSTP-SVD) algorithm that achieves a balance between reconstruction accuracy and computational efficiency. Experiments on image and video compression and completion tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [127] [Hierarchical Clustering Can Jointly Satisfy Richness, Consistency, and Scale Invariance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11173)
*Daichi Kuroda,Maximilien Dreveton,Matthias Grossglauser,Patrick Thiran*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Kleinberg's Impossibility Theorem shows no flat clustering method can satisfy scale invariance, richness, and consistency simultaneously. This paper demonstrates that the hierarchical analog of these axioms ARE jointly satisfiable - there exist uncountably many admissible hierarchical clustering methods. These methods form a partially ordered set with no greatest element but with a common structural backbone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether Kleinberg's impossibility result (which applies to flat clustering) persists when the output is a hierarchy rather than a single partition.

Method: Axiomatic analysis using the hierarchical analogs of Kleinberg's three axioms (scale invariance, richness, consistency). The authors construct admissible hierarchical clustering methods including well-separated cluster methods and non-binary single linkage.

Result: The hierarchical versions of the three axioms are jointly satisfiable. There exist uncountably many admissible hierarchical clustering methods. These methods form a partial order under refinement, with no greatest element and uncountably many pairwise incompatible maximal elements. However, every admissible method contains a hierarchy of well-separated clusters, and any finite collection shares a common nontrivial backbone.

Conclusion: The diversity among admissible hierarchical clustering methods is substantial but constrained - while no single "best" method exists, all admissible methods share common structural properties, revealing a meaningful theory of hierarchical clustering despite the impossibility result in the flat case.

Abstract: Despite its ubiquity, clustering lacks a universally accepted definition of what is a cluster. Kleinberg's Impossibility Theorem formalizes this difficulty by showing that no flat clustering method can simultaneously satisfy three natural axioms: scale invariance, richness, and consistency. In this paper, we ask whether this impossibility persists when the output is a hierarchy rather than a single partition. We show that, in contrast to the flat clustering setting, the hierarchical analog of these axioms are jointly satisfiable. In fact, there exist uncountably many hierarchical clustering methods satisfying these axioms, which we call admissible. We explicitly construct several admissible methods, including methods based on well-separated clusters and a non-binary version of single linkage. For certain pairs of admissible methods, the hierarchy produced by one always refines that produced by the other. This refinement relation defines a partial order on the class of admissible methods. This partially ordered set has no greatest element and contains uncountably many pairwise incompatible maximal elements, revealing substantial diversity among admissible methods. Nevertheless, this diversity is constrained: every admissible method contains a hierarchy of sufficiently well-separated clusters, and every finite collection of admissible methods shares such a nontrivial common backbone.

</details>


### [128] [Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES) under Time-Varying Loss Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11207)
*Rahul Vaze*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper extends the Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES) framework to handle time-varying loss functions in addition to evolving feasible regions. The projected proximal algorithm achieves O(T^(1-β)) regret and O(T^β) movement cost for convex functions, and O(1) regret with O(log T) movement cost for strongly convex functions. Lower bounds are also established.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original CONES framework assumed fixed objective functions with evolving feasible regions. This paper generalizes it to allow both loss functions and feasible sets to change over time, bridging the gap between static optimization and online learning with constraints.

Method: The projected proximal algorithm is employed, analyzing its performance under both convex and strongly convex loss functions. The analysis uses techniques from online convex optimization and convex geometry.

Result: For convex loss functions: O(T^(1-β)) regret and O(T^β) movement cost for any β∈[0,1). For strongly convex functions: O(1) regret and O(log T) movement cost. Lower bounds show Ω(T^((1-β)/2)) movement cost for any weakly adaptive algorithm with O(T^β) regret, and Ω(log T) for sublinear anytime regret.

Conclusion: The paper establishes tight bounds for online convex optimization with nested evolving feasible sets, showing that the projected proximal algorithm is optimal (or near-optimal) in both convex and strongly convex settings. The results generalize nested convex body chasing (NCBC) to an optimization-oriented framework.

Abstract: Convex Optimization with Nested Evolving Feasible Sets (CONES)} was introduced in \cite{CONESVaze} where the objective function \(f\) remains fixed but the feasible region evolves over time as a nested sequence \(S_1 \supseteq S_2 \supseteq \cdots \supseteq S_T\). The goal of an online algorithm is to simultaneously minimize the regret with respect to hindsight static optimal benchmark and the total movement cost $M_\cA(T)$ while ensuring feasibility at all times. CONES is an optimization-oriented generalization of the well-known \emph{nested convex body chasing} (NCBC). In this paper, we extend CONES to allow for loss functions $f_t'$s to also change over time. When all loss functions are convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm achieves $O(T^{1-β}), O(T^β)$ simultaneous regret and movement cost, respectively, for any $β\in [0,1)$, over a time horizon of $T$. We also show that any {\it weakly adaptive} online algorithm with $O(T^β)$ regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(T^{\frac{1-β}{2}}\right)$ for any $β\in [0,1)$. When all loss functions are strongly convex, we show that the projected proximal algorithm simultaneously achieves $O(1)$ regret and a movement cost of $O(\log T)$. To complement this, we show that any online algorithm with sublinear {\it anytime} regret has a movement cost of $Ω\left(\log T\right)$.

</details>


### [129] [REVA: Reusable Evidence View Aggregation for Context-Efficient RAG Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11209)
*Tuan Nguyen,Qiran Hu,Banruo Liu,Khoa D. Doan,Kok-Seng Wong,Fan Lai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: REVA is a novel RAG compression framework that aggregates historical query-document-model interactions into reusable evidence views, reducing inference costs while improving generation quality by 1.0-5.8 points over existing methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG improves knowledge-intensive LLM applications but longer contexts increase latency, KV cache memory, and token costs. Existing post-retrieval compressors operate independently per query, rely on auxiliary models, and introduce online overhead that can offset the benefits of shorter prompts.

Method: REVA mines the target generator's historical attention traces into a document-keyed, budget-agnostic score store. It maps token-level attention to readable word units, aggregates importance across repeated document accesses, and renders budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order and the standard RAG interface.

Result: Across four representative benchmarks and modern LLMs, REVA improves generation quality by 1.0-5.8 points over existing advances, while reducing compression overhead by a factor of 5.3 to 15.6, adding less than 40 ms of latency.

Conclusion: REVA successfully addresses RAG compression limitations by reusing historical attention data, achieving better quality than existing compressors while significantly reducing overhead and latency.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) improves knowledge-intensive large language model (LLM) applications by conditioning generation on retrieved documents, but longer contexts increase latency, key-value (KV) cache memory, and token cost. Post-retrieval compression can reduce this cost, yet existing compressors often operate independently for each query, rely on auxiliary models or rewriting, and introduce online overhead that can offset the benefit of shorter prompts. We revisit RAG compression from a data-mining perspective by aggregating historical query--document--model interactions into reusable evidence views. We first show that modern compressors have unstable gains over simple truncation and can add substantial inference-time latency. We then propose Reusable Evidence View Aggregation (REVA), a framework that mines the target generator's historical attention traces into a document-keyed, budget-agnostic score store. REVA maps token-level attention to readable word units, aggregates importance across repeated document accesses, and renders budget-specific plain-text views that preserve document order and the standard RAG interface. Across four representative benchmarks and modern LLMs, REVA improves generation quality by 1.0--5.8 points over existing advances, while reducing compression overhead by a factor of 5.3 to 15.6, adding less than 40 ms of latency.

</details>


### [130] [Legible Failures: Detecting and Repairing In-Context Binding Errors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11216)
*Manas Venkata Sai Ravulapalli,Samrath Singh Chadha,Abhinav M. Hari*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Linear probes can recover correct entity-obligation bindings from frozen language model hidden states even when the model outputs incorrect bindings, demonstrating that models sometimes "know" but fail to use this knowledge. Probes can be used to detect failures and steer the model toward correct outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether incorrect model outputs on entity-obligation binding tasks are due to missing information or failed utilization of stored information. The study aims to investigate whether language models possess correct knowledge but fail to express it in their outputs.

Method: Evaluated 16 public checkpoints across 3 seeds each. Used linear probes trained on a training fold, layer selection on validation fold, and testing on disjoint test fold. Employed query-entity counterfactual to rule out token presence/recency. Tested failure detection using probe-output disagreement sign and steering the residual stream toward probe-decoded bindings.

Result: On incorrect model outputs, probe accuracy exceeds the baseline (1/K = 0.125) by +0.196 (95% CI [+0.101, +0.296]). Probe-output disagreement sign improves failure detection over model confidence by +0.079 AUROC. Steering the residual stream raises accuracy by +0.168 (95% CI [+0.066, +0.280]) across all 8 models tested.

Conclusion: In-context binding is a setting where probes are actionable—contrary to prior studies reporting probe-detected errors are resistant to interventions. Probes can both detect failures and actively improve model outputs through residual stream steering.

Abstract: A wrong answer does not show whether the model lacked the needed information or held it and failed to use it. On an entity-obligation binding task, a language model can emit an incorrect prompt-supplied binding while a linear probe can recover the correct one from its frozen hidden state. We measure how often this occurs across 16 public checkpoints, each evaluated with three seeds. We fit a probe on a training fold, select its layer on a validation fold, and report results on a disjoint test fold. On the trials each model gets wrong, probe accuracy exceeds the strict present-obligation baseline, 1/K = 0.125, by +0.196 (95% CI [+0.101, +0.296], bootstrapped over models). A query-entity counterfactual rules out token presence and recency. A score built from the sign of probe-output disagreement improves failure detection over the model's own confidence by +0.079 AUROC (95% CI [+0.036, +0.126]). Raw probe confidence gives no measurable improvement over model confidence. Steering the residual stream toward the probe-decoded binding, with no gold label, raises accuracy on all eight models tested by a mean of +0.168 (95% CI [+0.066, +0.280]). Where recent studies report that probe-detected errors are resistant to interventions, we find that in-context binding is a setting in which probes are actionable.

</details>


### [131] [Polyhedral Geometry of Time-to-First-Spike Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11227)
*Manjot Singh,Guido Montúfar,Gitta Kutyniok*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper studies the expressivity of spiking neural networks (SNNs) using the time-to-first-spike model, showing that SNNs can generate richer input space partitions than conventional ReLU networks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spiking neural networks provide an event-driven computation framework complementary to conventional feedforward networks, but their expressivity properties remain poorly understood compared to standard neural networks.

Method: The authors analyze the time-to-first-spike model with continuous piecewise linear input-output maps governed by causal feasibility constraints. They derive maxout-like representations for neuron firing times and formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal sets to bound the maximal number of causal regions in both shallow and multilayer feedforward spiking networks.

Result: Each neuron's firing time admits a maxout-like representation with exponentially many, highly constrained affine pieces. The theoretical and experimental results demonstrate that spiking networks can generate richer partitions of the input space than conventional feedforward ReLU networks.

Conclusion: Spiking neural networks possess greater expressive capacity than conventional ReLU networks, enabling more complex input space partitioning through their event-driven, asynchronous computation model.

Abstract: We study the expressivity of spiking neural networks, which provide a natural framework for asynchronous, event-driven computation complementary to conventional feedforward neural networks. We consider the time-to-first-spike model in a setting for which the input-output map is continuous and piecewise linear, with affine pieces governed by causal feasibility constraints that determine which presynaptic spikes occur before a neuron fires. We first show that each neuron's firing time admits a maxout-like representation with exponentially many, highly constrained affine pieces. We then formalize causal regions as polyhedral regions with fixed causal sets and derive upper and lower bounds on the maximal number of causal regions in both shallow and multilayer feedforward spiking networks. Our theoretical and experimental results show that spiking networks can generate richer partitions of the input space than conventional feedforward ReLU networks.

</details>


### [132] [Solving Few-Shot Multiobjective Multitask Optimization via Iterative Sequential Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11228)
*Tingyang Wei,Haofeng Wu,Ananda Phan Iman,Zhao Wei,Jiao Liu,Yew-Soon Ong*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses few-shot multiobjective multitask optimization (MTO) by proposing Iterative Sequential Transfer (IST), which converts simultaneous MTO into sequential transfer problems, focusing evaluations on one target per iteration and using likelihood-informed task prioritization to maximize transfer utility under tight evaluation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MTO methods rely on aligning elite solution distributions across tasks, which becomes a critical bottleneck in few-shot optimization regimes where restricted evaluation budgets prevent identifying elite solutions needed for beneficial transfer. This problem is especially severe in multiobjective multitask problems requiring approximation of continuous Pareto manifolds.

Method: The paper proposes IST with two key components: (1) Reformulating MTO as sequential transfer optimization problems that concentrate evaluations on a single target per iteration, and (2) A likelihood-informed task prioritization mechanism to identify which task is most ready for knowledge integration, thereby maximizing transfer utility.

Result: Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method under tight evaluation budgets.

Conclusion: IST successfully circumvents the bottleneck of requiring elite solution distributions for knowledge transfer in few-shot MTO scenarios, providing an effective approach for multiobjective multitask optimization under limited evaluation budgets.

Abstract: Applying knowledge transfer across multiple optimization tasks, multitask optimization (MTO) emerges as a promising approach to solving synergistic optimization tasks simultaneously. However, the development of effective knowledge transfer mechanisms in MTO fundamentally relies on aligning elite solution distributions across tasks. This dependency creates a critical bottleneck in few-shot optimization regimes, as restricted evaluation budgets impede the identification of elite solution distributions required for beneficial transfer. This challenge is exacerbated in multiobjective multitask problems, where each optimizer must approximate a continuous Pareto manifold rather than a single optimal point. This paper introduces Iterative Sequential Transfer (IST) to circumvent this bottleneck. We model MTO as a sequence of sequential transfer optimization problems, concentrating evaluations on a single target per iteration. We propose a likelihood-informed task prioritization mechanism to maximize transfer utility by identifying the task most likely ready for knowledge integration. Empirical results on benchmark and real-world problems verify the effectiveness of the proposed method under tight budgets.

</details>


### [133] [MUtE: A Dual Framework for Concept Erasure and Counterfactual Interventions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11253)
*Antoine Saillenfest*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel framework for erasing concept-specific information from neural network representations while preserving unrelated information. The approach derives optimal bounds for concept erasure and implements a deterministic dual counterfactual mapping with translational bias constraints, applicable to algorithmic fairness and counterfactual text generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to improveMitigating bias in machine learning models and enabling better interpretation of model decisions by transforming representations to make target concepts unpredictable while preserving concept-unrelated information. The work bridges theoretical optimal bounds with practical representation learning.

Method: The paper derives a novel class of erasure functions that induce a deterministic, dual counterfactual mapping. The implementation imposes a translational bias on counterfactual trajectories - a constraint aligned with how concepts geometrically manifest in modern language models. This framework enables navigation between concept erasure and counterfactual generation.

Result: The empirical results demonstrate efficacy in improving downstream algorithmic fairness and generating counterfactual texts. The translational bias constraint proves effective for concept manipulation in language models.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully bridges theoretical optimality with practical representation learning, providing a unified approach for concept erasure and counterfactual generation that improves both fairness and interpretability.

Abstract: Erasing concept-specific information from representations has been proven useful for mitigating bias or interpreting model decisions. The joint objective is to transform the original representations such that the target concept becomes unpredictable, while maximally preserving concept-unrelated information. In this work, we revisit the optimal bounds of concept erasure to derive a novel class of erasure functions that naturally induce a deterministic, dual counterfactual mapping. Bridging the gap between theoretical optimality and practical representation learning, we design an implementation that imposes a translational bias on counterfactual trajectories - a constraint that aligns with how many concepts geometrically manifest in modern language models. Our framework enables seamless navigation between concept erasure and counterfactual generation. We empirically demonstrate its efficacy in improving downstream algorithmic fairness and generating counterfactual texts.

</details>


### [134] [A Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model for Traffic Flow Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11314)
*Xue Qiu,Jianli Xiao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes DF-LLM, a Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model for traffic flow prediction that addresses limitations of traditional neural networks (limited to singular feature modeling) and LLMs (insufficient spatial topological information and spatiotemporal correlation mining). The model integrates three core components: spatiotemporal embedding module, spatiotemporal fusion module, and LLM backbone with differentiated parameter adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional neural networks struggle with accuracy limits due to reliance on singular feature modeling, while LLMs lack sufficient capture of spatial topological information and spatiotemporal correlation. Traffic flow prediction is crucial for intelligent transportation systems to alleviate congestion and improve resource allocation efficiency.

Method: The DF-LLM model incorporates: (1) Spatiotemporal embedding module for synergistic representation of multi-scale spatiotemporal features; (2) Spatiotemporal fusion module integrating spatial topology and dynamic dependencies via graph convolution; (3) LLM backbone with differentiated parameter adaptation strategy to balance training efficiency and traffic data adaptability; (4) Context aggregation attention module to strengthen global dependencies; (5) Residual connections to mitigate gradient vanishing in deep networks.

Result: Experiments show that DF-LLM achieved better performance compared to baseline methods on all four datasets based on the evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: The proposed DF-LLM effectively combines LLMs with spatiotemporal modeling for traffic flow prediction, achieving state-of-the-art performance across four datasets through its innovative architecture components.

Abstract: Traffic flow prediction is a core supporting technology for intelligent transportation systems. It uses historical data to infer future traffic dynamics in specific areas, thereby helping to alleviate congestion and improve resource allocation efficiency. Traditional neural networks struggle to break through accuracy limits due to their reliance on singular feature modeling, while large language models (LLMs) suffer from insufficient capture of spatial topological information and mining spatiotemporal correlation. This study proposes a Dynamic Fusion Large Language Model (DF-LLM) for traffic flow prediction. The model incorporates three core components: spatiotemporal embedding module, spatiotemporal fusion module, and LLM backbone. The spatiotemporal embedding module enables synergistic representation of multi-scale spatiotemporal features. The spatiotemporal fusion module integrates spatial topology and dynamic dependencies via graph convolution. The LLM backbone adopts a differentiated parameter adaptation strategy to balance training efficiency and traffic data adaptability. Additionally, it introduces a context aggregation attention module to strengthens global dependencies. More importantly, the LLM backbone takes the residual connections to mitigate the gradient vanishing in deep networks. Experiments show that DF-LLM has achieved better performance by comparing the metrics on all the four datasets.

</details>


### [135] [Reification as a Transferable Vocabulary: Zero-Shot Link Prediction with Vanilla GNNs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11347)
*Camille Pradel*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes reifying knowledge graphs as graphs where each fact becomes a node connected to its subject, object, and relation type via six meta-relations. Using this representation with standard GNNs (GAT, GINE, GraphSAGE, R-GCN) trained on a single graph achieves zero-shot transfer to 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks, matching ULTRA—a dedicated foundation model pretrained on three graphs. The approach also extends to relational databases for foreign-key prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current knowledge graph foundation models like ULTRA hard-code the transfer mechanism into their architecture. This work aims to move that mechanism into the representation itself, making transferability a property of how the graph is encoded rather than the model architecture. This allows using simpler, off-the-shelf GNNs instead of specialized architectures.

Method: The key innovation is "reification"—representing every fact as a node connected to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations. Relation types become anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. Five textbook GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum and mean+max aggregation, GraphSAGE, R-GCN) were trained on a single knowledge graph (4,245 triples) for 30 minutes on one NVIDIA A100, then evaluated on 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. The same representation was applied to relational databases, treating rows as entities and foreign-key columns as relation types.

Result: The best model (GAT) matches ULTRA's performance across ULTRA's own evaluation suite, despite ULTRA being pretrained on three graphs while this approach uses only one graph. On two unseen relational databases, the model pretrained on three knowledge graphs ranks foreign-key targets far above random initialization and degree controls, demonstrating zero-shot transfer to database schema.

Conclusion: Transferability can be achieved through representation design rather than specialized architectures. The fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations provides a universal language for encoding knowledge graphs that enables standard GNNs to transfer zero-shot to unseen graphs and databases. This work opens the door to using commodity neural network architectures for knowledge graph foundation models, with code, checkpoints, and evaluation pipeline released for reproducibility.

Abstract: Knowledge graph foundation models such as ULTRA achieve zero-shot link prediction on unseen graphs through dedicated architectures that hard-code a transfer mechanism. In this work we move that mechanism out of the architecture and into the representation, by \emph{reifying} the input graph: every fact becomes a node, connected to its subject, object, and relation type through a fixed vocabulary of six meta-relations, with relation types as anonymous shared nodes rather than model parameters. On this representation, five textbook GNNs (GAT, GINE with sum and with mean+max aggregation, GraphSAGE, R-GCN), each trained on a single knowledge graph of 4,245 triples for 30 minutes on one NVIDIA A100, transfer zero-shot to 40 inductive link-prediction benchmarks. The best of them, an off-the-shelf GAT, matches ULTRA, a dedicated foundation model pretrained on three graphs, across ULTRA's own evaluation suite. The same fixed vocabulary extends to relational databases, a row becoming an entity and a foreign-key column a relation type; a preliminary probe on two unseen databases, with no cell values, schema text or in-context labels, shows a model of this family pretrained on three knowledge graphs ranking foreign-key targets far above random-initialization and degree controls. We release the code, the checkpoints, and the evaluation pipeline for all 40 benchmarks.

</details>


### [136] [Local Robustness Quantification for Naive Bayes Classifiers and Generative Forests: a General Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11366)
*Adrián Detavernier,Jasper De Bock*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents methods for quantifying the robustness of predictions made by generative classifiers (naive Bayes and generative forests) whose underlying distribution is a Probabilistic Graphical Model. Robustness is defined as the maximum perturbation tolerance of a prediction without changing its outcome, considering various perturbation types including epsilon-contamination, total variation distance, and chi-squared divergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the trustworthiness of machine learning predictions by developing a robustness quantification framework. The authors aim to provide a metric that can indicate whether a prediction is reliable by measuring how much the underlying distribution can be perturbed before the prediction changes.

Method: The paper develops analytical methods for calculating robustness metrics for two types of generative classifiers: naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests. The approach considers perturbations to the local models of the PGMs within general neighborhoods, specifically focusing on epsilon-contamination, total variation distance, and chi-squared divergence balls as perturbation measures.

Result: The methods were tested on benchmark datasets. The results demonstrate that robustness values serve as effective indicators for prediction trustworthiness, and the approach was compared with other existing indicators.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that robustness quantification is a valuable tool for assessing the reliability of generative classifier predictions. The proposed methods provide a principled way to evaluate prediction trustworthiness, with potential applications in safety-critical machine learning deployments.

Abstract: We provide methods for calculating the robustness of the predictions of two types of generative classifiers whose underlying distribution is a Probabilistic Graphical Model (PGM): naive Bayes classifiers and generative forests (a probabilistic extension of random forests). Following the paradigm of robustness quantification, we define the robustness of a prediction as the extent to which the distribution of the classifier can be perturbed without changing this prediction. We consider perturbations obtained by varying the local models of the PGMs within general neighborhoods and focus in particular on epsilon-contamination, total variation distance and chi-squared divergence balls. We test our methods on benchmark datasets, demonstrate that the robustness value of a prediction serves as an indicator for its trustworthiness and compare our approach with other such indicators.

</details>


### [137] [Prevalence Determines Precision:Silent Contamination in Detector-Defined Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11449)
*Jia Huang,Yankai Wan,Yangjun Ou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper reveals that ML datasets constructed using detectors contain phantom (false positive) contamination that acts as a second signal with detector-inherited shape, not additive noise. Using a detector on three pools with phantom rates of 81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%, the authors demonstrate that naive precision transfer produces +422% error, while Bayes' theorem predicts accurately within 3.3%. Contamination affects different estimators differently, and a common normalization can create undefined expectations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand why ML dataset precision differs from detector quality predictions, and to show that contamination (false positives) is not random noise but a structured second signal that invalidates common statistical assumptions.

Method: The authors used one detector and one time period across three pools, comparing a detector-defined event dataset against an independent official index that labels each detected item as real or phantom. They analyzed the detected response curve as a convex combination of true-event and phantom components, and compared different estimator behaviors under contamination.

Result: Key results: (1) Phantom rates varied dramatically across pools: 81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%; (2) Transferring precision from high-rate pools to low-rate pool predicted 0.955 vs measured 0.183 (+422% error); (3) Bayes expression predicted all three within 3.3%; (4) The detected response curve was an exact convex combination (residual 1.1e-16) with phantoms outnumbering true events 473 to 308; (5) A common normalization returned 0.40 where the well-defined estimator returned 0.10 on identical data.

Conclusion: Contamination in detector-defined datasets is a second signal with detector-inherited shape, not additive noise. Simple precision transfer across pools fails dramatically, while Bayes' theorem accurately accounts for true-positive prevalence. Common normalizations can create estimators with undefined expectations, and contamination direction depends on the specific estimator used.

Abstract: Many ML datasets are constructed by running a detector, heuristic, or model over candidate pools; accepted items become labels. Dataset precision is then governed by true-positive prevalence in each pool via Bayes, not solely by detector quality. Using one instrument and period, we hold a detector-defined event dataset plus an independent official index labeling every detected item as real or phantom. One detector, three pools yield phantom rates 81.7%, 9.0%, and 0.0%. Transferring precision from the two high-rate pools to the low-rate pool predicts 0.955 versus measured 0.183, a +422% error; the Bayes expression predicts all three within 3.3%. The detected response curve is an exact convex combination of a true-event and a phantom component (residual 1.1e-16), with phantoms outnumbering true events 473 to 308, so contamination is a second signal with detector-inherited shape, not additive noise. Contamination direction depends on the estimator: on identical windows one statistic is diluted and another inflated because its denominator is also contaminated. A common normalization turns the estimator into a mean of ratios whose expectation need not exist; on the same 335 events it returns 0.40 where the well-defined estimator returns 0.10.

</details>


### [138] [Combining Synthetic and Real Data for Low-Resource Historical OCR: A Manchu Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11495)
*Yan Hon Michael Chung,Hanlin Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses low-resource OCR for Manchu language (critically endangered) by combining synthetic and real historical training data with vision-language models (VLMs) and a compact CRNN. The study achieves 95.09-96.28% word accuracy using real training images, significantly improving over synthetic-only baselines (87.4-87.92%). Joint and sequential training yield similar results, and ensemble voting can boost accuracy to 98.27%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manchu is a critically endangered language with extensive Qing empire archival records that are digitized but difficult to search at scale. Previous work showed a substantial synthetic-to-real gap (87.4% accuracy with synthetic-only training). The study aims to determine how synthetic and real historical training data should be combined for optimal low-resource OCR performance.

Method: The study used 60,000 synthetic and 20,306 real historical word images. Three pretrained VLMs and a compact CRNN were evaluated under four training regimes: synthetic-only, real-only, joint synthetic-real, and sequential synthetic-to-real training. The evaluation followed a common checkpoint-selection and archival evaluation protocol.

Result: Introducing real training images raised accuracy to 95.09-96.28% word accuracy (from ~87% with synthetic-only). Synthetic supplementation substantially improved all three VLMs, with marginal effects for CRNN being sensitive to training objective. Joint and sequential training yielded similar archival accuracy. A compact CRNN also reached leading performance with real images. Ensemble voting among strong recognizers achieved 98.27% accuracy without additional training, with an 18th-century Manchu dictionary providing conflict resolution.

Conclusion: Real historical training data is crucial for closing the synthetic-to-real gap in low-resource OCR. Model scale alone does not determine recognition accuracy—compact CRNNs can match VLMs when real data is available. Complementary errors among recognizers enable effective ensemble voting, and historical dictionaries can adjudicate disagreements. The study provides practical guidelines for low-resource historical OCR.

Abstract: Manchu, now critically endangered, was one of the principal languages of the Qing empire (1636-1912), and its extensive archival record is increasingly digitized but remains difficult to search and analyze at scale. Previous work showed that vision-language models (VLMs) trained only on synthetic Manchu word images can reach 87.4% word accuracy on real Qing manuscripts and prints, leaving a substantial synthetic-to-real gap. This study examines how synthetic and real historical training data should be combined for low-resource OCR. Using 60,000 synthetic and 20,306 real historical word images, we evaluate three pretrained VLMs and a compact convolutional recurrent neural network (CRNN) under four regimes: synthetic-only, real-only, joint synthetic-real, and sequential synthetic-to-real training, following a common checkpoint-selection and archival evaluation protocol. Introducing real training images raises the leading configurations to between 95.09% and 96.28% word accuracy, while no synthetic-only configuration exceeds 87.92%. Synthetic supplementation substantially improves all three VLMs, whereas its marginal effect for the CRNN is sensitive to the training objective. Joint and sequential training yield broadly similar archival accuracy under the tested practical pipelines. A compact CRNN also reaches the leading performance range once real images are available, showing that model scale alone does not determine recognition accuracy. Finally, complementary errors among strong recognizers allow voting to raise accuracy to 98.27% without additional training, while an eighteenth-century Manchu dictionary provides a principled rule for adjudicating disagreements.

</details>


### [139] [Generalized Score Matching for Parameter Estimation on Convex Domains](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11521)
*Nishanth Shetty,Saisuchith Mahajan,Chandra Sekhar Seelamantula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper develops a generalized score matching framework for learning unnormalized probabilistic models, addressing the intractable partition function problem in maximum likelihood estimation. The approach extends classical score matching to convex domains and provides theoretical guarantees including convexity for exponential family models and consistency of the estimator.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Maximum likelihood estimation for unnormalized models requires evaluating and differentiating the partition function, which is often computationally intractable. Score matching offers an alternative by fitting the score function without relying on the normalizing constant, but existing variants have limited applicability.

Method: The authors construct the generalized score matching objective starting from Minimum Probability Flow (MPF) learning on a convex subset of R^d. They establish theoretical properties including: (1) showing it's a proper local scoring rule of second-order, (2) proving convexity for exponential family models, and (3) establishing consistency of the finite-sample estimator under standard regularity conditions.

Result: The objective recovers the true density when minimized (theoretical guarantee). For exponential family models on convex domains, the objective is convex, and the finite-sample estimator is consistent. Experiments demonstrate the framework on parameter estimation for exponential family densities and generative modeling tasks.

Conclusion: The generalized score matching framework provides a theoretically grounded and practically applicable method for learning unnormalized models on constrained domains where the partition function is analytically intractable. The framework naturally encompasses classical score matching and domain-adapted variants.

Abstract: Maximum likelihood (ML) estimation is a principled and statistically efficient approach for learning probabilistic models. However, for unnormalized models, ML estimation requires evaluating the partition function and differentiating through it, which may not always be tractable. Score matching provides a practically viable alternative that circumvents this obstacle by fitting the score in a way that eliminates dependence on the normalizing constant. We derive the generalized score matching objective on a convex subset of $\mathbb{R}^{d}$ constructively starting from Minimum Probability Flow (MPF) learning, and show how classical score matching as well as domain-adapted variants for non-negative data arise naturally within the proposed framework. We show that the resulting objective is a {\it proper local scoring rule} of second-order, which provides the theoretical guarantee that the true density is recovered when the objective is minimized. Furthermore, for a model belonging to the exponential family, we establish convexity of the objective together with consistency of the finite-sample estimator under standard regularity conditions. Our derivation sheds new light on the scope and applicability of generalized score matching in various problem settings. We compare generalized score matching-based estimators on constrained domains, where the partition function is analytically intractable. We provide experimental results on parameter estimation for model densities belonging to the exponential family defined over convex subsets of $\mathbb{R}^{d}$, and a generative modeling use-case to demonstrate broader applicability of the proposed generalized score matching framework.

</details>


### [140] [Particle GFlowNets: Rethinking Generative Marginalization Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11538)
*Tiago da Silva,Diego Mesquita,Salem Lahlou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper establishes that Generative Marginalization Models (MaMs) are equivalent to Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets), extending MaMs' sampling to non-autoregressive processes. The proposed Particle GFlowNets method uses an automatic full-state rejuvenation criterion derived from the Gelman-Rubin statistic, achieving significant training acceleration in large combinatorial spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MaMs were previously considered distinct from GFlowNets despite both being used for inference in discrete stochastic models. Additionally, the authors aimed to extend MaMs' sampling strategy beyond autoregressive settings to non-autoregressive generative processes.

Method: 1) Demonstrated mathematical equivalence between MaMs and GFlowNets by showing MaMs learn both marginal and conditional probabilities of a persistent-block Gibbs sampler; 2) Extended to non-autoregressive processes by developing an automatic criterion for full-state rejuvenation based on the Gelman-Rubin statistic, enabling faster learning convergence.

Result: Particle GFlowNets markedly accelerates training in large combinatorial spaces compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: The equivalence between MaMs and GFlowNets is established, unifying two previously distinct paradigms. The introduced Particle GFlowNets with Gelman-Rubin-based rejuvenation provides a practical approach for efficient sampling in large combinatorial spaces.

Abstract: Generative Marginalization Models (MaMs) have been recently introduced as efficient neural sampling models for any-order autoregressive modelling of discrete distributions. By learning both the marginal and conditional probabilities of a persistent-block Gibbs sampler, MaMs enable fast posterior evaluation with a single neural network forward pass. While prior work has considered MaMs to be distinct from Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets), a well-established paradigm for inference in discrete stochastic models, we show that they are equivalent. Then, we also extend MaMs' sampling strategy to non-autoregressive generative processes. In particular, we describe an automatic criterion for full-state rejuvenation of the Gibbs sampler, derived from the Gelman-Rubin statistic, which plays a key role in speeding up learning convergence. Our experiments show that our method, called Particle GFlowNets, markedly accelerates training in large combinatorial spaces.

</details>


### [141] [A Dataset and Model for Imputing Water Surface Elevation on a Large and Extremely Sparse Spatiotemporal Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11580)
*Ruben Cartuyvels,Karim Douch,Gabriele Bertoli,Mounia El Baz,Artemis Vrettou,Sébastien Lefèvre,Diego Fernandez Prieto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AmazonSWE is a large-scale dataset for training spatiotemporal graph imputation methods to estimate water surface elevation (WSE) across 19K+ river sections in the Amazon basin over 10 years, addressing the challenge of sparse satellite altimetry observations (fewer than 1% observed per day).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Continuous monitoring of river WSE is critical for flood forecasting and water resource management, but existing in situ gauges are scarce globally. Satellite altimetry offers a solution but suffers from sparse temporal coverage, necessitating new imputation methods adapted to the unique topology and sparsity of river networks.

Method: The authors propose a bidirectional selective state space model that samples connected subgraphs and flattens space and time into a single token sequence with topology-aware positional encodings, specifically designed for directed acyclic river topologies.

Result: The proposed model reduces RMSE by 18-39% compared to the state-of-the-art SWOT-based WSE densification method, while producing predictions for every river section rather than only those with sufficient satellite coverage.

Conclusion: This work demonstrates that specialized graph imputation models adapted to river network topology can significantly improve WSE estimation, with potential for societal impact in flood forecasting and water resource management across data-sparse regions.

Abstract: Continuous monitoring of water surface elevation across river networks is critical for flood forecasting, water resource management, and understanding the global water cycle. Yet, the scarcity of in situ gauges across much of the globe constrains the development of reliable modeling frameworks. Satellite altimetry has the potential to alleviate this problem but its use is currently hindered by sparse temporal coverage. To this end, we introduce AmazonSWE, a dataset for training and evaluating large-scale spatiotemporal graph imputation methods that integrates processed satellite altimetry measurements from a range of sources, including the recent wide-swath SWOT sensor. The dataset covers over 19K river sections and 10 years (2016-2026) in the Amazon river basin, with in situ gauges held out for evaluation. Besides contributing a novel real-world use case with the potential for societal impact, AmazonSWE introduces significant technical challenges: with fewer than 1% of sections observed per day, the dataset is far sparser than existing imputation benchmarks, and its directed acyclic river topology is both structurally different from and larger than graphs in existing datasets. We show that prior spatiotemporal graph imputation methods are not adapted to this topology, scale and sparsity, and propose a simple bidirectional selective state space model that outperforms them by sampling connected subgraphs and flattening space and time into a single token sequence with topology-aware positional encodings. Compared to the state-of-the-art published method for SWOT-based WSE densification, which integrates statistics with physical modeling, our model reduces RMSE against in situ gauges by 18-39%, while producing predictions for every river section rather than only those with sufficient nearby satellite coverage.

</details>


### [142] [LoaDiff: Conditional Generation of Electricity Consumption Time Series for Energy Analytics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11639)
*Mariia Baranova,Adrien Petralia,Etienne Le Naour,Nathan Etourneau,Guillaume Hofmann,Themis Palpanas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LoaDiff is a diffusion-based generative model designed to create realistic synthetic smart-meter load curves (year-long, sub-hourly) for residential electricity consumption.
