It addresses the challenge of restricted access to granular smart-meter data by generating synthetic alternatives that can be conditioned on household attributes and dynamic contextual variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The energy transition is increasing residential electricity consumption complexity through distributed generation, electrified appliances, and demand-response programs. Understanding these evolving behaviors requires granular smart-meter data for applications like load forecasting, appliance detection, and demand-side flexibility analysis. However, such data faces strict access restrictions and data-protection regulations, creating a need for realistic synthetic alternatives.

Method: LoaDiff uses a diffusion-based generative model architecture to produce year-long, sub-hourly smart-meter load curves. The model supports flexible conditioning on: (1) Static household attributes (e.g., appliance ownership), and (2) Dynamic contextual variables (calendar information, outdoor temperature). Evaluation was conducted against multiple generative baselines on three residential electricity-consumption datasets.

Result: LoaDiff demonstrates: (1) Generation of realistic and diverse load profiles, (2) Favorable trade-off between generation quality and limited memorization risk, (3) Preservation of information useful for downstream energy applications (load forecasting and appliance detection), and (4) Coherent response to changes in conditioning variables under alternative temperature conditions.

Conclusion: LoaDiff successfully addresses the need for synthetic smart-meter data by providing a diffusion-based approach that generates high-quality, diverse, and conditionally controllable load profiles while maintaining privacy through memorization avoidance and preserving utility for downstream energy applications.

Abstract: The energy transition is reshaping residential electricity consumption through the increasing adoption of distributed generation, electrified appliances, and demand-response programs. Understanding these evolving behaviors requires access to granular smart-meter data for applications such as load forecasting, appliance detection, and demand-side flexibility analysis. However, such data are subject to strict access restrictions and data-protection regulations. Thus, realistic synthetic alternatives are necessary. In this paper, we introduce LoaDiff, a diffusion-based generative model for year-long, sub-hourly smart-meter load curves. LoaDiff supports flexible conditioning on static household attributes, such as appliance ownership, and dynamic contextual variables, including calendar information and outdoor temperature. We evaluate the model against multiple generative baselines on three residential electricity-consumption datasets. Our experiments assess four complementary dimensions: fidelity and diversity, training-record memorization risk, downstream utility for load forecasting and appliance detection, and conditional controllability under alternative temperature conditions. The results show that LoaDiff generates realistic and diverse load profiles, achieves a favorable trade-off between generation quality and limited evidence of memorization, preserves information useful for downstream energy applications, and responds coherently to changes in conditioning variables.

</details>


### [143] [RDDMPI: Residual Denoising Diffusion Model for Probabilistic Multivariate Time Series Imputation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11648)
*Ramiro Valdes Jara,David Chapman,Adam Meyers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RDDMPI is a conditional residual diffusion framework for multivariate time series imputation that decomposes the problem into baseline-residual: a pretrained model captures dominant signal while a diffusion process models residual uncertainty, with reliability-aware conditioning to adaptively control baseline influence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing diffusion-based imputation methods operate directly in original data space, requiring the denoising network to simultaneously capture global structure, temporal dynamics, and stochastic variability. This creates unnecessary complexity when modern deterministic imputers can already provide accurate initial reconstructions.

Method: RDDMPI proposes a baseline-residual decomposition approach: (1) A pretrained model captures the dominant signal as baseline; (2) A diffusion process models residual uncertainty in residual space; (3) The reverse denoising process is conditioned on both baseline-completed signal and its latent representation; (4) A reliability-aware conditioning mechanism adaptively controls the influence of baseline information during residual generation.

Result: Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that RDDMPI consistently improves both reconstruction accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

Conclusion: The residual diffusion formulation simplifies the diffusion learning objective by focusing on structured correction terms rather than reconstructing the full signal, achieving superior performance in both accuracy and uncertainty quantification for multivariate time series imputation.

Abstract: Multivariate time series imputation (MTSI) aims to recover missing values in temporal data composed of multiple interdependent variables. This problem is central to real-world applications such as healthcare monitoring, traffic networks, and energy systems. Recent diffusion-based approaches have shown strong potential for probabilistic imputation by learning to generate missing values through iterative denoising. However, most existing approaches perform diffusion directly in the original data space, requiring the denoising network to simultaneously capture global structure, temporal dynamics, and stochastic variability. This makes the generative task unnecessarily complex, especially when modern deterministic imputers can already provide accurate initial reconstructions. To address this limitation, we propose RDDMPI, a conditional residual diffusion framework that operates directly in residual space. Instead of modeling the full missing signal directly, we reformulate probabilistic imputation as a baseline-residual decomposition, where a pretrained model captures the dominant signal and a diffusion process models the residual uncertainty. To better exploit deterministic guidance, \model{} conditions the reverse denoising process on both the baseline-completed signal and its latent representation, while a reliability-aware conditioning mechanism adaptively controls the influence of baseline information during residual generation. This formulation simplifies the diffusion learning objective, enabling it to focus on structured correction terms rather than reconstructing the full signal. Experiments on multiple benchmark datasets demonstrate that RDDMPI consistently improves both reconstruction accuracy and uncertainty quantification.

</details>


### [144] [Musec: MomentUm SpEctral Clipping for Stable Muon-type Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11655)
*Zhuanghua Liu,Menglian Wang,Luo Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Musec is a new optimizer that stabilizes Muon training by clipping singular values that exceed a threshold instead of flattening all singular values to one. It provides architecture-agnostic stabilization with theoretical convergence guarantees and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Muon optimizer is effective for LLM training but suffers from training instability due to spectral flattening, causing loss spikes and unbounded weight growth. Existing solutions like weight/attention-logit clipping are architecture-specific and don't address instability across all components.

Method: Musec replaces spectral flattening with spectral clipping - clipping singular values exceeding a threshold while preserving the momentum's spectral structure. Soft Musec implements this using a smooth spectral saturation function approximated by coupled Newton-Schulz iterations.

Result: Soft Musec consistently improves training stability over existing Muon variants across various learning rates and model sizes. It remains stable in settings where other Muon variants diverge while matching their performance under well-tuned configurations.

Conclusion: Musec provides an effective optimizer-level solution for stabilizing Muon training. The approach is architecture-agnostic, theoretically grounded with convergence guarantees, and empirically validated across various settings. Soft Musec represents a practical implementation that balances stability with performance.

Abstract: Muon has emerged as a highly effective optimizer for large language model training, often achieving superior convergence and performance compared with the widely adopted Adam and AdamW optimizers. Nevertheless, Muon is prone to training instability due to its spectral flattening, manifested by loss spikes and unbounded growth of model weights. Existing approaches primarily rely on weight or attention-logit clipping, which require architecture-specific modifications and do not directly address instability across all model components. We propose MomentUm SpEctral Clipping (Musec), which replaces Muon's spectral flattening with spectral clipping: rather than setting all singular values of the momentum matrix to approximately one, Musec clips singular values that exceed a threshold while preserving the underlying spectral structure of the momentum. Our strategy provides an optimizer-level, architecture-agnostic mechanism for stabilizing Muon training. We further develop Soft Musec, an efficient implementation that uses a smooth spectral saturation function approximated by coupled Newton-Schulz iterations. Theoretically, we establish convergence guarantees for Musec in nonconvex nonsmooth stochastic optimization. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first convergence guarantee for Muon-type methods in the nonconvex nonsmooth setting. We provide empirical studies to show that Soft Musec consistently improves training stability over existing Muon variants across a wide range of learning rates and model sizes. Notably, Soft Musec remains stable in settings where existing Muon variants diverge, while matching their performance under well-tuned configurations.

</details>


### [145] [Learnware and AI Model Management System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11656)
*Zhi-Hua Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes the concept of "learnware" (Model + Specification) to enable AI model management systems that allow models trained by different developers for different tasks to be identified, reused, and assembled without accessing training data. The Learnware Dock System (LDS) is presented as an implementation of this vision.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The transition from file storage to database management systems transformed data management; AI needs an analogous shift from model storage to model management. Existing model pools only store models without enabling proper identification, reuse, or assembly. Additionally, developers won't share training data, and users won't share their raw data, creating a fundamental challenge - model functionality may not be fully understood even by developers themselves.

Method: The authors propose upgrading the basic unit of management from a machine learning model to a "learnware" (Model + Specification). The specification is generated through a machine learning process that preserves data privacy (theoretically established data-preservation property). Specifications are generated according to a published reference, making them comparable across models and enabling them to serve as a collaboration protocol.

Result: The paper presents the Learnware Dock System (LDS), which provides a path toward powerful AI model management systems. Specifications generated according to a published reference are comparable across models and can serve as an AI model collaboration protocol for independently developed models, including intelligent agents, to collaborate.

Conclusion: The learnware concept (Model + Specification) enables AI model management systems that preserve data privacy while allowing model identification, reuse, and assembly. The LDS represents a significant step toward transforming AI model storage into comprehensive AI model management, with specifications serving as a collaboration protocol for independent models and agents.

Abstract: The transition from file storage to database management systems transformed stored data into managed resources. AI now faces an analogous transition from AI model storage to AI model management. Existing model pools essentially serve as \textit{AI model storage systems}. What is needed instead are \textit{AI model management systems} that enable models trained by different developers, for different tasks, with different data, and under different objectives to be identified, reused, and even assembled to address future user tasks. Because AI model developers are generally unwilling to share their training data, such systems should operate without accessing the training data of model developers and, ideally, without accessing raw data of future users. This requirement poses a fundamental challenge: the functionality of a modern AI model may not be fully understood even by the developer who trained it. How, then, can a system identify which models are useful for a given user task, let alone assemble models developed independently for different purposes? At first glance, this objective may appear unattainable. It becomes possible, however, by upgrading the basic unit of management from a machine learning model to a \textit{learnware}. \textit{Learnware = Model + Specification}. The specification, whose assignment transforms a trained model into a learnware, is generated with the help of a machine learning process without disclosing the training data of the developer and has a theoretically established data-preservation property. The \textit{Learnware Dock System (LDS)} provides a path toward powerful AI model management systems. Because specifications are generated according to a published reference and are comparable across models, they can also serve as an AI model \textit{collaboration protocol} through which independently developed models, including intelligent agents, can collaborate.

</details>


### [146] [Why Does Post-Training Quantization Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11716)
*Yuxiang Chen,Michael Beyer,Jun Zhu,Jianfei Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Post-training quantization compresses LLMs by reducing weight precision. This paper investigates why quantization works despite introducing errors that should accumulate with depth. The authors identify two mechanisms: (1) error cancellation where each layer's quantization error tends to oppose the inherited error from previous layers, and (2) LM-head geometry that preserves high-ranked token predictions. These mechanisms explain how pretrained models maintain performance despite quantization noise accumulating through many layers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key question addressed is: why does post-training quantization work? The naive expectation is that quantization errors should accumulate with depth and corrupt predictions, but quantized pretrained models maintain performance despite never being trained with quantization noise. This phenomenon needed explanation.

Method: The authors compared full-precision and quantized forward passes, performing quantitative analysis to identify error propagation patterns. They analyzed LM-head geometry and token probability preservation across multiple models and quantization settings.

Result: Two mechanisms were identified: (1) Error counteraction - each layer's quantization error tends to oppose the error inherited from previous layers, causing partial cancellation and slowing hidden-state error growth. This property develops during pretraining. (2) LM-head geometry preferentially preserves scores and probabilities of high-ranked tokens (the model's most confident predictions).

Conclusion: These two mechanisms explain why quantization error passing through numerous layers still produces only small output changes. The findings were verified across different models and quantization settings, providing a principled understanding of post-training quantization robustness in pretrained language models.

Abstract: Post-training quantization compresses large language models (LLMs) by storing their weights at reduced precision, and each quantized weight introduces an error into the hidden states. Naively, these errors should accumulate with depth and corrupt next-token prediction; randomly initialized models accumulate these discrepancies rapidly, whereas quantized pretrained models accumulate much less hidden-state error and largely maintain downstream task performance, even though they were never trained with quantization noise. This raises the question we address: why does post-training quantization work? Comparing full-precision and quantized forward passes, we identify two mechanisms that characterize pretrained quantization robustness. First, the error a layer newly introduces tends to oppose the error it inherits from the layer's input. The two cancel partially such that the discrepancy between full-precision and quantized passes grows slowly. This counteracting residual interaction develops during pretraining. Our quantitative analysis identifies it as a major factor slowing hidden-error growth. Second, LM-head geometry preferentially preserves the scores and probabilities of high-ranked tokens, which typically represent the model's most confident predictions. Together, these mechanisms explain why quantization error that passes through numerous layers can still produce only small output changes, and we verify the findings across models and quantization settings.

</details>


### [147] [Predicting Privacy Leakage from Weight Spectral Density](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11780)
*Richard J. Preen,Jim Smith*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether inexpensive spectral metrics from the heavy-tailed self-regularisation framework can serve as proxies for membership inference attack (MIA) vulnerability, avoiding the need for computationally expensive shadow models. The study evaluates WeightWatcher spectral metrics on image and tabular classification tasks and finds that stable rank positively correlates with MIA success while Log alpha-Norm negatively correlates with MIA vulnerability at low false-positive regimes, with stronger associations than conventional generalisation measures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current state-of-the-art membership inference attacks require training computationally expensive shadow models, making large-scale privacy evaluation impractical. This creates a need for inexpensive methods to assess MIA vulnerability without the computational overhead of shadow model training.

Method: The researchers evaluated several WeightWatcher spectral metrics (stable rank and Log alpha-Norm) on image and tabular classification tasks. They compared the relationship between these spectral metrics and MIA privacy leakage against conventional measures of generalisation, using correlation analysis across multiple datasets.

Result: Stable rank exhibits a strong positive correlation with overall MIA success, while Log alpha-Norm shows a consistent negative correlation with MIA vulnerability at the low false-positive regime. These associations are observed to be stronger than those obtained using the generalisation gap, indicating spectral metrics capture privacy leakage information not fully captured by conventional overfitting measures.

Conclusion: Neural network spectra contain information about privacy leakage that is not fully captured by conventional measures of overfitting. Spectral analysis represents a promising direction for scalable privacy auditing, as it provides a computationally efficient alternative to shadow model-based MIA evaluation.

Abstract: Membership inference attacks (MIAs) are widely used to audit the privacy disclosure risk of machine learning models, however current state-of-the-art attacks require training computationally expensive shadow models, making large-scale privacy evaluation impractical. In this work, we investigate whether inexpensive spectral metrics derived from the heavy-tailed self-regularisation framework can serve as proxies for MIA vulnerability. We evaluate several WeightWatcher spectral metrics on image and tabular classification tasks and compare their relationship with MIA privacy leakage against conventional measures of generalisation. Across datasets, stable rank exhibits a strong positive correlation with overall MIA success, while Log alpha-Norm shows a consistent negative correlation with MIA vulnerability at the low false-positive regime. These associations are observed to be stronger than those obtained using the generalisation gap. The results indicate that neural network spectra may contain information about privacy leakage that is not fully captured by conventional measures of overfitting, motivating spectral analysis as a promising direction for scalable privacy auditing.

</details>


### [148] [Dynamic language model representations for multi-objective reaction optimisation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11790)
*Joshua W. Sin,David Ming Segura,Bojana Ranković,Siu Lun Chau,Marius D. R. Lutz,Andrea Anelli,Ryan P. Burwood,Kurt Püntener,Maximilian J. Notheis,Raphael Bigler,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces a text-based reaction representation learning approach for multi-objective Bayesian optimization of chemical reactions. By fine-tuning a language model on textual reaction conditions and combining it with Gaussian process surrogates, the method achieves faster optimization convergence than traditional approaches. The approach was validated prospectively on palladium-catalyzed cyanation (94% yield) and asymmetric hydrogenation (84% yield, 99.6% ee), demonstrating practical utility in high-throughput experimentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that existing reaction representations (one-hot encoding, molecular descriptors) are either chemically uninformative or cannot handle structurally and functionally diverse components. Creating appropriate representations requires extensive expert effort and must be revisited for each new reaction system. This creates a significant bottleneck in model-driven reaction optimization.

Method: The approach bypasses the need for hand-crafted representations by learning reaction representations dynamically from text. A fine-tuned language model encodes textual descriptions of reaction conditions, which are then used jointly with Gaussian process surrogates within a multi-objective Bayesian optimization framework. This yields task-adaptive representations that can handle diverse reaction components without manual feature engineering.

Result: The method was tested on nickel- and palladium-catalyzed cross-couplings in both sequential and parallel experimentation regimes, achieving optimization convergence faster than descriptor libraries or one-hot encoding. In prospective applications: (1) Pd-catalyzed cyanation with mixed ligand denticity and heterogeneous additives reached 94% isolated yield at gram scale after 2 rounds (192 reactions, <3% design space); (2) Three-objective asymmetric hydrogenation across chiral Ir and Ru catalyst families achieved 84% isolated yield and 99.6% enantiomeric excess under similar conditions.

Conclusion: Text-based reaction representation learning combined with multi-objective Bayesian optimization provides a powerful approach for chemical reaction optimization that eliminates the need for manual representation engineering. The method successfully handles diverse catalytic systems and multiple objectives, delivering high-yielding conditions that translate effectively from high-throughput screening to preparative scale.

Abstract: Optimising chemical reactions across multiple objectives, such as yield, selectivity, and safety, is central to chemical synthesis, and model-driven approaches depend critically on how reaction components are represented. Established featurisations are either chemically uninformative, as with one-hot encodings, or, as with molecular descriptors, do not readily extend across chemically distinct components. For structurally and functionally diverse components, it is therefore unclear what a shared representation should contain. Constructing such a representation is itself a challenging research undertaking that must be revisited for each new reaction system. Here we bypass this step by learning the reaction representation dynamically from text. Textual descriptions of reaction conditions are encoded by a fine-tuned language model trained jointly with Gaussian process surrogates, yielding task-adaptive representations within a multi-objective Bayesian optimisation loop. Across nickel- and palladium-catalysed cross-couplings in both sequential and parallel experimentation regimes, this approach reaches optimisation convergence in fewer experiments than descriptor libraries or one-hot encoding. Applied prospectively to a palladium-catalysed cyanation spanning mixed ligand denticity and heterogeneous additives, and to a three-objective asymmetric hydrogenation across chiral iridium and ruthenium catalyst families, two rounds of high-throughput experimentation (192 reactions, under 3% of each design space) delivered conditions translating directly to gram scale in 94% and 84% isolated yield, the latter at 99.6% enantiomeric excess.

</details>


### [149] [Thinking with Looped Flows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11801)
*Ayhan Suleymanzade,Chanhyuk Lee,Floor Eijkelboom,Nicholas M. Boffi,İsmail İlkan Ceylan,Jinwoo Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Looped flows is a novel approach that trains recurrent models using local denoising objectives with progressively decreasing noise levels, enabling effective computation transfer across time steps even with limited gradient coverage. Inference integrates probability flow velocity parameterized by the learned denoiser, allowing multiple valid predictions from different initial noise samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that looped models (recurrent models that iteratively update hidden states during inference) face a training-inference gap: while inference can involve many update steps, training only backpropagates through 1-2 updates. This makes it difficult to train early updates to support future ones, limiting the model's ability to leverage additional computation for harder problems.

Method: 1. Training: Use local denoising objectives with temporal association through progressively decreasing noise levels and shared noise. This incentivizes recurrent states to transfer useful computation over time.

2. Inference: Formulate as integrating velocity of probability flow parameterized by learned denoiser, coupled with recurrent states. This enables:
   - Solving harder problems by spending more computation (finer temporal grid)
   - Multiple valid predictions from different initial noise samples

Result: Outperforms prior state-of-the-art looped models across six reasoning benchmarks, achieving 58.8% test accuracy on ARC-AGI-1 and 12.2% on ARC-AGI-2. Also includes two multi-solution benchmarks.

Conclusion: Looped flows successfully bridges the training-inference gap in recurrent models by using denoising-based training objectives. The approach enables models to effectively leverage additional computation time and produces multiple valid solutions, demonstrating significant improvements on reasoning benchmarks.

Abstract: Humans and machines often solve harder problems by spending more time on computation. In deep learning, looped models implement this idea during inference by recurrently updating a hidden state. In practice, however, their training backpropagates through only one or a few updates, making it hard to train early updates to support future ones. We propose looped flows, an approach that sidesteps this issue by training the recurrence with local denoising objectives. By imposing temporal association across denoising objectives through progressively decreasing noise levels and shared noise, the model is incentivized to learn recurrent states that transfer useful computation over time, even when gradients cover only a few updates. We then formulate inference as integrating the velocity of a probability flow parameterized by the learned denoiser, coupled with recurrent states. This allows solving harder problems by spending more computation through a finer temporal grid and enables multiple valid predictions from different initial noise samples. Across six reasoning benchmarks including two multi-solution benchmarks, looped flows outperform prior state-of-the-art looped models overall, achieving 58.8% test accuracy on ARC-AGI-1 and 12.2% on ARC-AGI-2.

</details>


### [150] [Model-Aware Schedules Improve Generation via Fiberwise Optimal Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11842)
*Luyi Jia,Boyan Zhang,Yilun Liu,Steffen Rulands*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper introduces a model-aware schedule construction for diffusion and flow-matching models based on fiberwise optimal transport. By defining a fiberwise prediction risk and combining it with kinetic action on coefficient paths, the authors derive a closed-form optimal time allocation. This approach consistently outperforms model-agnostic baselines, achieving a 38.6% relative FID reduction on CIFAR-10 for flow matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current diffusion and flow-matching schedules are model-agnostic and ignore prediction error. The kinetic action approach, while motivated by optimal transport, doesn't account for model-specific prediction characteristics. This work aims to create model-aware schedules that adapt to the specific prediction risk profiles of trained models.

Method: The authors develop a fiberwise optimal transport framework: (1) At fixed time and state on probability paths, compatible signal/noise decompositions form an affine fiber; (2) Define fiberwise prediction risk by averaging optimal-transport costs between true and predictor-induced decompositions; (3) Combine this risk with coefficient-path kinetic action on a fixed coefficient curve to obtain closed-form optimal time allocation; (4) Extend to general linear prediction targets; (5) Estimate risk profile from early baseline checkpoint.

Result: Model-aware schedules consistently outperform strong baselines. Key results include: 38.6% relative FID reduction for flow matching on CIFAR-10 at 16 function evaluations; normalized fiberwise-risk profiles align across independently trained models in different settings; schedule deformations used in training also align, suggesting empirical universality; pretrained-checkpoint diagnostics extend normalized-risk agreement to larger conditional latent diffusion and 2-RF models; frozen analytic allocation template retains most improvements without further risk estimation.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that model-aware schedule construction based on fiberwise optimal transport significantly improves diffusion and flow-matching models. The empirically observed universality across models and settings, along with the effectiveness of a frozen analytic allocation template, suggests practical deployability without extensive model-specific fitting.

Abstract: Diffusion and flow-matching schedules control the signal and noise coefficients that mix data and noise along affine probability paths. Minimizing a kinetic action defined on coefficient paths, motivated by optimal transport, helps explain strong baselines but remains model-agnostic and ignores prediction error. Here we introduce a model-aware schedule construction based on fiberwise optimal transport. At a fixed time and state on the probability path, compatible signal/noise decompositions form an affine fiber. We define a fiberwise prediction risk by averaging optimal-transport costs between the true and predictor-induced decompositions within these fibers. On a fixed coefficient curve, combining this risk with coefficient-path kinetic action yields a closed-form optimal time allocation. This construction extends to general linear prediction targets, and the risk profile can be estimated from an early baseline checkpoint. We evaluate DDPMs and flow matching across prediction targets, training configurations, risk-estimation checkpoints, datasets, and architectures. Our model-aware schedules consistently outperform strong baselines, including a 38.6% relative FID reduction for flow matching on CIFAR-10 at 16 function evaluations. Each model-agnostic kinetic baseline determines its own kinetic reference coordinate. In these coordinates, fiberwise-risk profiles from independently trained models in different settings align closely after normalization to unit area. The resulting schedule deformations used in training also align, suggesting empirical universality across the evaluated models and settings. Pretrained-checkpoint diagnostics extend this normalized-risk agreement to larger conditional latent diffusion and 2-RF models. A frozen analytic allocation template retains most of the model-aware improvement without further risk estimation or model-specific fitting.

</details>


### [151] [AdamX: Cosine similarity meets gradient descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11867)
*Francisco Caldas,Ruben Belo,Cláudia Soares*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: AdamX is a first-order optimizer that uses cosine similarity as an adaptive mechanism to control update magnitudes, combined with a variance rectification scheme for smoother early-stage training. It is model-agnostic, scalable, and achieves competitive convergence rates across benchmark datasets and architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation appears to be improving first-order optimization by introducing an adaptive mechanism (cosine similarity) to better control update magnitudes, along with addressing early-stage training instability through variance rectification.

Method: The method involves two key components: 1) Using cosine similarity as an adaptive mechanism for controlling update magnitudes, and 2) A variance rectification scheme to promote smoother optimization during early training stages. The optimizer is designed to be scalable, model-agnostic, and easily integrable into existing training pipelines.

Result: AdamX achieves competitive convergence rates across a range of benchmark datasets and architectures, evaluated in terms of the number of epochs required to reach predefined performance thresholds under a fixed hyperparameter budget.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that AdamX is a viable first-order optimizer that can compete with existing methods, offering a straightforward approach to adaptive optimization with benefits in early-stage training stability.

Abstract: We introduce AdamX, a first-order optimizer that incorporates cosine similarity as an adaptive mechanism for controlling update magnitudes. The proposed method is scalable, model-agnostic, and straightforward to integrate into existing training pipelines. We further introduce a variance rectification scheme that promotes smoother optimization during the early stages of training. Overall, we provide empirical evidence that AdamX achieves competitive convergence rates across a range of benchmark datasets and architectures. Performance is evaluated in terms of the number of epochs required to reach predefined performance thresholds under a fixed hyperparameter budget. Code and Experiments available at: https://github.com/FranciscoCaldas/adamX.

</details>


### [152] [The Last AI Built by Humans: Toward Genuine Recursive Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11873)
*Yi Duan,Ying Liu,Zirui Tang,Haodong Chen,Jun Zhou,Yumou Liu,Bangrui Xu,Yukai Wu,Sidi Chen,Yuhan Zhou,Haoyu Wang,Xiaoyou Yu,Shaokun Han,Xuzhou Zhu,Le Zhou,Bolin Lu,Wei Zhou,Jiachen Liu,Nuozhou Fang,Jiaxin Tian,Ruoyu Chen,Yuxuan Li,Kai Zuo,Kaiyan Zhang,Jiantao Qiu,Conghui He,Guoliang Li,Bowen Zhou,Zhiyuan Liu,Zhoufutu Wen,Jihua Kang,Xuanhe Zhou,Fan Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) for AI systems, using the Headroom-Closed Index (HCI) to identify limitations in existing LLMs. It presents a development roadmap for RSI progressing through five autonomy levels: improvement-execution, improvement-strategy, experience-acquisition, environment-adaptation, and recursive meta-improvement. The work examines RSI across application scenarios including scientific discovery, embodied intelligence, and software engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLMs have fundamental limitations that need to be addressed. The authors aim to enable AI systems to autonomously improve their capabilities and improvement processes through experience and feedback, moving beyond static model architectures.

Method: The paper employs the Headroom-Closed Index (HCI) to diagnose problems in existing LLMs, proposes a conceptual framework for RSI with a multi-stage development roadmap, and analyzes RSI requirements across different application domains.

Result: The paper maps RSI development across scenarios (scientific discovery, embodied intelligence, software engineering), identifies distinct requirements and development speeds for each, and connects theoretical RSI research with practical system implementations.

Conclusion: Key challenges to achieving genuine recursive self-improvement in AI systems are identified, though the specific challenges are not detailed in this abstract.

Abstract: Recursive self-improvement (RSI) enables AI systems to turn experience and feedback into persistent changes that improve both their capabilities and the process of future improvement. We first use the Headroom-Closed Index (HCI) to reveal the problems of existing LLMs, then introduce the RSI concept and its development roadmap: from improvement-execution autonomy, improvement-strategy autonomy, experience-acquisition autonomy, and environment-adaptation autonomy, to recursive meta-improvement. Next we examine RSI across scenarios (e.g., scientific discovery, embodied intelligence, software engineering), highlighting their distinct requirements and development speeds. Drawing on diverse industry practices and preliminary empirical evidence, we connect RSI research with practical systems and identify key challenges to achieving genuine RSI.

</details>


### [153] [CoRA-NAS: Coarse Ranking and Anchor-Residual Refinement for Neural Architecture Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11884)
*Yifan Yang,Zhaoyan Wang,Zheng Gao,Xiaoyu Li,Jiaojiao Jiang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CoRA-NAS is a two-stage framework for neural architecture search that combines static ranking priors with low-cost learning-curve refinement. It uses CoRA-Rank to aggregate proxies through log-rank consensus and consensus gate, then CoRA-Refine to extrapolate early validation curves and learn residual corrections with ExtraTrees. The method achieves high Spearman correlations (0.946 on NAS-Bench-201, 0.715 on NAS-Bench-101, 0.786 on TransNAS-Bench-101, 0.894 on NATS-SSS) using only ~1% of full training cost, and selects architectures reaching 73.32% accuracy on NAS-Bench-201/CIFAR-100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-cost proxies for ranking neural architectures are unreliable across different search spaces. Existing methods lack robustness and consistency when applied to various NAS benchmarks. The authors aim to develop a framework that maintains high ranking correlation across different search spaces while using minimal computational cost.

Method: The proposed CoRA-NAS has two stages: (1) CoRA-Rank aggregates capacity and structure-at-initialization proxies through equal-weight log-rank consensus and a target-free consensus gate; (2) CoRA-Refine samples anchors across the prior, extrapolates early validation curves, and propagates learned residual correction using an ExtraTrees model. The refinement uses ~1% of full training cost. Fully trained accuracy labels are not used for fitting the ranker, with one configuration used across spaces but space-specific architecture encodings.

Result: CoRA-Refine achieves mean Spearman correlations of 0.946, 0.715, 0.786, and 0.894 on NAS-Bench-201, NAS-Bench-101, TransNAS-Bench-101, and NATS-SSS respectively. The worst-space correlation of 0.715 is the highest among compared methods. On NAS-Bench-201/CIFAR-100, the selected architecture achieves 73.32% accuracy, very close to the ground-truth best of 73.37%. On pure size space, refinement recovers the static prior's shortfall relative to parameter count.

Conclusion: CoRA-NAS successfully combines cross-space ranking robustness with low-cost architecture selection. The two-stage approach using coarse ranking followed by anchor-based refinement achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple NAS benchmarks, demonstrating consistent high correlation without requiring full training labels. The method maintains robustness across different search spaces while using minimal computational resources.

Abstract: Zero-cost proxies rank architectures cheaply, but their reliability varies across search spaces. We introduce CoRA-NAS (COarse Ranking + Anchor-residual), a two-stage framework combining a static ranking prior with low-cost learning-curve refinement. CoRA-Rank aggregates capacity and structure-at-initialization proxies through an equal-weight log-rank consensus and a target-free consensus gate. CoRA-Refine samples anchors across this prior, extrapolates their early validation curves, and propagates a learned residual correction with an ExtraTrees model. The refinement uses approximately 1% of the cost of fully training the candidate set. Fully trained architecture-accuracy labels are not used to fit the ranker. One configuration is used across spaces, with space-specific architecture encodings. Across NAS-Bench-201, NAS-Bench-101, TransNAS-Bench-101, and NATS-SSS, CoRA-Refine achieves mean Spearman correlations of 0.946, 0.715, 0.786, and 0.894, respectively. Its worst-space correlation of 0.715 is the highest among the compared methods. On NAS-Bench-201/CIFAR-100, its selected architecture reaches 73.32% accuracy, near the reported ground-truth best of 73.37%. On the pure size space, refinement recovers the static prior's shortfall relative to parameter count, while remaining tied with the strongest capacity proxies within noise. The resulting framework combines cross-space ranking robustness with low-cost architecture selection.

</details>


### [154] [CausalArena: Benchmarking Causal Discovery in the Foundation Model Era](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11897)
*Zi-Rong Li,Si-Yang Liu,Tian-Zuo Wang,Han-Jia Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CausalArena is a unified benchmark for evaluating causal discovery methods, addressing inconsistencies in existing evaluations by providing controlled synthetic SCMs, semantic operational SCMs, formula-grounded SCMs, and real-world datasets. Experiments reveal that performance rankings shift across different benchmark regimes, highlighting the importance of benchmark diversity and pretraining-evaluation overlap in the foundation model era.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing causal discovery evaluations rely on diverse structural causal models (SCMs) with varying graph families, mechanisms, and protocols, making comparisons difficult. The emergence of causal discovery foundation models (CDFMs) further complicates evaluation since performance may reflect pretraining-test overlap rather than true causal discovery ability. This creates interpretability challenges when evaluating on fixed synthetic benchmarks.

Method: The paper introduces CausalArena, a unified and evolvable benchmark with four components: (1) Synthetic SCMs providing controlled breadth over structures and mechanisms; (2) Semantic operational SCMs offering human-auditable, semantically grounded environments beyond standard synthetic generators; (3) Formula-grounded SCMs testing discovery under explicit scientific mechanisms; (4) Public real-world datasets for external validity. Experiments evaluate classical, neural, and pretrained causal discovery methods across these components.

Result: Experiments reveal substantial ranking shifts across SCM families and benchmark protocols. Strong performance in one benchmark regime does not reliably transfer to others, demonstrating that method rankings are highly dependent on the evaluation setting.

Conclusion: Benchmark diversity and pretraining-evaluation overlap are central challenges for evaluating causal discovery in the foundation model era. The community needs varied and representative benchmarks to properly assess causal discovery methods, especially as CDFMs become more prevalent.

Abstract: Causal discovery aims to uncover causal structures from data and is fundamental to scientific reasoning and intervention-based decision making. Its evaluation relies heavily on structural causal models (SCMs), which specify a causal graph together with the mechanisms that generate data, yet existing studies differ substantially in graph families, mechanisms, and evaluation protocols. The emergence of causal discovery foundation models (CDFMs) further complicates evaluation: performance may reflect not only causal discovery ability, but also overlap between pretraining environments and test SCMs, making results on fixed synthetic benchmarks difficult to interpret. We introduce CausalArena, a unified and evolvable benchmark for causal discovery under a common protocol. Synthetic SCMs supply controlled breadth over structures and mechanisms; semantic operational SCMs provide human-auditable, semantically grounded environments beyond standard synthetic generators; and formula-grounded SCMs test discovery under explicit scientific mechanisms. Public real-world datasets provide an additional external-validity check. Experiments across classical, neural, and pretrained methods reveal substantial ranking shifts across SCM families and protocols, showing that strong performance in one benchmark regime does not reliably transfer to others. These results highlight benchmark diversity and pretraining--evaluation overlap as central challenges for evaluating causal discovery in the foundation model era.

</details>


### [155] [TART: A Modular Tool for Technique-Aware Audio-to-Tablature Guitar Transcription](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11904)
*Akshaj Gupta,Hwi Joo Park,Andrea Guzman,Shamak Gowda,Samhita Konduri,Jiachen Lian,Robin Netzorg,Gopala Anumanchipalli*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TART is a four-stage audio-to-tablature pipeline for automatic music transcription (AMT) on guitar, designed to capture expressive techniques, assign correct string-fret combinations, and generalize to noisy real-world audio. It consists of audio-to-MIDI transcription, expressive technique classification, audio-conditioned T5 encoder-decoder for string-fret assignment, and automated tablature generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing AMT systems for guitar fail in three key areas: (1) inability to capture expressive techniques (slides, bends, percussive hits), (2) incorrect string-fret combination assignments, and (3) poor generalization from clean training data to noisy real-world audio. These limitations hinder practical music transcription applications.

Method: TART uses a modular four-stage pipeline: (1) audio-to-MIDI transcription model, (2) expressive technique classifier, (3) audio-conditioned T5 encoder-decoder for string-fret assignment, and (4) automated tablature generator. The system is evaluated in a zero-shot setting on GuitarSet, EGDB, and two augmented benchmarks (Noisy GuitarSet and Noisy EGDB).

Result: TART achieves 81.35% audio-to-MIDI F50 (+6.67 points over best prior baseline), 71.8% string-fret Tab F1 (+8.5 points over best prior baseline), and 54.08% end-to-end Tab F1, averaged across four benchmarks.

Conclusion: TART is the first framework to generate guitar tablature with both fingering and expressive technique annotations directly from guitar audio, representing a significant advancement in guitar transcription technology.

Abstract: Automatic Music Transcription (AMT) for guitar remains limited by three challenges: existing systems often fail to capture expressive techniques such as slides, bends, and percussive hits; they often assign notes to incorrect string-fret combinations; and they are typically trained on clean recordings, limiting their generalization to noisy real-world audio. To address these challenges, we propose TART, a modular four-stage audio-to-tablature pipeline consisting of (1) an audio-to-MIDI transcription model, (2) an expressive technique classifier, (3) an audio-conditioned T5 encoder-decoder for string-fret assignment, and (4) an automated tablature generator. We evaluate TART in a zero-shot setting on GuitarSet, EGDB, and two augmented benchmarks, Noisy GuitarSet and Noisy EGDB. Averaged across these four benchmarks, TART achieves 81.35% audio-to-MIDI F50 (+6.67 points over the best prior baseline), 71.8% string-fret Tab F1 (+8.5 points over the best prior baseline), and 54.08% end-to-end Tab F1. To our knowledge, TART is the first framework to generate guitar tablature with both fingering and expressive technique annotations directly from guitar audio.

</details>


### [156] [From Protocols to Evidence: Bounded Claims for AI in Service of the Common Good](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11910)
*Nitesh V. Chawla,Paulo Benanti*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper argues that AI deployment is not merely a governance challenge but an intervention that reveals and can either repair or compound existing institutional failures. The authors propose a "rupture test" framework that links institutional baselines to AI system evaluation, distinguishing between evidence-bounded deployment and measurement-bounded governance. They draw on Pope Leo XIV's Magnifica Humanitas as a moral framework and present RISE AI as an architecture for bounded, evidence-based claims about Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to address the gap between AI governance principles and practical implementation. They argue that existing frameworks (EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001) have translated principles into protocols but fail to examine what these protocols actually establish, whose power remains unchallenged, and where measurement must stop. The motivation is to develop a more comprehensive approach that evaluates both the AI system and the institutional context into which it is deployed.

Method: The paper develops a theoretical framework combining moral philosophy (drawing on Magnifica Humanitas), institutional analysis, and governance assessment. The authors propose the "rupture test" methodology that links institutional baselines to system evaluation, and introduce RISE AI as an architecture for making bounded, evidence-based claims about AI systems across four dimensions: Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment.

Result: The paper establishes a distinction between "evidence-bounded deployment" (limiting claims to what has actually been evaluated) and "measurement-bounded governance" (recording constraints that favorable evidence cannot override). Within these bounds, RISE AI provides an architecture for responsible AI claims. The key insight is that responsible AI requires not just better engineering, but also institutional repair and continued moral and political judgment.

Conclusion: Responsible AI governance cannot rely solely on technical protocols and assurance practices. It must evaluate both the AI system and the institutional "rupture" into which it is introduced. The authors conclude that responsible AI requires three elements: better engineering, institutional repair, and continued moral and political judgment - all within clear evidentiary and measurement boundaries.

Abstract: Artificial Intelligence does more than create a governance problem. It can also reveal where institutions have already failed to provide responsiveness, belonging, care, and accountability. Once deployed, AI becomes an intervention in those conditions. It can repair, compound, substitute for, or conceal the failures it encounters. Responsible AI must therefore evaluate both the system and the institutional rupture into which it is introduced. The move from principles to protocols is already underway. The EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF, ISO/IEC 42001, and assurance practices translate commitments into roles, requirements, records, oversight, and assessment. The harder questions are what these protocols actually establish, whose power they leave untouched, and where measurement must stop. Pope Leo XIV's Magnifica Humanitas provides a broader moral frame centered on dignity, technological power, and the common good. Drawing on that frame, we develop a rupture test that links institutional baselines to system evaluation. We distinguish evidence-bounded deployment, which limits claims to what has actually been evaluated, from measurement-bounded governance, which records constraints that favorable evidence cannot override. Within those limits, RISE AI provides an architecture for making bounded, evidence-based claims about Responsibility, Inclusivity, Safety, and Empowerment. Responsible AI requires better engineering, institutional repair, and continued moral and political judgment.

</details>


### [157] [Data Scarcity and Model Sparsity: Mixtures-of-Experts Overfit More to Repeated Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11917)
*Atindra Jha,Margaret Li,Jure Leskovec,Percy Liang,Luke Zettlemoyer*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates the effects of data repetition on Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models compared to dense Transformers. The authors find that MoEs degrade more rapidly under data repetition than dense models - while dense models can handle 8x repetition with minimal degradation, MoEs begin suffering at 4x and underperform after 32x. The effect scales with sparsity (total parameters). Some regularization methods like dropout and masking-based regularization can mitigate this overfitting, but no method fully matches all-unique training performance. The study also identifies that MoE routing stabilizes early in training and expert specialization correlates with overfitting to repeated data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to understand how data repetition (a common practice as human-written text becomes exhausted) affects sparse architectures like MoE, which have become dominant due to their compute efficiency. Prior work studied data repetition for dense Transformers, but the effects on MoE remained unexplored despite their increased adoption.

Method: The authors varied data repetition rates across single- and multi-domain data mixes, and across different MoE settings (expert count and granularity). They tested models ranging from 80M to 1B active parameters (8.5B total). They experimented with existing regularization methods including dropout and masking-based regularization as potential remedies.

Result: Key findings: (1) MoEs degrade more rapidly under data repetition than dense models, with effect increasing with sparsity dictated by total rather than active parameters; (2) 80M dense models can repeat data over 8x with minimal degradation while MoEs suffer at 4x and deteriorate rapidly; (3) After 32x repetition, MoEs underperform dense models in all-unique data settings; (4) Strong masking-based regularization can enable MoEs to outperform dense models even at 64x repetition, but no method fully matches all-unique training data performance; (5) MoE routing universally stabilizes early in training and expert specialization correlates with overfitting to repeated data.

Conclusion: The paper addresses the adverse interactions between sparsity and data repetition. It presents evidence for the core mechanisms of overfitting (early routing stabilization and expert over-specialization) and potential remediation through regularization. The authors suggest future methods should focus on disrupting memorization patterns to reduce over-specialization in model parameters. The work highlights an important limitation of MoE architectures in data-constrained environments.

Abstract: As the supply of human-written text is exhausted, it has become standard practice to repeat language model training data. Prior work has studied data repetition for densely activated Transformers, but the effects of data repetition remains largely unexplored for recently dominant sparse architectures such as Mixture-of-Experts (MoE), despite their increased compute efficiency. We vary data repetition rates across single- and multi-domain data mixes, and across MoE settings, including expert count and granularity. We consistently find, for models ranging from 80M to 1B active (8.5B total) parameters, that MoEs degrade more rapidly under data repetition. This effect increases with sparsity, dictated by total rather than active parameters. While 80M dense models can repeat data over 8x with minimal degradation, MoEs instead begin to suffer at 4x, and deteriorate rapidly, ceding their performance benefits in all-unique data settings to underperform dense models after 32x. We experiment with existing regularization methods as a potential remedy. We find that some methods, such as dropout, can mitigate overfitting. In particular, with strong masking-based regularization, MoEs are able to outperform dense models even when data is repeated more than 64 times. However, no method fully matches the performance of all-unique training data. Finally, we analyze internal mechanisms correlated with MoE overfitting in high repetition regimes, and find that MoE routing universally stabilizes early in training, and that expert specialization correlates with overfitting to repeated data. In sum, our work addresses the adverse interactions between sparsity and data repetition: we present evidence for the core mechanisms of overfitting and its potential remediation, and suggest promising avenues for future methods to reduce over-specialization in model parameters by disrupting memorization patterns.

</details>


### [158] [General Quantification of Covariate and Concept Shifts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11918)
*Hongbo Chen,Li Charlie Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses the limitation of existing learning bound theory for generalization under distribution shift, which is non-estimable from samples. The authors propose γ*-concept shifts using entropic optimal transport, derive a general error bound unifying covariate and concept shifts, and develop the DataShifts algorithm with concentration guarantees for practical estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing learning bound theory for distribution shift is limited to narrow, idealized settings and cannot be estimated from samples. The current definition of concept shift breaks when source and target supports mismatch, creating a gap between theory and practical applications.

Method: The authors leverage entropic optimal transport to propose γ*-concept shifts, which generalizes the existing concept shift definition. They derive a general error bound that unifies covariate and γ*-concept shifts, applicable to broad loss functions, label spaces, and stochastic labeling. They develop estimators for these shifts with concentration guarantees and create the DataShifts algorithm.

Result: A general error bound that unifies covariate and γ*-concept shifts, applicable to broad settings including various loss functions, label spaces, and stochastic labeling. The DataShifts algorithm can quantify distribution shifts and estimate error bounds in most applications.

Conclusion: This work bridges the gap between theory and practical applications by providing a rigorous and general framework (γ*-concept shifts and DataShifts algorithm) for analyzing learning error under distribution shift, with theoretical guarantees and practical estimators.

Abstract: Generalization under distribution shift remains a core challenge in modern machine learning, yet existing learning bound theory is limited to narrow, idealized settings and is non-estimable from samples. In this paper, we bridge the gap between theory and practical applications. We first show that existing definition of concept shift breaks when the source and target supports mismatch. Leveraging entropic optimal transport, we propose a key notion: $γ^{*}\!$-concept shifts, and derive a general error bound unifying covariate and $γ^{*}\!$-concept shifts, which applies to broad loss functions, label spaces, and stochastic labeling. We further develop estimators for these shifts with concentration guarantees, and the DataShifts algorithm, which can quantify distribution shifts and estimate the error bound in most applications - a rigorous and general tool for analyzing learning error under distribution shift.

</details>

### [159] [Multi-Faceted Evaluation and Mitigation of Emotion Hallucinations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11154)
*Bowen Zeng,Peipei Song,Weidong Chen,Shengeng Tang,Song Ye,Yuanhong Zhong,Beier Zhu,Xun Yang*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper addresses emotion hallucinations in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) by proposing EHR, an evaluator measuring hallucinations across six cognitive facets (expression, action, audio, instinct, logic, conclusion), and HMER, a training-free mitigation framework using dual-memory mechanisms for targeted hallucination suppression while preserving reliable reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs show strong potential in open-ended emotion understanding but frequently generate emotion hallucinations. Existing evaluation methods are insufficient because: 1) emotion understanding spans multiple cognitive facets from multimodal perception to psychological reasoning, and 2) emotional interpretations are expressed in free-form language, making closed-ended evaluation protocols inadequate.

Method: The paper proposes two contributions: (1) EHR (Emotion Hallucination Rate) - an evaluator quantifying emotion hallucinations across six facets: expression, action, audio, instinct, logic, and conclusion; (2) HMER (Hallucination-aware Memory-guided Emotion Reasoning) - a training-free mitigation framework with two memory components: Hallucination Memory (records localized hallucinated claims for targeted logit rectification) and Anchor Memory (preserves reliable intermediate reasoning states to stabilize generation). The method selectively suppresses unreliable cues while preserving trustworthy reasoning context.

Result: Experiments on 19 MLLMs reveal: (1) emotion hallucinations are prevalent across diverse models; (2) existing mitigation methods often reduce hallucinations in some facets while aggravating them in others, exposing the limitation of coarse-grained correction; (3) HMER demonstrates effective fine-grained mitigation across diverse hallucination facets and model architectures.

Conclusion: The study reveals the need for facet-aware hallucination localization and mitigation in MLLMs. Coarse-grained correction approaches are insufficient because they may reduce hallucinations in one facet while exacerbating them in another. The proposed HMER framework, with its dual-memory mechanism enabling targeted logit rectification and reasoning stabilization, provides an effective training-free solution for fine-grained emotion hallucination mitigation across diverse model architectures.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential in open-ended emotion understanding, yet they often generate emotion hallucinations. Evaluating such hallucinations is particularly challenging for two reasons. First, emotion understanding spans multiple cognitive facets, from multimodal perception to psychological reasoning. Second, emotional interpretations are expressed in free-form language, making existing closed-ended protocols insufficient for evaluation. To address these challenges, we introduce EHR (Emotion Hallucination Rate), an evaluator that quantifies emotion hallucinations across six facets: expression, action, audio, instinct, logic, and conclusion. Using EHR, we reveal that existing mitigation methods often reduce hallucinations in some facets while aggravating them in others, exposing the limitation of coarse-grained correction and the need for facet-aware localization and mitigation. Motivated by this finding, we propose HMER (Hallucination-aware Memory-guided Emotion Reasoning), a training-free framework for emotion hallucination mitigation. HMER maintains a Hallucination Memory that records localized hallucinated claims and enables targeted logit rectification, together with an Anchor Memory that preserves reliable intermediate reasoning states to stabilize subsequent generation. By selectively suppressing unreliable cues while preserving trustworthy reasoning context, HMER enables fine-grained mitigation across diverse hallucination facets. Extensive experiments on 19 MLLMs demonstrate the prevalence of emotion hallucinations and the effectiveness of our framework across diverse model architectures.

</details>


### [160] [Multimodal Temporal Modeling for Continuous Group Emotion Recognition in Multi-party Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11164)
*Soma Iwata,Koji Inoue,Muyun Wu,Taiga Mori,Divesh Lala,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper addresses continuous Group Emotion Recognition (GER) in multi-party dialogues at one-second resolution, introducing a "Mixed state" to capture emotional divergence among participants. The authors propose a multimodal temporal framework using sliding-window context and demonstrate that their temporal Transformer outperforms LLM-based models and unimodal baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on group emotion recognition addressed the task at the utterance level or using coarse-grained time windows, which is insufficient to capture emotional dynamics in real-time multi-party dialogue scenarios. Understanding group-level valence and arousal as a continuous process is crucial for creating natural dialogue agents.

Method: The authors constructed a dataset with frame-level soft labels based on the TEIDAN corpus and proposed a multimodal temporal framework integrating audio and video information using a sliding-window context. They evaluated a temporal Transformer model against LLM-based baselines and unimodal inputs (audio-only, video-only).

Result: The temporal Transformer outperforms simple baselines and shows stronger temporal agreement with ground-truth labels than the LLM-based model. Context length has limited effect, while audio-visual input outperforms either unimodal input on continuous-label metrics. Recognition errors are larger in intervals with high Mixed values, indicating emotional divergence is a key challenge.

Conclusion: Continuous group emotion recognition at fine temporal resolution is feasible and valuable. The "Mixed state" concept successfully captures emotional divergence, which remains a significant challenge. Multimodal (audio-visual) input is essential for accurate continuous GER, and temporal Transformers are more suitable than LLM-based approaches for this task.

Abstract: To realize natural behavior in dialogue agents in multi-party dialogue scenarios, it is important to understand group emotion such as valence and arousal as a whole. Most prior work addressed this task at the utterance level or using a coarse-grained time window, which is not sufficient to capture emotional dynamics. In this study, we formulate continuous recognition of the Group Emotion at a one-second resolution. Moreover, we also introduce the Mixed state, which captures the emotional divergence among participants in the group. We constructed a dataset with frame-level soft labels based on the TEIDAN corpus and propose a multimodal temporal framework that integrates audio and video information using a sliding-window context. Experimental results demonstrate that the temporal Transformer outperforms simple baselines and shows stronger temporal agreement with the ground-truth labels than the LLM-based model. The effect of context length is limited, whereas audio-visual input outperforms either unimodal input on the continuous-label metrics. Additionally, our analysis shows larger Group Emotion recognition errors in intervals with high Mixed values, exposing emotional divergence as a key challenge for group emotion recognition.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [161] [Probabilistic Focal Search: Accelerating Bounded-Suboptimal Search via Lower-Bound Advancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10584)
*Minh Vu Duc,Trung Le Huu,Hà Minh Hoàng,Trung Thanh Nguyen,Phuong Khanh Nguyen,Huynh Thi Thanh Binh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Probabilistic Focal Search (PFS), a variant of Focal Search that combines heuristic guidance with probabilistic expansion of minimum-f nodes to overcome f_min plateaus that delay FOCAL admission. The approach also includes a transfer to Dynamic Potential Search (PDPS) and an anytime extension (APFS). Experiments on N-Puzzle, Pancake Sorting, TSP, and GCTSP show up to 90% reduction in node expansions when FOCAL admission is the bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Focal Search (FS) uses heuristic guidance within FOCAL (frontier nodes eligible under threshold w*f_min), but its deterministic policy may leave f_min unchanged for many expansions, causing delayed FOCAL admission that limits search progress. The authors seek a mechanism to advance the lower bound and enlarge FOCAL more efficiently.

Method: PFS modifies FS by introducing a probabilistic scheduler: with probability p, it follows the original FS guided choice; with probability 1-p, it expands a minimum-f OPEN node. This hybrid approach balances heuristic guidance with lower-bound advancement. The same mechanism is transferred to Dynamic Potential Search (yielding PDPS), and an anytime extension APFS is developed.

Result: Across benchmarks (N-Puzzle, Pancake Sorting, TSP, GCTSP), PFS achieves largest gains when long f_min plateaus delay useful FOCAL admissions—in such settings, node expansions can be reduced by about 90% or more. The benefit is smaller when deterministic search already advances efficiently (e.g., Pancake Sorting). For the anytime family, APFS outperforms all tested algorithms on GCTSP. The PDPS transfer shows the mechanism transfers to potential guidance, though effectiveness remains domain- and bound-dependent.

Conclusion: The probabilistic factor is most useful when FOCAL admission is a search bottleneck. The approach successfully reduces search effort in such scenarios while maintaining bounded-suboptimality. The mechanism transfers to other search algorithms (like Dynamic Potential Search) and enables effective anytime behavior, with APFS demonstrating superior performance on generalized covering problems.

Abstract: Bounded-suboptimal search seeks a solution within a factor $w$ of optimal while reducing search effort. Focal Search (FS) uses heuristic guidance within FOCAL, the frontier nodes eligible under the threshold $w f_{\min}$, but its deterministic policy may leave $f_{\min}$ unchanged for many expansions. We introduce Probabilistic Focal Search (PFS), which follows the FS guided choice with probability $p$ and expands a minimum-$f$ OPEN node with probability $1-p$. The latter branch encourages the lower bound to advance, enlarging FOCAL and admitting nodes that may lead to feasible solutions. By balancing guidance and lower-bound advancement, this mechanism can reduce time to a bounded solution when progress is limited by delayed FOCAL admission. As a secondary transfer experiment, we apply the same scheduler to Dynamic Potential Search, yielding Probabilistic Dynamic Potential Search (PDPS). We benchmark PFS against FS on N-Puzzle, Pancake Sorting, and the Traveling Salesperson Problem (TSP), and evaluate its anytime extension on the Generalized Covering TSP (GCTSP), using multiple $w$ and $p$ values. Across these benchmarks, the largest gains occur when long $f_{\min}$ plateaus delay useful FOCAL admissions; in such settings, the probabilistic factor may reduce node expansions by about 90\% or more (e.g., on N-Puzzle and TSP). For the anytime algorithm family, Anytime Probabilistic Focal Search (APFS) outperforms all tested algorithms in evaluating anytime methods on GCTSP. We also observe that the benefit is smaller when the deterministic search already advances efficiently (e.g., Pancake Sorting), indicating that the probabilistic factor is most useful when FOCAL admission is a search bottleneck. The PDPS transfer shows that the mechanism also transfers to potential guidance, although its common-success effects remain domain- and bound-dependent.

</details>


### [162] [Automating Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) Formulation Generation from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10629)
*Niloy Kumar Mondal,Md Rizwan Parvez*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a multi-agent framework that automatically generates QUBO (Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization) formulations from natural-language problem descriptions, along with a benchmark (QUBOBench) containing 100 problems across 12 domains. The framework achieves 68% accuracy, outperforming baseline by 22%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Translating natural-language problem descriptions into correct QUBO formulations is difficult, time-consuming, and requires substantial domain expertise. The process involves identifying binary variables, constraints, objective functions, penalty terms, and suitable penalty weights.

Method: End-to-end multi-agent framework for automatic QUBO generation from natural-language problem descriptions, supported by structured or unstructured test cases. The framework includes iterative self-repair as a key component.

Result: The framework achieves 68% accuracy on QUBOBench (100 problems across 12 application domains), outperforming a direct single-call baseline by 22%. Iterative self-repair was identified as the most important component.

Conclusion: The proposed multi-agent framework successfully automates QUBO formulation generation, with iterative self-repair being the critical contributor to performance. The benchmark and resources are open-sourced for further research.

Abstract: Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) is a central formulation for combinatorial optimization and has gained increasing attention due to its compatibility with quantum, hybrid quantum-classical, and quantum-inspired solvers. However, translating natural-language problem descriptions into correct QUBO formulations remains difficult, requiring the identification of binary variables, constraints, objective functions, penalty terms, and suitable penalty weights. This process is time-consuming and often demands substantial domain expertise. To address this challenge, we propose an end-to-end multi-agent framework that automatically generates QUBO formulations from natural-language problem descriptions, supported by structured or unstructured test cases. To evaluate its performance, We also introduce QUBOBench, a benchmark containing 100 combinatorial optimization problems across 12 application domains, curated from peer-reviewed literature, competitions, and canonical NP-hard problems. Experimental results show that our framework achieves 68% accuracy on QUBOBench, outperforming a direct single-call baseline by 22%. Further analysis identifies iterative self-repair as the most important component contributing to improved performance. The data and code are open-sourced at https://quitttcat.github.io/QuantumQUBOAgent.

</details>


### [163] [A Multi-Stage Rule-Chaining Framework for Compositional and Interpretable Cognitive Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10654)
*Deblina Kar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a multi-stage rule-chaining framework for solving ARC (Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus) tasks, integrating three complementary solvers: deterministic rule discovery, pattern-composition engine, and structural abstraction layer. The system achieves over 95% accuracy, solving 230/240 ARC-AGI-2 test tasks, demonstrating that rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ARC benchmark is designed to test cognitive generalization - the ability to infer and apply abstract rules from limited examples. Current approaches struggle with compositional reasoning across symbolic, structural, and conceptual levels. This work aims to bridge symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis to achieve interpretable cognitive generalization without task-specific tuning.

Method: The proposed framework employs a three-stage progressive fallback hierarchy: (1) A deterministic rule discovery module that induces atomic transformations through geometric, color, and object-based analysis; (2) A pattern-composition engine that reconstructs outputs via block merging, repetition, and spatial heuristics; (3) A structural abstraction layer that infers hierarchical and nested relationships across grids. Each stage reuses prior reasoning traces to enhance interpretability and generalization.

Result: The system achieved: 995/1000 tasks trained successfully, 105/120 tasks evaluated, and 230/240 ARC-AGI-2 test tasks solved. Overall accuracy exceeded 95% with strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, and abstract categories.

Conclusion: The multi-stage rule-chaining framework demonstrates that rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction without relying on task-specific tuning. The architecture successfully bridges symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis, providing interpretable insight into cognitive generalization.

Abstract: The Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC) benchmarks cognitive generalization, the ability to infer and apply abstract rules from limited examples. This paper presents a multi-stage rule-chaining framework that performs compositional reasoning across symbolic, structural, and conceptual levels. The framework integrates three complementary solvers:
  (1) a deterministic rule discovery module that induces atomic transformations through geometric, color, and object-based analysis;
  (2) a pattern-composition engine that reconstructs outputs via block merging, repetition, and spatial heuristics; and
  (3) a structural abstraction layer that infers hierarchical and nested relationships across grids.
