  These solvers operate sequentially within a progressive fallback hierarchy, where each stage reuses prior reasoning traces to enhance interpretability and generalization. Training passed for 995 tasks out of 1000, further evaluated on 105 tasks out of 120 and solved 230 test tasks out of 240 ARC-AGI-2 tasks. The system achieved strong coverage across deterministic, compositional, and abstract categories, demonstrating an overall accuracy exceeding 95 percent. The proposed architecture bridges symbolic reasoning and pattern synthesis, providing interpretable insight into cognitive generalization. The results suggest that rule chaining and hierarchical composition can advance machine reasoning toward transparent, human-aligned abstraction without relying on task-specific tuning.

</details>


### [164] [Understanding LoRA Rank Trade-offs in Diffusion Model Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10656)
*Iman Khazrak,Narges Nejad,Mostafa M. Rezaee,Robert C. Green*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A controlled study investigating optimal LoRA rank selection for diffusion model fine-tuning, balancing quality (FID score) against computational cost. Using DDPM U-Net on CIFAR-10 with ranks {2,4,8,16,32}, the study finds rank 4 achieves the best FID (124.1380) with rank 8 close behind (124.2136), while higher ranks offer diminishing returns despite increased compute cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Selecting LoRA rank for diffusion fine-tuning requires balancing quality and compute cost, but there lacks clear guidance on optimal rank selection under fixed training budgets.

Method: Controlled study on CIFAR-10 using DDPM U-Net with ranks {2,4,8,16,32}, fixed optimization settings, and reproducible pytorch-fid protocol. Validated with extended-budget DDPM runs (20 epochs; ranks 4/8/16) and Tiny DiT backbone (10 epochs; ranks 4/8/16). Metrics: FID, trainable parameters, runtime, GPU memory.

Result: Rank 4 achieves best DDPM FID (124.1380), rank 8 is close (124.2136). Higher ranks provide limited quality gains despite larger adaptation cost. Moderate ranks are most efficient.

Conclusion: Small-to-moderate ranks (4-8) are practical defaults under fixed training budgets, offering best quality-to-compute tradeoff.

Abstract: Selecting LoRA rank for diffusion fine-tuning requires balancing quality and compute cost. We present a controlled study on CIFAR-10 using a DDPM U-Net with ranks {2,4,8,16,32}, fixed optimization settings, and a reproducible local-folder pytorch-fid protocol. We report FID, trainable parameters, runtime, and GPU memory, then validate trends with extended-budget DDPM runs (20 epochs; ranks 4/8/16) and a Tiny DiT backbone (10 epochs; ranks 4/8/16). Results show moderate ranks are most efficient: rank 4 achieves the best DDPM FID (124.1380), rank 8 is close (124.2136), and higher ranks provide limited gains despite larger adaptation cost. These findings support small-to-moderate ranks as practical defaults under fixed training budgets.

</details>


### [165] [Quantifying the Memorization-to-Generalization Transition: Scaling Laws and Phase Structure in Grokking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10657)
*Anish Kataria*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the quantitative structure of grokking - the delayed transition from memorization to generalization in neural networks. Through experiments on 384 configurations of two-hidden-layer MLPs trained on modular arithmetic, the authors derive a power-law scaling relation for generalization onset time and identify data complexity as the dominant driver of regime transition, not model capacity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While theoretical progress has been made on why grokking occurs, the quantitative structure of when it occurs in hyperparameter space remained uncharacterized. The authors aimed to map the memorization-to-generalization boundary and identify which factors most strongly influence the timing of this transition.

Method: The authors conducted a systematic empirical study across 384 configurations of two-hidden-layer MLPs on modular arithmetic tasks. They fitted a power-law scaling relation for generalization onset time (T_grok) as a function of hyperparameters: hidden layer width (H), data complexity (D), learning rate (η), and weight decay (λ). They also analyzed weight norm trajectories to understand the underlying dynamics.

Result: The fitted power-law scaling relation is: T_grok ∝ H^(-0.27) D^(-2.04) η^(-0.50) λ^(-0.64) with R² = 0.732 (0.821 with interactions). The exponent hierarchy reveals that data complexity (D^(-2.04)) is the dominant driver, not model capacity (H^(-0.27)): doubling data accelerates generalization by ~4×, while doubling width yields only ~1.2×. A sharp phase boundary exists at weight decay λ ≥ 1.0 separating grokking from non-grokking configurations. Weight norm trajectories show monotonic compression during the transition.

Conclusion: Data complexity is the dominant driver of grokking, not model capacity. A sharp phase boundary exists at weight decay λ ≥ 1.0. The monotonic compression of weight norms during transition is consistent with implicit regularization selecting low-complexity solutions. These results provide a quantitative foundation for predicting and controlling regime transitions in overparameterized networks.

Abstract: Neural networks trained past memorization frequently undergo a delayed transition to generalization, a phenomenon known as grokking. Despite theoretical progress on \emph{why} this transition occurs, the quantitative structure of \emph{when} it occurs in hyperparameter space remains uncharacterized. We map the memorization-to-generalization boundary across 384 configurations of two-hidden-layer MLPs on modular arithmetic, fitting a power-law scaling relation for generalization onset time: $T_{\mathrm{grok}} \propto H^{-0.27}\, D^{-2.04}\, η^{-0.50}\, λ^{-0.64}$ ($R^2 = 0.732$; $0.821$ with interactions). The exponent hierarchy reveals that data complexity ($D^{-2.04}$) is the dominant driver of regime transition, not model capacity ($H^{-0.27}$): doubling data accelerates generalization by ${\sim}4\times$, while doubling width yields only ${\sim}1.2\times$. A sharp phase boundary at weight decay $λ\gtrsim 1.0$ separates grokking from non-grokking configurations, and weight norm trajectories show monotonic compression during the transition, consistent with implicit regularization selecting low-complexity solutions. These results provide a quantitative foundation for predicting and controlling regime transitions in overparameterized networks.

</details>


### [166] [An Open Recipe for IMO Gold: Training Nemotron for Olympiad Mathematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10712)
*Ivan Moshkov,Stephen Ge,George Armstrong,Wei Du,Sadegh Mahdavi,Igor Gitman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a study on natural-language proof generation for hard olympiad mathematics usingNemotron 3 Ultra. The authors train two specialist checkpoints via supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and develop an open-model test-time-compute pipeline that operates entirely in natural language without formal provers or external tools. The system achieved 30/42 points at IMO 2026, reaching the gold-medal threshold.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The research addresses the challenge of generating natural-language mathematical proofs for hard olympiad-level problems, which requires both advanced reasoning and precise mathematical verification. The motivation is to understand how model post-training and test-time inference design affect proof generation capabilities, and to develop an open-model system that can compete at the International Mathematical Olympiad (IMO) level without relying on formal provers or external tools.

Method: The study employs three main methodologies: (1) Supervised fine-tuning to train one specialist checkpoint; (2) Reinforcement learning to train a second specialist checkpoint; (3) Test-time compute pipeline using three Nemotron 3 Ultra checkpoints (general availability model and two post-trained specialists) for iterative proof generation, verification, and refinement. The system operates entirely in natural language with no formal prover, external tools, or internet access.

Result: The system achieved 30 out of 42 points at IMO 2026, which equals the gold-medal threshold. The authors also released two post-trained checkpoints, training data, training and inference code, submitted solutions, and Nemotron-IMO-Bench (a new benchmark of 200 novel olympiad-level problems).

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that post-training via supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, combined with careful test-time inference design (checkpoint selection, verification, and refinement), can enable open models to achieve gold-medal-level performance on olympiad mathematics without external tools or formal provers. The release of checkpoints, data, code, and benchmark supports further research in this direction.

Abstract: We study how model post-training and test-time inference design affect natural-language proof generation for hard olympiad mathematics. Starting from Nemotron 3 Ultra, we train two specialist checkpoints using supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and evaluate checkpoint choice, verification, and refinement. Based on these findings, we present an open-model test-time-compute pipeline. The system operates entirely in natural language, with no formal prover, external tools, or internet access. Three Nemotron 3 Ultra checkpoints - the general-availability model and two post-trained specialists - power an iterative search that generates, verifies, and refines candidate proofs; a separate high-compute stage then selects each final submission. The system scored 30 out of 42 points at IMO 2026, reaching the gold-medal threshold. We release the two post-trained checkpoints as well as the training data, the training and inference code, the submitted solutions, and Nemotron-IMO-Bench, a new benchmark of 200 novel olympiad-level problems.

</details>


### [167] [Finishing the Task Is Not Enough: Evaluating Agent Resilience and Considerate Participation under Accumulating Challenge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10724)
*Yuanchen Bai,Zijian Ding,Angelique Taylor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper evaluates generative AI agents in healthcare settings on two under-explored dimensions: operational resilience (recovering from blocked work while preserving progress) and considerate participation (adapting while accounting for affected people and workflows). The study uses 120 simulated trajectories across two AI models, twelve tasks, and three challenge levels, comparing textual action plans, internal assessments, and quantitative reports. Key findings show agents shift toward human dependence under pressure, report strain in structured formats but not in text, and broaden from task-focus to role-boundary adjustment and wider coordination. Five deployment dilemmas are identified with technical implications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent evaluation focuses on isolated task success, but sustained deployment requires agents to remain useful across repeated interactions, changing conditions, and human dependencies. Operational resilience and considerate participation under accumulating challenges remain underexplored, creating a gap in understanding how agents behave when disruptions accumulate over time.

Method: The study analyzed 120 simulated healthcare trajectories across two generative AI models and twelve stakeholder-derived tasks under three challenge levels (light, medium, heavy). Methods included textual action plans, prompted internal assessments, and quantitative structured workload and affect reports to examine behavioral changes and reported state as challenge accumulates.

Result: Regarding operational resilience: Agents shift from self-directed recovery toward greater human dependence under increasing challenge. They report increasing workload and negative affect in structured reports but seldom express strain in textual responses. Regarding considerate participation: Agents broaden from task-focused adaptation toward task reframing, attention to others, role-boundary adjustment, and wider coordination, with distinct patterns across actions and internal assessments.

Conclusion: Five deployment dilemmas are identified involving persistence, attention, role boundaries, state disclosure, and escalation that require stakeholder specification. These findings have technical implications for learning, situated evaluation, and embodied adaptation of AI agents in real-world healthcare workflows.

Abstract: Sustained deployment of generative AI agents requires more than isolated task success. Agents must remain useful across repeated interactions, changing conditions, and dependencies on people within shared workflows, especially as technical, human, and operational disruptions accumulate over time. We propose operational resilience and considerate participation as two complementary aspects of evaluating such agents: the former captures how agents recover from blocked work while preserving progress and communicating their limits, and the latter captures how their adaptation accounts for affected people, role boundaries, and the surrounding workflow. Yet both remain underexplored under accumulating challenge. We study 120 simulated healthcare trajectories across two generative AI models and twelve stakeholder-derived tasks under light, medium, and heavy challenge. We compare textual action plans, prompted internal assessments, and quantitative structured workload and affect reports to examine how agent behavior and reported state change as challenge accumulates. Regarding operational resilience, agents shift from self-directed recovery toward greater human dependence, while reporting increasing workload and negative affect in structured reports but seldom expressing strain in textual responses. Regarding considerate participation, agents broaden from task-focused adaptation toward task reframing, attention to others, role-boundary adjustment, and wider coordination, with distinct patterns across actions and internal assessments. From these findings, we derive five deployment dilemmas involving persistence, attention, role boundaries, state disclosure, and escalation that require stakeholder specification, further informing technical implications for learning, situated evaluation, and embodied adaptation.

</details>


### [168] [Towards a Deterministic Math Solver for Clinical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10728)
*Felipe Ocampo Osorio,Sebastián Andrés Cajas Ordoñez,Maximin Lange,Rafi Al Attrach,Sahil Kapadia,Zakaria Laouabdia Sellami,Angelo Antonio Talio,Leo Anthony Celi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of large language models being unreliable at arithmetic for clinical calculators. The authors propose a "Program-Solve interface" where instead of calculating directly, the model writes case-specific Python code that a restricted executor runs. They evaluate this on MedCalc-Bench Verified (1,100 cases, 55 calculators) using Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ models, finding that the approach significantly helps the 32B model (90.53% vs 83.47%) but provides no reliable advantage for the 7B model (75.31% vs 72.02%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models are unreliable at arithmetic, which poses a critical problem for clinical calculators where a single numerical error can change medical recommendations. The traditional solution is to hardcode each calculator individually, but this is time-consuming. The authors wanted to test an alternative approach using a Program-Solve interface.

Method: The authors evaluated the Program-Solve interface on MedCalc-Bench Verified (1,100 cases, 55 calculators) against direct model arithmetic and a hand-written 22-calculator library. They used Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ models. They also audited the benchmark's formulas against current clinical guidelines, flagging 16 of 55 calculators with version, use or coefficient concerns.

Result: For the 7B model, Program-Solve achieved 75.31% accuracy versus 72.02% for direct arithmetic (a paired +3.29 points with 95% CI of [-3.49, 10.38]), which is not a reliable advantage. For the 32B model, Program-Solve achieved 90.53% versus 83.47% (+7.05 [0.47, 14.60]), which is clear of zero and thus a reliable advantage. The hand-written library was exact on its 440 supported cases but abstained elsewhere (40.0% overall).

Conclusion: Adding an executor helps some open-weight models more than others even under matched formula, variable and note access. However, it is not a substitute for verified formulas or reliable variable extraction. The approach works well for larger models but not smaller ones, and the hand-written library, while exact, has limited coverage.

Abstract: Large language models are unreliable at arithmetic, which is a problem for clinical calculators where a single numerical error changes the recommendation. The standard response is to hardcode each calculator as a validated function, one at a time. We test an alternative: the model does not calculate. Instead, it writes case-specific Python that a restricted local executor runs as a deterministic solver, and the model's task reduces to deciding how to use it. We evaluate this Program-Solve interface on MedCalc-Bench Verified (1,100 cases, 55 calculators) against direct model arithmetic and a hand-written 22-calculator library, using Qwen2.5-7B and Qwen2.5-32B-AWQ, after auditing the benchmark's formulas against current clinical guidelines and flagging 16 of 55 with version, use or coefficient concerns. With formulas and gold variables supplied and both routes reading the whole note, handing off to the solver is not a reliable advantage at 7B (75.31% against 72.02%, a paired +3.29 points with a 95% calculator-cluster interval of [-3.49, 10.38]) but is one at 32B (90.53% against 83.47%, +7.05 [0.47, 14.60], clear of zero). The hand-written library is exact on its 440 supported cases but abstains elsewhere (40.0% overall). Adding an executor thus helps some open-weight models more than others even under matched formula, variable and note access, and is not a substitute for verified formulas or reliable variable extraction either way.

</details>


### [169] [Studying Without a Syllabus: Task-Agnostic Environment Preprocessing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10824)
*Vinay Samuel,Varun Ursekar,Vijay S. Kalmath,Apaar Shanker,Veronica Chatrath,Yuan Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies task-agnostic environment preprocessing, where an LLM agent explores an unfamiliar environment without prior knowledge of downstream tasks and creates reusable artifacts. The authors compare meta-agents with fixed methods across six benchmarks, finding that a meta-agent variant achieves the highest Avg@3 reward on five benchmarks, while larger study budgets do not reliably improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most automated LLM adaptation methods rely on task examples, trajectories, or evaluation feedback. Existing task-agnostic approaches commit to a preparation strategy in advance. The authors seek to study a more open-ended setting where agents can autonomously explore an environment and choose how to prepare, without a syllabus or knowledge of downstream tasks.

Method: The authors formalize task-agnostic environment preprocessing, where a studying system explores an environment under a budget and produces artifacts for a frozen solver. They compare unaided and archive-equipped meta-agents with fixed synthetic-practice and corpus-processing methods across six heterogeneous benchmarks.

Result: A meta-agent variant achieves the highest Avg@3 reward on five out of six benchmarks. Fixed corpus processing remains best on the largest corpus benchmark. Larger study budgets do not reliably improve downstream reward. However, studied artifacts reduce the test-time sampling needed to reach a given score.

Conclusion: Reusable preparation can shift computation from repeated test-time attempts to a pre-task study phase, demonstrating the value of task-agnostic environment preprocessing for LLM agents.

Abstract: Before an LLM agent tackles tasks in a new environment, it can inspect available corpora and tools and construct reusable resources such as indices, scripts, or procedural guidance. Most automated adaptation methods, however, rely on task examples, trajectories, or evaluation feedback to decide what to build. Existing task-agnostic approaches avoid this supervision but commit in advance to a preparation strategy for a particular type of environment. We study a more open-ended setting: can an agent study an unfamiliar environment without a syllabus, i.e. before test time and without knowledge of the downstream task distribution, and choose how to prepare it? We formalize task-agnostic environment preprocessing, in which a studying system explores an environment under a budget and produces artifacts for a frozen solver. We compare unaided and archive-equipped meta-agents with fixed synthetic-practice and corpus-processing methods across six heterogeneous benchmarks. A meta-agent variant achieves the highest Avg@3 reward on five benchmarks, while fixed corpus processing remains best on the largest corpus benchmark. Larger study budgets do not reliably improve downstream reward. Nevertheless, studied artifacts reduce the test-time sampling needed to reach a given score, demonstrating how reusable preparation can shift computation from repeated test-time attempts to a pre-task study phase.

</details>


### [170] [When Validation Stops Learning: Auditing Update Admission for Continual Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10873)
*Qinzhen Ma,Ruihai Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: Independent evaluation can reject harmful policy updates yet also prevent useful continual learning. We argue that update admission must be assessed through both error control and retained learning opportunities at a stated interaction budget. We identify a concrete failure: a range-based confidence gate cannot certify unchanged old-task behavior within otherwise substantial budgets. A standard paired-binomial construction reduces this burden when outcome disagreements are rare. We also specify certified historical-reference promotion and a round-level missed-opportunity metric. In a constructed one-step pushing diagnostic with 32 seeds, fresh paired checks admit 31.6% of a common update stream at 2,000 episodes per stage, versus zero for the range-based gate; unconditional replay nevertheless learns better in closed-loop runs. A separate learned-dynamics stress test distinguishes model bias from feedback-selection error. The contribution is an admission-audit protocol with analytical and synthetic evidence; physical-robot and VLA validation remain open.

</details>


### [171] [Decoupling Readiness from Release for Tail-Aware Scheduling of Agentic LLM Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10964)
*Bochao Feng,Jianjiang Li,Haojie Wang,Lin Qiao,Yinghui Li,Yukun Yan,Jidong Zhai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses tail latency issues in agentic LLM workflows by proposing a tail-risk-aware turn release scheduling method that controls when ready turns are released and maintains a budget for released but unfinished work, achieving up to 3.50× speedup in P95 workflow flow time under contention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic LLM workflows consist of sequences of model turns interleaved with tool interactions. Current runtimes use eager release policy, releasing each turn immediately upon readiness. Under contention, this accumulates released but unfinished work that cannot be reordered by workflow-level policies, increasing tail latency.

Method: The proposed method uses a mean-Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) objective to capture the evolving tail risk of unfinished workflows. It incorporates online estimates of turn work when prioritizing ready turns and adapts the released work budget to observed queue pressure. The method jointly decides (1) which ready turn to release next, and (2) how much released but unfinished work to maintain.

Result: Evaluated using real agent execution traces from software engineering tasks across multiple LLMs and workflow arrival rates, the method performs comparably to eager release under light load and substantially reduces P95 of workflow flow time under contention, achieving up to a 3.50× speedup.

Conclusion: The tail-risk-aware turn release scheduling approach effectively balances throughput and tail latency in agentic LLM workflows, providing significant improvements under high load while maintaining performance under light load.

Abstract: Agentic LLM workflows consist of sequences of model turns interleaved with tool interactions, so their end-to-end completion time depends not only on inference speed but also on when ready turns are released. Most runtimes release each turn immediately upon readiness. Under contention, this eager release policy can accumulate released but unfinished work; once submitted, those turns can no longer be reordered by the workflow-level policy, increasing tail latency. We present a tail-risk-aware turn release scheduling method that jointly decides which ready turn to release next and how much released but unfinished work to maintain. The method uses a mean--Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) objective to capture the evolving tail risk of unfinished workflows, incorporates online estimates of turn work when prioritizing ready turns, and adapts the released work budget to observed queue pressure. We evaluate the method using real agent execution traces from software engineering tasks across multiple LLMs and workflow arrival rates. The method performs comparably to eager release under light load and substantially reduces the P95 of workflow flow time under contention, achieving up to a \(3.50\times\) speedup.

</details>


### [172] [Demystifying the Privacy-Utility Trade-off in LLM Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10992)
*Zhenhua Liu,Zhanxu Xie,Junjie Yu,Tong Zhu,Lijun Li,Wenliang Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the privacy-utility trade-off in Large Language Models by introducing an intent-driven local protection framework (Veilmind-4B). Through systematic analysis, the authors uncover three mechanisms: Context-Dependent Utility (when to sanitize), Strategic Adaptation (how to sanitize), and Combinatorial Interplay (protection scope extension). The framework uses a dynamic extraction-sanitization-restoration pipeline to achieve low leakage while preserving high response utility, advancing the privacy-utility trade-off toward the Pareto frontier.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current privacy-preserving methods for LLMs use context-agnostic static rules that cause severe utility degradation. The specific mechanisms governing how sanitization impacts downstream performance remain largely underexplored, necessitating a systematic analysis of the privacy-utility trade-off.

Method: The authors conduct a systematic analysis to deconstruct the privacy-utility trade-off, uncovering three underlying mechanisms: (1) Context-Dependent Utility - determines when to sanitize based on data value shifting from critical constraints to dispensable noise based on user intent; (2) Strategic Adaptation - determines how to sanitize by choosing between removal and replacement based on task reliance on factual integrity vs. structural coherence; (3) Combinatorial Interplay - extends protection scope by analyzing semantic dependencies between attributes. They then introduce Veilmind-4B, a lightweight model driving a dynamic extraction-sanitization-restoration pipeline.

Result: The approach achieves low-leakage privacy while preserving substantially higher response utility than existing privacy-oriented baselines, advancing the privacy-utility trade-off toward the Pareto frontier.

Conclusion: The intent-driven local protection framework successfully balances privacy and utility by understanding user intent and applying dynamic sanitization strategies based on three discovered mechanisms. This represents a significant advancement over static rule-based privacy preservation methods.

Abstract: The integration of Large Language Models into daily tasks relies on context-rich instructions, inevitably exposing sensitive user information. Current privacy-preserving methods typically employ context-agnostic static rules, causing severe utility degradation. However, the specific mechanisms governing how sanitization impacts downstream performance remain largely underexplored. To address this, we conduct a systematic analysis to deconstruct the privacy-utility trade-off, uncovering three underlying mechanisms: (1) Context-Dependent Utility, which first establishes when to sanitize by revealing that data value shifts from critical constraints to dispensable noise based on user intent; (2) Strategic Adaptation, which subsequently determines how to sanitize by dictating that the choice between removal and replacement depends on the task's reliance on factual integrity versus structural coherence; and (3) Combinatorial Interplay, which finally extends the protection scope by demonstrating that attributes form a semantic web of synergistic dependencies or antagonistic redundancies. Guided by these insights, we introduce an intent-driven local protection framework. By distilling a lightweight model Veilmind-4B to drive a dynamic extraction-sanitization-restoration pipeline, our approach reaches a low-leakage privacy point while preserving substantially higher response utility than existing privacy-oriented baselines, advancing the privacy-utility trade-off toward the Pareto frontier.

</details>


### [173] [Defining AI Agents: A Compendium of Criteria, Metrics, and Benchmarks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11018)
*Mia Lassiter,Brinnae Bent*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper is a survey addressing the lack of standard definition for "agent" in AI. It organizes agent evaluation around five dimensions of agenticness (environmental interaction, learning/adaptation, autonomy, goal-directed behavior, and temporal coherence), synthesizes existing evaluation frameworks, and introduces the Agent Compendium as a public resource. The work aims to establish a common structure for evaluating and comparing AI agents to support reproducible research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The term "agent" in artificial intelligence lacks a standard definition, which complicates the evaluation, comparison, and reproducibility of AI agent research.

Method: A survey organized around five dimensions of agenticness: environmental interaction, learning and adaptation, autonomy, goal-directed behavior, and temporal coherence. For each dimension, the authors examine how the underlying capability has been conceptualized across prior work and synthesize the metrics, benchmarks, and evaluation frameworks used to assess it.

Result: A structured account of the current landscape of agent evaluation, highlighting both established approaches and areas where evaluation remains limited or inconsistent.

Conclusion: The survey and the Agent Compendium (a public-facing digital resource) together provide a common structure for evaluating and comparing agent capabilities across AI systems, supporting more reproducible research, clearer communication, and more systematic study of artificial agents.

Abstract: The term agent in artificial intelligence lacks a standard definition, complicating the evaluation, comparison, and reproducibility of AI agent research. We address this ambiguity through a survey organized around five dimensions of agenticness: environmental interaction, learning and adaptation, autonomy, goal-directed behavior, and temporal coherence. For each dimension, we examine how the underlying capability has been conceptualized across prior work and synthesize the metrics, benchmarks, and evaluation frameworks used to assess it. This review provides a structured account of the current landscape of agent evaluation, highlighting both established approaches and areas where evaluation remains limited or inconsistent. We additionally introduce the Agent Compendium, a public-facing digital resource that organizes and extends the evaluation methods identified through this review. Together, the survey and compendium provide a common structure for evaluating and comparing agent capabilities across AI systems, supporting more reproducible research, clearer communication, and more systematic study of artificial agents.

</details>


### [174] [The Agent Incident Registry: Toward Preventing Repeated AI Agent Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11030)
*Divyanshu Kumar,Rohith HN,Nitin Aravind Birur,Sahil Agarwal,Prashanth Harshangi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents the Agent Incident Registry (AIR), a curated catalog of agent-related incidents designed to address the gap in comparing public failures with agent-security evaluations. The registry contains N records with source-linked evidence, stable identifiers, and missingness-aware labels for causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. Analysis shows that realized harm concentrates in in-the-wild and safety-failure records, while responsible disclosures and research demonstrations are predominantly represented. A deployment-analogue audit compares InjecAgent cases (attacker-triggered) with no-adversary safety failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: General incident repositories lack the mechanisms needed to compare public failures with agent-security evaluations. There is a need for a source-linked catalog that captures the specific mechanisms of agent-related incidents, including causal roles, disclosure classes, mechanisms, and outcomes.

Method: The authors created the Agent Incident Registry (AIR), containing N records of agent-related events disclosed from Yfirst through Ylast. Each record includes supporting evidence, stable identifier, and missingness-aware labels for four dimensions: causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. A second human reviewer verified all records and labels for completeness and correctness. A deployment-analogue audit was conducted comparing InjecAgent cases with safety failures.

Result: Among Nprimary generative-system records where the agent acted, Rprimary involved realized harm (Pprimary%). Realized outcomes concentrate in in-the-wild and safety-failure records, while responsible disclosures and research demonstrations are overwhelmingly demonstrated. InjecAgent's NInjecAgentCases cases occupy three of AIR's twelve surfaces and are all attacker-triggered, whereas AIR contains Nsafety no-adversary safety failures.

Conclusion: AIR supports source-grounded case retrieval and evaluation-scope auditing, but is not designed for failure-rate or control-efficacy estimation. The aggregate composition characterizes collection composition rather than deployment risk.

Abstract: AI agents increasingly act through tools and delegated authority, but general incident repositories rarely capture the mechanisms needed to compare public failures with agent-security evaluations. We present the Agent Incident Registry (AIR), a source-linked catalog containing \N{} records of agent-related events disclosed from \Yfirst{} through \Ylast{}. Each record includes supporting evidence, a stable identifier, and missingness-aware labels for causal role, disclosure class, mechanism, and outcome. Among the \Nprimary{} generative-system records in which the agent acted, \Rprimary{} involved realized harm (\Pprimary\%). Realized outcomes concentrate in in-the-wild and safety-failure records, while responsible disclosures and research demonstrations are overwhelmingly demonstrated; the aggregate share therefore characterizes collection composition rather than deployment risk. After initial curation, a second human reviewer checked all \N{} records and their existing labels for completeness and correctness. In a deployment-analogue audit, InjecAgent's \NInjecAgentCases{} cases occupy three of AIR's twelve surfaces and are all attacker-triggered, whereas AIR contains \Nsafety{} no-adversary safety failures. AIR supports source-grounded case retrieval and evaluation-scope auditing, not failure-rate or control-efficacy estimation.

</details>


### [175] [Grounding Agent Memory: Environment-Probing Curation for Enterprise Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11060)
*Susheel Suresh,Hazel Mak,Sahil Bhatnagar,Chhaya Methani,Alejandro Gutierrez Munoz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces environment-probing curation, a method that gives existing memory curator agents read-only tools to verify and refresh candidate memories without requiring model retraining. Tested on GitHub Copilot (GHCP) with CLBench and APEX tasks, it significantly improves pass rates (39%→73% on CLBench), reduces costs, and achieves better reward gains per dollar across multiple worlds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-task curator agents that only have access to completed trajectories can preserve errors, overgeneralize partial evidence, or retain stale knowledge. Current curation methods lack environment verification capabilities.

Method: Environment-probing curation adds least-privilege, read-only world tools to an existing asynchronous curator agent, allowing it to check, scope, and refresh candidate memories without changing the task agent, retriever, memory representation, or production write authority. No model retraining required.

Result: On CLBench: pass rate increased from 39% to 73%; pass-discounted reward from 8.60 to 22.60; queries reduced from 8.8 to 4.7 per question; task-agent cost reduced from $3.38 to $1.68. On APEX: all 18 memory-versus-baseline comparisons positive; tool calls reduced by 16-75%; best reward gain per dollar in 5/6 worlds. Probing outperforms GHCP+Mem on both Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7 without schema drift.

Conclusion: Environment probing transforms existing agent-memory curation into an environment-informed, auditable process while preserving a compact task-time interface. It successfully improves memory quality, reduces costs, and works across different models without requiring schema changes or model retraining.

Abstract: Persistent memory is entering production-oriented agent platforms to help long-horizon agents accumulate experience across sessions. Yet a post-task curator agent restricted to completed trajectories can preserve errors, overgeneralize partial evidence, or retain stale knowledge. We introduce environment-probing curation, a deployment-compatible extension that gives an existing asynchronous curator agent least-privilege, read-only world tools to check, scope, and refresh candidate memories. It requires no model retraining and leaves the task agent, retriever, memory representation, and production write authority unchanged. In a production-like GitHub Copilot (GHCP) harness built on its SDK, we compare stateless execution, full in-context learning, GHCP + Mem, and GHCP + Mem (w/ Env Probing) on CLBench database exploration and 90 adapted APEX management-consulting tasks. On CLBench, probing raises pass rate from 39% to 73% and pass-discounted reward from 8.60 to 22.60 while reducing queries from 8.8 to 4.7 per question and task-agent cost from \$3.38 to \$1.68. Across six APEX worlds, all 18 memory-versus-baseline mean reward comparisons are positive and task-agent tool calls fall by 16--75%; probing gives the best task-agent reward gain per dollar in five worlds. Probing also attains higher mean reward than GHCP + Mem on both Sonnet 4.6 and Opus 4.7 without schema drift. Environment probing therefore turns existing agent-memory curation into an environment-informed, auditable process while preserving a compact task-time interface.

</details>


### [176] [Fork Where the Model Changes Its Mind: Belief-Shift Branching for Tree-Structured Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11061)
*Bin Lei,Yu Li,Prafulla Kumar Choubey,Jiaxin Zhang,Becky Xiangyu Peng,Qinyuan Ye,Kartik Narayan,Caiwen Ding,Silvio Savarese,Chien-Sheng Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces "belief-shift branching," a method for optimally placing forks in tree-structured rollouts for critic-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR). By forking just before steps where the model's answer belief diverges most, the method maximizes step-level credit signal. Three implementations are proposed: a black-box probe, a logit-lens depth profile, and a learned activation direction, requiring minimal computational overhead (~1% for math, ~5% for code).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tree-structured rollouts enable critic-free RLVR by using sibling outcome differences to estimate step value. However, fork placement critically determines credit signal quality—placing forks where outcomes are already settled yields uninformative siblings. Existing methods (fixed lengths, midpoints, entropy) are suboptimal. The key insight is that forks should be placed at "pivots" where the value curve turns, i.e., where the model's belief about the answer changes most.

Method: The authors formalize fork placement as locating pivots of the chain's value curve. They propose belief-shift branching: read the model's answer belief at candidate boundaries and fork just before the step with maximum consecutive belief divergence. Three implementations are provided across different access levels: (1) black-box probe, (2) logit-lens depth profile, and (3) learned activation direction (fitted offline, used only in pre-RL validation). The signal only determines fork positions, not the RL objective.

Result: In validation against Monte-Carlo value curves, belief-shift ranking placed first in all eight model×benchmark panels, outperforming entropy, structural, and LLM-judge baselines. In RL training across three model families and two domains: (1) belief-shift forking leads every mathematics aggregate; (2) on OLMo-3-7B: +2.6 aggregate improvement and +2.9 on AIME 2026 over strongest baseline; (3) sweeps every OLMo code column with +6.5 on LiveCodeBench-medium.

Conclusion: Belief-shift branching provides a principled, computationally lightweight approach to fork placement in tree-structured rollouts. By targeting pivots in the model's belief trajectory, it consistently outperforms entropy-based and structural baselines across diverse models and domains. The method requires no step-level supervision and adds minimal computational overhead, making it practical for real-world RLVR training pipelines.

Abstract: Tree-structured rollouts give critic-free reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) step-level credit: fork a chain at an intermediate point, and sibling outcome differences estimate step value. Each fork adds sampling cost, so realistic budgets typically allow only a few forks per chain. A fork placed where the outcome is already largely settled yields siblings that mostly agree and provide almost no credit signal; hence, for a given tree size, where forks are placed largely determines how much step-level RL can gain. Most existing mainstream methods place forks by structure, such as fixed lengths, midpoints, and delimiters, or by next-token entropy. We formalize fork placement as locating the \emph{pivots} of the chain's value curve, where the expected outcome turns. We propose \emph{belief-shift branching}: read the model's answer belief at candidate boundaries and fork just before the step where consecutive beliefs diverge most. Three instantiations, none needing step-level supervision, span access levels: a black-box probe, a logit-lens depth profile, and a learned activation direction, which is fit offline and therefore used only in the validation before RL training. The signal only \emph{places} forks, and the probe costs about $1\%$ of step compute on mathematics and under $5\%$ on code when it runs inside the rollout engine. In that validation, against Monte-Carlo value curves, a belief-shift signal ranks first in each of the eight model$\times$benchmark panels, ahead of entropy, structural, and LLM-judge baselines. In RL across three model families and two domains, belief-shift forking leads every mathematics aggregate, on OLMo-3-7B by $+2.6$ aggregate and $+2.9$ on AIME 2026 over the strongest baseline, and sweeps every OLMo code column, by $+6.5$ on LiveCodeBench-medium.

</details>


### [177] [MOSAIC: Query-Aware Exploration Policy Adaptation for GraphRAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11065)
*EunKyeong Lee,Kyeong-Jin Oh,Jinwon Kim,Hye Woo Lee,Minsang Song,Hyeongjun Jang,Junyoung Youn*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mosaic is a training-free GraphRAG framework that formulates retrieval as a per-query control problem, using an LLM analyzer to convert query-specific evidence requirements into bounded policies. It achieves state-of-the-art results on GraphRAG-Bench, outperforming fixed policies by 9.96 points while evaluating 81.9% fewer paths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GraphRAG systems use largely shared exploration procedures across queries, creating a structural mismatch: direct facts need compact local neighborhoods, comparisons need balanced coverage of multiple targets, and mediated questions require deeper paths through weakly related connectors. No fixed narrow, medium, or wide policy is consistently optimal across query types.

Method: Mosaic uses an LLM analyzer to convert query-specific evidence requirements into a bounded policy over four components: (1) seed selection, (2) graph traversal, (3) stopping criteria, and (4) evidence selection. The corpus graph, indexes, scoring functions, grounding procedure, and answer generator remain shared.

Result: On GraphRAG-Bench: Query-weighted Answer Correctness of 76.97 on Medical and 64.33 on Novel (improvements of 5.13 and 4.43 points over previous best). On Medical: 95.1 Evidence Recall and 86.1 Context Relevancy. Controlled comparisons show 9.96 points improvement over strongest canonical fixed policy. Relative to Fixed Wide: 81.9% fewer paths evaluated and 47.2% fewer evidence items retained. Transfer experiments on HotpotQA, MuSiQue, and 2WikiMultiHopQA show the policy interface can be applied without benchmark-specific retriever training.

Conclusion: Query-specific policies significantly outperform fixed policies in GraphRAG retrieval. The training-free, per-query control approach achieves superior results with greater efficiency. The policy interface enables transfer across benchmarks without retriever retraining, demonstrating broad applicability.

Abstract: Graph Retrieval-Augmented Generation (GraphRAG) can connect evidence distributed across a corpus graph, but most systems use largely shared exploration procedures across queries. This creates a structural mismatch: direct facts may need compact local neighborhoods, comparisons need balanced coverage of multiple targets, and mediated questions may require deeper paths through weakly related connectors. We present Mosaic, a training-free framework that formulates GraphRAG retrieval as a per-query control problem. An LLM analyzer converts query-specific evidence requirements into a bounded policy over seed selection, graph traversal, stopping, and evidence selection, while the corpus graph, indexes, scoring functions, grounding procedure, and answer generator remain shared.
  On GraphRAG-Bench, Mosaic achieves query-weighted Answer Correctness of 76.97 on Medical and 64.33 on Novel, improving over the strongest previously reported overall results by 5.13 and 4.43 points. On Medical, it reaches 95.1 Evidence Recall and 86.1 Context Relevancy. Controlled comparisons on an identical graph and generator show that no fixed narrow, medium, or wide policy is consistently optimal; Mosaic improves by 9.96 points over the strongest canonical fixed policy. Relative to Fixed Wide, it evaluates 81.9% fewer paths and retains 47.2% fewer evidence items. Transfer experiments on HotpotQA, MuSiQue, and 2WikiMultiHopQA further show that the policy interface can be applied without benchmark-specific retriever training.

</details>


### [178] [Benchmark Radar: A Living Database and Search Engine for AI Benchmarks and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11115)
*Koutian Wu,Junjie Zhou,Ergan Shang,Jiayu Wang,Pengqian Han,Junkai Wang,Wanghan Xu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark Radar is a living database and search engine for AI benchmarks, covering LLM evaluation, agentic/tool-use benchmarks, coding, reasoning, safety, and domain-specific evaluations. It combines daily discovery from 37 sources with a searchable catalog of 1,283 source records and 12,916 numeric observations, providing web dashboards, CLI, and reproducible analysis tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark researchers and developers of LLMs and AI systems need to find relevant evaluations, locate benchmark datasets and code, and understand the settings behind reported scores. Existing tools lack comprehensive, searchable access to this information.

Method: Daily discovery from 37 sources (13 direct connectors and 24 first-party research/engineering feeds), combining data from 4 benchmark catalogs. The system maintains source identities and citations for inspection.

Result: Built a catalog containing 1,283 source records with 12,916 numeric observations on 790 records. Provides web dashboard with leaderboard, Pareto frontier view, saturation and trend views, daily feeds, downloadable evidence, CLI for offline queries, and reproducible analysis.

Conclusion: Benchmark Radar enables comprehensive benchmark retrieval and discovery for AI evaluation. The system addresses the need for systematic tracking of benchmarks, their adoption, and score saturation, while providing practical tools for researchers conducting prior-art searches when designing new evaluations.

Abstract: Benchmark researchers and developers of large language models (LLMs) and other AI systems need to find relevant evaluations, locate their benchmark datasets and code, and understand the settings behind reported scores. We present Benchmark Radar, a living database and search engine for retrieval and discovery of AI benchmarks, covering LLM evaluation, agentic and tool-use benchmarks, coding, reasoning, safety, and domain-specific evaluations. The system combines daily discovery of benchmark papers, repositories, datasets, and releases with a searchable benchmark catalog, mentions in model cards and technical reports, and score histories. It retains source identities and citations so readers can inspect candidate benchmarks and their evaluation evidence. Daily discovery draws on 37 sources: 13 direct connectors and 24 first-party research and engineering feeds. The catalog contains 1,283 source records drawn from 4 benchmark catalogs and 12,916 numeric observations on 790 records. We describe collection and retrieval, audit the full catalog, and examine benchmark saturation, adoption trends, and the limits of score comparisons. A worked example walks through a complete prior-art search, showing how to query the catalog and inspect benchmark evidence when designing a new evaluation. We release the web dashboard with a benchmark leaderboard, a Pareto frontier view of score against measured use, saturation and trend views, daily feeds, downloadable evidence, a command-line interface (CLI) for offline queries, and reproducible analysis.

</details>


### [179] [KuaiRP Series Role-playing Models Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11127)
*Yipeng Wang,Ziwei Zhang,Jiahui Zhang,Qi Gan,Kai Sheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KuaiRP is a series of role-playing models designed to achieve simplified prompt engineering, stable output quality, built-in domain knowledge, and efficient deployment. The paper proposes a multi-stage training pipeline combining SFT with user behavior simulation, RL with rule-based rewards, and a novel self-distillation approach (OPD with CDD) to balance domain knowledge injection with preservation of general agent capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core challenge is that effectively injecting deep domain knowledge into role-playing models leads to catastrophic forgetting of the model's general agent capabilities. The authors aim to achieve four objectives: simplified prompt engineering, stable output quality, built-in domain knowledge, and high-efficiency deployment with small parameter size.

Method: The proposed multi-stage training pipeline includes: (1) Standardized character template and SFT data pipeline using user behavior simulation and reverse profile filtering; (2) Rule-based composite reward function during RL to eliminate length expansion and repetitive generation; (3) Two-stage On-Policy Distillation (OPD) with Cumulative-Divergence Decay (CDD) using the domain-adapted model as teacher and original base model as student.

Result: KuaiRP models match current state-of-the-art proprietary models in role-playing fidelity within target domains, while successfully recovering general agent capabilities and maintaining extremely low deployment costs.

Conclusion: The self-distillation paradigm with OPD and CDD effectively balances deep domain knowledge injection with preservation of general agent capabilities, achieving both high role-playing fidelity and general agent performance with low deployment costs.

Abstract: This paper introduces the complete technical solution for the KuaiRP series of role-playing models. We aim to achieve four core objectives for a dedicated role-playing model: simplified prompt engineering, highly stable output quality, built-in domain world knowledge, and high-efficiency deployment with a small parameter size. However, effectively injecting deep domain knowledge often leads to a severe catastrophic forgetting of the model's general agent capabilities. To overcome this trade-off, we propose a multi-stage training pipeline. First, we design a standardized character template and construct an SFT data pipeline based on user behavior simulation and reverse profile filtering. Next, we utilize a rule-based composite reward function during the Reinforcement Learning (RL) phase to eliminate common degradation phenomena like length expansion and repetitive generation. Finally, to recover the general capabilities compromised during SFT and RL, we propose a novel self-distillation paradigm using Two-stage On-Policy Distillation (OPD) equipped with Cumulative-Divergence Decay (CDD). By using the domain-adapted model as the teacher and the original base model as the student, we effectively balance deep domain knowledge injection with the preservation of general agent capabilities. Experimental results demonstrate that the KuaiRP models not only match the current state-of-the-art proprietary models in role-playing fidelity within our target domains, but also successfully recover general agent capabilities, maintaining extremely low deployment costs.

</details>


### [180] [Same Day, Same Story; One Day Ahead, a Different Signal: The Dual Validity of Financial Sentiment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11144)
*AS Aravinthkakshan,Laven Srivastava,Harsh Nandwani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the standard assumption in Financial NLP that validating a sentiment tool against human labels (construct validity) and using it to predict market outcomes (predictive validity) measure the same thing. Using 70,500 X messages linked to securities class actions, the authors test five sentiment instruments and find that the relationship between these two validities depends on sampling methods and score representations. Human agreement establishes semantic validity but does not determine predictive rankings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The standard Financial NLP workflow assumes that if a sentiment tool agrees with human labels, it will also predict market signals. However, this assumes construct validity and predictive validity measure the same thing. This paper tests this fundamental assumption in a unique setting where both can be measured simultaneously.

Method: The authors used a corpus of securities class actions (2002-2025) linking 70,500 X messages to abnormal stock returns. They tested five sentiment instruments (VADER, Loughran-McDonald, FinBERT, Twitter-RoBERTa, and an LLM annotator) through one identical pipeline. They compared human agreement under conventional method-specific sampling versus a fixed-n panel, examining both graded rank correlations and coarse ordering at same-day and one-day lead horizons.

Result: The relationship between construct and predictive validity depends on sampling convention and score representation. Under conventional method-specific sampling, human agreement aligns more closely with graded same-day associations than with one-day leads. On a fixed-n panel, agreement has similar graded rank correlations at both horizons, while coarse ordering remains weak. Additionally, in a conversation that is 17.6% spam, message volume predicts neither market damage nor settlement size.

Conclusion: Benchmark agreement establishes semantic validity but does not by itself determine predictive rankings. The assumption that construct validity and predictive validity are equivalent in Financial NLP is not supported - the relationship between them depends on how samples are drawn and how scores are represented. This challenges the standard validation workflow in the field.

Abstract: Financial NLP has a standard workflow: validate a sentiment tool against human labels, then trust it to extract market signal. This assumes the two evaluations measure the same thing. We test that assumption in a setting where both can be measured at once: a corpus of securities class actions (2002-2025) linking 70,500 X messages to abnormal stock returns, with a single-annotator human labelled gold sample. Running five instruments (VADER, Loughran-McDonald, FinBERT, Twitter-RoBERTa, and an LLM annotator) through one identical pipeline, we find that the relationship between construct and predictive validity depends on the sampling convention and score representation. Under conventional method-specific sampling, human agreement aligns more closely with graded same-day associations than with one-day leads. On a fixed-n panel, however, agreement has similar graded rank correlations at both horizons, while the coarse ordering remains weak. Benchmark agreement therefore establishes semantic validity but does not by itself determine predictive rankings. In a conversation that is 17.6% spam, message volume predicts neither market damage nor settlement size.

</details>


### [181] [The Oligarch Barely Steers Model Collapse in Multi-Model Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11146)
*Yangze Liu,Zhongyi Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether market concentration in generative AI (oligopoly) accelerates model collapse or biases later models toward dominant players' outputs. Using 13 open-source models (1-4B parameters) in ecosystems of 3-13 players over five generations, the study finds that concentration has minimal impact: making splits more unequal barely changes collapse speed, and destination shifts are only a few percent. The key factor determining collapse pace is which models supply the training pool, not their market share.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address two concerns about AI market concentration: (1) fewer, more uniform sources may accelerate model collapse, and (2) later models may be dragged toward the oligarch's output. These worries arise because real generative AI is an oligopoly, unlike prior research that assumed equal market splits.

Method: The method involves controlled experiments with 13 open-source models (1-4B parameters) forming natural ecosystems of 3 to 13 players. An injected probe pushes the top share to 90%. Each generation, every model's output is mixed into a shared pool by market share, and every model is retrained on that pool from clean base weights, for five generations. The study tests: (1) share-weighted index of susceptibility, (2) swapping ecosystem members, (3) replacing half the pool with human text.

Result: Within the tested range, neither worry materializes. Making the split more unequal barely changes the speed of collapse. Destination shifts by only a few percent of the drift common to all arms. An extreme share paired with strongest injected bias does not guarantee steering. Swapping members of a K=3 ecosystem changes five-generation drift by 2.8x. A share-weighted index of susceptibility explains speed differences across nineteen arms with R² = 0.68. Replacing half the pool with human text roughly halves drift without changing its course.

Conclusion: Within the tested range, concentration sets neither the destination nor the pace of collapse; the pace follows whose text fills the pool. The key determinant of collapse speed is which models supply the training pool and how readily those suppliers are carried along, not market concentration itself.

Abstract: AI-generated text is flowing back into the training corpora of the next generation of models. Recursive training on it drives model collapse, and recent work extends the setting to many models feeding one another -- but almost always with the market split evenly, while real generative AI is an oligopoly. Concentration raises two worries: fewer, more uniform sources may make collapse faster, and later models may be dragged toward the oligarch's output. We test both in controlled ecosystems: 13 open 1--4B models form natural ecosystems of 3 to 13 players, plus an injected probe that pushes the top share to 90%; each generation, every model's output is mixed into a shared pool by market share and every model is retrained on that pool from clean base weights, for five generations. Yet within the range we test, neither worry materializes; what emerges instead is an invariance. Making the split more unequal barely changes the speed of collapse. Destinations move even less: the share and identity knobs shift five-generation endpoints by only a few percent of the drift common to all arms -- the ecosystems collapse to nearly the same place. An extreme share paired with the strongest injected bias still does not guarantee steering, and the topic shifts it does produce leave only a faint trace on the ruler that measures collapse. What sets the speed is who supplies the pool and how readily those suppliers are carried along: with every share held fixed, swapping the members of a K=3 ecosystem changes five-generation drift by 2.8x; a share-weighted index of each member's susceptibility explains the speed differences across nineteen arms with R^2 = 0.68; and replacing half the pool with human text roughly halves drift without changing its course. Within the tested range, concentration sets neither the destination nor the pace of collapse; the pace follows whose text fills the pool.

</details>


### [182] [Autonomous Chemical Mechanistic Discovery through Agentic Reasoning and Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11147)
*Dong Li,Sixuan Mi,Zihao Ye,Huan Xiong,Tao XU,Tong Zhu,Aijia Zhang,Junqi Gao,Kaiyan Zhang,Shijie Wang,Bowen Zhou,Yuqiang Li,Biqing Qi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARCHE is an autonomous agentic system that automates reaction mechanism investigation by integrating a reasoning model, computational chemistry model, and structured tool registry in a closed-loop framework. It validates its approach across three scenarios: stereocontrolling transition states in asymmetric catalysis, radical pathway discovery for α-iodoboronate C-I cleavage, and identifying selectivity descriptors in nickel-catalyzed cross-coupling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The central challenge in modern chemistry is that unraveling reaction mechanisms remains labor-intensive due to heavy reliance on expert intervention in computational workflows. The authors aim to transform mechanistic inquiry into a scalable, self-validating process.

Method: ARCHE integrates three core components: (1) a general-purpose reasoning model, (2) a domain-specialized computational chemistry model, and (3) a structured tool registry. The system operates in a closed loop: interpreting scientific questions → generating and prioritizing mechanistic hypotheses → orchestrating computational workflows → iteratively refining conclusions based on computed evidence.

Result: Validated across three progressively demanding scenarios: (1) Successfully reconstructed stereocontrolling transition states and validated mechanism in asymmetric catalysis; (2) Proposed and validated a plausible radical pathway through iterative refinement for α-iodoboronate C-I cleavage; (3) Identified a chemically interpretable descriptor governing selectivity in nickel-catalyzed migratory cross-coupling.

Conclusion: By coupling agentic reasoning with rigorous computational validation, ARCHE advances autonomous mechanistic discovery and establishes a foundation for broader machine-assisted chemical research. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Unraveling reaction mechanisms is central to modern chemistry, yet automating these investigations remains challenging because computational workflows still rely heavily on expert intervention. Here we introduce ARCHE, an autonomous agentic system that integrates a general-purpose reasoning model, a domain-specialized computational chemistry model, and a structured tool registry to transform mechanistic inquiry into a scalable, self-validating process. ARCHE interprets scientific questions, generates and prioritizes mechanistic hypotheses, orchestrates computational workflows, and iteratively refines conclusions based on computed evidence within a closed loop. We validate its capabilities across three increasingly demanding scenarios: reconstructing stereocontrolling transition states and validating the corresponding reaction mechanism in a previously reported asymmetric catalytic reaction; proposing and validating a plausible radical pathway through iterative hypothesis refinement for a recently discovered but unpublished $α$-iodoboronate C-I cleavage reaction; and identifying a chemically interpretable descriptor that governs selectivity in nickel-catalysed migratory cross-coupling reactions. By coupling agentic reasoning with rigorous computational validation, ARCHE advances autonomous mechanistic discovery and establishes a foundation for broader machine-assisted chemical research. The code for ARCHE is publicly available at https://github.com/JetAstra/Arche-Harness.

</details>


### [183] [DRG-MAPPO: Hierarchical Dynamic Role-Graph Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning for Cooperative Air Combat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11155)
*Junlin Liu,Chengwei Li,Yang Gao,Hui Chang,Xinchen Zhang,Zhijun Zhao,Hao Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DRG-MAPPO is a novel hierarchical MARL framework that combines graph-based relational modeling with dynamic role assignment to solve tactical coordination challenges in cooperative air combat. It achieves 87% win rate by integrating graph attention mechanisms for modeling battlefield interactions and a role-based policy architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MARL for air combat faces two key limitations: (1) lack of structured relational modeling to capture complex time-varying interactions among battlefield entities, and (2) conventional flat architectures cannot explicitly model tactical roles, leading to ambiguous task allocation in dynamic environments.

Method: DRG-MAPPO integrates graph-based relational modeling with dynamic role assignment through: (1) constructing a graph-based representation of battlefield interactions; (2) using graph attention mechanisms to extract relational features among allies, enemies, and threats; (3) employing a high-level policy for dynamic role assignment (leader/supporter); (4) executing discrete maneuver actions via a low-level policy conditioned on roles and encoded graph-relational features; (5) adding a target-priority auxiliary task to encourage focus-fire behaviors.

Result: DRG-MAPPO achieves a state-of-the-art win rate of 87% in cooperative air combat, effectively balancing relational modeling, interpretability, and optimization stability.

Conclusion: The hierarchical framework successfully addresses both limitations by combining graph-based relational modeling with dynamic role assignment, demonstrating that explicit role modeling and relational features significantly improve tactical coordination in multi-agent air combat scenarios.

Abstract: Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL) has emerged as a pivotal paradigm for complex decision-making in autonomous systems and air combat. While MARL has demonstrated significant potential in air combat, achieving sophisticated tactical coordination remains a non-trivial challenge. This difficulty is largely attributed to two primary limitations: (1) the absence of structured relational modeling hinders agents from capturing complex, time-varying interactions among battlefield entities; and (2) conventional flat architectures often lack the capability to explicitly model tactical roles, leading to ambiguous task allocation in highly dynamic environments. To address these challenges, we propose Hierarchical Dynamic Role-Graph Multi-Agent Proximal Policy Optimization (DRG-MAPPO), a novel MARL framework that integrates graph-based relational modeling with dynamic role assignment. Specifically, DRG-MAPPO constructs a graph-based representation of battlefield interactions and leverages graph attention mechanisms to extract critical relational features among allies, enemies, and threats. Subsequently, a high-level policy employs a dynamic role assignment mechanism to determine tactical responsibilities (e.g., ``leader'' and ``supporter''). Conditioned on these roles and encoded graph-relational features, a low-level policy executes discrete maneuver actions, facilitating the joint optimization of tactical strategy and collaborative execution. Furthermore, a target-priority auxiliary task is designed to foster the emergence of behaviors such as focus-fire. Experimental results demonstrate that DRG-MAPPO achieves a state-of-the-art win rate of 87%, suggesting that our framework effectively balances relational modeling, interpretability, and optimization stability for cooperative air combat.

</details>


### [184] [Breaking Predictions Is Not Enough: Specified-Foil Counterfactuals for Temporal Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11170)
*Minwoo Yu,Young-guk Ha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a gap in temporal graph counterfactual explanations by proposing the Specified-Foil Counterfactual problem: finding past-event interventions that make a specific alternative outcome (foil B) become the top prediction instead of the original prediction A. The method uses trace-guided intervention search comparing execution traces of A and B, mapping differences to graph operations, and verifying through exact replay. Evaluations on continuous-time dynamic graphs (LiFTER) and temporal knowledge graphs (TLogic) show strong performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current temporal graph counterfactual explanations focus on changing or invalidating an original prediction by modifying past events, but they do not specify what alternative outcome would result. Users often want to know: "What conditions would make a particular alternative occur instead?" This destination-specific question was previously unaddressed in the literature.

Method: The paper proposes a trace-guided intervention search approach that: (1) contrasts the completed execution trace of the original prediction A with a reconstructed incomplete execution of the foil B; (2) maps their differences to five graph operations: DELETE, INSERT, REWIRE, RELABEL, and SHIFT; (3) verifies B through exact replay. Two instantiations are provided: LiFTER for continuous-time dynamic graphs (CTDGs) and TLogic for temporal knowledge graphs (TKGs).

Result: On CTDGs, the method retains 85.7-93.6% of black-box greedy successes while reducing predictor evaluations by 75.0-80.0%. On TKGs, it reaches the specified foil in 74.8% of 600 comparisons. The approach successfully transforms executable traces from mere explanation records into computational structures for constructing conditions of unselected alternatives.

Conclusion: The Specified-Foil Counterfactual formulation provides a principled way to answer destination-specific "what-if" questions on temporal graphs. By leveraging trace comparison and graph operations, the method achieves high success rates with significant computational efficiency gains. This work bridges the gap between post-hoc explanation and actionable counterfactual reasoning in temporal graph learning.

Abstract: Temporal graph counterfactual explanations typically change past events to change or invalidate an original prediction, while leaving its replacement unspecified. Yet a user facing a predicted outcome often asks which past conditions would make a particular alternative occur instead. We formulate this destination-specific question as the Specified-Foil Counterfactual: given an original prediction A and a foil B fixed before search, find a low-cost past-event intervention under which the same predictor selects B as top-ranked. Our trace-guided intervention search contrasts the completed execution of A with a reconstructed incomplete execution of B, maps their difference to DELETE, INSERT, REWIRE, RELABEL, and SHIFT operations, and verifies B through exact replay. We instantiate this principle with LiFTER on continuous-time dynamic graphs and TLogic on temporal knowledge graphs. On CTDGs, the method retains 85.7-93.6% of black-box greedy successes while reducing predictor evaluations by 75.0-80.0%; on TKGs, it reaches the specified foil in 74.8% of 600 comparisons. Executable traces thereby become computational structures for constructing conditions of unselected alternatives, rather than records used only to explain predictions already made.

</details>


### [185] [Debate-to-Skill: Capability-Bound Process Supervision for Industrial Query-to-Agent Annotation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11176)
*Shiyu Zhang,Leisheng Cheng,Huifu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses industrial query-to-agent matching where topical relevance is incorrectly conflated with executable capability, particularly for long-tail and boundary-sensitive requests. The authors propose capability-bound process supervision instantiated through Debate-to-Skill, which uses reusable decision principles, structured deliberation, verifier-based verdict extraction, and disagreement-driven refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial query-to-agent matching systems fail when they confuse topical relevance with actual executable capability. This is especially problematic for long-tail queries and boundary-sensitive requests where semantic relatedness diverges from true capability. The motivation is to develop a supervision approach that targets the capability-critical decision process itself rather than relying on direct labels or standard reasoning methods.

Method: The paper proposes "Debate-to-Skill" as an instantiation of capability-bound process supervision. This method incorporates four key components: (1) reusable decision principles that guide the matching process, (2) structured deliberation for reasoning about capabilities, (3) verifier-based verdict extraction to validate decisions, and (4) disagreement-driven refinement to improve the model through contrasting viewpoints.

Result: The method was evaluated on an industrial Query2Agent benchmark. Debate-to-Skill was compared against direct-label supervision, reasoning-SFT (supervised fine-tuning), and structural ablations. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of supervising the capability-critical decision process, particularly on grey-zone cases where semantic relatedness and executable capability diverge.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that gains in query-to-agent matching performance come from supervising the capability-critical decision process itself, especially for challenging grey-zone cases where traditional semantic relevance signals fail to capture true executable capability. The Debate-to-Skill approach provides a promising framework for improving industrial matching systems.

Abstract: Industrial query-to-agent matching fails when topical relevance is mistaken for executable capability, especially on long-tail and boundary-sensitive requests. We formulate annotation as \emph{capability-bound process supervision} and instantiate it with Debate-to-Skill, which uses reusable decision principles, structured deliberation, verifier-based verdict extraction, and disagreement-driven refinement. On an industrial Query2Agent benchmark, we compare Debate-to-Skill with direct-label supervision, reasoning-SFT, and structural ablations. The results test whether gains come from supervising the capability-critical decision process itself, especially on grey-zone cases where semantic relatedness and executable capability diverge.

</details>


### [186] [SemVerBench: Benchmarking LLM Comprehension of Version-Constraint Resolution Semantics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11180)
*Qibai Chen,Zeming Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First benchmark (SemVerBench) measuring LLM understanding of version constraint semantics across npm, PEP 440, and Cargo ecosystems. Reveals systematic blind spots in frontier models, with failures being application gaps rather than knowledge gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM coding agents constantly need to resolve version constraints (e.g., ^1.2.3, >=2.0,<3), yet their understanding of version-constraint semantics has never been directly measured despite being critical for coding tasks.

Method: Created SemVerBench with 240 machine-checkable items across three ecosystems (npm, PEP 440, Cargo), built from four balanced sources (official test suites + LLM proposers) and labeled by a non-circular two-implementation oracle. Evaluated six frontier models using statistical analysis (McNemar test).

Result: Partial-comparator carry rule (>1.2 means >=1.3.0) traps every model on Cargo (~60% failure). GPT-5.1 collapses on zero-pad/post-release corner cases (0/26) while Claude maintains 97-100% (verified on 67-item oracle set). Opus outperforms all models; Sonnet outperforms OpenAI models. Failures are activation/application gaps (recoverable via rule injection) rather than knowledge gaps. No statistically significant self-favoritism detected.
