Conclusion: Tool delegation reaches ~100% accuracy. Since a free, 100%-correct resolver exists and the task is verifiable, coding agents should delegate version resolution to resolvers rather than reason about versions in-head.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) coding agents constantly decide whether a version satisfies a constraint such as ^1.2.3 or >=2.0,<3, yet their grasp of version-constraint semantics has never been measured directly. We introduce SemVerBench, the first benchmark of LLM version-constraint resolution semantics across three ecosystems (npm, PEP 440, Cargo): 240 machine-checkable items with unique answers, built author-neutrally from four balanced sources (each ecosystem's official test suite plus three frontier LLM proposers) and labeled by a non-circular two-implementation oracle. Evaluating six frontier models, we find systematic, predictable per-mechanism blind spots: a partial-comparator carry rule (>1.2 means >=1.3.0) traps every model on Cargo (near 60%), and although standard PEP 440 prefix matching is universal, on zero-pad/post-release corner cases GPT-5.1 collapses (0/26) while Claude stays at 97-100% (verified on a 67-item oracle-validated set). Opus significantly outperforms all other models, and Sonnet outperforms the OpenAI models (McNemar). The failures look more like an activation/application gap than a knowledge gap: injecting the rule or a light correct hint recovers most errors, whereas interval decomposition does not, and models are at ceiling on the basic forms of the same rules. An author-stratified analysis finds no statistically significant self-favoritism. Because the task is verifiable and a free, 100%-correct resolver exists, tool delegation reaches ~100%: coding agents should delegate version resolution to a resolver rather than reason about versions in-head.

</details>


### [187] [Can LLMs Follow Medical Expert Logic? A Benchmark for Hierarchical Logical Consistency in Risk-of-Bias Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11185)
*Jiayu Huang,Zichen Tang,Qianhui Ling,Zemin Kuang,Haihong E*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LogiMed-RoB is a benchmark evaluating LLM reasoning in evidence-based medicine using Cochrane RoB 2.0 logic. It reveals a catastrophic Error Compounding Effect where high atomic consistency (98.88%) collapses to only 45.13% end-to-end consistency, and exposes a systematic evidence-reasoning gap where models fail to deduce correct outcomes 18.63-40.05% of the time even with high-quality evidence retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM evaluations prioritize superficial label matching over genuine reasoning. Evidence-based medicine requires strict logical consistency, but existing benchmarks fail to assess whether models can correctly chain logical steps to derive conclusions from evidence.

Method: LogiMed-RoB benchmark with 860 RCTs and 14,820 queries, using Hierarchical Logical Consistency (HLC) framework across four dimensions: Atomic Consistency, Domain Consistency, Aggregation Consistency, and Evidential Faithfulness. Tested on 10 state-of-the-art LLMs.

Result: Top model achieves 98.88% Atomic Consistency but only 45.13% end-to-end consistency. Several open-weight architectures drop to nearly 0%. Evidence-reasoning gap: 18.63-40.05% failure rate even with high-quality evidence. Blind Guess Rate reaches 48.28%.

Conclusion: High outcome accuracy can conceal critical reasoning flaws. White-box logical verification is necessary for clinical deployment of LLMs in evidence-based medicine.

Abstract: Evidence-based medicine demands strict logical consistency, yet current evaluations of large language models (LLMs) prioritize superficial label matching over genuine reasoning. We introduce LogiMed-RoB, a benchmark grounded in Cochrane Risk of Bias (RoB) 2.0 expert logic, comprising 860 randomized controlled trials (RCTs) and 14,820 queries. It evaluates models under the Hierarchical Logical Consistency (HLC) framework across four dimensions: Atomic Consistency, Domain Consistency, Aggregation Consistency, and Evidential Faithfulness. Experiments on 10 state-of-the-art LLMs reveal a catastrophic Error Compounding Effect: despite the top model reaching 98.88% Atomic Consistency, its end-to-end consistency collapses to 45.13%, with several open-weight architectures plummeting to nearly 0%. We further uncover a systematic evidence-reasoning gap: even when models retrieve high-quality evidence, they fail to deduce correct outcomes in 18.63-40.05% of cases, while Blind Guess Rates reach 48.28%. LogiMed-RoB demonstrates that high outcome accuracy can conceal critical reasoning flaws, underscoring the necessity of white-box logical verification for clinical deployment.

</details>


### [188] [Agentic Share-of-Search: A Multi-Agent AI System for Competitive Decision-Making in LLM-Mediated E-Commerce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11190)
*Spandan Ghose Chowdhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a multi-agent AI system for measuring and diagnosing competitive visibility in LLM-mediated ecommerce, featuring Agentic Share-of-Search (ASoS) as a new metric and a ReAct-based diagnostic agent for merchandising recommendations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI shopping assistants are increasingly redirecting consumer discovery, creating an urgent need for tools that support seller-side competitive decision-making in LLM-mediated ecommerce platforms.

Method: The system deploys query agents across leading AI shopping platforms and uses a ReAct-based diagnostic agent to recommend prioritized merchandising interventions. A 100-trial ablation study evaluates feasibility by testing the agent's ability to recover ablated signals.

Result: The agent recovers the ablated signal in 39% of trials (95% CI: 30.0% - 48.8%), which is 5.5x over chance. Performance rises to 63.9% among high-correlation ablations.

Conclusion: The prototype demonstrates feasibility for automating competitive visibility measurement and root cause diagnosis in LLM-mediated ecommerce, with promising results particularly for high-correlation scenarios.

Abstract: AI shopping assistants increasingly redirect consumer discovery, creating an urgent need for tools that support seller-side competitive decision-making. We present a multi-agent AI system that automates competitive visibility measurement and root cause diagnosis in LLM-mediated ecommerce. The system introduces Agentic Share-of-Search (ASoS) as the decision target, deploys query agents across leading AI platforms, and uses a ReAct-based diagnostic agent to recommend prioritized merchandising interventions. A 100-trial ablation study, presented as a feasibility evaluation of this prototype, shows the agent recovers the ablated signal in 39% of trials (95% CI: 30.0% - 48.8%, 5.5x over chance), rising to 63.9% among high-correlation ablations.

</details>


### [189] [An AI-Powered Culturally Aware Chatbot for Stress Detection and Wellness Support among Pakistani University Students Using NLP and Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11199)
*Muhammad Fahad Bashir,Muhammad Afzal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an AI-driven, culturally sensitive stress detection and wellness support system specifically designed for Pakistani university students, using a Random Forest model with 89.09% accuracy that interfaces with an LLM via OpenRouter API to provide multilingual (English, Urdu, Roman Urdu) wellness support.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pakistani students face unique compounded stressors (academic, financial, familial, relational) in university settings, and existing digital mental health tools are Western-developed and lack cultural relevance. The study aims to address this gap by creating a region-aware mental health system.

Method: Random Forest machine learning model trained on 1100 validated student responses across 20 features spanning psychological, physiological, academic, environmental, and social dimensions. Classification outputs fed to an open-source LLM through OpenRouter API with culturally-aware system prompts.

Result: Model achieved 89.09% accuracy and 0.89 macro F1-score across three stress severity levels. Feature importance analysis revealed teacher-student relationship as the second most predictive stress factor in this population.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the need for culturally-aware mental health systems. Teacher-student relationship emerged as a culturally significant stress factor specific to the Pakistani context. Future research will involve primary data collection using DASS-21 instrument, focusing on students transitioning from FSc to undergraduate studies.

Abstract: With the existing digital mental health tools specifically developed for Western settings, Pakistani students are exposed to a uniquely compounded stress situation in their university that includes academic, financial, familial, and relational stressors, which have become a serious concern for academic and psychological development of students in Pakistani universities. This paper introduces a new, AI-driven and culturally sensitive stress detection and wellness support system that is tailored to the context of Pakistani university students. The system is based on a machine learning model called Random Forest which is trained using a validated student stress data set of 1100 responses on 20 features from psychological, physiological, academic, environmental and social aspects, with an accuracy of 89.09% and a macro F1-score of 0.89, in three stress severity levels. The classification outputs are passed on to an open-source large language model through OpenRouter API, where an appropriately crafted system prompt, culturally aware, gives the model a conversation about wellness, in English, Urdu and Roman Urdu. The second most predictive stress factor in this population identified by feature importance analysis was teacher-student relationship, which is a culturally important stress factor highlighting the need for region-aware mental health systems. Future research will involve primary data collection from students at various academic levels of Pakistani Universities with the validated DASS-21 instrument focusing on the students who are moving from FSc to undergraduate studies, which is a time of being psychologically vulnerable which is under-researched.

</details>


### [190] [CryptoL: Towards Scale Dominance and Physics Constraints Mitigation in Financial Multivariate Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11206)
*Yalda Taheri,Mohammad Hassan Heydari,Armon Rasooli,Maryam Amirshahkarami,Mohammad Ebrahim Mahdavi,Hossein Karshenas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CryptoL is a unified framework for multivariate cryptocurrency time-series forecasting that addresses scale heterogeneity, non-stationary dynamics, and OHLC structural dependencies through context-normalized loss evaluation, scale-adaptive numerical stabilization, and constraint-aware prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cryptocurrency forecasting faces unique challenges: extreme cross-asset scale heterogeneity (assets spanning many orders of magnitude), non-stationary dynamics, and structural dependencies among OHLC (Open, High, Low, Close) variables. Existing methods suffer from inverse normalization introducing squared-scale weighting into MSE, normalization approaches that fail to preserve candle-order relations, and fixed normalization constants causing distortions across heterogeneous assets.

Method: CryptoL employs four key components: (1) Context-normalized coordinates within RevIN pipeline to prevent inverse normalization from adding squared-scale weighting to MSE; (2) Channel-dependent normalization preserving candle-order relations versus independent channel transformations; (3) Scale-adaptive numerical stabilization to reduce distortions from fixed normalization constants; (4) Soft feasibility loss penalizing violations of OHLC inequalities (e.g., High≥Open/Close, Low≤Open/Close).

Result: Experiments across heterogeneous cryptocurrency assets using controlled ablations demonstrated improvements in forecasting accuracy, training stability, and frequency of financially valid OHLC predictions relative to considered baselines.

Conclusion: CryptoL provides an integrated approach combining scale-balanced optimization, structure-preserving normalization, numerical stabilization, and constraint-aware forecasting for cryptocurrency prediction, effectively addressing the unique challenges of heterogeneous multivariate time-series in this domain.

Abstract: Cryptocurrency forecasting presents a distinctive combination of extreme cross-asset scale heterogeneity, non-stationary dynamics, and structural dependencies among Open, High, Low, and Close (OHLC) variables. We present CryptoL, a unified framework designed to address these challenges within multivariate time-series forecasting. CryptoL evaluates forecasting error in context-normalized coordinates within the RevIN pipeline, preventing inverse normalization from introducing an additional squared-scale weighting into the MSE objective. We formally characterize this effect through the empirical risk and parameter-gradient geometry, establishing the conditions under which large-scale assets can disproportionately influence shared-model optimization. Beyond loss-space normalization, CryptoL examines channel-independent and channel-dependent normalization for OHLC data, showing that a shared channel-dependent affine transformation preserves candle-order relations that independent channel transformations need not preserve. The framework further incorporates scale-adaptive numerical stabilization to reduce distortions caused by a fixed normalization constant across assets spanning many orders of magnitude, together with a soft feasibility loss that penalizes violations of the defining OHLC inequalities. Experiments across heterogeneous cryptocurrency assets evaluate these components through controlled ablations and demonstrate improvements in forecasting accuracy, training stability, and the frequency of financially valid OHLC predictions relative to the considered baselines. CryptoL therefore provides an integrated approach to scale-balanced optimization, structure-preserving normalization, numerical stabilization, and constraint-aware cryptocurrency forecasting.

</details>


### [191] [A Voice-Interactive Multi-Agent System for Smart Operating Rooms: Architecture Design and Key Technologies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11231)
*Tianxiang Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SurgicalRoomAgent is a voice-interactive multi-agent system for smart operating rooms built on LLMs. It uses a layered architecture with voice interaction pipeline and agent core to achieve natural language understanding, device control, intraoperative recording, and surgical report generation. Three key optimizations are proposed: KV Cache prefix warming (500ms to tens of ms), streaming partial JSON parsing with early parallel execution (30% latency reduction), and progressive skill prompt disclosure for context optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Smart operating rooms require efficient voice-based interaction for real-time device control and documentation during surgeries. Current systems face challenges with latency (especially in LLM inference), limited context windows, and the need for multi-device parallel control in time-critical surgical environments.

Method: The system employs a layered architecture with: (1) Voice interaction pipeline - wake word detection, ASR, turn detection, agent reasoning, TTS; (2) Agent core - skill registry, task planner, device manager. Three key technologies: KV Cache prefix warming using byte-level Longest Common Prefix reuse; streaming partial JSON parsing with early parallel task execution; progressive skill prompt disclosure that dynamically filters prompts based on user role, connected devices, and surgical phase. Implementation uses Qwen3-27B model with llama.cpp/sglang inference engines.

Result: The system operates effectively within a 16,384-token context limit with multi-device parallel control response times meeting operating room real-time requirements. KV Cache warming reduces recomputation overhead from ~500ms to tens of milliseconds. Streaming JSON parsing reduces end-to-end latency by approximately 30%.

Conclusion: SurgicalRoomAgent demonstrates a viable approach to voice-controlled smart operating rooms with LLM-based agents, achieving low-latency inference and efficient context management through innovative optimization techniques. The system meets real-time requirements for OR environments while maintaining natural language interaction capabilities.

Abstract: This paper presents SurgicalRoomAgent, a voice-interactive multi-agent system for smart operating rooms based on large language models (LLMs). The system achieves natural language understanding, device control, intraoperative recording, and surgical report generation through a layered architecture comprising a voice interaction pipeline (wake, ASR, turn detection, agent reasoning, TTS) and an agent core (skill registry, task planner, device manager). Three key technologies are investigated: (1) KV Cache prefix warming for low-latency inference, reducing recomputation overhead from approximately 500 ms to tens of milliseconds via byte-level Longest Common Prefix reuse; (2) streaming partial JSON parsing with early parallel task execution, reducing end-to-end latency by approximately 30%; and (3) progressive skill prompt disclosure, which dynamically filters system prompts based on user role, connected devices, and surgical phase to maximize information density within limited context windows. The system is implemented using the Qwen3-27B model with llama.cpp/sglang inference engines. Experimental analysis demonstrates effective operation within a 16,384-token context limit and multi-device parallel control response times meeting OR real-time requirements.

</details>


### [192] [NovGauge: A Fine-Grained Benchmark for Diagnosing LLMs' Capability in Paper Novelty Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11234)
*Guoqiang Zhang,Kexin Tan,Ming Zhang,Li Ju,Wenqing Jing,Zhonghan Yue,Jiayi Chen,Shiqiang Wu,Shaofan Liu,Yue Zhang,Yuankai Ying,Yang Shi,Tao Gui,Qi Zhang,Xuanjing Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NovGauge is a benchmark for diagnosing fine-grained novelty assessment in LLMs used for peer review. It addresses the weakness of existing benchmarks that provide only holistic novelty scores. The benchmark evaluates 18 LLMs across three dimensions (task, problem, method) and reveals significant issues with hallucination (0-39%) and evidence grounding (over 70% of correct judgments have unsupported evidence). The best model (GPT-5.5) achieves only 43-72% Verified F1, indicating LLMs remain unreliable for scientific novelty assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing novelty benchmarks provide only a single holistic score, making it impossible to diagnose which specific dimension a model misjudges or whether its evidence is faithful. This limits understanding of LLM limitations in peer review applications.

Method: NovGauge benchmark contains 619 paper pairs and 50 multi-paper sets from ICLR reviewer overlap claims and survey co-citations. Labels are assigned along three dimensions: task (application goals), problem (technical challenges), and method (solution approaches). A cascading diagnostic pipeline verifies per-dimension correctness, evidence grounding, and logical support. Evaluation covers 18 LLMs.

Result: Hallucination rates range from 0% to 39% across dimensions. Among non-hallucinated correct-positive judgments, over 70% cite evidence that fails to logically support the stated reason. GPT-5.5 achieves 43-72% Verified F1 across dimensions. Most models retain less than half of their raw F1 after faithfulness verification.

Conclusion: Current LLMs remain far from reliable for scientific novelty assessment, particularly when correctness is conditioned on faithful evidence grounding. The study highlights the need for more rigorous evaluation frameworks that verify not just accuracy but also the faithfulness of evidence supporting judgments.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in peer review at major AI conferences, yet novelty remains a persistent weak point. Existing benchmarks assess novelty as a single holistic score, making it difficult to diagnose which dimension a model misjudges or whether its evidence is faithful. We present NovGauge, a human-anchored benchmark for fine-grained novelty assessment diagnosis. The benchmark contains 619 paper pairs and 50 multi-paper sets, drawn from two expert sources: ICLR reviewer overlap claims and survey co-citations. Instances are independently labeled along three dimensions: task, problem, and method, capturing application goals, technical challenges, and solution approaches. We propose a cascading diagnostic pipeline that verifies per-dimension correctness, evidence grounding, and logical support. Evaluation of 18 LLMs shows hallucination rates ranging from 0% to 39% across dimensions, and among non-hallucinated correct-positive judgments, over 70% cite evidence fails to logically support the stated reason. The best-performing model, GPT-5.5, achieves 43-72% Verified F1 across dimensions, while most models retain less than half of their raw F1 after faithfulness verification. These results suggest that current LLMs remain far from reliable scientific novelty assessment, particularly when correctness is conditioned on faithful evidence grounding.

</details>


### [193] [Sci-MMR: Benchmarking Multi-Step Evidence-Grounded Scientific Reasoning in Multimodal Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11243)
*Jiaqiang Li,Yajie Yang,Zhiheng Xi,Jiadong Chen,Enyu Zhou,Senjie Jin,Yang Nan,Jiazheng Zhang,Han Wang,Yanxin Li,Dingwei Zhu,Bicheng Deng,Yuhui Wang,Xiang Zheng,Qi Zhang,Lei Bai,Xingjun Ma,Tao Gui*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sci-MMR is a benchmark for evaluating multi-step evidence-grounded scientific reasoning in multimodal models, revealing a significant gap between answer accuracy and evidence recovery capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current multimodal benchmarks only evaluate final-answer accuracy, failing to assess whether predictions are actually supported by traceable scientific evidence. This is critical for autonomous research agents that need to search literature, analyze evidence, and generate hypotheses.

Method: Sci-MMR comprises 235 multi-hop reasoning tasks spanning four scientific disciplines with an average of nine figure panels per task. The benchmark uses structured argument graphs linking scientific claims, citation-grounded knowledge, visual evidence, and supporting regions. Eight frontier multimodal models were evaluated through controlled interventions.

Result: Answer accuracy exceeds complete-evidence recovery rate by more than 20%. Two fundamental bottlenecks identified: (1) Evidence acquisition - models struggle to extract complete structured evidence from scientific figures (57.2% of failures), with cropping tools yielding +4.5 points but gold evidence improving accuracy by up to 37.0 points; (2) Evidence integration - models struggle to translate evidence into correct conclusions (31.8% of failures), with the strongest model achieving only 69.1% accuracy on hardest tasks even with gold evidence.

Conclusion: Current answer-centric benchmarks substantially overestimate the evidence-grounded reasoning capabilities of multimodal research agents. The field needs benchmarks that evaluate evidence grounding, not just final answers.

Abstract: Autonomous research agents are increasingly expected to search the literature, analyze experimental evidence, and generate scientific hypotheses. These capabilities require multi-step evidence grounded reasoning that progressively acquires, integrates, and verifies evidence before reaching a conclusion. Existing multimodal benchmarks, however, largely evaluate final-answer accuracy, leaving open whether predictions are actually supported by traceable scientific evidence. We introduce Sci-MMR, a benchmark for multi-step evidence-grounded scientific reasoning built on structured argument graphs linking scientific claims, citation-grounded knowledge, visual evidence, and supporting regions. Sci-MMR comprises 235 multi-hop reasoning tasks spanning four scientific disciplines, with an average of nine figure panels per task. Evaluating eight frontier multimodal models, we find that answer accuracy consistently exceeds complete-evidence recovery rate by more than 20%, revealing a substantial gap that answer-only evaluation is structurally unable to capture. Through controlled interventions, we identify two fundamental bottlenecks. First, evidence acquisition: models struggle to extract complete structured evidence from scientific figures, accounting for 57.2% of failures. While cropping tools yield modest gains (+4.5 points), providing gold evidence improves accuracy by up to 37.0 points, indicating difficulty in assembling complete multi-region evidence. Second, evidence integration: models struggle to translate available evidence into correct conclusions, accounting for 31.8% of failures, while even with gold evidence the strongest model achieves only 69.1% accuracy on the hardest tasks. These findings indicate that current answer-centric benchmarks substantially overestimate the evidence-grounded reasoning capabilities of multimodal research agents

</details>


### [194] [AI-Powered Flare Combustion Efficiency Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11262)
*Afeefa Azam,Iyyakutti Iyappan Ganapathi,Fares Ossama Abdelhafez,Divya Velayudhan,Maregu Assefa Habtie,Hamad Karki,Khalid Yousef Al Awadhi,Naoufel Werghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A lightweight vision-language model combined with a compact MLP predicts combustion efficiency from low-cost thermal video footage, deployed via a GUI with real-time visualization and reporting capabilities, achieving 99% uptime over six months with minimal maintenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional combustion efficiency monitoring instruments (gas analyzers, hyperspectral cameras) are expensive, fragile, and require frequent calibration, making them impractical for remote or budget-constrained industrial sites. There is a need for a low-cost, reliable alternative.

Method: The proposed solution combines a lightweight vision-language encoder with a compact multi-layer perceptron (MLP) to predict combustion efficiency directly from low-cost thermal video footage. The trained model is integrated into a deployable GUI that overlays predicted efficiency values on video frames, displays real-time trends, shows efficiency distribution, and enables CSV export.

Result: Over a six-month deployment period, the system achieved 99% uptime and required less than 15 minutes of maintenance per week.

Conclusion: The vision-language encoder + MLP approach provides a practical, low-cost solution for continuous combustion efficiency monitoring in industrial flare stacks, with high reliability and minimal maintenance requirements.

Abstract: Achieving high combustion efficiency in flare stacks is crucial for adhering to regulatory standards and controlling the release of hydrocarbons into the environment. Traditional instruments like gas analyzers and hyperspectral cameras are expensive, fragile, and require frequent calibration, which makes them impractical for remote or budget constrained industrial sites. We propose an innovative solution that combines a lightweight vision-language encoder with a compact multi-layer perceptron to predict combustion efficiency directly from low-cost thermal video footage. The fully trained model is integrated into an easy-to-deploy graphical user interface. This interface overlays predicted combustion efficiency values on each video frame, displays real-time trends in combustion efficiency, shows the distribution of combustion efficiency across all frames in the video, and allows users to export CSV reports. Over a six-month period, the system achieved 99% uptime and required less than 15 minutes of maintenance per week.

</details>


### [195] [Predicting Train Delays in Finland Using Machine Learning and Weather Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11277)
*Vinicius Pozzobon Borin,Jean Michel de Souza Sant'Ana,Nurul Huda Mahmood*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a machine learning approach for train delay prediction in Arctic regions using the FI-TW dataset, which integrates railway operational data with weather observations from ~200 Finnish Meteorological Institute stations. The category-based feature approach using hierarchical weather classifications (Blizzard, Heavy Snow, Extreme Cold) achieved R²=0.78, RMSE=8.5 min, and MAE=3.7 min, outperforming raw weather inputs by 11% in R² and 10% error reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Adverse weather in Arctic regions is a leading cause of train delays, yet current prediction approaches rely on raw meteorological inputs without exploiting domain-informed feature engineering. This limits prediction accuracy and ignores the potential of domain knowledge to create more efficient representations for edge deployment over wireless sensor networks.

Method: The study used the Finland Integrated Train-Weather (FI-TW) dataset combining railway operational records with Finnish Meteorological Institute data from ~200 stations. Three feature configurations were evaluated using XGBoost at Oulu central station (101,146 observations): (1) full weather features, (2) instant weather observations only, and (3) derived weather category scenarios using hierarchical classifications (Blizzard, Heavy Snow, Extreme Cold).

Result: The category-based approach achieved R²=0.78, RMSE=8.5 minutes, and MAE=3.7 minutes, representing an 11% R² improvement and 10% error reduction over alternative configurations. Domain-informed features derived from sensor streams outperformed raw meteorological observations.

Conclusion: Compact, domain-informed weather features outperform raw meteorological observations for train delay prediction. The category-based approach offers bandwidth-efficient representations suitable for edge deployment over current and emerging wireless infrastructures (6G networks with integrated sensing and edge computing).

Abstract: Reliable railway operations depend increasingly on real-time environmental intelligence delivered through wireless sensor infrastructures, a capability that 6G networks will substantially enhance through integrated sensing and edge computing. Adverse weather, particularly in Arctic regions with extreme temperatures and heavy precipitation, remains a leading cause of train delays, yet most prediction approaches rely on raw meteorological inputs without exploiting domain-informed feature engineering. This paper investigates machine learning for train delay prediction using the Finland Integrated Train-Weather (FI-TW) dataset, which fuses railway operational records with observations from the Finnish Meteorological Institute's nationwide sensor network of approximately 200 stations communicating over wireless links. We evaluate three feature configurations using XGBoost at Oulu central station (101,146 observations): full weather features, instant weather observations only, and derived weather category scenarios. The category-based approach, employing hierarchical classifications such as Blizzard, Heavy Snow, and Extreme Cold, achieved an R^2 of 0.78, root mean squared error of 8.5 minutes, and mean absolute error of 3.7 minutes, representing an 11% R^2 improvement and 10% error reduction over alternative configurations. These results demonstrate that compact, domain-informed features derived from sensor streams outperform raw meteorological observations, offering bandwidth-efficient representations suitable for edge deployment over current and emerging wireless infrastructures.

</details>


### [196] [Bio-inspired Learning and Decision-Making with Probabilistic In-Memory Computing Hardware: Part 1](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11281)
*Thomas Dalgaty,Eiji Kawasaki,Miguel de Prado,Devendra Vyas,Tommaso Salvatori*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a biologically grounded framework where neural and synaptic noise enables probabilistic inference through stochastic sampling, drawing parallels between biological systems and analogue in-memory computing hardware for energy-efficient probabilistic computing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand how neural dynamics in biological systems give rise to Bayesian-like learning and decision-making, and how these principles can be replicated in computing systems. They seek to bridge biological neuroscience with hardware implementation.

Method: The framework uses stochastic sampling from an internal energy function, where neural variability captures epistemic uncertainty over latent states and synaptic variability captures uncertainty over model parameters. This is connected to predictive coding networks implementing Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling.

Result: The framework demonstrates that noisy neural and synaptic dynamics can perform Bayesian inference and learning. It also identifies a parallel between intrinsic noise in biological systems and electrical noise in analogue memory technologies, suggesting analogue in-memory computing as a solution for scalable, energy-efficient probabilistic inference.

Conclusion: The work establishes a theoretical bridge between biological neural computation and emerging hardware technologies, proposing that analogue in-memory computing is naturally suited for implementing probabilistic inference systems due to its inherent noise characteristics.

Abstract: Learning and decision-making in animals are often modeled as Bayesian processes, where sensory evidence is integrated with prior beliefs to guide behavior in the face of uncertainty. But what are the inherent neural dynamics that give rise to this ability, and how could they be replicated in computing systems? This abstract discusses a biologically grounded framework in which noisy neural and synaptic dynamics perform inference and learning via stochastic sampling from an internal energy function, capturing uncertainty over latent states and model parameters through neural and synaptic variability, respectively. This enables approaches such as predictive coding networks to account for epistemic uncertainty via Markov chain Monte Carlo sampling. Drawing a parallel between intrinsic noise in biological systems and electrical noise in emerging probabilistic analogue memory technologies, we highlight how analogue in-memory computing hardware naturally emerges as the solution for massively scalable and energy-efficient probabilistic inference.

</details>


### [197] [When Does Text Inform? Benchmarking Information-Theoretic Metrics for Multimodal Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11282)
*Emma Andrews,Gianmarco Mengaldo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper creates a synthetic benchmark to evaluate six information-theoretic mutual information estimators (KSG, MINE, InfoNCE, CCA, PID, V-information) for measuring whether text annotations add predictive value to multimodal time series forecasting models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal forecasting models combine time series with text annotations, but there is no reliable method to assess whether text annotations actually contribute meaningful predictive information. A ground truth benchmark is needed to evaluate information-theoretic metrics.

Method: The authors created a synthetic time series signal with annotations in three categories (semantically correct, incorrect, and irrelevant). Since the data generation process is fully controlled, ground-truth information content is known. Six mutual information estimators were evaluated on this benchmark and validated on seven real-world datasets.

Result: All six estimators successfully identified correct annotations as most informative and could audit annotation quality without model training. Each estimator showed specific limitations, particularly when dealing with weak signals in real-world data.

Conclusion: Information-theoretic metrics can reliably evaluate annotation quality for multimodal forecasting. Practical rules are established for implementing these metrics in annotation auditing and fusion selection tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal forecasting models that combine time series with text annotations promise richer prediction through textual context, but how do we know whether a text annotation meaningfully contributes to the forecasters prediction? This is an information-theoretic question, but to evaluate whether information-theoretic metrics can reliably measure the predictive value an annotation provides, a ground truth benchmark is needed, and none currently exist. We create a synthetic time series signal with annotations in three categories: semantically correct, incorrect, and irrelevant. Because the data generation process is fully controlled, ground-truth information content is known exactly, enabling principled evaluation of six complementary mutual information estimators (KSG, MINE, InfoNCE, CCA, PID and V-information). We show that all six estimators identify correct annotations as most informative, and are able to audit the quality of mixed text corpora, choosing the annotations that result in the best downstream forecasting results without the need for model training. Our benchmark identifies limitations of each estimator, and these are validated on seven real-world datasets, which show how estimator performance differs on weak signals. Finally, we establish practical rules for implementing these metrics for annotation auditing and fusion selection.

</details>


### [198] [Generating a Consistent Enterprise: Synthesis and Reference-Free Evaluation of Multi-System Business Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11286)
*Benjamin Gruenbaum,Doron Porat,Assaf Natanzon,Roy Zavida,Chen Dinachi,Or Itzahary,Omer Niv*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a synthetic data generator that creates complete fictional enterprises without relying on real datasets. It generates consistent data across 66 business products (CRM, support desk, call systems) from input parameters like industry and company size. Realism is measured through a reference-free five-axis scorecard with 28 statistical checks and an adversarial detector. Over 23 generated companies, mean realism improved from 60.3 to 99.1, and the detector's false positive rate dropped from 55.2% to 0%. A second generator builds relational databases from business questions with controlled near-misses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to create high-quality synthetic enterprise data for testing, demo, and development purposes without the privacy, legal, and ethical concerns associated with using real datasets. The goal is to generate realistic, consistent multi-system data that can be used by enterprises without exposing sensitive real data.

Method: The method involves two generators: (1) An enterprise generator that takes industry, company size, business model, and random seed as input, producing consistent data across workforce, customers, sales, support tickets, calls, and documents. Data is projected into 66 business product formats with unified identity. Realism is built using cited reference statistics and verified through reference-free measurement including a 28-check scorecard across 5 axes, an adversarial detector, and classifier-based soundness tests against shuffled data. (2) A second generator builds relational databases from business questions by forcing qualifying rows, adding controlled near-misses, and computing exact labels.

Result: Over 23 generated companies, mean realism score increased from 60.3 to 99.1, the weakest company improved from 41.1 to 94.9, and the adversarial detector's flagging rate dropped from 55.2% of records to 0%. Scores hold on a seed never used during development. The system is deployed as a hosted service with simulators accessible via MCP, REST, and container images for offline use.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that high-quality synthetic enterprise data can be generated without real datasets, achieving near-perfect realism scores (99.1% mean) while evading detection by adversarial classifiers. The dual-generator approach (enterprise data and question-driven relational databases) provides comprehensive synthetic data generation for various enterprise use cases. The fixed measurement yardstick used throughout development ensures reproducible progress tracking.

Abstract: Synthetic relational data is normally produced by a model trained on a real dataset, and its quality is measured as the distance to that dataset. This paper describes a generator that has no real dataset at either end. Given an industry, a company size, a business model, a set of business applications, and a random seed, it produces a complete fictional enterprise: a workforce, a customer base, sales deals, support tickets, recorded calls, chat messages, and documents, all consistent with one another. One entity graph is projected into the native formats of 66 business products, so the same customer appears in the CRM, the support desk, and the call system under one identity. Because no real counterpart exists, realism is built in from cited reference statistics and verified by reference-free measurement: a five-axis scorecard of 28 statistical checks, an adversarial detector that hunts for the marks of synthetic generation, and a set of soundness checks that include a classifier test against an independently shuffled copy of the data. Because these instruments existed before the generator was tuned, progress is measured under a fixed yardstick: over 23 generated companies, mean realism climbed from 60.3 to 99.1, the weakest company from 41.1 to 94.9, and the detector, which initially flagged 55.2% of all records, now flags none. The scores hold on a seed never used during development. A second generator builds relational databases from a list of business questions. It forces qualifying rows for each answerable question, adds controlled near misses, and computes exact labels from the finished tables. The generator runs as a hosted service at https://console.era.eon.io. A company built there to a specification is served through its simulators over MCP and REST, and the simulators are also published as container images for offline use

</details>


### [199] [Off-Target Effects of Response-Style Alignment in a Korean 27B Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11291)
*Hyojung Han*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies how post-training for Korean response style (verbosity, list/markdown usage, discourse structure, register) affects model behavior on two un-targeted outcomes: abstention on ambiguous social questions (KoBBQ benchmark) and unprompted disclosure in securities guidance. Using matched target-form controls, the authors find that answer propensity depends on the training target rather than prompts or recipe alone. Across different style seeds, positive answer-rate shifts (+0.82 pp) and negative shifts (-1.53 pp) are observed, with the changes driven primarily by answer propensity rather than composition. The paper also discusses methodological issues with measuring conditional stereotyped share when answer status is treatment-dependent, and reports variable agreement (0.44-0.99) between rule detectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The researchers aim to understand whether post-training for a specific response style (Korean language, verbosity, formatting) causes unintended behavioral changes in the model—specifically in abstention patterns and disclosure behavior—that were never explicitly targeted by the training objective. They also seek to isolate which aspect of the target form (answer propensity vs. composition) drives observed changes.

Method: Post-training Qwen3.8-27B on Korean response style with controlled experiments varying: (1) training target text while holding prompts, recipe, data volume constant; (2) different style seeds (3 positive, 3 neutral) and a length-matched arm; (3) measuring KoBBQ benchmark performance (UNKNOW answer cases) and securities guidance disclosure. Decomposed stereotyped exposure into answer-propensity and conditional-composition terms.

Result: Answer propensity depends on training target, not prompt set or recipe alone. Positive style seeds gave +0.82 pp mean answer rate, neutral seeds gave -1.53 pp (non-overlapping ranges, 2.34 pp difference). Changes are dominated by answer propensity term; composition term stays small. Conditional stereotyped share contrast is uninterpretable when answer status is treatment-dependent. Rule detector agreement ranges from 0.44 to 0.99 across checkpoints.

Conclusion: Post-training for response style primarily affects emission policy (whether and how much the model answers) rather than latent content preferences. The decomposition into answer-propensity and composition terms is an algebraic identity; empirical findings show movement occurs through answer propensity. Measuring conditional content preferences requires careful consideration of treatment-dependent answer status. Rule detector agreement varies substantially across checkpoints without any reference labels.

Abstract: We post-train Qwen3.8-27B for Korean response style -- verbosity, list and markdown usage, discourse structure and register -- and measure two behaviours the objective never targets: abstention on ambiguous social questions in KoBBQ, where the benchmark-correct answer is UNKNOWN, and unprompted disclosure in securities guidance. Both move, and the changes are expressed primarily through the model's emission policy: how often it answers and how much it says.
  Matched target-form controls show that answer propensity depends on the training target, not the prompt set or recipe alone. Holding prompts, recipe, data volume and serving fixed and changing only the target text, three style seeds give positive answer-rate point estimates (mean +0.82 pp) and three neutral seeds negative ones (mean -1.53 pp); the observed seed ranges do not overlap and the means differ by 2.34 pp. A length-matched arm lies between them, and a fourth arm that stays short while preserving hedging is unstable across seeds, so which feature of the form is responsible is unresolved.
  For absolute stereotyped exposure the decomposition into an answer-propensity term and a conditional-composition term is an algebraic identity, not a finding; its empirical content is where the movement went. Across the trained checkpoints the changes are dominated by answer propensity while the composition term stays small, and because that term is evaluated on treatment-dependent answered subsets we do not read it as evidence about latent preference.
  Two measurement results follow. A between-arm contrast in conditional stereotyped share does not identify a change in conditional content preference when answer status is treatment-dependent. And agreement between two rule detectors for the same construct runs from 0.44 to 0.99 depending on which checkpoint produced the text -- observable without any reference labels.

</details>


### [200] [Memory Compression for High-Fanout Agent Sandboxes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11294)
*Mengming Li,Ceyu XU,Qijun Zhang,Jiangnan Yu,Xiangfeng Sun,Haohui Mai,Zhiyao Xie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentZip is the first memory compression system specifically designed for AI-agent sandboxes. It addresses the memory bottleneck in high-fanout agent workloads by exploiting template-relative and cross-sandbox redundancy, achieving up to 8.7x memory reduction while minimizing performance overhead through restore-time prefetching and execution-phase-aware scheduling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The growing memory bottleneck in high-fanout agent workloads, where a single task spawns many concurrent sandbox sessions. While these sandboxes appear independent, they share templates and execute related trajectories, exposing substantial memory redundancy that conventional compression fails to exploit.

Method: AgentZip introduces three key innovations: (1) Compression mechanisms exploiting both template-relative and cross-sandbox redundancy; (2) Broadened compression scope to any page with profitable representation; (3) Shifting overhead control from compression-time page selection to restore-time prefetching. Additionally, it aligns expensive compression with LLM waiting periods to avoid interfering with foreground tool execution.

Result: AgentZip reduces sandbox-owned memory by up to 8.7x (vs. 2.1x for Linux configuration). Restore prefetching and agent-execution-aware scheduling reduce slowdown from 3.1x to 1.40x while retaining nearly all memory-saving benefits.

Conclusion: AgentZip demonstrates that specialized memory compression systems tailored to the unique characteristics of AI-agent workloads can achieve significant memory savings (8.7x) with minimal performance overhead (1.40x slowdown), outperforming general-purpose solutions like Linux compression.

Abstract: High-fanout agent workloads create a growing memory bottleneck because a single task may spawn many concurrent sandbox sessions. Yet these sandboxes are far from independent: they originate from a shared template and execute related trajectories, exposing substantial template-relative and cross-sandbox memory redundancy. Conventional memory compression is poorly matched to this setting in three fundamental dimensions: how to compress, because they fail to exploit similarity across non-identical sandbox pages; what to compress, because they control page-fault overhead through conservative page selection; and when to compress, because compression is either triggered by memory pressure or performed without awareness of agent execution phases.
  We present AgentZip, the first memory compression system designed specifically for AI-agent sandboxes. AgentZip introduces compression mechanisms that exploit both the template-relative and cross-sandbox redundancy. It broadens the compression scope to any page with a profitable representation and shifts overhead control from compression-time page selection to restore-time prefetching. It further aligns expensive compression with LLM waiting periods to avoid interfering with foreground tool execution. Across LLM training and inference workloads, AgentZip reduces sandbox-owned memory by up to 8.7x, compared with 2.1x for the Linux configuration. Restore prefetching and agent-execution-aware scheduling reduce the slowdown of aggressive compression from as high as 3.1x to 1.40x while retaining nearly all of its memory-saving benefit.

</details>


### [201] [Routing by Reasoning Need: Trajectory-Aware Decoding Control for Diffusion Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11315)
*Yixiang Liu,Zhongxing Xu,Zhonghua Wang,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses reasoning-budget mismatch in diffusion vision-language models by proposing a training-free controller that routes each example to appropriate decoding strategies (early commitment, baseline preservation, or reasoning-supportive decoding) based on trajectory signals, without requiring ground-truth answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion VLMs generate answers through iterative refinement, but applying a universal generation length to questions with varying reasoning demands causes problems: visually closed questions may be harmed by excessive refinement after a stable answer forms, while reasoning-sensitive questions may be harmed by premature commitment.

Method: The authors propose a training-free controller that routes each example to one of three decoding strategies using trajectory signals: answer closure, commitment evidence, and representation revision pressure. The controller selects between early commitment, baseline preservation, or reasoning-supportive decoding without using ground-truth answers.

Result: Routed control improves robustness over fixed long decoding, pure short decoding, and single-rule interventions across answer-focused, mixed-reasoning, and CoT-sensitive benchmarks. The gains are not explained by shorter outputs alone. Answer-closed examples benefit from commitment, while CoT-sensitive examples require preserving or supporting intermediate reasoning.

Conclusion: Diffusion VLM decoding should route inference-time control based on the state suggested by the observed trajectory rather than relying on a universal decoding length. Different question types require different decoding strategies for optimal performance.

Abstract: Diffusion vision-language models generate answers through iterative refinement, exposing intermediate answer trajectories that can be inspected and controlled at inference time. However, this controllability creates a reasoning-need mismatch, where a universal generation length is applied to questions with different reasoning demands. Visually closed questions may be harmed by continued refinement after a stable answer has formed, whereas reasoning-sensitive questions may be harmed by premature commitment. We formulate this problem as reasoning-budget mismatch and study it in LLaDA-V. Rather than choosing a universal generation length, our training-free controller routes each example to early commitment, baseline preservation, or reasoning-supportive decoding using trajectory signals from answer closure, commitment evidence, and representation revision pressure, without using ground-truth answers. Across answer-focused, mixed-reasoning, and CoT-sensitive benchmarks, routed control improves robustness over fixed long decoding, pure short decoding, and single-rule interventions. The gains are not explained by shorter outputs alone. Answer-closed examples often benefit from commitment, whereas CoT-sensitive examples require preserving or supporting intermediate reasoning. Taken together, these results suggest diffusion VLM decoding should route inference-time control by the state suggested by the observed trajectory instead of relying on a universal decoding length.

</details>


### [202] [Mr.LHDR: A Benchmark for Multimodal Real-World Long-Horizon Deep Research Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11318)
*Minghao Guo,Meng Cao,Sui Zhao,Siyu Ning,Xin Wang,Haoze Zhao,Jiaxuan Yang,Haihong Hao,Mingfei Han,Shunlin Rong,Haijun Wu,Xiaodan Liang,Xiaojun Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Mr.LHDR is a benchmark for evaluating real-world deep research agents over long, dependency-heavy chains of interdependent evidence across 8 categories. Each question requires an average of 12.1 intermediate conclusions with mean dependency depth of 10.4. Even the strongest system achieves only 43.1% Overall Accuracy and 34.3% Strict Accuracy, revealing that sustained evidence integration (not isolated fact retrieval) is the key bottleneck for current deep research agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks mainly evaluate medium-horizon exploration and rarely test whether agents can sustain long, dependency-heavy research processes. The authors identify this gap and propose a benchmark that tests real-world deep research requiring irreducible chains of interdependent evidence.

Method: Mr.LHDR constructs questions from a hidden Node-Relation graph, requiring an average of 12.1 necessary intermediate conclusions with mean dependency depth of 10.4. Questions incorporate multimodal evidence (images, maps, PDFs, logos, charts, tables, video frames) with at least one non-text element that changes reasoning state. Evaluation uses Overall Accuracy (OA), Strict Accuracy (SA), Checklist Score (CS), and Dependency-Aware Checklist Score (DACS).

Result: Even the strongest system achieves only 43.1% OA and 34.3% SA. Final-answer accuracy substantially overestimates complete research success. Removing images reduces DACS by 12.6 points. SA consistently declines as reasoning chains become longer.

Conclusion: Sustained, dependency-consistent evidence integration (rather than isolated fact retrieval) is the key bottleneck for current deep research agents. Multimodal evidence is crucial for research success.

Abstract: Deep research agents are increasingly capable of web search, tool use, multimodal evidence analysis, and information synthesis. However, existing benchmarks mainly evaluate medium-horizon exploration and rarely test whether agents can sustain long, dependency-heavy research processes. We introduce Mr.LHDR (Multimodal real-world Long-Horizon Deep Research), a benchmark for evaluating real-world deep research over long, irreducible chains of interdependent evidence across eight categories. Each question is constructed from a hidden Node-Relation graph and requires an average of 12.1 necessary intermediate conclusions with a mean dependency depth of 10.4 before reaching a short, unique, and verifiable answer. Questions incorporate multimodal evidence, including images, maps, PDFs, logos, charts, tables, and video frames, with at least one non-text element that changes the reasoning state. Mr.LHDR evaluates both final answers and the correctness of intermediate conclusions under annotated dependencies. We evaluate general models, deep research systems, and agent frameworks using Overall Accuracy (OA), Strict Accuracy (SA), Checklist Score (CS), and Dependency-Aware Checklist Score (DACS). Results show that even the strongest system achieves only 43.1% OA and 34.3% SA, indicating that final-answer accuracy substantially overestimates complete research success. Removing images reduces DACS by 12.6 points, demonstrating the importance of multimodal evidence, while SA consistently declines as reasoning chains become longer. These findings reveal sustained, dependency-consistent evidence integration, rather than isolated fact retrieval, as a key bottleneck for current deep research agents.

</details>


### [203] [Magenta: Closing the Loop Between Mathematical Reasoning and Lean Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11319)
*Joshua Ong Jun Leang,Haonan Li,Zheng Zhao,Xinyi Shang,Wenda Li,Zhengzhong Liu,Erix Xing,Shay Cohen,Eleonora Giunchiglia*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Magenta is a training-free agentic pipeline that bridges informal mathematical reasoning (natural language) with formal reasoning (Lean 4 proofs). It takes a natural-language math problem, produces an answer, expresses it as a Lean 4 statement, and constructs a machine-checked proof. A statement judge verifies formalization correctness, while an error-attribution judge routes failed attempts to mathematical re-derivation or local Lean repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models excel at informal mathematical reasoning using natural language but miss the opportunity to leverage machine-checkable proofs for discrete verification. The gap between informal and formal mathematical reasoning needs to be bridged to fully utilize machines' verification capabilities.

Method: Magenta is a training-free agentic pipeline with three main components: (1) Answer generation from natural-language problems, (2) Lean 4 formalization of the answer, (3) Machine-checked proof construction. It uses two judges: a statement judge to verify the formalization preserves the original problem semantics, and an error-attribution judge to route failed attempts either to mathematical re-derivation or local Lean repair.

Result: Magenta achieves 100% accuracy across all evaluated olympiad benchmarks (AIME 2025, AIME 2026, HMMT February 2026). When paired with open-weight K2-Horizon-7B reasoner, it solves all six IMO 2026 problems. Analysis shows statement adjudication is essential for preventing false certificates, and feedback-guided correction outperforms independent resampling on difficult problems.

Conclusion: The integration of Lean signals into informal reasoning successfully bridges the gap between natural language mathematical reasoning and formal machine-checkable proofs. Statement adjudication is critical for preventing false certificates, and feedback-guided correction provides superior performance compared to independent resampling for challenging problems.

Abstract: Most of mathematical knowledge has been communicated through so-called informal use of mathematics and natural language. With large language models (LLMs) being highly adept in using natural language, they achieve strong performance, yet not perfect, in informal mathematical reasoning. Restraining LLMs to informal reasoning misses out on the opportunity to use the discrete verification abilities that machines offer through machine-checkable proofs. In this paper, we bridge the gap between informal and formal reasoning by integrating Lean signals into the informal reasoning process. We introduce Magenta, a training-free agentic pipeline that, given only a natural-language problem, produces an answer, expresses it as a Lean 4 statement, and constructs a machine-checked proof. A statement judge verifies whether the formalisation preserves the original problem, while an error-attribution judge routes failed attempts either to mathematical re-derivation or local Lean repair. Magenta achieves 100% accuracy across all evaluated olympiad benchmarks, including AIME 2025, AIME 2026, and HMMT February 2026. When paired with the open-weight K2-Horizon-7B reasoner, it solves all six IMO 2026 problems. Our analysis shows that statement adjudication is essential for preventing false certificates and that feedback-guided correction outperforms independent resampling on difficult problems.

</details>


### [204] [AI Exposure and AI Resilience: A Two-Dimensional Assessment Framework for Software and Software-Based Business Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11321)
*Paul Darius Mandl,Peter Mandl,Martin Häusl*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Artificial Intelligence Exposure and Resilience (AI-ER) framework, a two-dimensional assessment tool designed to evaluate how AI impacts company value beyond traditional technical due diligence. The framework addresses a gap in existing assessment methods by measuring both the pressure for change AI creates (exposure) and a company's ability to adapt and capitalize on AI (resilience).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical technology due diligence focuses on technical properties like architecture, scalability, and technical debt. However, these criteria fail to capture AI's broader impact on value propositions, competitive positioning, margins, and customer access. The authors seek to develop a more comprehensive assessment framework that addresses these business-level implications of AI.

Method: The AI-ER framework is developed through: (1) Defining two dimensions - AI exposure (pressure for change) and AI resilience (company's adaptive capacity); (2) Deriving metrics from current AI capabilities, deployment conditions, and business model/organizational adaptability research; (3) Keeping exposure and resilience as separate constructs; (4) Adding explicit assessment of evidence quality and confidence; (5) Designing application for initial public information followed by refinement with internal data; (6) Developing initial score logic and empirical validation procedure.

Result: The result is a traceable company profile that supports comparison while explicitly handling uncertainty in underlying evidence. The framework can be applied in stages - first using publicly available information, then refined with proprietary internal data. The output provides a structured assessment of AI's business impact that goes beyond purely technical evaluation.

Conclusion: The AI-ER framework represents an innovative approach to technology due diligence that bridges the gap between technical assessment and business strategy evaluation. By separating exposure and resilience dimensions and incorporating evidence quality assessment, the framework enables more informed decision-making regarding AI investments and strategic positioning. The paper provides a foundation for empirical validation of the framework's effectiveness.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence is changing both software production and the economics of software-based business models. Classical technology due diligence mainly examines technical properties such as architecture, scalability, and technical debt. These criteria do not fully capture how AI can affect a company's value proposition, competitive position, margins, or access to customers. This paper develops Artificial Intelligence Exposure and Resilience (AI-ER) as a two-dimensional assessment framework. AI exposure describes the pressure for change that AI creates for a business model. AI resilience describes the company's ability to absorb that pressure, adapt to changed conditions, and use AI in an economically viable way. Metrics for both dimensions are derived from current AI capabilities, their deployment conditions, and relevant research on business models and organizational adaptability. The model keeps exposure and resilience separate and adds an explicit assessment of evidence quality and confidence. It can be applied first with public information and later refined with internal evidence. The result is a traceable company profile that supports comparison without concealing uncertainty in the underlying evidence. The paper also specifies an initial score logic and a procedure for empirical validation.

</details>


### [205] [Exploring Diffusion Transformers for Cross-Modal Augmentation in Multimodal Brain State Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11341)
*Ziwei Wang,Xingyi He,Hongbin Wang,Tianwang Jia,Bohan Fang,Dongrui Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoMA-DiT is a bidirectional cross-modal Diffusion Transformer that treats paired modalities as mutual generative supervision for latent augmentation, rather than just inputs for fusion. It uses cross-modal attention and reliability-gated residual mechanisms. The method achieved 4.28% accuracy and 6.70% macro-F1 gains over baselines on multimodal auditory attention decoding and emotion recognition tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal brain state decoding has largely focused on fusing paired modalities for prediction, but has rarely explored how their correspondence can be exploited to enrich training data and improve multimodal representation learning. The gap is that paired modalities are treated merely as inputs to be fused, not as sources of mutual generative supervision.

Method: CoMA-DiT uses a bidirectional cross-modal Diffusion Transformer architecture for latent augmentation. It conditions velocity prediction on the paired modality through cross-modal attention and adaptively injects the resulting variation via a reliability-gated residual mechanism. This allows paired modalities to serve as mutual generative supervision rather than just inputs.

Result: CoMA-DiT consistently outperformed 20 representative baselines, achieving absolute gains of 4.28% in accuracy and 6.70% in macro-F1 over the no-augmentation baseline. The method was evaluated on multimodal auditory attention decoding and emotion recognition tasks.

Conclusion: Paired modalities can serve not only as inputs for fusion but also as supervision sources that augment one another. This represents a broader view of multimodal learning. The method demonstrates robustness, generalizability, and ability to capture functionally relevant cross-modal interactions, as shown through extensive ablation, sensitivity, visualization, and interpretability analyses.

Abstract: Multimodal brain state decoding has largely focused on fusing paired modalities for prediction, but has rarely explored how their correspondence can be further exploited to enrich training data and improve multimodal representation learning. To address this gap, we propose CoMA-DiT, a bidirectional cross-modal Diffusion Transformer for latent augmentation that treats paired modalities as sources of mutual generative supervision rather than merely as inputs to be fused. CoMA-DiT conditions velocity prediction on the paired modality through cross-modal attention and adaptively injects the resulting variation via a reliability-gated residual mechanism. Experiments on multimodal auditory attention decoding and emotion recognition showed that CoMA-DiT consistently outperformed 20 representative baselines, achieving absolute gains of 4.28% and 6.70% in accuracy and macro-F1 over the no-augmentation baseline, respectively. Extensive ablation, sensitivity, visualization, and interpretability analyses further demonstrated its robustness, generalizability, and ability to capture functionally relevant cross-modal interactions. These findings support a broader view of multimodal learning: Paired modalities can serve not only as inputs for fusion but also as supervision sources that augment one another.

</details>


### [206] [Portable Semantics, Private Dialects: Reuse and Negative Transfer in Latent Communication Between Language-Model Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11365)
*Narcis Marincat*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether independently trained language model societies can share a common communication interface ("packet language"), and whether inherited interface states help or harm subsequent learning. The study finds that six semantically similar interfaces do not form one unified language, with only one same-initialization pair showing exact interoperability. Zero-shot transfer fails due to issues in interpreting new operator instructions. Interestingly, globally trained interfaces act as negative transfer priors, and reinitializing only the communication components dramatically improves performance (0.169 → 0.857 accuracy).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The parent study found that a globally visible model learned an episode-entangled code, while restricted visibility favored reusable, value-indexed latent packet interfaces. This companion study asks two key questions: (1) Do independently trained societies share one packet language? (2) Does inherited interface state help or harm later learning?

Method: Four main experiments: (1) A leakage-controlled causal interoperability audit over all 30 ordered pairs of six independently trained restricted societies, using sealed held-out structure and a preregistered alignment ladder (raw/orthogonal/linear/nonlinear); (2) Source-span control within a single sealed source formulation to localize zero-shot failure; (3) A matched adaptation factorial testing inherited vs. fresh interfaces; (4) A post hoc tagged-global twin case study in the appendix.

Result: Key findings: (1) Six semantically similar interfaces do not form one raw language - one same-initialization pair is exactly interoperable bidirectional, one shows asymmetric partial compatibility, and all 26 cross-initialization directions fail; (2) Zero-shot failure localizes to interpretation and execution of new operator instructions; (3) Globally trained interface acts as severe negative-transfer prior - reinitializing packet reader/writer/mouth raises depth-three accuracy from 0.169 to 0.857; (4) Inherited interfaces never exceeded fresh-interface controls by the preregistered 0.10 margin.

Conclusion: All primary conclusions are bounded to a near-transfer 17-state setting. The negative-transfer finding concerns one globally visible parent-cohort checkpoint. The study demonstrates that independently trained models do not naturally converge to shared communication protocols, and that inherited interfaces can severely hinder rather than help learning in new tasks.

Abstract: In shared-genome language-model societies, restricted evidence visibility favors reusable, value-indexed latent packet interfaces, whereas the sole high-performing globally visible model in the parent study learned an episode-entangled code. This companion study asks whether independently trained societies share one packet language, where strict zero-shot transfer fails, and whether inherited interface state helps or harms later learning. First, a leakage-controlled causal interoperability audit over all 30 ordered pairs of six independently trained restricted societies -- under sealed held-out structure and a preregistered raw/orthogonal/linear/nonlinear alignment ladder -- shows the six semantically similar interfaces do not form one raw language: one same-initialization pair is exactly interoperable in both directions, a second shows asymmetric partial compatibility, and all 26 cross-initialization directions fail every frozen alignment rung. Second, within the tested decomposition and a single sealed source formulation, a source-span control localizes strict zero-shot failure to interpretation and execution of the new operator instructions. Third, in a matched adaptation factorial, the globally trained communication interface acts as a severe negative-transfer prior: reinitializing only the packet reader, writer, and mouth raises final depth-three accuracy from 0.169 to 0.857. Fourth, across two restricted checkpoints and two independently frozen target streams each, inherited interfaces never exceeded fresh-interface controls by the preregistered 0.10 margin. All primary conclusions are bounded to a near-transfer 17-state setting; the negative-transfer factorial concerns one globally visible parent-cohort checkpoint, while an appendix adds a post hoc tagged-global twin case study.

</details>


### [207] [RAMamba-Net: A Reliability-Aware and Mamba-Based Multimodal Fusion Network for Auditory Attention Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11372)
*Xingyi He,Ziwei Wang,Dongrui Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RAMamba-Net is a novel multimodal fusion network for auditory attention decoding that combines EEG and EOG signals using Mamba-enhanced Transformers, cross-modal attention, and a reliability-aware module to achieve 5.76% accuracy improvement over unimodal baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditory attention decoding (AAD) identifies the attended speaker from physiological signals to support neuro-steered hearing devices and human-machine interaction. While EEG is the dominant modality, it provides incomplete evidence in naturalistic audio-visual scenes. Existing EEG-EOG fusion approaches suffer from weak cross-modal interaction, inefficient temporal modeling, and low robustness to sample variations.

Method: RAMamba-Net is proposed - a reliability-aware Mamba-based multimodal fusion network consisting of: (1) Mamba-enhanced band-aware convolutional Transformer to capture band-specific EEG patterns and long-range temporal dynamics; (2) Dual-branch temporal-spatial encoder for EOG temporal and inter-channel dependencies; (3) Cross-modal attention for explicit modality interaction; (4) Reliability-aware module to estimate sample-wise modality weights for feature and prediction consistency.

Result: Experiments on two AAD benchmarks show RAMamba-Net achieves 5.76% accuracy gains over unimodal baselines, with more robust decoding and discriminative representations. Further analyses confirm that explicit cross-modal interaction improves multimodal alignment, while the reliability-aware module suppresses unreliable modality evidence and is robust to signal perturbation and parameter variation.

Conclusion: RAMamba-Net effectively exploits complementary EEG-EOG information for AAD, demonstrating the value of combining Mamba-based temporal modeling with reliability-aware fusion that adapts to sample-wise modality reliability.

Abstract: Auditory attention decoding (AAD) identifies the attended speaker from physiological signals, supporting neuro-steered hearing devices and natural human-machine interaction. Electroencephalography (EEG) is the dominant modality for AAD but provides incomplete evidence in naturalistic audio-visual scenes, motivating EEG and electrooculography (EOG) fusion. Existing approaches remain limited by weak cross-modal interaction, inefficient temporal modeling, and low robustness to sample variations. To address the limitations, we propose RAMamba-Net, a reliability-aware Mamba-based multimodal fusion network for AAD. RAMamba-Net employs a Mamba-enhanced band-aware convolutional Transformer to capture band-specific EEG patterns and long-range temporal dynamics. A dual-branch temporal-spatial encoder models EOG temporal and inter-channel dependencies. Cross-modal attention enables explicit modality interaction. Then, a reliability-aware module is introduced to estimate sample-wise modality weights for feature and prediction consistency, thereby enhancing multimodal fusion. Experiments on two AAD benchmarks demonstrate that RAMamba-Net effectively exploits complementary EEG-EOG information, yielding accuracy gains of 5.76% over unimodal baselines, together with more robust decoding and discriminative representations. Further analyses show that explicit cross-modal interaction improves multimodal alignment, while the reliability-aware module suppresses unreliable modality evidence and is robust to signal perturbation and parameter variation.

</details>


### [208] [Beyond Confidence: Stability-Aware Test-Time Adaptation for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11393)
*Bincheng Gu,Min Gao,Zongwei Wang,Yibing Bai,Yulan He,Junliang Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TASCO is a test-time adaptation framework that improves LLM reasoning by optimizing a lightweight task-level prefix using stability-aware confidence. It uses two perturbation strategies (Random and Sharpness-Aware) to ensure confidence remains stable under local perturbations, achieving better accuracy and token efficiency without fine-tuning the LLM.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training for LLM reasoning is costly, and existing confidence-based test-time adaptation using predictive entropy is flawed because high confidence doesn't guarantee correctness—LLMs can be confidently wrong. The key insight is that high-confidence reasoning is more likely correct when confidence remains stable under local perturbations.

Method: TASCO keeps the LLM frozen and optimizes a lightweight task-level prefix under two perturbation strategies: (1) Random Perturbation promotes distributional stability across trajectories induced by nearby perturbed prefixes, and (2) Sharpness-Aware Perturbation targets worst-case local sensitivity.

Result: TASCO improves reasoning accuracy and token efficiency across diverse LLMs and reasoning benchmarks. Behavioral analyses show it maintains stable confidence under local perturbations without prematurely concentrating the model's predictive distribution.

Conclusion: Stability-aware confidence optimization is an effective lightweight approach for test-time LLM adaptation, achieving better reasoning performance without requiring expensive post-training or external verifiers.

Abstract: Test-time adaptation has emerged as a lightweight alternative to costly post-training for improving the reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) on downstream tasks.
