Predictive entropy provides a model-derived signal for such adaptation, guiding models toward higher-confidence reasoning states without external verifiers or reward models. However, higher confidence does not necessarily imply correctness, as LLMs may remain highly confident along incorrect reasoning trajectories. We observe that high-confidence reasoning is more likely to be correct when confidence remains stable under local perturbations. Based on this observation, we propose Test-Time Adaptation via Stability-Aware Confidence Optimization (TASCO), a framework that incorporates local stability into confidence-based test-time adaptation while keeping the LLM frozen. TASCO operationalizes local stability by optimizing a lightweight task-level prefix under two alternative perturbation strategies: Random Perturbation promotes distributional stability across trajectories induced by nearby perturbed prefixes, whereas Sharpness-Aware Perturbation targets worst-case local sensitivity. Experiments demonstrate that TASCO improves reasoning accuracy and token efficiency across diverse LLMs and reasoning benchmarks, while behavioral analyses show that it maintains stable confidence under local perturbations without prematurely concentrating the model's predictive distribution.

</details>


### [209] [From Queries to Narratives: Cultural Heritage Data Stories for Knowledge Graph Exploration and Quality Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11403)
*Tabea Tietz,Torsten Schrade,Etienne Posthumus,Linnaea Söhn,Jonatan Jalle Steller,Jörg Waitelonis,Harald Sack*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents data stories as a solution to make cultural-heritage knowledge graphs (like NFDI4Culture-KG) more accessible for exploration and quality assessment. Data stories are narrative documents that combine explanatory text, images, executable SPARQL queries, and visualized results. The LODEON platform with Sparnatural and AI-assisted authoring is introduced as a proof-of-concept.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cultural-heritage KGs contain millions of triples but are difficult for users to discover and explore. Writing SPARQL queries requires in-depth understanding of the data model, which many domain researchers are unwilling to invest. The data is highly specialized, heterogeneous, and constantly growing, making it challenging to know what it contains or which questions it can answer.

Method: The authors propose "data stories" as narrative documents that integrate explanatory text and images with executable SPARQL queries and their visualized results. They introduce LODEON, an authoring platform including Sparnatural and AI-supported authoring assistants. Every claim in the narrative is backed by an explicit query, making the stories transparent and reproducible.

Result: The paper presents a proof-of-concept platform (LODEON) and reflects on lessons learned from hands-on seminars and workshops. Early experience suggests that data stories make cultural-heritage KGs more accessible for both exploration and quality assessment.

Conclusion: Data stories can lower barriers to exploring cultural-heritage knowledge graphs, create reproducible narratives, and surface data-quality issues hidden in aggregate statistics. The LODEON platform demonstrates the feasibility of this approach, with early user feedback supporting its effectiveness.

Abstract: Cultural-heritage KGs such as the NFDI4Culture-KG contain millions of triples about artworks, music, inscriptions, historical events, and the people and places connected to them. For many users, however, discovering this knowledge can be difficult. While SPARQL can be learned, writing meaningful queries first requires an in-depth understanding of the graph's data model, an investment many domain researchers and practitioners are unwilling to make. Even with existing user interfaces, a starting point and some guidance are usually needed, because the data contained in the graph is highly specialized, heterogeneous, and constantly growing, making it challenging to know what it contains or which questions it can answer. In this paper, we present data stories as a way not only to lower this barrier, but also to turn exploration into data-quality assessment, and thus combine accessible querying with the discovery of issues that remain hidden in aggregate statistics. In this contribution, a data story is understood as a narrative document that integrates explanatory text and images with executable SPARQL queries and their visualized results. It is described how they are authored against the graph and how they serve several purposes: guiding users through an unfamiliar graph, creating reproducible narratives, and surfacing data-quality issues previously hidden in aggregate statistics. The authoring platform LODEON including its Sparnatural and AI-supported authoring assistants is introduced as a proof-of-concept. Within the authoring environment, every claim made about the data can be backed by an explicit query, making these narratives transparent and reproducible. This paper also reflects on lessons learned from hands-on seminars and workshops. Early experience suggests that such data stories make cultural-heritage knowledge graphs more accessible for both exploration and quality assessment.

</details>


### [210] [LLMs as Post-hoc Auditors of Physiological Plausibility in Symbolic Regression: A Clinician-Evaluated Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11431)
*Jorge López-Varela,J. Ignacio Hidalgo,José-Manuel Muñoz,Omar Costilla-Reyes,Esther Maqueda,Jesus Moreno-Fernandez,Tomás González-Vidal,J. Manuel Velasco,Oscar Garnica*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) as post-processing tools to improve the interpretability and plausibility assessment of Symbolic Regression models derived from grammar-based Genetic Programming, specifically for body fat percentage estimation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While Symbolic Regression can produce interpretable mathematical expressions from multivariate data, evolved models are often complex or scientifically inconsistent. The researchers aimed to investigate whether LLMs can assist in analyzing and ranking these expressions according to medical plausibility and interpretability.

Method: Four symbolic expressions were analyzed by three LLMs over three repeated runs (total: 36 assessments per LLM). A panel of three clinicians then evaluated the resulting interpretations and rankings to assess their validity and usefulness.

Result: Comparative model-ranking outputs from LLMs received more favorable clinician assessments than isolated term-level interpretations. However, LLMs also produced physiologically and mathematically questionable explanations, indicating limitations in their autonomous validation capabilities.

Conclusion: LLMs are better suited for comparative auditing under expert oversight rather than autonomous validation of Symbolic Regression models. The study suggests LLMs can assist in explainability tasks but require human expert oversight to ensure medical and mathematical validity.

Abstract: Genetic Programming and its variants, such as grammatical evolution, are widely used in Symbolic Regression to derive mathematical expressions from multivariate data. In addition to predictive accuracy, models are appreciated for their potential to provide interpretability, offering explicit equations that relate input variables to outcomes. However, achieving interpretability and plausibility remains challenging, as evolved models may be complex or scientifically inconsistent. In this study, we explore whether Large Language Models, can assist in improving the explainability of Symbolic Regression models generated by evolutionary computation methods. Building upon our previous work on estimating body fat percentage using grammar-based Genetic Programming , we investigate the use of LLMs as post-processing tools to analyze and rank evolved expressions according to their interpretability and medical plausibility. Four symbolic expressions are analysed by three LLMs over three repeated runs, and the resulting interpretations and rankings are assessed by a panel of three clinicians. Across the three LLMs, comparative model-ranking outputs received more favorable clinician assessments than isolated term-level interpretations. However, the LLMs also produced physiologically and mathematically questionable explanations, indicating that they are better suited to comparative auditing under expert oversight than to autonomous validation.\blfootnote{The present work is an extended version of a paper submitted into a journal.

</details>


### [211] [Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades for Efficient Heterogeneous Model Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11446)
*Yilin Zhang,Han Jiang,Cai Xu,Ying Liu,Wei Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CAUC is a post-hoc framework for heterogeneous model collaboration that calibrates each model's confidence scores to establish a common reliability scale for deployment decisions, decoupling policies from specific model pools or budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing approaches for heterogeneous model collaboration either rely on trained routers (tied to fixed task and model pools) or raw-confidence cascades (with unreliable thresholds). These methods adapt poorly to changing model pools and deployment budgets, requiring a unified decision criterion.

Method: Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades (CAUC) independently calibrates each model's confidence using validation data, establishing a common reliability scale for accepting early predictions, invoking stronger models, or selectively combining outputs.

Result: On six language benchmarks: 1.9% average relative accuracy improvement over strong-model-only inference while avoiding ~47% of strong-model calls. On image classification benchmarks: maintains or improves predictive performance while reducing GFLOPs by up to 57%.

Conclusion: Calibration gives confidence thresholds an explicit selective-risk interpretation, whereas uncalibrated scores offer no comparable reliability guarantee. CAUC provides a flexible framework that generalizes across different model pools and operating budgets.

Abstract: Heterogeneous model collaboration seeks to exploit the complementary strengths of different models to balance predictive performance and inference cost. Existing approaches typically rely either on trained routers, which tie routing decisions to a fixed task and model pool, or on raw-confidence cascades, whose thresholds lack consistent reliability semantics across heterogeneous models. Consequently, these approaches adapt poorly to changing model pools and deployment budgets. We propose Calibration-Aware Uncertainty Cascades (CAUC), a simple post-hoc framework that independently calibrates each model's confidence and selects deployment policies using validation data. The resulting calibrated confidence scores establish a common reliability scale for accepting an early prediction, invoking a stronger model, or selectively combining model outputs. This unified decision criterion decouples deployment policies from any particular model pool or operating budget. We further show theoretically that calibration gives confidence thresholds an explicit selective-risk interpretation, whereas uncalibrated scores offer no comparable reliability guarantee. Extensive experiments demonstrate that, across six language benchmarks, CAUC achieves an average relative accuracy improvement of 1.9% over strong-model-only inference while avoiding approximately 47% of strong-model calls. On image classification benchmarks, it maintains or improves predictive performance while reducing measured GFLOPs by up to 57%.

</details>


### [212] [RouteRepair: Instance-Level Failure Diagnosis and Targeted Repair in LLM-Based Automated Heuristic Design for Routing Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11452)
*Binghao Ji,Di Huang,Jiahui Fang,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RouteRepair is a novel LLM-based method that diagnoses and repairs weaknesses in routing heuristics by analyzing instance-level performance, applying targeted modifications to specific heuristic components while preserving well-performing behaviors, and validating interventions through matched parent-child evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based automated heuristic design methods for routing optimization suffer from a critical limitation: aggregate evaluation masks recurrent failures on particular instance structures. This prevents identifying and fixing specific weaknesses in generated heuristics.

Method: RouteRepair combines routing evidence, solver behavior, and program context to define bounded repair objectives. It diagnoses parent-specific weaknesses from instance-level performance and applies targeted modifications to corresponding heuristic components while protecting behavior that already performs well. Each intervention is validated through matched parent-child evaluation of failure recovery and collateral degradation. Experiments cover TSP and CVRP using constructive search, guided local search (GLS), and ant colony optimization (ACO).

Result: RouteRepair-GLS reduces the mean TSP optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587% (56.6% improvement). The constructive CVRP heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to the savings heuristic. Generated ACO priors also outperform matched hand-designed priors.

Conclusion: Failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can improve routing heuristics on difficult instances while preserving performance on cases they already solve well. This approach successfully addresses the limitation of aggregate evaluation by focusing on instance-specific weaknesses.

Abstract: Efficient routing optimization is essential to freight transportation, urban logistics, and shared mobility, where high-quality heuristics are often required under limited computational budgets. Recent large language model (LLM)-based automated heuristic design methods can generate effective routing rules, but aggregate evaluation may mask recurrent failures on particular instance structures. To address this limitation, this study develops RouteRepair, which diagnoses parent-specific weaknesses from instance-level performance and applies targeted modifications to the corresponding heuristic components while protecting behavior that already performs well. Routing evidence, solver behavior, and program context are combined to define bounded repair objectives, and each intervention is validated through matched parent-child evaluation of failure recovery and collateral degradation. Experiments on the traveling salesman problem (TSP) and capacitated vehicle routing problem (CVRP) span constructive search, guided local search, and ant colony optimization. RouteRepair-GLS reduces the mean TSP optimality gap from 1.7476% to 0.7587%, while the constructive CVRP heuristic lowers average route cost by 1.91% relative to the savings heuristic; the generated ACO priors also outperform matched hand-designed priors. These results show that failure-aware, evidence-constrained refinement can improve routing heuristics on difficult instances while preserving performance on cases they already solve well.

</details>


### [213] [Flexible and Interpretable Accent Distance Measurements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11458)
*Charles McGhee,Mark J. F. Gales,Kate M. Knill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes using articulatory representations (from articulatory inversion) combined with optimal transport as a new framework for comparing accents between speakers. This approach aims to be both interpretable (like phonetic formant analysis) and applicable to arbitrary recording types (like accent embeddings).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the limitations of existing accent comparison methods: phonetic approaches (vowel formants) are interpretable but time-consuming and not suitable for connected speech, while accent embeddings from TTS research are flexible but not interpretable. The authors seek a method that is both interpretable and broadly applicable.

Method: The paper proposes two key innovations: (1) Using articulatory representations derived through articulatory inversion as an interpretable basis for accent comparison, and (2) Applying optimal transport as a mathematical framework to enable accent comparison across arbitrary recording types.

Result: The paper demonstrates that articulatory representations combined with optimal transport can serve as an interpretable framework for accent comparison that works across different recording types.

Conclusion: Articulatory representations from articulatory inversion, when paired with optimal transport, provide an interpretable and flexible approach for comparing accents across different speech contexts and recording types.

Abstract: Determining the differences between two speakers' accents is a fundamental task in linguistics and speech technology research. The methodology used to measure these differences depends on the specific research area. A phonetics researcher may demonstrate accent variation by comparing vowel formants in paired recordings of individual words. These results will be interpretable, but the recordings will be time-consuming to collect and may not be representative of connected speech. Accented Text-to-Speech (TTS) research has pushed towards using accent embeddings derived from accent classification tasks. These embeddings can be produced from any speech recording, but are not readily interpretable. In this paper, we demonstrate that articulatory representations created through articulatory inversion can be used as an interpretable basis for accent comparison and that optimal transport provides a framework for accent comparison across arbitrary recording types.

</details>


### [214] [The Convention Gap: Towards Measuring Implicit Communication in Cooperative AI Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11489)
*Makoto Fukushima,Hua-Dong Xiong,Ehsan Moradi Pari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "convention gap" metric to measure implicit communication in cooperative AI, defined as the difference between predicted failure probability from literal message content and observed failure rate. Using the card game Hanabi, analysis of ~101,000 game plays reveals humans have a +26.2pp gap while AI-AI pairs show nearly zero gap (-0.7pp), suggesting human cooperation relies on implicit conventions not captured by AI-AI benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation benchmarks test AI agents against other AIs, but human cooperation relies on implicit conventions—shared protocols for interpreting meaning beyond literal messages. This study argues that AI-AI benchmarks may not capture these implicit communication mechanisms, potentially leading to misleading assessments of AI effectiveness in human-AI collaboration.

Method: The authors proposed the "convention gap" metric and replayed approximately 101,000 play actions from three public datasets: human-human (hanab.live), AI-AI (HOAD), and human-AI (HanabiData). They computed the exact posterior failure probability using Hanabi's finite deck and deterministic hint constraints, then compared predicted failure rates against observed failure rates across different game configurations.

Result: The convention gap was +26.2 percentage points (pp) in human pairs, -0.7pp in AI pairs, and +16.4pp in human-AI pairs. The gap was concentrated on plays of cards that received no hints (+46pp in human pairs). Among human-AI games, human failure rates varied from 14.4% to 34.4% depending on the AI partner, with gaps ranging from +24.1 to +6.2pp. Notably, the partner eliciting the largest gap produced the fewest human failures. Off-Belief Learning agents showed a gap of +1.6pp at the convention-free level, rising to +21.7pp with convention content.

Conclusion: Convention compatibility, rather than AI-AI performance, may predict an AI's effectiveness with human partners. The gap metric successfully separates human from AI play at the agent level and provides a more meaningful measure of collaborative capability than traditional game scores, which depended on roster composition rather than revealing cooperative mechanisms.

Abstract: Cooperative AI agents are evaluated against other AIs, yet human cooperation relies on implicit conventions---shared protocols for reading meaning beyond the literal message---which AI-AI benchmarks may not capture. We propose the \emph{convention gap}, the difference between the failure probability predicted from the literal content of communication and the observed failure rate, as a metric of implicit communication. In the card game Hanabi, the finite deck and deterministic hint constraints make this posterior exactly computable. We replayed about 101,000 play actions from three public datasets of human-human (hanab.live), AI-AI (HOAD), and human-AI (HanabiData) games. The gap was +26.2 percentage points (pp) in human pairs, $-$0.7~pp in AI pairs, and +16.4~pp in human-AI pairs, and was concentrated on plays of cards that had received no hints (+46~pp in human pairs). Within human-AI play, the literal information available to humans was similar across the three AI partners (mean predicted failure 38--41\%), but human failure rates ranged from 14.4\% to 34.4\% and the gap from +24.1 to +6.2~pp; the partner eliciting the largest gap produced the fewest human failures. Game score carried different information: it depended on each corpus's roster composition, whereas the gap separated human from AI play at the agent level. As a known-answer check, Off-Belief Learning agents, whose convention content is controlled by construction, gave a gap of +1.6~pp at the convention-free level, rising monotonically to +21.7~pp. These results suggest that convention compatibility, rather than AI-AI performance, may predict an AI's effectiveness with human partners.

</details>


### [215] [Published Unlearning Numbers Move Per Checkpoint, and Not Because the Removed Data Survives: An Audit of 263 Released Batch-Normalized Checkpoints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11490)
*Junlong Shen Xingyu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents an audit methodology for verifying machine unlearning in released model checkpoints, specifically focusing on batch-normalization statistics as a side channel that reveals whether training data has been properly removed. The study analyzes 221 released checkpoints and finds that 47 can be shown to retain traces of removed data, with real but narrow consequences for downstream decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to develop reliable methods to verify if machine learning models have truly "forgotten" or removed specific training data, which is important for privacy and regulatory compliance (right to be forgotten). The authors identify that standard evaluation methods may not capture whether data has actually been unlearned, as models can retain traces of removed data in various ways.

Method: The method involves: 1) Comparing unlearned models against retrained references; 2) Using batch-normalization statistics as a side channel (these are shipped with models but not updated by gradient steps); 3) Refitting models on "kept" data at bit-identical weights to detect drift; 4) Testing whether exchanging kept records for removed ones in a fitting pool affects model behavior; 5) Measuring how far a checkpoint's shipped state has drifted from any refit.

Result: Results show: 47 out of 221 released checkpoints (47/221) move past the spread their release's seeds show when analyzed; 12 verdicts cross a decision threshold; 4 clear a measured recalibration budget; 2 clear it on every replicate; a population trained near its own criterion yields none. The key finding is that batch-normalization statistics serve as a side channel revealing whether data was truly unlearned, and the moving property is the checkpoint itself, not the unlearning method.

Conclusion: The conclusion is that batch-normalization statistics provide a verifiable side channel for unlearning audits in vision models. The paper recommends that releases should name their fitting convention alongside the checkpoint number, particularly for batch-normalized vision models where this channel exists. The consequences for published decisions are real but narrow.

Abstract: An unlearning audit reads its verdict off numbers that an unlearned model and its retrained reference each publish, and both also ship batch-normalization statistics that no gradient step wrote and no release records. Refitting them on kept data at bit-identical weights moves 47 of 221 released checkpoints past the spread their own release's seeds show, several inside a method whose average does not move: what moves is the checkpoint's property, not its method's. What does the moving is not the removed data surviving in the state: exchanging kept records for removed ones inside a fixed fitting pool moves a published cell by almost nothing, while how far a checkpoint's shipped state has drifted from any refit does track it. The consequence for a published decision is real but narrow: twelve verdicts cross, four clear a measured recalibration budget, two clear it on every replicate, and a population we trained and sited near its own criterion yields none. A release should therefore name the fitting convention beside the number, on the batch-normalized vision models where this channel exists.

</details>


### [216] [From Document Silos to Process Intelligence: A Multi-Layer Knowledge Graph for CMC Process Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11493)
*Reza Amirmoshiri,Faryad Sahneh,Yasser Jangjou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a modular agentic-AI platform that transforms fragmented CMC (Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls) process development documents into a queryable dual-layer knowledge graph. The system uses a lexical base layer and an intelligence layer with LLM agents operating across both. Evaluation on 505 questions from 38 Sanofi development reports showed 95% Tier-1 accuracy and 85% Tier-2 pass rate, revealing limitations of traditional accuracy metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The traditional CMC process development generates enormous technical information across multiple stages, but this knowledge is fragmented across functions and heterogeneous formats, causing traceability gaps and significant knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing. There was a need for a unified, queryable system to manage this fragmented knowledge.

Method: The platform converts heterogeneous process-development documents (digital, scanned, handwritten, multilingual) into a dual-layer knowledge graph. The base layer is a lexical graph with Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy for lossless ingestion. The intelligence layer extracts ontology-aligned entities and bridges cross-document concepts through a provenance-anchored domain graph. LLM agents operate across both layers, selecting the optimal retrieval path for each question. A router agent selects between layers based on question type. Evaluation used a novel three-tier protocol on 505 questions from 38 Sanofi development reports.

Result: Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy reached 95%, indicating strong platform reliability. However, the stricter Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate was 85%, with degradation on comparative and corpus-wide questions. This revealed a failure taxonomy that Tier-1 accuracy alone fails to capture.

Conclusion: The dual-layer knowledge graph architecture successfully transforms fragmented CMC documents into structured process intelligence. The three-tier evaluation protocol provides a more rigorous assessment than traditional accuracy metrics, revealing failure modes in comparative and corpus-wide questions. Graph-based architectures are anticipated to see broader adoption in pharma for transforming fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence.

Abstract: Chemistry, Manufacturing and Controls (CMC) process development generates an enormous body of technical information across a multi-stage, knowledge-intensive continuum from drug discovery to commercial manufacturing. This knowledge is traditionally fragmented across functions and heterogeneous formats, causing traceability gaps and significant knowledge-management costs during technology transfer and regulatory filing. We present a modular agentic-AI platform that converts a heterogeneous corpus of process-development documents into a queryable, dual-layer knowledge graph. A base knowledge layer builds a lexical graph with a Document-Section-Chunk hierarchy through lossless ingestion of digital, scanned, handwritten, and multilingual documents, while an intelligence layer extracts ontology-aligned entities and bridges cross-document concepts through a provenance-anchored domain graph. LLM agents operate across both layers, selecting the retrieval path best suited to each question. We evaluate the lexical layer with a novel three-tier protocol measuring the deployment-fidelity of a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system on proprietary data, demonstrated on 505 questions curated from 38 development reports of a Sanofi small-molecule program. Tier-1 multiple-choice accuracy of 95% signals strong platform reliability; the stricter Tier-2 LLM-judge pass rate of 85%, which degrades on comparative and corpus-wide questions, reveals a failure taxonomy that Tier-1 accuracy alone fails to capture. A router agent selects between layers according to question type. We anticipate this protocol will enable future designers of agentic platforms to assess their systems against nonpublic databases, and that graph-based architectures will see broader adoption in pharma as a means of transforming fragmented document repositories into structured process intelligence.

</details>


### [217] [ActMap: Single-Pass Uncertainty Quantification from Generation-Time Activation Maps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11498)
*Jacopo Dardini,Roberta Calegari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ActMap compresses LLM hidden-state trajectories during generation into a compact 12×32×128 tensor that enables fast, accurate uncertainty estimation from a single generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Practical UQ for LLMs requires determining answer trustworthiness from a single generation, but existing methods either sample multiple times (inefficient), use only output token probabilities (limited), or reduce internal computations to single hidden states (lossy).

Method: ActMap is a white-box representation capturing temporal-statistic channels across all layers and generated tokens during generation. It produces a fixed 96 KiB tensor (12×32×128) with preserved structure across transformer depth and pooled hidden coordinates. A lightweight Vision Transformer classifier reads this map to estimate correctness probability in under a millisecond.

Result: ActMap consistently outperforms sampling, token-probability, attention, and embedding baselines across short-answer QA, direct-answer math, and summarization factuality with three 7-8B instruction-tuned models. It matches ACT-ViT (trained on 67× larger tensors) at essentially the same mean AUROC while achieving lower calibration error on 10/12 model-dataset pairs.

Conclusion: ActMap provides a practical primitive for scalable model oversight, supporting abstention, routing, and selective verification from a single generation with no measurable generation overhead and fixed shape across model architectures.

Abstract: Practical uncertainty quantification (UQ) for large language models must decide,
  from a single generation, whether a specific answer should be trusted. Existing
  methods either sample multiple generations, read only output-token probabilities,
  or reduce the model's internal computation to a single hidden state. We introduce
  ActMap, a white-box representation that compresses the generation-time hidden-
  state trajectory (every layer, every generated token) into a fixed $12 \times 32
  \times 128$ tensor of temporal-statistic channels that preserves structure across
  transformer depth and pooled hidden coordinates. The map is captured during the
  generation pass with no measurable overhead, has a fixed shape across model
  depths and hidden sizes, and occupies 96 KiB: a compact artifact that can be
  retained for audit-relevant generations and probed directly, with occlusion
  analysis localizing the classifier's signal to mid-depth regions of the map. A
  lightweight classifier, instantiated as a compact Vision Transformer, reads an
  estimated correctness probability from each map in a fraction of a millisecond;
  capacity-matched MLPs perform comparably, indicating the representation itself
  carries the result. Trained and evaluated in-domain on short-answer QA, direct-
  answer math, and summarization factuality with three instruction-tuned 7-8B
  models, ActMap consistently outperforms sampling, token-probability, attention,
  and embedding baselines, and matches ACT-ViT, a detector trained on dense
  activation tensors $67 \times$ larger, at essentially the same mean AUROC with
  lower calibration error on ten of twelve pairs. The resulting score supports
  abstention, routing, and selective verification from a single generation, making
  it a practical primitive for scalable oversight of deployed models.

</details>


### [218] [Extending SMT Solving with Non-Ground Clause Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11509)
*Yasmine Briefs,Christoph Weidenbach*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a new calculus for non-ground SMT solving that combines ground instantiations with non-ground conflict analysis. The key innovation is performing resolution steps in conflict analysis on original non-ground clauses rather than just ground instances, producing more general learned clauses. The calculus subsumes multiple existing approaches including CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and Resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantifier instantiation approaches in non-ground SMT solving generate ground instances and use CDCL(T)-style reasoning. When conflicts occur, conflict analysis only learns ground clauses, even though the conflict originates from non-ground clauses. This limits proof efficiency, as non-ground reasoning can yield exponentially shorter proofs than purely ground reasoning. The authors aim to bridge this gap by developing a calculus that leverages both ground instantiation and non-ground conflict analysis.

Method: The proposed calculus integrates three components: (1) ground instantiation rules for generating instances from quantified formulas, (2) CDCL(T)-style reasoning rules for SAT/SMT solving on ground instances, and (3) non-ground conflict analysis where resolution steps are performed on original non-ground clauses rather than their ground instances. The solver operates on ground instances, but conflict analysis traces resolution back to the original non-ground clauses, producing more general learned clauses. The approach also incorporates chronological backtracking.

Result: The learned clauses produced by non-ground conflict analysis are typically more general than ground conflict clauses. With a suitable strategy, these learned clauses are non-redundant. The theoretical contribution demonstrates that this calculus provides a unified framework that simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and even Resolution, establishing connections between these diverse proof systems.

Conclusion: The proposed calculus offers a common setting for CDCL(T)-style SMT solving, various instantiation-based procedures, and non-ground clause learning. It successfully combines the practical benefits of ground instantiation with the theoretical advantages of non-ground reasoning, potentially enabling more efficient proofs in SMT solving. The framework's ability to simulate multiple existing systems demonstrates its comprehensiveness and theoretical foundation.

Abstract: Quantifier instantiation is currently the main approach to non-ground SMT solving: solvers generate ground instances and solve the resulting ground SMT problems with CDCL(T)-style reasoning. When a conflict is found, conflict analysis learns only a ground clause, even though the conflict comes from instances of non-ground clauses. Yet non-ground reasoning can give exponentially shorter proofs than purely ground reasoning. We propose a calculus that consists of ground instantiations, CDCL(T)-style rules, and non-ground conflict analysis. The solver reasons on ground instances, but the resolution steps of conflict analysis are performed on their original non-ground clauses. This produces learned clauses that are typically more general than the ground conflict. With a suitable strategy, the learned clauses are even non-redundant. We also show how chronological backtracking can be included in SMT solving. Our calculus gives a common setting for CDCL(T)-style SMT solving, a range of instantiation-based procedures, and non-ground clause learning, and we prove that it simulates CDCL, SCL(FOL), SCL(T), and even Resolution.

</details>


### [219] [Lightweight LiDAR-Based Cone Detection Framework Using Random Forest for Formula Student Driverless](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11527)
*Márk Mező-Kerekes,Péter Praksz,Chang Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A lightweight LiDAR-only perception pipeline for Formula Student Driverless vehicles that runs on CPU-only hardware, achieving 98.33% F1-score with 3.13 ms runtime through ground removal, IMU motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and Random Forest classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Formula Student Driverless vehicles require reliable, low-latency perception, but existing pipelines rely on deep learning and multi-sensor fusion with GPU acceleration, which are computationally expensive for resource-constrained platforms.

Method: LiDAR-only perception pipeline combining ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and geometric feature-based Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis reduced model inputs from 12 to 7 features.

Result: Achieves 98.33% F1-score on 2,371 labeled clusters from real FSD events, with end-to-end runtime of 3.13 ms on CPU-only hardware. Feature reduction from 12 to 7 preserved performance.

Conclusion: Provides a practical and reproducible baseline for resource-constrained autonomous racing teams, with released dataset, labeling tool, and trained models.

Abstract: Reliable, low-latency perception is crucial for Formula Student Driverless vehicles, yet many existing pipelines rely on deep learning and multi-sensor fusion, often requiring GPU acceleration. This paper presents a lightweight LiDAR-only perception pipeline tailored for CPU execution, combining ground removal, IMU-based motion compensation, DBSCAN clustering, and geometric feature-based Random Forest classification. Feature importance analysis reduced the model input from 12 to 7 features while preserving performance. Evaluated on 2,371 labeled clusters collected from real FSD events, the pipeline achieves an F1-score of 98.33% and an end-to-end runtime of 3.13 ms on CPU-only hardware. The released dataset, labeling tool, and trained models provide a practical and reproducible baseline for other resource-constrained autonomous racing teams.

</details>


### [220] [Prompt Revision as a Source of Cultural Bias in Text-to-Image Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11532)
*Aleksandra Urman,Elsa Lichtenegger,Salima Jaoua,Azza Bouleimen,Robin Forsberg,Corinna Hertweck,Stefania Ionescu,Nicolò Pagan,Ancsa Hannak,Joachim Baumann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces WORLDVIEW, a multilingual benchmark of 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings, to audit the silent prompt revision layer in commercial text-to-image systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5). The study reveals that non-Western and non-Anglophone cultural contexts are disproportionately marked, flattened into narrow vocabularies, and reduced to stereotypes by the revision layer, which itself is identified as a causal source of cultural bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audits of cultural bias in text-to-image systems only examine final images and treat generation as a single pipeline, making it impossible to identify where bias originates. The silent prompt revision step—hidden from users—had never been audited as a potential source of cultural stereotyping.

Method: The authors created WORLDVIEW, a multilingual benchmark with 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. They conducted a three-step analysis examining: (1) how heavily each cultural context is marked by the revision layer, (2) whether the revision layer flattens context into a narrow vocabulary, and (3) whether that vocabulary contains stereotypical representations. They also compared images generated from original versus revised prompts on models without a revision layer to establish causality.

Result: Relative to a no-context English baseline, the US is the least-marked context, while non-Western and non-Anglophone contexts are marked far more heavily, flattened into narrow vocabularies applied across topically diverse prompts, and reduced to recognizable cultural stereotypes. The revision layer itself was identified as a previously undocumented, causal source of this stereotyping.

Conclusion: To locate and fix cultural bias in text-to-image systems, researchers must audit the system as deployed (including the revision layer), not the model alone. The revision layer acts as a hidden amplifier of cultural stereotypes, particularly targeting non-Western and non-Anglophone contexts.

Abstract: Commercial text-to-image systems silently revise user prompts before generating images, a step users typically cannot disable or even see. Yet, existing audits of cultural bias examine only the final images and treat generation as a single pipeline, so they cannot tell where the bias originates. We introduce WORLDVIEW, a multilingual benchmark of 8,960 prompts across 15 languages and 31 language-context pairings. Using it, we audit the revision layer in three systems (DALL-E-3, Imagen-4, GPT-Image-1.5) through a three-step analysis of how heavily it marks each cultural context, whether it flattens that context into a narrow vocabulary, and whether that vocabulary is stereotypical. Relative to a no-context English baseline, the US is the least-marked context, while non-Western and non-Anglophone contexts are marked far more heavily, flattened into narrow vocabularies applied across topically diverse prompts, and reduced to recognizable cultural stereotypes. Comparing images from original versus revised prompts on models without a revision layer, we identify the layer itself as a previously undocumented, causal source of this stereotyping. To locate cultural bias, and fix it, we must audit the system as deployed, not the model alone.

</details>


### [221] [Characterizing Job Power Elasticity for Power-Flexible AI Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11542)
*Philip Colangelo,Charles Dawson,Shayan Sengupta,Ayse Coskun,Varun Sivaram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents the first systematic characterization of job power elasticity in LLM training, introducing the Power Flexibility Index (PFI) to quantify performance costs of power reductions. Through 131 LLM training runs on H200 and H100 GPUs, they demonstrate that PFI-aware power allocation can recover 63% of the performance gap under 30% power reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM training is a rapidly growing source of electricity demand in data centers, with power availability being a primary bottleneck to AI infrastructure growth. Making power consumption flexible could unlock additional power for AI, limit electricity price increases, and improve grid utilization. However, understanding how training performance changes when GPU power is reduced is essential first.

Method: The authors collected data from 131 LLM training runs on H200 (plus 24 validation runs and 34 matched H100 runs), including dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining and fine-tuning tasks, up to 32 GPUs. They introduced the Power Flexibility Index (PFI), a normalized metric quantifying performance cost of power reductions. They identified telemetry signals that predict PFI at runtime and demonstrated PFI-aware power allocation under power constraints.

Result: LLM training jobs exhibit substantial but variable power elasticity. Telemetry signals can predict PFI at runtime. Under a 30% power reduction, PFI-aware power allocation recovers ~1.5k tokens/s per job, achieving 63% of the performance gap between equal-weight allocation and an oracle with perfect information.

Conclusion: Power elasticity is a measurable property of training jobs. PFI provides a control primitive for SLA-aware power flexibility. This work establishes a foundation for power-aware, grid-responsive AI infrastructure that can maximize total tokens/second throughput under power constraints.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) training is among the fastest-growing sources of electricity demand in modern data centers, and power availability is a primary bottleneck to continued AI infrastructure growth. Making the power consumption of these workloads flexible could unlock additional power for AI growth, limit increases in electricity prices, and improve the utilization of existing grid infrastructure. However, to realize this flexibility, we must first understand how the performance of training workloads changes when GPU power is reduced.
  This paper presents the first systematic characterization of \emph{job power elasticity} (the sensitivity of throughput to power reductions) in LLM training. To quantify elasticity, we introduce the \emph{Power Flexibility Index (PFI)}, a normalized metric that quantifies the performance cost of power reductions and provides a control primitive for SLA-aware power flexibility.
  We collect data from 131 LLM training runs on H200 (plus 24 H200 validation runs and 34 matched H100 runs), including both dense and mixture-of-experts models, pretraining and fine-tuning tasks, and up to 32 GPUs. We find that LLM training jobs exhibit substantial but variable power elasticity, and we identify telemetry signals that predict PFI at runtime. Finally, we demonstrate that PFI-aware power allocation maximizes total tokens/second throughput under power constraints. Under a 30\% power reduction, PFI-aware power allocation recovers ~1.5k tokens/s per job, 63\% of the performance gap between an equal-weight allocation and an oracle with perfect information. Our results establish power elasticity as a measurable property of training jobs and provide a foundation for power-aware, grid-responsive AI infrastructure.

</details>


### [222] [Enabling Knowledge Graph Understanding at Scale with the EXplore Your Graphs ENgine (EXYGEN)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11569)
*Harshdeep Singh,Yurui Zhu,Giovanni Colavizza,Matteo Romanello*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EXYGEN is a framework for knowledge graph (KG) understanding that enables conversational access to KGs at scale using LLMs without task-specific fine-tuning. It integrates VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas into a RAG pipeline and introduces a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy for very large KGs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses two key challenges: (1) Can LLMs perform text-to-SPARQL generation effectively using only automatically derived structured metadata and small graph samples rather than task-specific fine-tuning? (2) How to generate structured metadata from very large KGs where metadata generation becomes computationally intractable?

Method: For the first question, the authors integrate VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas into a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and ablate KG-derived context on the SciQA benchmark. For the second question, they introduce a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy that preserves structural diversity while remaining computationally tractable.

Result: The best configuration (combining ShEx schemas, retrieved triples, and example question-query pairs) achieves an exact match of 0.419 on execution results without any LLM fine-tuning. Lexical metrics like F1 poorly predict query correctness. Larger general-purpose LLMs outperform smaller code-specialized ones with sufficient context. On OpenCitations Meta and GESIS, the sampling strategy retains high predicate coverage with minimal triple loss and reduces runtime by over 80x; on ORKG, sampling is the only tractable path to obtain complete metadata.

Conclusion: Structured schema context and lightweight prompting can substantially reduce reliance on fine-tuning for scalable conversational access to KGs. However, closing the remaining gap to fully fine-tuned approaches will likely require reducing dependence on curated question-query exemplars (through synthetic generation or execution-feedback-driven approaches) and validating these findings beyond a single benchmark.

Abstract: We present EXYGEN (EXplore Your Graphs ENgine), a framework for knowledge graph (KG) understanding that enables conversational access to KGs at scale. We address two questions in sequence. First, how effectively can LLMs perform text-to-SPARQL generation given only automatically derived structured metadata and small graph samples, rather than task-specific fine-tuning? We integrate VoID descriptions and ShEx schemas into a retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipeline and ablate KG-derived context on the SciQA benchmark. Our best configuration -- combining ShEx schemas, retrieved triples, and example question-query pairs -- reaches an exact match of 0.419 on execution results without any LLM fine-tuning. We further find that lexical metrics such as F1 poorly predict query correctness, and that larger general-purpose LLMs can outperform smaller code-specialized ones once given sufficient context. Second, we ask how to generate the structured metadata that this method relies on from very large KGs, where KG metadata generation becomes computationally intractable. We introduce a predicate-coverage-aware parallel graph sampling strategy that preserves structural diversity while remaining computationally tractable. On OpenCitations Meta and GESIS, it retains high predicate coverage with minimal triple loss and reduces runtime by over 80x; on ORKG, sampling is not just faster but the only tractable path to obtain complete metadata. Together, these results show that structured schema context and lightweight prompting can substantially reduce reliance on fine-tuning for scalable conversational access to KGs, though closing the remaining gap to fully fine-tuned approaches will likely require reducing dependence on curated question-query exemplars -- whether through synthetic generation or an execution-feedback-driven approach -- and validating these findings beyond a single benchmark.

</details>


### [223] [Making Alternative Data Work: Context-Augmented LLMs for Financial Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11607)
*Jihoon Kwon,Lawrence Liu,Daekyung Park,Sumin Kim,Haverty Jack,Hoyoung Lee,Katherine Bjorkman,Josh McKenney,Peter Laurelli,Nicole Kagan,Zach Golkhou,Thorsten Neumann,Edward Tong,Pete Petersen,Yoon Kim,Alejandro Lopez-Lira,Yongjae Lee,Chanyeol Choi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes using large language models (LLMs) with a two-agent framework to forecast firm financial performance by integrating alternative data (consumer transactions, web traffic, prediction markets) with traditional financial information through in-context learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Alternative data can provide timely signals for forecasting firm performance but has limitations: limited historical coverage, relevance only to specific targets/firms, and distribution across heterogeneous channels. Conventional forecasting approaches struggle to incorporate this data flexibly, while LLMs can interpret instructions, learn from in-context examples, and combine heterogeneous information without task-specific parameter updates.

Method: The authors propose a two-agent framework: (1) the first agent identifies which firms each alternative data channel is likely to be informative for, and (2) the second agent predicts revenue using firm- and channel-specific context. They evaluate this framework across four commercial alternative data channels.

Result: Adding alternative data in context alongside other financial information improves the LLM's forecasting relative to either source alone, and these forecasts are more accurate than those of standard forecasting baselines.

Conclusion: LLMs provide a flexible and practical approach to integrating alternative data with heterogeneous financial information for firm performance forecasting.

Abstract: When forecasting a firm's future financial performance, alternative data - data collected from non-traditional sources such as consumer transactions, web traffic, and prediction markets - can provide timely signals about firms' operating activities and broader market conditions. These signals may reveal information that is not captured by traditional public sources and can therefore provide complementary information for forecasting firms' future financial performance. However, firm-level alternative data often have limited historical coverage, are relevant only to specific prediction targets or subsets of firms, and are distributed across numerous heterogeneous channels, making them difficult to incorporate flexibly into conventional forecasting approaches. Meanwhile, large language models (LLMs) can interpret instructions, learn from in-context examples, and generate predictions by combining heterogeneous information without task-specific parameter updates. Motivated by this potential flexibility, we investigate whether an LLM can forecast firm performance by integrating alternative data with other financial information through in-context learning. We propose a two-agent framework that first identifies the firms for which each alternative data channel is likely to be informative and then predicts revenue using firm- and channel-specific context. We evaluate the framework across four commercial alternative data channels. In our experiments, adding alternative data in context alongside other financial information improves the LLM's forecasting relative to either source alone, and these forecasts are more accurate than those of standard forecasting baselines. These findings suggest that LLMs provide a flexible and practical approach to integrating alternative data with heterogeneous financial information.

</details>


### [224] [Distributed Optimization of Modular Production Systems using Model-based Reinforcement Learning with Inverse Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11615)
*Andreas Schwung,Steve Yuwono,Sofiene Lassoued,Dorothea Schwung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a novel model-based reinforcement learning framework for controlling highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems. The approach uses approximate inverse process models within RL policy training, separating actuation dynamics from state space dynamics and enabling RL training directly in task space.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the control challenges of highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems that require efficient self-learning control capabilities. Traditional methods struggle with the complexity of these systems, necessitating a data-driven approach that can handle heterogeneous production modules.

Method: The method employs a novel model-based RL framework that incorporates approximate inverse process models. It uses a lightweight feedforward architecture for the inverse models, which are integrated into the policy network of standard RL algorithms. This approach disentangles actuation dynamics from state space dynamics, allowing RL training to occur solely in task space.

Result: The approach was applied to a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous production modules. The results demonstrate efficiency improvements for modular manufacturing units in terms of both performance and training speed, with particularly notable improvements for off-policy algorithms.

Conclusion: The proposed framework successfully enables data-driven self-learning control for flexible modular manufacturing systems, achieving improved performance and faster training compared to traditional approaches, especially when using off-policy RL algorithms.

Abstract: This paper presents a novel approach for data-driven self-learning control of highly flexible, modular manufacturing systems. Specifically, we employ a novel framework for model-based reinforcement learning which introduces approximate inverse process models within the training of reinforcement policies. This approach disentangles the learning of actuation dynamics and the dynamics in state space, resulting in RL-based training solely within the task space. We propose a lightweight feedforward architecture for approximate inverse models and integrate them within the policy network of standard RL algorithms. We apply the approach to a laboratory modular production testbed with heterogeneous production modules. The results underline the efficiency improvements for modular manufacturing units in terms of both performance and training speed, particularly for off-policy algorithms.

</details>


### [225] [MAPLE: Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11636)
*Kesheng Chen,Yamin Hu,Wenjian Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAPLE is an LLM-based optimization agent that maintains optimization problems through successive natural-language requests, combining language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It preserves optimization state, accepted plans, and candidate solutions for subsequent updates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain practitioners lack OR expertise and dedicated support. Existing methods centered on isolated requests offer limited support for rapid adaptation in dynamic real-world operations where demand, resources, and priorities change. There's a need for systems that preserve earlier decisions and reuse useful search results across updates.

Method: MAPLE combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It retains the optimization program, accepted plans, earlier updates, and candidate solutions for subsequent requests. Evaluated on NLDO benchmark with 15 trajectories and 180 updates spanning selection, scheduling, rostering, routing, and cloud-resource placement.

Result: MAPLE completes all trajectories and achieves online scalar quality of 0.951 and Pareto hypervolume ratio of 0.875. Maintaining executable state improves update validity and preserves useful search information across substantial revisions.

Conclusion: MAPLE successfully enables dynamic optimization maintenance through natural language, making optimization more accessible to domain practitioners while preserving state and search history across updates.

Abstract: Domain practitioners understand their business constraints but may lack operations-research expertise or dedicated support. LLM-based optimization agents translate natural-language requirements into models or solver programs that established optimization tools can execute. This progress makes optimization more accessible, but real-world operations are dynamic: changing demand, resources, and priorities require updates to data, constraints, and objectives. Methods centered on isolated requests offer limited support for rapid adaptation that preserves earlier decisions and reuses useful search results. We introduce MAPLE (Memory-Augmented Planning with Language and Evolution), an agent for maintaining optimization problems through successive natural-language requests. MAPLE combines language-based problem construction with mathematical programming and evolutionary search. It retains the optimization program, accepted plans, earlier updates, and candidate solutions for subsequent requests. We introduce NLDO, a benchmark of 15 trajectories and 180 updates spanning selection, scheduling, rostering, routing, and cloud-resource placement. In the main evaluation, MAPLE completes all trajectories and achieves online scalar quality of 0.951 and a Pareto hypervolume ratio of 0.875. Controlled comparisons further show that maintaining executable state improves update validity and can preserve useful search information across substantial revisions.

</details>


### [226] [Autonomy, Social Norms, and Alignment: Towards a Developmental Framework for Autonomous Artificial Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11660)
*Marica Notte,Ludovica Marinucci,Vieri Giuliano Santucci*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of aligning AI agents with human goals in dynamic, unstructured environments. The authors propose that current AI systems relying on pre-existing datasets and human feedback are insufficient for embodied agents in unknown contexts. They suggest using intrinsic motivations (curiosity, competence) and a developmental approach where norms are learned through experience, similar to how children acquire social norms. The novel contribution is framing regulatory sandboxes as pedagogical environments where AI alignment can develop as a formative process through interaction and cooperation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap between powerful large-scale AI models and their deployment in embodied agents stems from reliance on pre-existing data and human feedback, which fails in dynamic or unknown contexts. Current alignment approaches are insufficient for agents that must adapt through direct environmental interaction. The challenge is ensuring AI remains aligned with human goals while gaining autonomy in unstructured environments.

Method: The paper proposes a philosophical/conceptual framework: (1) Use intrinsic motivations (curiosity, competence) to guide exploration and learning; (2) Implement an epistemological process where norms are rooted in experience, starting from simple situated principles and building toward complex rules; (3) Treat regulatory sandboxes as pedagogical environments for progressive AI alignment training.

Result: The paper develops a theoretical framework suggesting that: (1) AI alignment should be a developmental process, not a fixed state; (2) Moral agent status should be gradually attributed based on demonstrated responsibility (following Dennett); (3) Regulatory sandboxes can serve as dynamic training spaces where AI learns alignment through increasingly complex scenarios; (4) Alignment emerges through interaction, cooperation with other moral agents, and progressive autonomy.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that AI alignment in dynamic environments requires a paradigm shift: from static rule-based approaches to a developmental, experiential learning model. Regulatory sandboxes represent novel pedagogical environments where AI can gradually develop alignment through interaction. This approach treats alignment as a formative process similar to human moral development, where agents earn progressively greater autonomy by demonstrating responsible behavior in increasingly complex scenarios.

Abstract: In recent years, artificial intelligence has made extraordinary progress thanks to large-scale models capable of generalization and the generation of complex outputs. However, transferring this potential into embodied agents reveals a significant limitation: the most advanced systems rely on pre-existing datasets and human feedback strategies that are powerful but insufficient in dynamic or unknown contexts. To adapt, an agent must acquire knowledge through direct interaction with its environment. One strategy to address this challenge involves introducing higher-level mechanisms, such as intrinsic motivations, which leverage curiosity and competence, to guide exploration and learning in complex environments. While this flexibility expands autonomy, it complicates the task of ensuring agents remain aligned with human goals. Alignment, already a challenge for artificial systems in general, becomes even more complex in unstructured and dynamic contexts where predefined rules prove insufficient. To be effective and adaptable, norms must be rooted in experience through an epistemological process that starting from simple, situated principles allows for the gradual construction of more complex rules through experience, autonomous learning, and cooperation with other moral agents. Similarly to children learning social norms by exploring their environment and participating in collective practices, artificial agents must also be educated toward alignment. Following Dennett, the status of a moral agent is not innate but is attributed gradually based on the ability to responsibly manage increasing degrees of freedom. From this perspective, the regulatory sandboxes can be viewed as pedagogical environments for AI: dynamic spaces where alignment develops as a formative process, progressively shaping autonomous behaviors through interaction and cooperation in scenarios of increasing complexity.

</details>


### [227] [Geospatial AI, Dataverse Metadata, and the Study of Place-Based Government](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11674)
*Danny EBanks,Devika Jain*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper describes building a knowledge graph from Harvard Dataverse's 150,000+ research datasets, organizing 102,650 datasets into a network of 215,985 nodes and 528,003 edges. About 43,991 datasets (42.9%) contain geospatial metadata, with 7,654 (17.4%) identified as policy-relevant. The main challenge is place resolution - the same location appears as disconnected nodes in the graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Harvard Dataverse hosts over 150,000 datasets with geographic information entered as free text by depositors, but this geographic metadata has never been assembled into a searchable structure. The researchers aimed to create a searchable knowledge graph to make this geospatial data discoverable and useful for policy research.

Method: The researchers constructed a knowledge graph from the repository's public data and metadata, organizing datasets within a network linking to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations. They used conservative keyword searches to identify policy-relevant datasets and created use cases demonstrating how community language models, stance detection with geographic aggregation, and partisan language tools can attach discourse to place.

Result: The graph contains 102,650 datasets within a 215,985-node network with 528,003 edges. Of these, 43,991 datasets (42.9%) carry at least one geospatial field, and 96.9% of all nodes sit in a single connected component. A conservative keyword search identifies 7,654 geospatially tagged datasets (17.4%) as directly policy-relevant, with elections and legislatures being the largest cluster, followed by government administration, health policy, transportation, and education. The coverage skews toward American, city-level data.

Conclusion: The graph provides a concrete setting for developing AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution. The central obstacle is place resolution - the same location appears as many disconnected nodes. The researchers document significant coverage skew toward American, city-level data, which limits the generalizability of the knowledge graph.

Abstract: Harvard Dataverse hosts over 150,000 research datasets, but the geographic information those datasets carry is entered as free text by depositors and has never been assembled into a searchable structure. We construct a knowledge graph from the repository's public data and metadata, organizing 102,650 datasets within a 215,985-node network of 528,003 edges linking datasets to keywords, publications, subjects, journals, and locations. Of those datasets, 43,991 (42.9 percent) carry at least one geospatial field, geographic coverage, geographic unit, or a bounding box and 96.9 percent of all nodes sit in a single connected component, so datasets remain reachable from one another even when their geospatial metadata share nothing in common. A conservative keyword search identifies 7,654 geospatially tagged datasets (17.4 percent) as directly policy-relevant, with elections and legislatures the largest cluster, followed by government administration, health policy, transportation, and education. Five datasets illustrate how this metadata behaves across policy domains and spatial scales, and an extended use case shows how community language models, stance detection with geographic aggregation, and partisan language bridging tools can attach discourse to place. The central obstacle is place resolution: the same location appears as many disconnected nodes. We argue that the graph provides a concrete setting for developing AI-driven metadata enrichment and entity resolution, and we document its coverage skew toward American, city-level data.

</details>


### [228] [COBRA-Skills: Contextual Bandit-Guided Evolution for Agent Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11682)
*Pingchen Lu,Xiangyi Wang,Xiang Li,Jie Mao,Zikun Qu,Junfeng Luo,Yao Shu,Bryan Kian Hsiang Low,Zhongxiang Dai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: COBRA-Skills is an efficient framework for optimizing reusable skills for LLM agents, using budgeted sequential optimization with contextual-bandit-guided prioritization and evidence-grounded skill evolution. It reduces optimization costs by 55-58% while achieving superior performance across 6 benchmarks and 3 target models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing skill optimization methods for LLM agents rely on costly execution-based evaluation and substantial task data, making them inefficient. There is a need for a more efficient approach that can optimize skills with limited data and reduced computational cost.

Method: COBRA-Skills formulates skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. It combines contextual-bandit-guided prioritization (to select promising candidates for evaluation) with evidence-grounded skill evolution (to refine skills based on execution feedback). This selective allocation approach minimizes unnecessary evaluations while continuously improving the skill population.

Result: COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models. It reduces optimization cost by 55-58% relative to SkillOpt, using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. The method remains robust to changes in agent harness and performs effectively when the target model itself is used for skill generation and refinement.

Conclusion: COBRA-Skills presents a highly efficient and effective approach for skill optimization in LLM agents. By intelligently allocating evaluations and continuously refining skills from execution feedback, it achieves superior performance with significantly reduced computational cost and data requirements, making it practical for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can benefit from reusable skills distilled from prior task experience, yet existing skill optimization methods often rely on costly execution-based evaluation and substantial task data. We introduce \textbf{COBRA-Skills}, an efficient framework that formulates skill optimization as budgeted sequential optimization over a dynamically evolving candidate space. COBRA-Skills couples contextual-bandit-guided prioritization with evidence-grounded skill evolution, selectively allocating evaluations to promising or informative candidates while continually refining the skill population from execution feedback. Across six heterogeneous agent benchmarks and three target models, COBRA-Skills consistently achieves the strongest average performance among compared methods, while reducing optimization cost by 55--58\% relative to SkillOpt and using only 50 unique optimization examples per benchmark. Further analyses show that COBRA-Skills remains robust to changes in the agent harness and performs effectively when the target model itself is used for skill generation and refinement.

</details>


### [229] [When Agents Disagree: Bayesian Backward Reasoning as a Label-Free Anchor for Multi-Agent Collective Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11709)
*Ken Chen,Wei Wang,Sachith Seneviratne,Hansani Weeratunge,Saman Halgamuge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of aggregating multiple LLM agent predictions when they disagree. The authors propose using Bayesian backward reasoning (reverse posterior) alongside traditional forward reasoning, then combine estimates using Jensen-Shannon divergence-based strategies (MinJS, FwdJS, LogLin) to achieve better collective decision-making than forward-only aggregation methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: When multiple LLM agents provide conflicting answers, existing collective decision-making methods (voting, LLM judges) rely on forward reasoning and share the same evidence-to-label factorization, potentially inheriting correlated errors. The authors aim to create differently factorized approximations of the underlying posterior to reduce shared errors across agents.

Method: The authors construct reverse posteriors for each instance through Bayesian backward reasoning from an explicit likelihood. They use Jensen-Shannon divergence to rank agents by cross-path consistency (how well their forward and reverse predictions agree). This signal underlies three strategies: hard selection (MinJS), soft reweighting (FwdJS), and log-linear fusion (LogLin). A two-stage calibration is also proposed for settings with labeled data.

Result: Evaluated on DDXPlus across five LLM backbones, all proposed strategies show consistent improvements. MinJS outperforms random selection across all backbones. FwdJS generally improves over the strongest baseline. LogLin achieves the best performance, with largest gains on subsets where agents disagree. The reverse posterior, despite weaker standalone accuracy, provides more useful complementary information than forward-only alternatives.

Conclusion: Combining forward and reverse posteriors with different factorizations reduces shared errors in LLM agent aggregation. The reverse posterior serves as a more useful anchor than forward-only alternatives. Jensen-Shannon divergence-based cross-path consistency is an effective signal for selecting/reweighting agents. LogLin fusion achieves state-of-the-art performance, especially when agents disagree.

Abstract: When multiple LLM agents yield conflicting answers, the decision-making process dictates whether agent diversity improves performance or merely compounds shared errors. Existing collective decision-making methods, including voting, electoral rules, and LLM judges, rely on forward reasoning: they map evidence to labels in one direction. Although these methods can combine diverse forward traces, they still aggregate estimates that share this evidence-to-label factorization and can inherit correlated errors within the forward pool. We therefore construct a reverse posterior for each instance through Bayesian backward reasoning from an explicit likelihood. The forward and reverse posteriors provide differently factorized approximations of the underlying posterior. Because estimates from different factorizations may tend to share the same error less often, we use Jensen-Shannon divergence to rank agents by cross-path consistency. This cross-path consistency signal underlies three strategies: hard selection (MinJS), soft reweighting (FwdJS), and log-linear fusion (LogLin). Evaluated on DDXPlus across five LLM backbones, our proposed strategies show consistent improvements: MinJS outperforms random selection across all backbones, FwdJS generally improves over the strongest baseline, and LogLin achieves the best performance among the evaluated methods, with its largest gains on the subset where the agents disagree. Despite its weaker standalone accuracy, the reverse posterior serves as a more useful anchor than forward-only alternatives, providing complementary information for collective decision-making. When labeled data are available, a lightweight two-stage calibration can further refine the reverse anchor and improve aggregation performance.

</details>


### [230] [SIRF: A Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model for Industrial Content Risk Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11752)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model) internalizes platform policies into LLM weights via continued pretraining (CPT) using EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting, and account-level CoT, enabling high-precision risk control at ultra-low latency without additional human annotation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Industrial content risk control requires not average accuracy but the ability to auto-handle risk under high precision and second-level latency constraints. Existing approaches fail to apply rules at high precision in ultra-low-latency verdict-only deployment scenarios.

Method: 1) Policy synthesis without human annotation using EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting, and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT); 2) Internalization into model weights via continued pretraining (CPT); 3) Deployment as tree-model adjudication layer with verdict-only output.

Result: SIRF-8B-SFT achieves 71.3% Black Recall@P95, a +15.1pp improvement over baseline (Qwen3-8B-SFT), using only ~70M CPT tokens. It recovers 20% more mis-penalized samples in deployment and transfers to freezing scenarios with ~70% relative mis-penalization reduction, while maintaining general ability.

Conclusion: Policy-grounded CPT effectively internalizes complex platform policies into model weights, enabling high-precision, ultra-low-latency risk control that matches or exceeds far larger systems. The approach is practical for industrial deployment and transferable to new scenarios at low cost.

Abstract: For industrial content risk control, the real deployment constraint is not average accuracy but how much risk can be auto-handled under high precision and second-level latency. We present SIRF (Spec-Internalized Risk Foundation Model), which internalizes a platform's complex policies, synthesized without additional human annotation via EntiGraph, MAGA rewriting and account-level chain-of-thought (CoT), into the weights via continued pretraining (CPT), so rules are applied at high precision under an ultra-low-latency, verdict-only deployment.
