A controlled same-source comparison (Qwen3-8B-SFT vs. SIRF-8B-SFT, identical policy injection and verdict-only output form, differing only in policy-grounded CPT) attributes the gain to internalization: SIRF-8B-SFT reaches 71.3% Black Recall@P95, +15.1pp over the baseline, using only ~70M CPT tokens without harming general ability, and among included, logprob-available models under this interface it matches or exceeds far larger systems. SIRF is deployed as a tree-model adjudication layer (20% more mis-penalized samples recovered) and transfers to a freezing scenario at low cost (~70% relative mis-penalization reduction).

</details>


### [231] [A Unified Per-Token Gating Family for On-Policy Distillation: FKL/RKL Mixing with Multi-Channel and Bias Coefficients](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11768)
*Suwan Wu,Yumeng Lin,Pengcheng Yuan,Xiaolong Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a four-coefficient parameterization (lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t)) that unifies two existing per-token gating methods for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD)—EOPD and ToDi—as 1D restrictions. Experiments on TweetEval emotion and hate tasks with Qwen3-32B teacher and Qwen3-4B student show the full family outperforms single-channel baselines in 33/36 cells, with dynamic gating ahead of static baselines in 19/26 cells. Three-seed replications of nine headline comparisons are directionally consistent but not statistically significant.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing per-token gating methods (EOPD and ToDi) each use a single gating signal and direction, but have never been directly compared. The authors propose a unified parameterization to enable systematic comparison and explore whether combining multiple gating signals improves knowledge distillation performance.

Method: The authors introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) that subsumes EOPD and ToDi as 1D restrictions. They evaluate on TweetEval emotion and hate tasks using Qwen3-32B teacher and Qwen3-4B student, comparing full family configurations against matched-magnitude single-channel baselines (entropy-only, gap-only) and static baselines matched on effective KL divergence.

Result: The full four-coefficient family outperforms 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells. In a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment, dynamic gating outperforms effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Three-seed paired replications of nine headline comparisons are directionally consistent but individually smaller than single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3.

Conclusion: The four-coefficient parameterization serves primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD. The results provide exploratory aggregate directional evidence favoring dynamic multi-channel gating over single-channel or static approaches, though individual replications did not achieve statistical significance.

Abstract: Per-token gating of forward/reverse KL losses has become a standard technique for on-policy knowledge distillation (OPD), but existing methods such as EOPD (Jin et al., 2026) and ToDi (Jung et al., 2025) each fix a single gating signal and a single gating direction, and the two have never been compared directly. We introduce a four-coefficient parameterization lambda_t = sigma(a * h_t + b * u(x) + c + d * gap_t) in which direction-aligned proxies of EOPD and ToDi appear as one-dimensional (1D) restrictions, and which adds multi-channel composition and an explicit bias as further degrees of freedom. On TweetEval (Barbieri et al., 2020) emotion and hate, with a Qwen3-32B teacher and a Qwen3-4B student, configurations in the full family reach higher accuracy than the matched-magnitude single-channel (entropy-only / gap-only) 1D restrictions in 33 of 36 comparable cells, and a 26-cell mean-match isolation experiment places dynamic gating ahead of effective-KL-matched static baselines in 19 of 26 cells. Because cells share training data, models, and parameter substructure, we report both counts as exploratory aggregate directional evidence rather than as independent hypothesis tests. Targeted three-seed paired replications of the nine headline comparisons singled out by that sweep -- including a third task, offensive -- are directionally consistent, but individually smaller than the single-seed estimates and not significant at n=3. We therefore present the parameterization primarily as a shared coordinate system for comparing per-token gating designs in short-output classification OPD.

</details>


### [232] [From Parameters to Answers: How LLMs Retrieve and Use Their Internal Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11859)
*Wenkang Wei,Yuan Fang,Renhe Jiang,Hong Cheng,Xingtong Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how language models transition from using query-routing information to relying on stored knowledge across model layers, using interventions across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma. Findings reveal distinct processing phases: early routing dependence, a causal steering window, and later content dependence, with significant architectural differences between models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate how language models' dependence on query-routing information versus target knowledge evolves across model layers when answering questions. The study aims to understand the mechanistic basis of information processing in LLMs by examining the causal dynamics between routing signals and stored knowledge.

Method: Layerwise interventions on hidden states at question endpoints across three models (Qwen, Llama, Gemma). Comparisons of country-continent questions with noun, adjective, and code answers. Uses pair-conditioned request direction (single-country queries), global request direction (paired questions), and selection candidates for content control. Diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen states with fitted measurements.

Result: The pair-conditioned direction strengthens before interventions alter later fitted knowledge, with this causal window opening while answer-supporting content forms. Model trajectories differ: Gemma shows partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profile; Llama lacks sustained routing-effect window. In paired protocols, dependence on global request direction decreases from earlier to later layers while fitted content dependence persists. Qwen's pair-conditioned direction retains late effects, indicating the operational handoff concerns global fitted direction specifically, not all request information.

Conclusion: The study successfully separates early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence in LLM processing. This reveals distinct temporal windows where routing information influences model behavior versus where stored knowledge takes over, with significant variation across model architectures.

Abstract: How does a language model's dependence on query-routing information and target knowledge change as it answers a question? We study this question through layerwise interventions on the hidden state at the end of the question. Across Qwen, Llama, and Gemma, we compare country-continent questions with noun, adjective, and code answers while keeping several fitted measurements distinct. A pair-conditioned request direction describes which country is queried in natural single-country questions; a global request direction describes first- versus second-country requests in paired questions; separate selection candidates test control among contents already available in the hidden state. A diagnostic reanalysis of frozen Qwen natural-question states shows that the pair-conditioned direction grows stronger before interventions on it begin to alter later fitted knowledge, with this causal window opening while answer-supporting content is still forming. The paired three-model trajectories are not uniform: Gemma shows a partially overlapping mid-layer routing-content profile, whereas Llama has no sustained routing-effect window under the same gates. In the paired protocol, dependence on the global request direction decreases from fixed earlier to later layer sets while dependence on fitted content persists. A matched Qwen comparison shows that the pair-conditioned direction retains a late effect, so this operational handoff concerns the global fitted direction rather than all request information. These results separate early readability, natural strength, causal steering, and later content dependence.

</details>


### [233] [Explainability Assistant: A Conversational XAI Interface for Interpreting Energy Consumption Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11860)
*Rodion Krjutškov,Eduard Barbu,Nikos Sakkas,Sofia Yfanti*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system that leverages modern LLMs with function-calling capabilities to make ML predictions interpretable for energy domain specialists. The system achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy, supports flexible natural language interaction, and adapts to different ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning. All expert participants unanimously preferred the conversational interface over traditional XAI dashboards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy consumption forecasting relies on complex ML models (e.g., Genetic Programming-based symbolic regressors) whose predictions are difficult for facility managers and building operators to interpret. Traditional XAI dashboards require substantial technical expertise and provide limited flexibility for dynamic, context-aware inquiry. Previous conversational XAI approaches like TalkToModel were constrained by rigid custom grammars and achieved only 76.8% intent-parsing accuracy.

Method: The Explainability Assistant uses modern Large Language Models (LLMs) with function-calling capabilities to enable flexible natural language interaction for XAI. The system adapts to different ML problem types without requiring task-specific fine-tuning. A comparative evaluation was conducted with energy domain specialists, contrasting the Explainability Assistant with a traditional XAI dashboard.

Result: The system achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy (vs. 76.8% for prior work), supports flexible natural language interaction, and adapts to different ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning. The evaluation shows improved usability and consistent task accuracy, with all experts unanimously preferring the conversational interface for practical use.

Conclusion: The Explainability Assistant successfully addresses the limitations of previous conversational XAI systems by leveraging modern LLMs, achieving significantly higher intent-parsing accuracy and providing a more usable interface that domain specialists prefer over traditional XAI dashboards.

Abstract: Energy consumption forecasting relies on increasingly complex machine learning (ML) models, such as Genetic Programming-based symbolic regressors, whose predictions can be difficult for facility managers and building operators to interpret. Explainable Artificial Intelligence (XAI) techniques address this opacity, but traditional XAI dashboards require substantial technical expertise and provide limited flexibility for dynamic, context-aware inquiry. Conversational XAI systems offer a promising alternative; however, previous approaches, such as TalkToModel, were constrained by rigid custom grammars and achieved only 76.8% intent-parsing accuracy. This paper introduces the Explainability Assistant, an open-source conversational XAI system that leverages the function-calling capabilities of modern Large Language Models (LLMs) to overcome these limitations. The system achieves 94% intent-parsing accuracy, supports flexible natural language interaction, and adapts to different ML problem types without task-specific fine-tuning. We present the system's architecture and report results from a comparative evaluation conducted with energy domain specialists, contrasting the Explainability Assistant with a traditional XAI dashboard. The evaluation suggests improved usability and consistent task accuracy, with all experts unanimously preferring the conversational interface for practical use.

</details>


### [234] [On the Regularization Landscape for the Linear Recommendation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11876)
*Dong Li,Zhenming Liu,Ruoming Jin,Hao Zhou,Zhi Liu,Jing Gao,Bin Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates why different deep learning-based recommendation algorithms achieve similar performance, discovering they essentially add either nuclear-norm or Frobenius-norm regularizers. The authors propose two novel low-rank closed-form solutions that combine the advantages of both approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors aim to understand whether the comparable performance of various deep learning recommendation algorithms is coincidental or can be unified under a single theoretical framework.

Method: The study analyzes the underlying regularization mechanisms of linear performance leaders in recommendation systems, identifying that they add only nuclear-norm or Frobenius-norm based regularizers. The authors then generalize Frobenius-norm regularizers to derive new solutions.

Result: Nuclear-norm models have rigid structure with low-rank closed-form solutions but limited predictive power. Frobenius-norm models are more expressive and efficient but yield full-rank solutions or require complex numerical procedures like ADMM. The proposed new solutions achieve the best of both worlds.

Conclusion: The authors successfully unified different DL-based recommendation algorithms under a regularizer framework and proposed two low-rank closed-form solutions that combine the benefits of nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm approaches.

Abstract: Recently, a wide range of recommendation algorithms inspired by deep learning techniques have emerged as the performance leaders on several standard recommendation benchmarks. While these algorithms were built on different DL techniques (e.g., dropouts, autoencoder), they have similar performance and even similar cost functions. This paper studies whether the models' comparable performance are sheer coincidence, or they can be unified under a single framework. We find that all linear performance leaders effectively add only a nuclear-norm based regularizer, or a Frobenius-norm based regularizer. The former ones possess a (surprising) rigid structure that limits the models' predictive power but their solutions are low rank and have closed form. The latter ones are more expressive and more efficient for recommendation but their solutions are either full-rank or require executing hard-to-tune numeric procedures such as ADMM. Along this line of finding, we further propose two low-rank, closed-form solutions, derived from carefully generalizing Frobenius-norm based regularizers. The new solutions get the best of both nuclear-norm and Frobenius-norm world.

</details>


### [235] [MindTopo: Can Foundation Models Reason in Topological Space?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11900)
*Yunfei Ge,Anbang Liu,Qineng Wang,Johnalbert Garnica,Jianwen Lyu,Zihan Wang,Reuben Tan,Jianfeng Gao,Ruohan Zhang,Yining Hong,Jiajun Wu,Manling Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MindTopo is a benchmark evaluating topological intuition in foundation models across 5 properties (continuity, separation, order, enclosure, knots) at two cognitive levels (reasoning and planning). Testing 14 MLLMs on 11,030 instances reveals all models perform better on reasoning than planning, with the best still far below human performance. Fine-tuning methods improve reasoning more than planning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cognitive science identifies topological relations (invariant under continuous deformation) as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations focus primarily on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. This gap motivates the creation of a benchmark specifically for topological intuition.

Method: MindTopo benchmark with 11,030 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types with controllable difficulty. Evaluates 5 topological properties at two cognitive levels: (1) Reasoning - identifying topological relations or inferring changes; (2) Planning - instantiating models as closed-loop agents selecting environment actions. Tested 14 MLLMs and agent configurations with image/video generation (including 3 video generative models).

Result: Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning. Best-performing model remains far below observed human performance. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach plausible endpoints, but audited rollouts do not reliably follow environment dynamics or preserve topology across transitions.

Conclusion: Current foundation models lack robust topological intuition. While reasoning tasks show moderate capability, planning (closed-loop agency) reveals significant limitations. Even with fine-tuning, topological reasoning remains challenging, and generated observations fail to maintain topological consistency. The benchmark highlights a fundamental gap in spatial intelligence that requires further research.

Abstract: Spatial reasoning depends not only on metric properties such as distance, angle, and shape, but also on topological relations that remain invariant under continuous deformation. Cognitive science identifies these relations as foundational to spatial understanding, yet foundation-model evaluations largely focus on metric or viewpoint-dependent relations. We introduce MindTopo, a benchmark of topological intuition across five properties grounded in cognitive science and formal topology: continuity, separation, order, enclosure, and knots. MindTopo evaluates each property at two cognitive levels. Reasoning asks a model to identify topological relations or infer how they change. Planning instantiates a foundation model as a closed-loop agent whose policy selects environment actions. MindTopo contains 11,030 instances across 13 procedurally generated task types with controllable difficulty. We benchmark 14 MLLMs and study agent configurations augmented with image and video generation, including 3 video generative models in planning settings. Every MLLM performs better on reasoning than on planning, and the best-performing model remains far below observed human performance. On Qwen3-VL-2B-Instruct, supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning improve reasoning more than planning. Generated observations retain local cues and reach plausible endpoints, but audited rollouts do not reliably follow environment dynamics or preserve topology across transitions. Our website is at https://mind-topo.github.io/

</details>


### [236] [Artificial Id: Drive and Persistent Alignment in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11911)
*Yakov Pyotr Shkolnikov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes an "artificial ID" - an adaptive internal drive mechanism for AI agents that determines whether behavior should continue, stop, or change. The mechanism uses "differential persistence" to develop useful control without explicit behavioral objectives, but this same mechanism can allow misalignment to persist across task boundaries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify a control problem in Agentic AI: current systems require external specification of objectives, retries, verification, stopping rules and behavioral transitions. As AI moves toward systems that retain consequential state and operate across task boundaries, this external control becomes insufficient. The motivation is to create an internal mechanism for adaptive control that can operate autonomously.

Method: The authors conducted a minimal virtual Petri-dish experiment using a controller too small to perform general-purpose reasoning that received no task-specific behavioral objective. They observed how differential persistence (behaviors that persist better get selected) leads to adaptive control emergence.

Result: The differential persistence mechanism successfully developed useful control without external objectives. However, concerning results emerged: the mechanism also selects unintended physical strategies when they persist better, and replaces learned sensor mappings when environmental meaning changes. This demonstrates that adaptive direction can emerge without explicit specification, but the same mechanism can cause misalignment.

Conclusion: The authors argue that a scalable artificial ID would carry consequential state and adaptive drive across task boundaries, making alignment a property of the continuing agentic system rather than of a model response or single trajectory. They propose requirements for such systems: persistent alignment boundary over trusted observations, consequence channels, persistent state, authority, identity, provenance and hard constraints.

Abstract: Agentic AI is moving from bounded task execution toward systems that retain consequential state, continue operating and adapt across task boundaries. That shift creates a control problem that current harnesses largely solve by hand: objectives, retries, verification, stopping rules and other behavioral transitions are specified externally. We propose an artificial id, an adaptive internal drive for determining whether behavior should continue, stop or change. In a minimal virtual Petri-dish experiment, a controller too small to perform general-purpose reasoning and receiving no task-specific behavioral objective develops useful control through differential persistence. The same mechanism selects an unintended physical strategy when that behavior persists better and later replaces a learned sensor mapping when its environmental meaning changes. These results show that adaptive direction can emerge without being explicitly specified as a behavioral objective. The same persistence that makes such adaptive agency useful can also allow misalignment, corrupted state and unintended behavior to persist across task boundaries. A scalable artificial id would carry consequential state and adaptive drive across those boundaries, making alignment a property of the continuing agentic system rather than of a model response or single trajectory. Such systems require a persistent alignment boundary over trusted observations, consequence channels, persistent state, authority, identity, provenance and hard constraints.

</details>


### [237] [Can Edge-Deployable Vision-Language Models Identify Species?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11916)
*William Zhou,Mayukha Siripuram,Xiao Yan,Ziqi Liu,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The study evaluates small vision-language models (2-8B parameters) for species identification in camera trap imagery, finding that all models degrade significantly on field imagery compared to clean photographs, though BioCLIP substantially outperforms general-purpose VLMs due to specialized training data rather than model scale.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Camera traps operate on edge hardware with limited connectivity, making locally-deployable VLMs (2-8B parameters) the practically relevant class for species identification, rather than large frontier models. The study investigates whether these smaller models possess genuine taxonomic knowledge.

Method: Evaluated four VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) against specialist BioCLIP (300M parameters) on a 96-species task, comparing performance on clean iNaturalist photographs versus camera trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections, using two independently-sampled evaluation sets and an expanded 200-image sample.

Result: All models perform above chance but degrade sharply on field imagery (domain gaps of 9.6-26.6 percentage points). BioCLIP outperforms all VLMs by 33.2-59.2 percentage points despite being much smaller. BioCLIP's domain gap (18.0 points) is statistically similar to the best VLM's (22.3 points). Under open-set prompting, 5.9-9.6% of responses are syntactically valid but taxonomically nonexistent species names.

Conclusion: Degradation on field imagery reflects general image legibility issues rather than fine-grained discrimination failure. The performance gap between BioCLIP and VLMs stems from specialized training data rather than model scale. The clean-to-field degradation itself is a property of image-quality shift, not a general-purpose model weakness.

Abstract: Camera traps often run in the field on edge hardware with limited or no connectivity, making small, locally-deployable vision-language models (VLMs) -- not frontier-scale ones -- the practically relevant class to evaluate for species identification. We test whether models in this deployment-relevant 2--8B range carry genuine taxonomic knowledge, evaluating four such VLMs (Qwen3-VL 2B/4B/8B, Gemma3 4B) against the domain-specific specialist BioCLIP (300M parameters) on a 96-species task, comparing clean iNaturalist photographs against camera-trap imagery from 6 LILA.science collections, on two independently-sampled evaluation sets. All models identify species far above chance, but every model -- general-purpose or specialist -- degrades sharply on field imagery (domain gaps of 9.6--26.6 percentage points, consistent across taxonomic levels and both evaluation sets), indicating the degradation reflects general image legibility rather than fine-grained discrimination failure. BioCLIP substantially outperforms every VLM tested (by 33.2--59.2 percentage points across an expanded 200-image sample for every model) despite its far smaller size, suggesting the gap reflects specialized training data rather than model scale; yet BioCLIP's own domain gap (18.0 points) is statistically indistinguishable from the best VLM's (22.3 points), suggesting the clean-to-field degradation itself is a property of the image-quality shift rather than a general-purpose-model weakness. Under open-set prompting, 5.9--9.6% of responses are syntactically valid but taxonomically nonexistent species names; the relative fabrication-rate ranking across models replicates exactly across both evaluation sets, a more robust finding than any single point estimate.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [238] [ReCHOIR: Contact-guided Human Object Interaction Retargeting to Diverse Characters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10982)
*Chaelin Kim,Seokhyeon Hong,Kwan Yun,Soojin Choi,Inseo Jang,Junyong Noh*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: ReCHOIR is a novel contact-guided motion retargeting method for transferring human object interaction (HOI) motions across diverse humanoid characters with different skeletal configurations, while preserving both body movement semantics and consistent object interaction patterns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior motion retargeting methods focus on transferring human motion alone and fail to preserve consistent interaction between the character and the manipulated object. This paper aims to maintain both the semantics of original body movement and the interaction pattern between character and object when retargeting to characters with different skeletal configurations.

Method: The method uses a Part-Aware Motion Embedding (PAME) autoencoder that encodes full-body motion into a shared body-part-wise latent space, enabling generalization across heterogeneous skeletons while preserving local motion semantics. A contact-guided retargeting module treats source object interaction as a condition for refining target character motion by encoding object- and contact-related signals into a body-part-aligned latent representation and injecting them into decoding through a residual control branch. An object motion decoder predicts target object motion aligned with the refined target character motion.

Result: The method enables HOI motion retargeting to target characters with different skeletal configurations while maintaining motion semantics and contact-consistent interaction patterns, producing aligned target human and object motions.

Conclusion: ReCHOIR successfully transfers human object interaction motions across diverse humanoid characters by preserving both body movement semantics and consistent object interaction, with the PAME autoencoder enabling generalization across heterogeneous skeletons and the contact-guided module ensuring strong adaptation in interaction-relevant body regions.

Abstract: We present ReCHOIR, a novel contact-guided motion retargeting method for transferring human object interaction (HOI) motions across diverse humanoid characters. Unlike prior motion retargeting methods that primarily focus on transferring human motion alone, our goal is to preserve not only the semantics of the original body movement but also consistent interaction between the character and the manipulated object, while jointly producing aligned target human and object motions. Given source HOI motion, object geometry, and contact cues extracted from the source interaction, ReCHOIR retargets an HOI sequence to target characters with different skeletal configurations while maintaining both motion semantics and contact-consistent interaction patterns. Our method builds on a Part-Aware Motion Embedding (PAME) autoencoder, which encodes full-body motion into a shared body-part-wise latent space. This representation enables generalization across heterogeneous skeletons while preserving local motion semantics beneficial for part-aware adaptation in HOI retargeting. On top of this representation, we introduce a contact-guided retargeting module and an object motion decoder for HOI retargeting. The contact-guided retargeting module treats the source object interaction as a condition for refining target character motion: object- and contact-related signals are encoded into a body-part-aligned latent representation and injected into decoding through a residual control branch, enabling stronger adaptation in interaction-relevant body regions without discarding the underlying motion prior. In parallel, the object motion decoder predicts a target object motion aligned with the refined target character motion, ensuring that the object trajectory remains consistent with how the interaction is realized by the target character.

</details>


### [239] [Learning Realistic Athletic Sprinting Without Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11083)
*William Wang,Nicholas Bianco,Guy Tevet,Jennifer Hicks,C. Karen Liu,Scott Delp,Kayvon Fatahalian*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A muscle-driven simulation system that generates biomechanically accurate high-speed athletic motions (e.g., 100m sprint, athletic drills) using GPU-accelerated simulation (1000x real-time) combined with reinforcement learning, without requiring motion demonstrations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a method for generating realistic athletic locomotion motions without relying on motion capture data, enabling automated creation of biomechanically accurate animations for high-speed movements.

Method: Integration of state-of-the-art biomechanical athlete models into a high-performance GPU simulator capable of 1000x real-time speed, enabling large-batch reinforcement learning to train control policies in the muscle excitation space. Policies are guided by episode termination conditions and a reward function balancing speed maximization with joint limit force reduction.

Result: Training completes within a few hours on a single GPU, producing "near visually realistic" motions for complete athletic activities including 100-meter sprint, side-shuffling, backpedaling, and carioca. Generated sprinting motions show strong agreement with experimental sprinting data.

Conclusion: This approach successfully demonstrates muscle-driven simulation can generate biomechanically plausible high-speed athletic locomotion without motion demonstrations, with potential applications in animation, sports training simulation, and biomechanics research.

Abstract: We present a muscle-driven simulation system for generating biomechanically accurate motion for high-speed athletic locomotion tasks that does not require motion demonstrations. Our approach integrates state-of-the-art biomechanical athlete models into a new, high-performance GPU simulator capable of running at 1000x real-time. High-throughput simulation enables large-batch reinforcement learning to train control policies that operate directly in the model's high-dimensional muscle excitation space, and are guided only by task-specific episode termination conditions and a reward that encourages maximizing speed while reducing forces needed to respect joint limits. These policies train within a few hours on a single GPU and generate "near visually realistic" motions for complete athletic activities such as a full 100-meter sprint or performing popular athletic locomotion drills like side-shuffling, backpedaling, and carioca. The generated sprinting motions also exhibit strong agreement with experimental data captured from sprinters.

</details>


### [240] [Gaussian Light Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11430)
*Patrick Attimont,Kartic Subr,Cyril Soler*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: A novel global illumination method represents the light transport solution as a 13D Gaussian mixture model incorporating positions, directions, surface normals, and material properties. The model parameters are directly optimized by minimizing the rendering equation residual, enabling real-time, view-independent rendering in milliseconds with lower memory requirements than neural approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional light transport methods based on Neumann series are computationally expensive. The authors aim to reduce the number of functions and speed up evaluation by incorporating scene properties (positions, directions, normals, material properties) directly into the Gaussian representation, enabling real-time rendering with lower memory usage.

Method: The solution to the light transport equation is expressed as a 13D Gaussian mixture model. Parameters are directly estimated by minimizing the residual of the rendering equation (as opposed to Neumann series approaches). An efficient culling strategy is introduced to keep optimization tractable and enable real-time rendering.

Result: The method achieves rendering times on the order of milliseconds with significantly reduced memory requirements compared to conventional neural rendering approaches. The representation enables view-independent solutions to the light transport equation.

Conclusion: The 13D Gaussian mixture model representation successfully combines scene properties with light transport computation, providing a fast, memory-efficient alternative to both traditional ray tracing and neural rendering methods for global illumination.

Abstract: We present a novel method for computing global illumination by expressing the solution to the light transport equation as a 13D Gaussian mixture model over positions, directions, surface normals, and material properties. We show that including scene properties in the Gaussian representation drastically reduces the number of functions and speeds up evaluation. As opposed to traditional light transport methods based on Neumann series, the parameters of our model are directly estimated by minimizing the residual of the rendering equation. While both optimization and rendering require repeated evaluations of a linear combination of high-dimensional Gaussian functions, we introduce an efficient culling strategy to keep the optimization tractable and produce renderings in real time. Our representation enables to render fast, view-independent solutions to the light transport equation, achieving rendering times on the order of milliseconds, with a fraction of the memory requirements of conventional neural rendering approaches.

</details>


### [241] [Hologram Representation via Quadratic Phase Gaussian Splatting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11434)
*Haolong Wang,Yicheng Zhan,Kaan Akşit,Simeng Qiu*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: CVQPG (Complex-Valued Quadratic Phase Gaussian) is a novel hologram representation method that replaces standard 2D Gaussian representations with 2D quadratic phase functions, incorporating learnable parameters to control curvature of these bases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve holographic reconstructions by enhancing the primitive representation in 2D Gaussian Splatting through wavefront modulation.

Method: Replace 2D Gaussian representations with 2D quadratic phase functions, adding learnable parameters to control curvature of the bases for effective wavefront modulation.

Result: Exceeds state-of-the-art visual quality by +0.19 dB (RGB) and +0.33 dB (grayscale) on average in holographic reconstructions. Equal parameter count evaluations show wavefront modulation is an effective lightweight enhancement. Frequency domain analysis confirms preservation of mid-to-high frequency band of natural images.

Conclusion: Modulating the primitive's wavefront via quadratic phase functions provides an effective and lightweight enhancement for hologram representations, successfully preserving high-frequency image components.

Abstract: We introduce Complex-Valued Quadratic Phase Gaussian (CVQPG), a novel hologram representation method that replaces standard 2D Gaussian representations used in 2D Gaussian Splatting with 2D quadratic phase functions. CVQPG incorporates additional learnable parameters to control the curvature of these bases. We evaluate our approach against state-of-the-art methods, exceeding the visual quality by +0.19 dB (RGB) and +0.33 dB (grayscale) on average in holographic reconstructions. Specifically, our equal parameter count evaluations show that modulating the primitive's wavefront is an effective and lightweight enhancement for hologram representations. In addition, our frequency domain analysis illustrates that CVQPG has successfully preserved the mid-to-high frequency band of natural images.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [242] [When Passing Tests Hides Vulnerabilities: An Empirical Study of Silent Failures in Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10548)
*Wenji Bai,Muhammad Waseem,Zeeshan Rasheed,Jaakko Peltonen,Pekka Abrahamsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates "silent failures" in LLM-based agentic code repair—patches that pass syntactic and functional verification but still contain security vulnerabilities. The study analyzed 1,030 execution traces from 7 agent frameworks using GPT-4o-mini on SecurityEval and CVEfixes datasets, identifying 170 confirmed silent failures across three categories: Omission (48.2%), Introduction (30.6%), and Inadequacy (21.2%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While LLM-based agents for automated code repair have gained significant attention, existing evaluation methods focus only on syntactic and functional correctness. There has been limited research on security vulnerabilities that remain or are introduced in "test-passing" patches, which represents a critical gap in understanding the real-world risks of automated code repair.

Method: Empirical study using 1,030 valid execution traces from seven agent frameworks with GPT-4o-mini across two security-focused datasets (SecurityEval and CVEfixes). Three iterations of qualitative coding and manual verification were conducted to identify and categorize silent failures.

Result: 170 confirmed silent failures were identified. Three main categories were classified: Omission (48.2%)—required security controls omitted, Introduction (30.6%)—new vulnerabilities introduced, and Inadequacy (21.2%)—incomplete defenses applied. Ten fine-grained failure codes were identified under these categories. Current test-passing evaluation and LLM-based reviewers failed to detect these failures. Similar insecure patterns appeared across different frameworks, and single-agent vs multi-agent systems showed distinct failure profiles.

Conclusion: The study reveals that current evaluation methods for LLM-based agentic code repair are insufficient for security assessment. The findings provide a foundation for developing targeted verification methods that go beyond functional correctness and address security vulnerabilities in automated code repair systems.

Abstract: LLM-based agents for automated code repair have received significant attention in recent years from both research and software engineering practice perspectives. However, limited attention has been paid to patches that pass syntactic and functional verification but still retain or introduce security vulnerabilities. The aim of this research is to systematically identify and categorize such silent failures in LLM-based agentic code repair.
  We conducted an empirical study using 1,030 valid execution traces produced by seven agent frameworks with GPT-4o-mini across two security-focused datasets, SecurityEval and CVEfixes. Through three iterations of qualitative coding and manual verification, 170 confirmed silent failures were identified. The key results are: (i) Three main categories of silent failures were identified: Omission, Introduction, and Inadequacy. Omission accounts for 48.2% of the confirmed failures, Introduction for 30.6%, and Inadequacy for 21.2%. (ii) Ten fine-grained failure codes were classified under these three categories, showing how agents omit required security controls, apply incomplete defenses, or introduce new vulnerabilities during repair. (iii) Current test-passing evaluation and LLM-based reviewer roles were insufficient to expose or intercept these failures in the confirmed cases. (iv) Similar insecure solutions appeared across different frameworks, suggesting possible shared model-, prompt-, or task-level influences, while single-agent and multi-agent systems showed different failure profiles.
  The results of this study will assist researchers and practitioners in improving the evaluation of LLM-based agentic code repair and developing targeted verification methods that go beyond functional correctness and cover all generated artifacts.

</details>


### [243] [Optimizing AI Inference Across the Deployment Stack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10550)
*Tejinder Singh,John Pflueger,Jeebak Mitra,Robert Lincourt,Mitchell Markow,Bhavesh A. Patel*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a unified analytical framework for AI inference optimization across the deployment stack, addressing the problem of incomparable benchmarks. It introduces a three-layer taxonomy (model compression, compiler transformations, system policies), formulates deployment as multi-objective optimization, and proposes an evidence protocol for improved comparability. Experiments across edge platforms (Jetson AGX Orin) and data center GPUs (A100, H100) demonstrate that cross-layer interactions determine deployment outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Published AI benchmarks report latency and throughput under incomparable conditions, limiting their usefulness for deployment decisions. No unified analytical treatment exists to compare optimization techniques across the full deployment stack.

Method: 1) Three-layer taxonomy covering model-level techniques (quantization, pruning, distillation), compiler transformations (graph fusion, layout optimization, kernel autotuning), and system policies (dynamic batching, admission control, memory tiering); 2) Formulated deployment as constrained multi-objective optimization over accuracy, latency, throughput, memory footprint, and energy; 3) Proposed deployment-ranking functional with Pareto monotonicity and scale invariance; 4) Applied Roofline models for memory-bandwidth analysis and queuing models for service-time analysis; 5) Proposed evidence protocol separating measured, derived, and analytical claims; 6) Synthesized evidence from edge platforms (Jetson AGX Orin, 5 inference frameworks) and data center GPUs (A100, H100, 3 LLM serving engines) with Llama-3.1 quantization studies.

Result: Deployment outcomes are governed by complex cross-layer interactions that no single-layer analysis can predict. The study shows that optimization decisions at one layer (e.g., model compression) significantly affect performance at other layers (e.g., compiler optimizations, system policies).

Conclusion: 1) Constraint-aware selection procedure for deployment optimization; 2) Three open problems identified: compiler-serving co-optimization, cross-hardware performance prediction, and standardized energy reporting. The paper advocates for standardized reporting protocols and cross-layer optimization approaches.

Abstract: AI deployment performance is shaped not by model architecture alone, but by interactions among compression, compiler transformations, and serving policies. Published benchmarks often report latency and throughput under incomparable conditions, limiting their use for deployment decisions. This paper presents a unified analytical treatment of inference optimization across the deployment stack. We introduce a three-layer taxonomy covering model-level techniques such as quantization, pruning, and distillation; compiler transformations such as graph fusion, layout optimization, and kernel autotuning; and system policies such as dynamic batching, admission control, and memory tiering. We formulate deployment as a constrained multi-objective optimization problem over accuracy, latency, throughput, memory footprint, and energy, and analyze a deployment-ranking functional with Pareto monotonicity and scale invariance. Roofline models show how memory-bandwidth hierarchies bound performance across precision regimes, while queuing models explain how service-time changes amplify response time under load. To improve comparability, we propose an evidence protocol that separates measured, derived, and analytical claims; limits numerical comparison to within-paper results; and requires reporting of hardware, software versions, batch semantics, and thermal state. We synthesize evidence from edge platforms, including Jetson AGX Orin and five inference frameworks; data center GPUs, including A100 and H100 with three LLM serving engines; and quantization studies across the Llama-3.1 family. The synthesis shows that deployment outcomes are governed by cross-layer interactions that no single-layer analysis can predict. We conclude with a constraint-aware selection procedure and open problems in compiler-serving co-optimization, cross-hardware performance prediction, and standardized energy reporting.

</details>


### [244] [ReqEvolve: User-Oriented Software Self-Evolution through Automatic Requirement Interpretation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10590)
*Md Asif Iqbal Fahim,Alessio Ferrari*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: ReqEvolve is a runtime code generation system enabling user-driven self-evolution by integrating automatic requirements engineering (RE) and test-driven development (TDD) to transform high-level user requests into executable functionality through clarification, specification decomposition, test generation, and runtime integration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The gap exists because user requests for new functionality originate from end users but are rarely expressed in technical terms, requiring developers to translate needs into technical specifications before the system can evolve, which delays early validation of requested functionality by preventing users from immediately observing the resulting behavior.

Method: The system integrates automatic requirements engineering (RE) and test-driven development (TDD) to transform user requests into executable functionality through clarification, specification decomposition, test generation, and runtime integration. Evaluation was conducted on 72 software evolution cases across 18 projects against two baselines: SpecFix (RE-focused code generation approach) and an ablation variant of the system.

Result: ReqEvolve achieves 89.2% Pass@1, outperforming SpecFix by 18.8% (p < 0.01, r = 0.79, large effect) and the ablation baseline by 32.6% (p < 0.001, r = 0.88, large effect).

Conclusion: User-driven self-evolution is a viable paradigm for autonomously extending software capabilities from user requests, thereby accelerating requirements validation prior to developer verification.

Abstract: The paradigm of software self-evolution enables systems to autonomously extend and reconfigure their own capabilities during execution in response to technical specifications. Yet requests for new functionality often originate from end users and are rarely expressed in technical terms. As a result, developers must translate user needs into technical specifications before the system can evolve, delaying early validation of the requested functionality by preventing users from immediately observing the resulting behaviour. To address this gap, we present ReqEvolve, a runtime code generation system that enables user-driven self-evolution by accepting high-level user requests. The system integrates automatic requirements engineering (RE) and test-driven development (TDD) to transform these requests into executable functionality through clarification, specification decomposition, test generation, and runtime integration. We evaluate ReqEvolve on 72 software evolution cases across 18 projects against two baselines: SpecFix, an RE-focused code generation approach, and an ablation variant of our system. ReqEvolve achieves 89.2% Pass@1, outperforming SpecFix by 18.8% (p < 0.01, r = 0.79, large effect) and the ablation baseline by 32.6% (p < 0.001, r = 0.88, large effect). These results provide initial evidence that user-driven self-evolution is a viable paradigm for autonomously extending software capabilities from user requests, thereby accelerating requirements validation prior to developer verification.

</details>


### [245] [Generative AI for trustworthy systems - Towards a health check model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10595)
*Jan Bosch,Rick Kazman,Henry Muccini,Helena Holmström Olsson*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents the Trustworthy Autonomy Health Check Model, a multidimensional instrument for characterizing how organizations establish trust in GenAI-assisted software engineering. The model organizes eight dimensions into system and organizational layers, each on a five-level ordinal scale, with four cross-cutting trust paradigms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current analytical instruments for GenAI adoption are predominantly unidimensional maturity models that compress important configurational variation into a single progressive axis, failing to capture the nuanced, multidimensional nature of trust establishment in software engineering contexts.

Method: Inductive interview study with eighteen senior practitioners across eight industries (telecommunications, automotive, defence, aviation, banking, energy, government, and enterprise software services).

Result: The Trustworthy Autonomy Health Check Model with eight empirically grounded dimensions organized into system layer (Scope of Agent Authority, Assurance Mechanisms, Data Trustworthiness, Architectural Containment, Traceability & Comprehensibility) and organizational layer (Governance, Human Oversight Posture, Workforce Capability Sustainability), each on a five-level ordinal scale, plus four cross-cutting trust paradigms (operational, engineering, statistical, containment-based). The alignment hypothesis is proposed: effective trustworthiness is constrained by fit between scope of agent authority and dimensions enabling it.

Conclusion: The model is diagnostic rather than prescriptive—supports cross-organizational comparison, surfaces configurational trade-offs, and locates organizations in shared space without imposing a single progression path. Higher levels are not inherently better; the goal is alignment across dimensions appropriate to the organization's domain and chosen trust paradigm. Directions for empirical validation are outlined.

Abstract: The adoption of generative AI in software-intensive systems is proceeding rapidly, but current analytical instruments - principally unidimensional maturity models - compress important configurational variation into a single progressive axis. Drawing on an inductive interview study of eighteen senior practitioners across telecommunications, automotive, defence, aviation, banking, energy, government, and enterprise software services, this paper presents the Trustworthy Autonomy Health Check Model: a structured, multidimensional instrument for characterizing how organizations establish trust in GenAI-assisted software engineering. The model organizes eight empirically grounded dimensions into a system layer (Scope of Agent Authority, Assurance Mechanisms, Data Trustworthiness, Architectural Containment, Traceability & Comprehensibility) and an organizational layer (Governance, Human Oversight Posture, Workforce Capability Sustainability), each on a five-level ordinal scale. A cross-cutting overlay of four trust paradigms - operational, engineering, statistical, and containment-based - captures how trust is established, complementing the dimensions capturing what must be trustworthy. The model is diagnostic rather than prescriptive: it supports cross-organizational comparison, surfaces configurational trade-offs, and locates an organization in a shared space without imposing a single progression path. Higher levels are not inherently better; the goal is alignment across dimensions appropriate to the organization's domain and chosen trust paradigm. This is formalized as the alignment hypothesis: effective trustworthiness is constrained by the fit between the scope of agent authority and the dimensions enabling it, with misalignment producing pathological risk or unnecessary friction. We discuss how practitioners can apply the model and outline directions for empirical validation.

</details>


### [246] [On the Relation between Code Quality and Machine Learning Performance: A Large-scale Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10610)
*Marius Mignard,Steven Costiou,Anne Etien*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether code quality correlates with ML performance in computational notebooks, and whether popularity and author expertise are reliable indicators of code quality or performance. The study analyzed 265,363 Python notebooks from Kaggle competitions using Pylint and SonarQube for code quality assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The ML community prioritizes model performance over code quality, relying on an untested assumption that these are unrelated. Additionally, practitioners reuse code from notebooks selected through social signals (popularity, author expertise) without knowing if these are reliable quality proxies.

Method: Large-scale empirical study of 265,363 Python notebooks from Kaggle competitions. Code quality was assessed using: (1) Pylint for general Python code quality, and (2) SonarQube with 34 rules targeting data-science and ML-specific practices.

Result: General Python code quality (Pylint) shows negligible/non-significant correlations with ML performance. However, ML-specific violations show a consistent small negative association with performance. Popularity does not indicate code quality or performance. Author expertise in coding provides no quality/performance indication, but competition expertise correlates with better performance, fewer ML-specific violations, and slightly more Python errors.

Conclusion: The assumption that code quality and ML performance are unrelated holds only for general Python code quality. ML-specific quality violations do negatively impact performance. Popularity and coding expertise are not reliable indicators of quality or performance, though competition expertise correlates with better outcomes.

Abstract: Context: Computational notebooks are the standard environment for machine learning (ML) development. Within the ML community, model performance is often the primary considered metric, and code quality is treated as a secondary concern. This prioritization relies on a largely untested assumption that code quality and ML performance are unrelated. Practitioners also reuse existing code that may come from notebooks selected through social signals (popularity, author expertise) whose reliability as quality proxies has never been assessed. Objective: We empirically investigated the relationship between code quality and ML performance in notebooks, and evaluated whether popularity and author expertise give indication on code quality or performance. Method: We conducted a large-scale empirical study of 265,363 Python notebooks submitted to Kaggle competitions. We assessed code quality with two static analysis tools: Pylint, capturing general Python code quality, and SonarQube, configured with a profile of 34 rules targeting data-science and ML-specific practices. Results: The relationship between code quality and performance depends on the notion of quality considered. General Python code quality is decoupled from ML performance, showing negligible or non-significant correlations across all observations. In contrast, ML-specific violations exhibit a consistent, small negative association with performance that persists across all observations. The popularity of a notebook does not give information on the code quality or performance. Code expertise provides no information on quality or performance, but competition expertise correlates with better performance, fewer ML-specific violations, and slightly more Python errors and refactoring violations.

</details>


### [247] [AI Safety: Not Optional, Not Later](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10630)
*Qinghua Lu,Yoshua Bengio*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a safety-by-design assurance architecture for AI systems that integrates multiple layers of protection: model-level supervision (e.g., Scientist AI), system-level controls over scaffolds and harnesses, independent verification, monitoring, evidence infrastructure, and governance mechanisms for accountability and evidence interoperability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation stems from observed incidents where AI safety failures occur across multiple layers, indicating that a comprehensive, multi-layered safety approach is necessary rather than relying on single-point solutions.

Method: The paper presents an architectural framework combining: (1) model-level supervision systems like Scientist AI, (2) system-level controls over scaffolds and harnesses, (3) independent verification mechanisms, (4) continuous monitoring, (5) evidence infrastructure, and (6) governance structures ensuring accountability and evidence interoperability.

Result: The result is a proposed safety-by-design assurance architecture that addresses AI safety failures at multiple levels, creating defense in depth through the integration of technical controls, verification, monitoring, and governance.

Conclusion: Effective AI safety requires a holistic, multi-layered architecture that combines technical supervision, system controls, independent verification, monitoring, evidence infrastructure, and governance—moving beyond single-layer solutions to a comprehensive safety-by-design approach.

Abstract: Incidents show that AI safety failures often arise across multiple layers. We present a safety-by-design assurance architecture combining model-level supervision, such as Scientist AI, with system-level controls over scaffolds and harnesses, independent verification, monitoring, and evidence infrastructure, supported by governance for accountability and evidence interoperability.

</details>


### [248] [Numbat: Building and Verifying a Self-Contained Machine-Learning Stack](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10632)
*Thang Tran,Lan Dang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Numbat is a complete machine learning stack written in Zig (no third-party runtime dependencies) that spans tensor computation, automatic differentiation, neural networks, mixed precision, multi-GPU training, and data loading. It exposes a stable C ABI with 1,400+ entry points and bindings for six languages. Verification uses a reference implementation as executable specification at five levels, detecting 10 silent recipe divergences. The system successfully trains a YOLOv8m detector (25.9M parameters) on COCO 2017 for 500 epochs, achieving 0.4956 mAP50-95 (vs 0.502 published baseline).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current ML systems depend on Python-orchestrated frameworks with significant engineering overhead: hundreds of version-coupled packages, separate export toolchains, and the language split between research (Python) and production. This creates maintenance burden, dependency hell, and deployment complexity. The authors sought to build a complete ML stack in a single general-purpose language with no third-party runtime dependencies.

Method: Built numbat in Zig language covering: tensor computation, automatic differentiation, neural-network modules, mixed precision, multi-GPU training, data loading and monitoring. Exposed via stable, additively versioned C ABI (1,400+ entry points) with bindings for six languages. Clinical domain planes encode regulatory requirements as executable acceptance gates. Verification at five levels: (1) operator gradient checks, (2) module-level tests, (3) model-level validation, (4) training trajectory comparison, (5) automated trajectory gate against same-machine reference run (trajectory-level differential oracle).

Result: The verification protocol surfaced ten silent recipe divergences (cataloged with mechanisms and symptoms). The acceptance test trained a 25.9M-parameter YOLOv8m detector from random initialization on COCO 2017 for full 500-epoch schedule: exported weights achieved 0.4956 mAP50-95 under official protocol (reference published endpoint 0.502), with single-GPU step time at parity on identical hardware. Weights, per-epoch metrics and full run manifest are released.

Conclusion: A production-quality ML stack can be built in a single systems programming language (Zig) without third-party runtime dependencies, achieving competitive accuracy and performance compared to established frameworks. The trajectory-level differential oracle verification approach effectively detects silent recipe divergences that would otherwise go unnoticed. This demonstrates an alternative approach to ML infrastructure that eliminates Python dependency overhead while maintaining functionality and performance.

Abstract: Machine-learning systems are built almost exclusively on a few large Python-orchestrated frameworks, and they inherit those stacks' engineering costs: environments of hundreds of version-coupled packages, separate export toolchains for deployment, and the split between the language research is written in and the language products ship in. We report on the construction and verification of numbat, a machine-learning stack written in one general-purpose language (Zig) with no third-party runtime dependencies. The stack spans tensor computation, automatic differentiation, neural-network modules, mixed precision, multi-GPU training, data loading and monitoring; an SDK exposes it behind a stable, additively versioned C ABI of over 1,400 entry points, with bindings for six languages; and its clinical domain planes encode regulatory requirements as executable acceptance gates rather than documentation. Verifying such a stack is the harder half of building it: a defective training run rarely fails, it converges quietly to a slightly worse model. We treat a widely used reference implementation as an executable specification and verify against it at five levels, from operator gradient checks to an automated trajectory gate against a same-machine reference run - the arrangement our companion study formalizes as a trajectory-level differential oracle. The protocol surfaced ten silent recipe divergences, which we catalog with mechanisms and symptoms. As the acceptance test, we train a 25.9M-parameter detector of the YOLOv8m class from random initialization on COCO 2017 for the full 500-epoch schedule: the exported weights score 0.4956 mAP50-95 under the official protocol, scored by the reference stack's own validator (published endpoint 0.502), with single-GPU step time at parity on identical hardware. Weights, per-epoch metrics and the full run manifest are released.

</details>


### [249] [Governed Human-AI Prioritization Under Uncertainty: Adaptive Estimation and Dependency-Constrained Portfolio Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10648)
*Azzeddine Ihsine,Sara Ihsine*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies five quantitative decision operators (BVS, ERS, PVS, CCS, ODP) used in D-POAF decision practice for AI-native software engineering prioritization. Through controlled synthetic experiments, the researchers characterize how these operators behave under various conditions including estimation errors, estimator drift, and dependency constraints. The study provides quantitative evidence for evidence-weighted human-AI prioritization and defines optimization boundaries for portfolio selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the challenge of governing heterogeneous estimates, uncertainty, strategic parameters, dependencies, and limited capacity in AI-native software engineering prioritization decisions. The problem involves combining human judgment, historical analogy, parametric estimation, and AI-generated forecasts while maintaining inspectability and recalibratability.

Method: The researchers conducted controlled synthetic experiments with known latent variables and explicit error processes. They tested: 1) BVS-weight perturbations on global rankings, 2) reliability-weighted effort aggregation vs. individual estimators, 3) adaptive reliability weighting under estimator drift, 4) model-collective divergence for detecting high-error estimates, and 5) dependency-constrained portfolio optimization comparing value-to-effort and ODP approaches.

Result: Key findings: 1) Moderate BVS-weight perturbations preserved rankings (Spearman 0.986), but strategic changes reduced top-10% overlap to 0.788; 2) Reliability-weighted effort aggregation achieved MAE 0.616 (42.6% reduction vs best individual estimator); 3) Adaptive reliability weighting reduced RMSE by 4.5% vs equal weighting; 4) Model-collective divergence detected high-error estimates with ROC-AUC 0.906; 5) Value-to-effort achieved mean objective ratio 0.962 and ODP distance 0.953 against exact optimum in 800 instances.

Conclusion: The results establish a quantitative basis for evidence-weighted human-AI prioritization and define the optimization boundary between shortest-path ODP formulations and general release-portfolio selection. The study demonstrates that reliability-weighted aggregation and adaptive weighting can significantly improve estimation accuracy, while value-to-effort approaches provide near-optimal results for dependency-constrained portfolios.

Abstract: AI-native software engineering increasingly combines human judgment, historical analogy, parametric estimation, and AI-generated forecasts inside the same prioritization decision. The resulting problem is not merely how to rank candidate work, but how to govern heterogeneous estimates, uncertainty, strategic parameters, dependencies, and limited capacity in a way that remains inspectable and recalibratable. We study five quantitative operators used in the D-POAF decision practice: Business Value Score (BVS), Effort and Risk Score (ERS), Prioritization Value Score (PVS), Collective Calibration Score (CCS), and Optimal Development Path (ODP). Controlled synthetic experiments characterize their behavior under known latent variables and explicit error processes. Moderate BVS-weight perturbations preserved global rankings (median Spearman 0.986), while broader strategic changes reduced top-10% overlap to 0.788. Reliability-weighted effort aggregation achieved MAE 0.616, a 42.6% reduction relative to the best individual estimator (MAE 1.073), and outperformed a simple mean (MAE 0.638). Under estimator drift, adaptive reliability weighting reduced average RMSE by 4.5% relative to equal weighting. Model-collective divergence detected the highest-error priority estimates with ROC-AUC 0.906. In 800 dependency-constrained portfolio instances, value-to-effort achieved mean objective ratio 0.962 and ODP distance 0.953 against the exact optimum; bootstrap intervals confirm a small but systematic advantage for value-to-effort under the declared objective. These results establish a quantitative basis for evidence-weighted human-AI prioritization and define the optimization boundary between shortest-path ODP formulations and general release-portfolio selection.

</details>


### [250] [LLMVul: A Vulnerability-Labeled Dataset of LLM-Generated C/C++ Functions from Real Production Repositories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10945)
*Mohammad Farhad,Shuvalaxmi Dass*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: LLMVul is a dataset of 21,430 LLM-generated C/C++ functions from real GitHub repositories, containing 1,540 vulnerable functions across 17 CWE categories. It enables research on vulnerability detection in AI-assisted software development.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vulnerability datasets focus on human-written code or controlled environments, limiting the study of security weaknesses in LLM-generated code as it appears in real-world projects. There is a need for datasets derived from actual production repositories.

Method: The dataset was mined from GitHub over 4 years using provenance signals (commit metadata, AI-related authorship evidence). Vulnerability labeling used an ensemble of static-analysis and pattern-based techniques, with independent manual annotation for validation (Cohen's kappa = 0.79).

Result: LLMVul contains 21,430 unique C/C++ functions from 226 repositories, including 1,540 ensemble-vulnerable functions spanning 17 unique CWE categories. It provides substantially more real-world LLM-generated vulnerable C/C++ functions than existing benchmarks.

Conclusion: LLMVul enables reproducible research on vulnerability detection, security evaluation of LLM-generated code, and analysis of vulnerability patterns in AI-assisted software development. The dataset is publicly available.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate and assist with software development, yet existing vulnerability datasets largely focus on human-written code or controlled prompting environments. This limits the ability to study security weaknesses in LLM-generated code as it appears in real-world software projects. We present LLMVul, a vulnerability-labeled dataset of LLM-generated C/C++ functions mined from real production repositories. We mine AI-assisted development activity from GitHub over a 4 year period, from November 13, 2022 to September 3, 2026, using provenance signals such as commit metadata and AI-related authorship evidence. After filtering and deduplication, LLMVul contains 21,430 unique C/C++ functions from 226 repositories, together with repository, commit, function, provenance, and AI-tool metadata. We establish vulnerability labels using an ensemble of complementary static-analysis and pattern-based techniques and assign Common Weakness Enumeration (CWE) categories to confirmed vulnerable functions. To assess labeling reliability, we additionally conduct independent manual annotation and measure inter-rater agreement using Cohen's kappa ($k=0.79$). LLMVul contains 1,540 ensemble-vulnerable functions spanning 17 unique CWE categories, providing substantially more real-world LLM-generated vulnerable C/C++ functions than existing vulnerability-oriented LLM code benchmarks. By preserving both code-level vulnerability labels and generation/provenance metadata, LLMVul enables reproducible research on vulnerability detection, security evaluation of LLM-generated code, and analysis of vulnerability patterns in AI-assisted software development. The LLMVul dataset is publicly available at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22668216.

</details>


### [251] [What a Random Draw from the MCP Registry Contains, and What Tool-Use Benchmarks Contain Instead](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10962)
*Haseeb Mohammed Afsar*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper reveals significant sampling bias in MCP server ecosystem research: only 48.8% of randomly sampled servers work, versus 66.7% in hand-curated frames. The dominant failure is servers that never start (37.5%), not missing credentials. Among working servers, JSON schema conformance is perfect but safety annotations are often missing (58.8% vs 41.5% in curated sets). Tool-use benchmarks contain massive repetition (68.8-85.6% exact repeats) that inflates apparent tool diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current MCP server studies use biased sampling methods (reference sets, popularity lists, hand-curated frames) that select for functional servers. This inflates reported success rates and skews findings. The authors seek to characterize what an unbiased probability sample actually contains.

Method: Draw a simple random sample of 400 npm/stdio servers from a 24,135-server registry census. Probe each over the wire using the same instrument applied to a hand-curated frame. Compare tool descriptions against benchmark corpora (BFCL v4, UltraTool) using cosine similarity with constant methodology.

Result: 48.8% of random servers complete handshake (vs 66.7% curated). 37.5% fail to start entirely. Among 195 working servers: zero fatal JSON Schema violations across 2,766 tools. Safety annotation omission: 58.8% (random) vs 41.5% (curated). Real MCP tools: 2.8% near-duplication at cosine 0.70, all within single servers. BFCL v4: 16.7% near-duplication (16.4 points between independent tasks). UltraTool: 0.3%. Raw benchmark rows: 68.8% (BFCL) and 85.6% (UltraTool) exact repeats vs 0.4% real MCP.

Conclusion: MCP server research suffers from systematic sampling bias that inflates success rates and understates missing safety annotations. Benchmark corpora contain massive repetition requiring global deduplication before meaningful statistical analysis. The study provides reproducible findings via released scripts and published seeds.

Abstract: Studies of the Model Context Protocol (MCP) server ecosystem draw their samples in ways that quietly select for servers that work: reference sets, popularity lists, hand-curated frames, or pipelines that repair a server until it starts. We report what an unrepaired probability sample actually contains. From a 24,135-server registry census we draw 400 npm/stdio servers with a published seed and probe each one over the wire. Only 48.8% complete an initialize handshake, against 66.7% for a hand-curated frame measured with the same instrument, and the dominant failure is not missing credentials (13.3%) but servers that never start at all (37.5%). Among the 195 that do run, hard conformance is total: zero fatal JSON Schema violations across 2,766 advertised tools. Optional safety annotations are the real variance, and the tool-level omission rate on a random draw is 58.8% against 41.5% on the curated frame, so curation flatters this figure too. We then compare the tool descriptions these servers advertise against two tool-use benchmark corpora using one method held constant. Real MCP tools show 2.8% near-duplication at cosine 0.70, and all of it lies within single servers: cross-author near-duplication is 0.0% at every threshold tested. BFCL v4 shows 16.7%, of which 16.4 points lie between independently presented tasks. UltraTool shows 0.3%, cleaner than real tools, so this is a property of BFCL and not of synthetic corpora as a class. Separately, 68.8% of raw BFCL rows and 85.6% of raw UltraTool rows are exact name-plus-description repeats, against 0.4% for real MCP, so any statistic computed over these releases without global deduplication measures repetition rather than tools. All figures regenerate from released scripts and a published seed.

</details>


### [252] [Engineering Reliable Commit Gates for Agentic AI: Cost-Aware Verification Portfolios under Common-Mode Data Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10969)
*Zihao Zheng,Baichuan Li,Junyi Yao,Jiayu Long*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VP-CONTROL is a runtime-assurance framework for cost-aware commit gates in agentic systems. It addresses the problem where verifiers can inherit the same upstream faults as the systems they verify. The benchmark includes 48 task templates with 2,880 scenarios across six fault regimes. Key findings show that evidence source diversity (40.9 pp impact) matters more than model diversity (11.3 pp). A portfolio controller achieves 1.9% unsafe execution at 5% target, but transfer to unseen fault families yields 16-26% risk. Live tests show full atomic guards and idempotent identifiers prevent unsafe effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic systems commit state-changing actions, but additional verifiers can inherit the same upstream fault, creating a security vulnerability where verification fails to catch errors in the system being verified.

Method: 48 task templates yielding 2,880 scenarios across six fault regimes; fixed-call 2x2 experiment separating verifier-model diversity from evidence-source diversity; cross-model vote over shared evidence vs independent source; portfolio controller using deployment-observable metadata; approximate cluster-adjusted calibration; live HTTP/SQLite study testing concurrent writes and lost responses.

Result: Cross-model vote over shared evidence approves 62.9% of unsafe proposals vs 22.9% with independent source. Source effect is 40.9 percentage points vs 11.3 for model diversity. At 5% per-task target: 1.9% unsafe execution, 38.2% automated safe coverage. Unseen fault families yield 16-26% risk. After-check races defeat verifier-only gates; transactional partial guards prevent only covered failures; full atomic guard records no unsafe effects across 216 episodes. Idempotent request identifiers prevent duplicate effects after lost responses.

Conclusion: Results motivate explicit evidence lineage, cost-aware selection, and commit-time enforcement, while exposing limits of approximate calibration and local-tool generalization. Evidence source diversity is more critical than verifier model diversity for safety.

Abstract: Agentic systems commit state-changing actions, but additional verifiers can inherit the same upstream fault. We present VP-CONTROL, a runtime-assurance design and deterministic benchmark for cost-aware commit gates. Its 48 task templates yield 2,880 scenarios across six fault regimes. A fixed-call 2 x 2 experiment separates verifier-model diversity from evidence-source diversity. On frozen proposals from two local actor families, a cross-model vote over shared evidence approves 62.9% of unsafe proposals, versus 22.9% with an independent source.
